[ Direct Connect ] What AWS service can be used to monitor the health and performance of a Direct Connect connection? ; AWS CloudWatch with Direct Connect metrics: ConnectionState (UP/DOWN), ConnectionBpsIngress/Egress, ConnectionPpsIngress/Egress, ConnectionLightLevelTx/Rx (optical signal strength). Also AWS Direct Connect console provides connection status. <br><br> *ConnectionState is the most critical metric: value 1 = connection up, value 0 = connection down. Set an alarm to notify operations immediately on state change.<br> *ConnectionLightLevelTx/Rx monitors the optical fiber signal; degrading light levels indicate physical layer issues before the connection fully drops, enabling proactive maintenance.<br> *AWS Personal Health Dashboard provides advance notice of planned DX maintenance; subscribe to Health events for your DX connections to receive proactive notifications.<br>

104. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the default value of the "Default Route Table Association" feature on a new Transit Gateway, and what does it mean? ; Enabled by default. Every new attachment automatically associates with the TGW's default route table without requiring manual configuration. <br><br> *When enabled, attachments inherit routing from the default route table immediately upon creation — useful for simple flat-network topologies where all VPCs should be able to reach each other.<br> *When disabled, new attachments have no route table association and therefore cannot route any traffic until you manually associate them with a specific TGW route table.<br> *Turn OFF default association when implementing network segmentation so you can explicitly control which route table each attachment uses.<br>

105. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the default value of the "Default Route Table Propagation" feature, and what risk exists when it is enabled? ; Enabled by default. Every new attachment automatically propagates its CIDR routes into the TGW default route table. The risk: all VPCs can see all routes, creating a flat network with no isolation between environments. <br><br> *With both default association and propagation ON, every new VPC attachment immediately becomes reachable from every other attachment — suitable for development/test, problematic for production isolation.<br> *The risk is accidental reachability: a new dev VPC attached to TGW automatically gains routing to prod VPCs if default propagation is enabled.<br> *Turn OFF propagation and manually control which attachments propagate routes into which route tables to enforce network segmentation.<br>

106. [ Transit Gateway ] A company must enable multicast routing in their AWS network for a financial market data feed. They currently have an existing Transit Gateway. What must they do? ; They cannot enable multicast on the existing TGW. They must create a NEW Transit Gateway with Multicast Support enabled, then migrate all attachments to the new TGW. <br><br> *Multicast Support is the only TGW feature that cannot be changed after creation — it must be enabled at creation time and is immutable thereafter.<br> *Migrating to a new TGW requires: create new TGW with multicast enabled, re-attach all VPCs and VPN/DX connections, update all route tables, test routing, then delete old TGW.<br> *Plan multicast requirements upfront — retroactive enablement requires a full TGW migration, which is a significant operational effort for large environments.<br>

107. [ Transit Gateway ] What protocol does AWS Transit Gateway multicast support use for group membership management? ; IGMPv2 (Internet Group Management Protocol version 2). TGW also supports static multicast group membership configuration without IGMP. <br><br> *IGMPv2 allows multicast receivers (group members) to dynamically join and leave multicast groups; sources send to the multicast group IP and TGW delivers to all members.<br> *Static membership is useful when clients cannot send IGMP join messages — you manually configure which ENIs are members of which multicast groups in the TGW console.<br> *Multicast traffic does NOT traverse TGW peering — multicast is constrained to within a single TGW's attached VPCs in the same region.<br>

108. [ Transit Gateway ] What does "DNS Support" on a Transit Gateway enable, and what VPC-level settings are required? ; DNS Support enables resolution of EC2 public DNS hostnames to their private IPs when queried from other VPCs attached to the same TGW. Requires VPC "Enable DNS Hostnames" and "Enable DNS Support" to be ON in the querying VPC. <br><br> *Without DNS Support, an EC2 public hostname (e.g., ec2-1-2-3-4.compute-1.amazonaws.com) resolves to the public IP even when queried from within the AWS network — traffic would egress and re-enter unnecessarily.<br> *With DNS Support enabled, the same hostname resolves to the private IP, keeping traffic on the private network path through TGW.<br> *If VPC-level DNS settings are disabled (EnableDnsHostnames=false or EnableDnsSupport=false), the TGW DNS Support flag has no effect.<br>

109. [ Transit Gateway ] How does "Security Groups Referencing" on Transit Gateway differ from standard Security Group behavior? ; Standard SGs only reference SG IDs within the same VPC. With TGW SG Referencing enabled, SG rules in VPC-A can reference SG IDs from VPC-B (connected via TGW), allowing cross-VPC micro-segmentation without CIDR-based rules. <br><br> *This feature is useful when you want to say "allow traffic from the app-server SG in VPC-B" instead of "allow traffic from 10.2.0.0/24" — SG-based rules are more precise and don't break when CIDRs change.<br> *Security Groups Referencing only works within the same AWS Region — it does not work across TGW peering to another region or across inter-region scenarios.<br> *Both the source SG (in VPC-B) and the destination SG (in VPC-A) must be in VPCs attached to the same TGW, and TGW SG Referencing must be enabled on that TGW.<br>

110. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the purpose of "VPN ECMP Support" on Transit Gateway, and what is required for it to work? ; VPN ECMP (Equal-Cost Multi-Path) enables load-balancing of traffic across multiple Site-to-Site VPN tunnels that advertise the same prefixes. Requires BGP — static VPN routing does not participate in ECMP. <br><br> *Each VPN tunnel supports up to 1.25 Gbps; with ECMP enabled and 8 tunnels advertising the same prefix, aggregate bandwidth reaches ~10 Gbps.<br> *BGP must advertise identical prefixes on all tunnels that should be ECMP-balanced; if one tunnel advertises a more specific route, traffic prefers that tunnel (longer prefix wins).<br> *Static VPN connections do not participate in ECMP even when the feature is enabled — ECMP only applies to BGP-advertised routes.<br>

111. [ Transit Gateway ] What are the private ASN ranges for BGP, and what is the default TGW Amazon-side ASN? ; 16-bit private: 64512–65534. 32-bit private: 4200000000–4294967294. Default TGW Amazon-side ASN: 64512. <br><br> *Private ASNs are not routable on the public internet — they are used for BGP sessions within private networks, just like RFC1918 private IPs.<br> *The TGW Amazon-side ASN can be customized at creation time but cannot be changed later without recreating the TGW; choose your ASN carefully, especially in multi-TGW environments where each TGW should have a unique ASN.<br> *On-premises ASN must differ from the TGW ASN — BGP requires different ASNs on both sides of an eBGP session; using the same ASN on both sides would be iBGP which is not supported here.<br>

112. [ Transit Gateway ] A company has a VPC peering mesh with 10 VPCs (45 peering connections). They want to add 5 more VPCs. How many connections would be needed without and with TGW? ; Without TGW: 15 VPCs would need 105 peering connections (N*(N-1)/2 = 15*14/2). With TGW: 15 attachments total. <br><br> *VPC peering is non-transitive: if A-peers-B and B-peers-C, A cannot route to C via B — each pair needs a direct peering connection.<br> *Transit Gateway solves this: every attachment can route to every other attachment through the TGW, with a single attachment per VPC instead of N-1 peering connections.<br> *The complexity argument for TGW becomes compelling at 4+ VPCs; below that, VPC peering may be simpler and cheaper (no attachment hourly costs).<br>

113. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the difference between a TGW route table Association and Propagation? ; Association: links one attachment to ONE route table — this RT determines how traffic FROM that attachment is routed. Propagation: attachment advertises its CIDRs INTO a route table — can propagate into MULTIPLE route tables simultaneously. <br><br> *An attachment can be associated with only one route table (1:1 relationship) but can propagate into many route tables (1:many).<br> *Example: Prod VPC associated with Prod RT, propagates into Prod RT AND Shared-Services RT. This lets Shared Services reach Prod, but Prod only routes via Prod RT rules.<br> *Static routes in a TGW route table can override or supplement propagated routes; propagated routes are automatically removed when an attachment is removed.<br>

114. [ Transit Gateway ] How can you prevent Dev VPCs from communicating with Prod VPCs while allowing both to access a Shared Services VPC via TGW? ; Create 3 TGW route tables: Prod-RT, Dev-RT, SharedSvc-RT. Associate Prod VPCs with Prod-RT, Dev with Dev-RT, Shared Services with SharedSvc-RT. Prod and Shared propagate into each other's RTs. Dev and Shared propagate into each other's RTs. Prod and Dev do NOT propagate into each other's RTs. <br><br> *Prod-RT has routes to Prod VPCs + Shared Services, but NOT to Dev VPCs — so Prod traffic can never reach Dev.<br> *Dev-RT has routes to Dev VPCs + Shared Services, but NOT to Prod VPCs — isolation is enforced at the TGW routing layer.<br> *SharedSvc-RT has routes to both Prod and Dev, allowing the shared services to respond to requests from either environment.<br>

115. [ Transit Gateway ] What is a TGW blackhole route and when is it used? ; A blackhole route explicitly drops traffic matching a specific destination CIDR, sending it to /dev/null. Used to block specific traffic patterns or prevent routing to certain destinations without removing propagated routes. <br><br> *Example: Add a blackhole route for 192.168.100.0/24 in the Prod RT to ensure Prod VPCs cannot reach a specific internal network segment, even if that CIDR appears elsewhere.<br> *Blackhole routes follow longest-prefix match: a specific blackhole route (/28) takes precedence over a more general allow route (/16).<br> *Useful for implementing deny-list routing policies in TGW route tables where you cannot use NACLs or SGs (e.g., blocking specific on-premises subnets from reaching cloud workloads).<br>

116. [ Transit Gateway ] What is a Connect attachment on Transit Gateway and when should it be used instead of a VPN attachment? ; Connect attachment enables SD-WAN appliances (Cisco, Fortinet, VMware) to connect to TGW via GRE tunnel with BGP. Use when you need more than 1.25 Gbps per tunnel or require advanced BGP capabilities with SD-WAN appliances. <br><br> *Connect attachment supports up to 20 Gbps per Connect peer (16x more than a single VPN tunnel) and up to 5 Connect peers per Connect attachment = 100 Gbps potential.<br> *Connect requires a Transport attachment (VPC or DX) as an underlay — the GRE tunnel runs over the VPC network, unlike VPN which connects directly to TGW.<br> *Connect is the recommended path for SD-WAN modernization: replace legacy hub-and-spoke MPLS with TGW + Connect for higher bandwidth at lower cost.<br>

117. [ Transit Gateway ] Can you attach two VPCs with the same CIDR block to the same Transit Gateway? ; Yes, you can attach them, but you cannot route between them because TGW uses longest-prefix matching and cannot distinguish between two identical CIDR blocks. Routing to overlapping CIDRs is undefined behavior. <br><br> *TGW does not prevent attachment of overlapping CIDRs — it is your responsibility to ensure non-overlapping CIDRs for any VPCs that need to communicate through the TGW.<br> *If two attachments have the same CIDR propagated to the same RT, TGW behavior is undefined and one route will shadow the other.<br> *Use Private NAT Gateway in front of an overlapping VPC to translate its addresses before routing via TGW — this is the workaround for legacy acquisitions with overlapping CIDRs.<br>

118. [ Transit Gateway ] How does inter-region TGW Peering differ from intra-region TGW attachments in terms of routing? ; Intra-region: dynamic routing via BGP propagation from VPN/DX attachments. Inter-region TGW Peering: STATIC routes only — you must manually add static routes in each TGW's route table for the remote TGW's CIDRs. No BGP between TGWs. <br><br> *This means any new VPC added to the remote TGW does NOT automatically appear in the local TGW's route table — you must manually update static routes.<br> *Inter-region TGW peering traffic is automatically encrypted on the AWS backbone — no additional encryption needed.<br> *For dynamic multi-region routing, consider using your on-premises BGP as the routing exchange point — both regional TGWs connect via DX/VPN to on-prem, which redistributes routes between them.<br>

119. [ Transit Gateway ] What charges are incurred for a Transit Gateway VPC attachment that has no traffic? ; You still pay the attachment-hour fee ($0.05/hr per attachment = ~$36/month) even with zero traffic. Data processing fee ($0.02/GB) only applies when traffic flows. <br><br> *TGW attachment costs are incurred regardless of utilization — an idle VPC attachment costs the same as a heavily used one per hour.<br> *For small environments with few VPCs and low traffic, VPC Peering (pay only for data transfer, no hourly attachment fee) can be significantly cheaper than TGW.<br> *The TGW cost model favors high-traffic, many-VPC environments where the centralization benefits and reduced operational complexity justify the attachment fees.<br>

120. [ Transit Gateway ] A company wants to share a Transit Gateway across multiple AWS accounts in their organization. What AWS service enables this? ; AWS Resource Access Manager (RAM). The TGW owner shares it with specific accounts or the entire AWS Organization; member accounts can then create attachments to the shared TGW. <br><br> *RAM sharing enables the centralized network account pattern: one account owns the TGW, all other accounts attach their VPCs — network topology is centrally managed.<br> *Sharing with the entire organization (aws:PrincipalOrgID condition) means new accounts automatically can attach to the TGW without additional sharing configuration.<br> *The TGW owner manages route tables, associations, and propagations; spoke account owners manage their VPC attachment requests (which the TGW owner can auto-accept or manually approve).<br>

121. [ Transit Gateway ] What is TGW Network Manager and when should it be used? ; TGW Network Manager is a centralized management and monitoring service for global networks built on TGW. It provides topology visualization, event monitoring, and integration with SD-WAN devices across multiple regions and accounts. <br><br> *Network Manager creates a global network view that aggregates all TGWs, VPCs, VPN connections, and DX connections across all regions and accounts in one dashboard.<br> *It monitors network performance using CloudWatch metrics and generates events for connection state changes, helping operations teams quickly identify and diagnose connectivity issues.<br> *Use TGW Network Manager when you have TGWs in multiple regions and need a single pane of glass for network operations and troubleshooting.<br>

122. [ Transit Gateway ] How does routing work when a VPC attached to TGW needs to send traffic to the internet and the TGW has a centralized egress VPC? ; Spoke VPC route table: 0.0.0.0/0 → TGW attachment. TGW route table (for spoke): 0.0.0.0/0 → Egress VPC attachment. Egress VPC route table: 0.0.0.0/0 → NAT GW, plus return routes for spoke CIDRs → TGW attachment. <br><br> *The routing is hairpin: spoke VPC → TGW → Egress VPC → NAT GW → IGW → Internet. Return traffic: Internet → IGW → Egress VPC → TGW → spoke VPC.<br> *The Egress VPC must have return routes for all spoke CIDR ranges pointing back to the TGW, otherwise return traffic is dropped (asymmetric routing fails).<br> *This centralized pattern is cost-effective when you have 5+ VPCs: one set of NAT GW + IGW resources shared vs one per VPC, offset by TGW data processing charges.<br>

123. [ Transit Gateway ] A company is migrating from a VPC peering mesh to Transit Gateway. What is the safest migration approach? ; Add TGW attachments to all VPCs, configure TGW route tables, then test connectivity via TGW. Once validated, update VPC route tables to prefer TGW routes over peering, verify, then remove peering connections. <br><br> *VPC peering and TGW can coexist simultaneously — both provide routes, and the most-specific route wins in VPC route tables.<br> *Running both in parallel during migration provides rollback capability: if TGW routing has issues, peering routes still function as a fallback.<br> *After removing peering connections, validate all application connectivity one final time before decommissioning; peering connections are easy to recreate if needed during a short rollback window.<br>

124. [ Transit Gateway ] What happens to TGW routing when a VPN attachment loses its BGP session? ; The VPN attachment's propagated routes are automatically withdrawn from all TGW route tables where they were propagated. Traffic that was routing to that VPN destination will find no route (or fall back to less-specific routes). <br><br> *TGW automatically removes dynamically propagated routes when the source attachment's BGP session goes down — this is standard BGP behavior applied to TGW propagation.<br> *Static routes in TGW route tables are NOT automatically removed when an attachment fails — static routes persist even when the attachment is unreachable.<br> *For automatic failover, ensure you have backup propagated routes (e.g., second VPN attachment or DX attachment) so that when primary fails, backup routes are already in the route table.<br>

125. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the maximum number of attachments supported per Transit Gateway? ; 5,000 attachments (default soft limit). This includes VPC, VPN, DX Gateway, TGW peering, and Connect attachments combined. <br><br> *This limit can be raised via AWS Support request for very large enterprise deployments.<br> *At 5,000 VPC attachments, TGW serves a massive multi-account organization; most enterprises never approach this limit.<br> *Each attachment in each AZ counts separately for subnet attachments within a VPC — a VPC with 3 subnets in 3 AZs uses 3 attachment slots in that AZ dimension.<br>

126. [ Transit Gateway ] How does TGW handle AZ-specific routing for VPC attachments? ; TGW creates an ENI in each subnet specified during VPC attachment. For best performance and to avoid cross-AZ charges, attach subnets in all AZs your workloads use, and configure instances/resources to use TGW attachment ENIs in their same AZ. <br><br> *Traffic entering TGW from a subnet in AZ-a uses the TGW ENI in AZ-a; if the destination VPC only has a TGW subnet in AZ-b, traffic crosses AZs (which incurs $0.01/GB charge).<br> *Best practice: attach at least one subnet per AZ in each VPC — use dedicated /28 subnets specifically for TGW attachment ENIs to keep them separate from workload subnets.<br> *AWS recommends NOT using the same subnet for TGW attachment and EC2 instances — dedicated subnets for TGW attachments simplify routing and security.<br>

127. [ Transit Gateway ] Can a Transit Gateway attachment VPC also have a Virtual Private Gateway (VGW) attached simultaneously? ; A VPC can attach to both a TGW and have a VGW, but you should not use both for the same routing purpose simultaneously as it creates complex routing. Use one or the other for on-premises connectivity per VPC. <br><br> *VGW attaches directly to the VPC for DX or VPN; TGW can also provide on-premises connectivity via its own DX/VPN attachments — mixing both in the same VPC for the same purpose creates routing conflicts.<br> *A valid use case is having VGW for a direct DX to one VPC and TGW for connecting that VPC to others — but the routing must be carefully planned to avoid loops.<br> *When migrating from VGW-based DX to TGW-based DX, run both temporarily, then remove VGW once TGW path is validated.<br>

128. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the difference between a TGW route table propagation from a VPC attachment vs from a VPN attachment? ; VPC attachment: propagates the VPC's CIDR block(s) as static routes (no BGP). VPN attachment: propagates routes received via BGP from the on-premises router (dynamic routes). <br><br> *VPC CIDR propagation is automatic and static — the VPC's CIDR is known at attachment time and propagated as a fixed route; you cannot filter which VPC subnets are propagated.<br> *VPN BGP propagation is dynamic — on-premises routers advertise specific prefixes via BGP, and these appear in TGW route tables; prefix filtering via route policies can control what's propagated.<br> *DX Gateway attachments also use BGP propagation for on-premises routes, similar to VPN attachments.<br>

129. [ Transit Gateway ] A company needs to inspect ALL traffic (north-south internet AND east-west between VPCs) centrally. What TGW architecture achieves this? ; Use TGW with 3 route table domains: Inspection RT (for inspection VPC), Spoke RT (for all spoke VPCs), Egress RT (for egress VPC). All spoke traffic routes to Inspection VPC first, then to destination. Internet egress also routes via Inspection VPC before Egress VPC. <br><br> *The inspection VPC hosts AWS Network Firewall (or GWLB + appliance) and has two TGW attachments or uses routing to enforce traffic flows through the inspection layer.<br> *For east-west: Spoke-A → TGW (Spoke RT) → Inspection VPC → TGW (Inspection RT) → Spoke-B. For north-south: Spoke → TGW → Inspection VPC → TGW → Egress VPC → IGW.<br> *This is the most secure but most complex architecture — adds latency and cost; justified for compliance requirements (PCI-DSS, financial, government).<br>

130. [ Transit Gateway ] What is Gateway Load Balancer (GWLB) and how does it integrate with TGW for security inspection? ; GWLB distributes traffic to a fleet of third-party network appliances transparently using GENEVE protocol (encapsulation), preserving original source/destination IPs. It integrates with TGW via a service VPC pattern where spoke VPCs route to GWLB endpoint in an inspection VPC. <br><br> *GWLB uses GENEVE tunneling to send the original packet (unchanged) to firewall appliances and return the inspected packet — the appliance sees real IPs for stateful inspection.<br> *Unlike traditional load balancers (ALB/NLB), GWLB operates at Layer 3 and is transparent — it doesn't terminate connections, it transparently routes packets through appliances.<br> *GWLB + TGW is the standard architecture for enterprise-grade centralized inspection with third-party firewalls (Palo Alto, Check Point, Fortinet) in AWS.<br>

131. [ Transit Gateway ] How many VPN tunnels can a single Site-to-Site VPN attachment to TGW have, and what is the bandwidth? ; Each VPN connection has 2 tunnels (active/active or active/standby). Each tunnel supports up to 1.25 Gbps. With ECMP enabled, both tunnels active = 2.5 Gbps. Multiple VPN connections with ECMP can aggregate further. <br><br> *Two tunnels per VPN connection is for HA — one tunnel per AZ on the AWS side. Both can be active simultaneously if ECMP is enabled and BGP is configured.<br> *To get more than 2.5 Gbps, add additional VPN connections to the TGW; with ECMP and BGP, traffic distributes across all tunnels from all connections.<br> *For bandwidth beyond what VPN provides (10+ Gbps), use Direct Connect (up to 100 Gbps per connection) or Connect attachment (up to 20 Gbps per peer).<br>

132. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the maximum aggregate bandwidth for a single Transit Gateway? ; 50 Gbps burst bandwidth. This is the maximum burst, not sustained; sustained bandwidth depends on attachment types and traffic patterns. <br><br> *50 Gbps is the aggregate burst limit across all attachments combined, not per-attachment.<br> *Individual attachment limits: VPC = limited by EC2/instance bandwidth, VPN = 1.25 Gbps/tunnel, DX = up to 100 Gbps (limited by DX connection), Connect = 20 Gbps/peer.<br> *For very high bandwidth requirements (hundreds of Gbps), consider multiple TGWs with TGW peering or dedicated DX connections per VPC instead of routing everything through one TGW.<br>

133. [ Transit Gateway ] What happens to existing TGW route table entries when the TGW default route table propagation is disabled? ; Existing entries are NOT removed. Disabling the feature only affects NEW attachments going forward — existing propagated routes remain in the route tables until manually removed or the attachment is detached. <br><br> *Feature flags affect future behavior, not existing state — you must manually clean up existing propagated routes after changing the flag if you want to remove them.<br> *This means disabling default propagation on a live TGW is safe — it doesn't break existing connectivity but prevents new attachments from auto-propagating.<br> *To remove existing propagated routes after changing the flag: go to each TGW route table, find the propagation from each attachment, and disable/remove it manually.<br>

134. [ Transit Gateway ] A company uses Direct Connect with a Transit VIF and a DX Gateway attached to TGW. What BGP ASN is required on the customer router? ; The customer router BGP ASN must be different from both the DX Gateway ASN and the TGW Amazon-side ASN. The DX Gateway itself is transparent to BGP — the BGP session is between the customer router and the TGW's BGP endpoint. <br><br> *DX Gateway does not have its own BGP ASN in the routing sense — it passes BGP between the customer router and the TGW attachment.<br> *The TGW Amazon-side ASN (default 64512) must differ from the customer router ASN; AWS will reject a BGP session where both sides use the same ASN.<br> *If TGW uses ASN 64512 (default), configure your on-premises router with any other private ASN (e.g., 65001) or a public ASN if you have one assigned.<br>

135. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the TGW Connect attachment transport requirement? ; Connect attachment requires an existing VPC attachment or DX attachment as the TRANSPORT — the GRE tunnel for Connect runs over this transport attachment. Connect cannot exist without an underlying transport. <br><br> *The Connect attachment creates a GRE tunnel over the transport attachment (VPC) — the SD-WAN appliance in the VPC terminates the GRE tunnel and runs BGP with TGW Connect peer.<br> *This means the SD-WAN appliance must be deployed in a VPC that is already attached to the TGW; it cannot connect via VPN tunnel directly.<br> *The advantage: Connect achieves up to 20 Gbps per peer (vs 1.25 Gbps per VPN tunnel) while still supporting dynamic BGP routing, making it ideal for high-bandwidth SD-WAN migrations.<br>

136. [ Transit Gateway ] When is VPC Peering more cost-effective than Transit Gateway? ; VPC Peering is more cost-effective when you have 3 or fewer VPCs with moderate traffic. VPC Peering has no attachment fee — you only pay $0.01/GB for cross-AZ data. TGW costs $0.05/hr per attachment (~$36/month each) plus $0.02/GB. <br><br> *For 2 VPCs, peering costs = only cross-AZ data transfer. For same 2 VPCs on TGW = 2 × $36/month + data processing fees — TGW costs more unless traffic is very high.<br> *The break-even point is roughly 4-5 VPCs where managing multiple peering connections becomes complex AND the attachment cost is offset by simplified management and routing flexibility.<br> *For large organizations with 10+ VPCs, TGW almost always wins on both cost (fewer NAT GWs, centralized egress) and operational simplicity.<br>

137. [ Transit Gateway ] What is required in a spoke VPC's route table to enable routing to other VPCs via TGW? ; Add routes in the VPC's route table with destination = other VPCs' CIDRs (or 0.0.0.0/0 for default) and target = TGW attachment ID. The TGW attachment in the VPC must also be active. <br><br> *VPC route tables are separate from TGW route tables — both must be correctly configured for end-to-end routing to work.<br> *VPC side: 10.2.0.0/16 → tgw-attach-xxx. TGW side: TGW route table must have route for 10.2.0.0/16 → correct attachment. Both must be in place.<br> *A common misconfiguration: TGW route table is correct but VPC route table still points to old peering connection or has no route to TGW — always check both ends when troubleshooting.<br>

138. [ Transit Gateway ] What monitoring metrics are available for Transit Gateway in CloudWatch? ; Key metrics: BytesIn, BytesOut, BytesDropCountBlackhole (traffic matching blackhole routes), BytesDropCountNoRoute (traffic with no matching route), PacketsIn, PacketsOut. <br><br> *BytesDropCountNoRoute is the most useful for troubleshooting: it indicates traffic arriving at TGW with no matching route — a sign of missing routes in the TGW route table or spoke VPC route tables.<br> *BytesDropCountBlackhole shows how much traffic is being intentionally dropped by blackhole routes — useful for validating that security-driven blackhole routes are working as intended.<br> *TGW metrics are published per attachment (VPC, VPN, etc.) — you can monitor each attachment's traffic independently to identify top talkers and unused attachments.<br>

139. [ Transit Gateway ] A company wants to connect offices in London, Tokyo, and New York to AWS workloads in eu-west-1, ap-northeast-1, and us-east-1. What TGW architecture minimizes complexity? ; Deploy one TGW per region. Connect regional offices to their nearest TGW via VPN or DX. Connect the 3 TGWs via TGW Inter-Region Peering. Add static routes for remote regions in each TGW. <br><br> *Each office connects to its local TGW for optimal latency; TGW peering provides cross-region connectivity for workloads in different regions to communicate.<br> *Static routes are required for inter-region peering — you must manually add remote region CIDR routes pointing to the TGW peering attachment.<br> *This is the standard multi-region global enterprise network architecture on AWS, replacing MPLS hub-and-spoke with TGW hub-and-spoke per region.<br>

140. [ Transit Gateway ] Can you modify the Amazon-side ASN of a Transit Gateway after creation? ; No. The TGW Amazon-side ASN is immutable — it cannot be changed after TGW creation. To change it, you must create a new TGW with the desired ASN and migrate all attachments. <br><br> *Choose the TGW ASN carefully at creation time, especially in environments with multiple TGWs or multi-region deployments where ASN uniqueness matters for BGP.<br> *The default ASN is 64512; if your organization has multiple TGWs that peer with each other or with on-premises using BGP, each TGW should have a unique ASN.<br> *For on-premises BGP sessions via VPN or DX, the customer router's ASN must always differ from the TGW's ASN — same ASN on both ends prevents BGP session establishment.<br>

141. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the difference between TGW route table "static routes" and "propagated routes"? ; Static routes: manually added by administrator, persist until manually deleted, take precedence over propagated routes of same prefix length. Propagated routes: dynamically added by TGW from attachment CIDRs (VPC) or BGP (VPN/DX), automatically removed when attachment detaches or BGP session drops. <br><br> *When both a static route and a propagated route exist for the same CIDR, the static route takes precedence — this is useful for route override scenarios.<br> *Static routes are used for inter-region peering (which doesn't support BGP propagation) and for adding default routes (0.0.0.0/0) pointing to an egress VPC.<br> *Propagated routes provide the dynamic routing backbone — especially important for VPN/DX where on-premises prefixes change and you don't want to manually update TGW route tables.<br>

142. [ Transit Gateway ] How should TGW subnet attachments be sized and configured in a VPC? ; Create dedicated /28 subnets specifically for TGW ENIs in each AZ. These subnets should only contain TGW attachment ENIs — no EC2 instances or other resources. Associate them with a route table that has only the local VPC route. <br><br> *Using /28 subnets is sufficient since each TGW attachment only needs one IP per AZ in the attachment subnet; smaller subnets reserve IP space for other uses.<br> *Dedicated subnets prevent Security Group rules from accidentally blocking TGW routing and simplify troubleshooting — you always know TGW ENIs are in dedicated subnets.<br> *The TGW attachment subnet route table should have only a local route — default routes pointing outward create routing loops.<br>

143. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the AWS recommendation for network account architecture with Transit Gateway in an AWS Organization? ; Create a dedicated Network/Connectivity Account that owns the TGW, DX connections, VPN connections, firewall/inspection VPCs, and centralized egress. Share TGW via RAM to all other accounts. Spoke account VPCs attach to the shared TGW. <br><br> *The Network Account pattern is part of AWS Landing Zone and Control Tower best practices: centralize connectivity resources, separate from workload accounts.<br> *This prevents workload account admins from accidentally misconfiguring TGW route tables or attaching unauthorized VPCs — network changes require access to the Network Account.<br> *The Network Account typically also owns Route 53 Resolver endpoints, DX, centralized logging VPCs, and security inspection infrastructure.<br>

144. [ Transit Gateway ] Can TGW route tables have both IPv4 and IPv6 routes? ; Yes. TGW supports dual-stack routing — you can have both IPv4 CIDR routes and IPv6 CIDR routes in the same TGW route table. <br><br> *VPC attachments propagate both IPv4 and IPv6 CIDR blocks if the VPC has IPv6 enabled — both appear as separate routes in the TGW route table.<br> *IPv6 routing through TGW follows the same rules as IPv4 (longest prefix match, association, propagation) but IPv6 addresses are used as destinations.<br> *Ensure TGW subnet attachments in VPCs with IPv6 have both IPv4 and IPv6 routes configured in their VPC route tables to support dual-stack traffic correctly.<br>

145. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the TGW peering attachment pricing model? ; Both the requester and acceptor TGW pay $0.05/hr for the peering attachment on their side (both sides pay). Inter-region data transfer charges apply on top (standard AWS inter-region rates). <br><br> *Both TGWs incur the attachment-hour fee — unlike VPC Peering where you only pay for data transfer, TGW peering has a fixed hourly cost per side.<br> *Inter-region data transfer charges are separate and depend on the source/destination regions — they are the same as standard inter-region transfer rates.<br> *For frequent small transfers across many regions, TGW peering fixed costs may outweigh the data transfer charges — evaluate total cost including attachment hours before deciding on inter-region TGW peering vs other options.<br>

146. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the maximum number of TGW route tables per Transit Gateway? ; 20 route tables per TGW by default (soft limit, can be increased). <br><br> *20 route tables supports significant segmentation — for example: 5 environment domains × 4 route tables per domain (prod inbound, prod outbound, dev, shared services).<br> *If your segmentation requirements exceed 20 route tables, consider whether multiple TGWs (with TGW peering between them) would better model your network topology.<br> *Each route table supports up to 10,000 routes by default, which is sufficient for large enterprise networks with hundreds of subnets.<br>

147. [ Transit Gateway ] A spoke VPC has traffic that should go to the internet for some destinations and to on-premises for others. Both routes are 0.0.0.0/0 — which wins? ; 0.0.0.0/0 is the least specific route. If both exist in the same TGW route table with the same prefix, the behavior is route conflict — only one 0.0.0.0/0 can exist per route table. Separate them with more-specific routes or separate route tables. <br><br> *TGW route tables only support ONE default route (0.0.0.0/0) — you cannot have two 0.0.0.0/0 entries pointing to different attachments in the same route table.<br> *The solution: use more specific routes for on-premises destinations (e.g., 172.16.0.0/12 → VPN attachment, 0.0.0.0/0 → Egress VPC for internet).<br> *Or: use separate TGW route tables per traffic type — internet-bound traffic associated with Egress RT, on-premises-bound traffic routed via more-specific routes.<br>

148. [ Transit Gateway ] How does Transit Gateway support AWS PrivateLink? ; TGW does not directly replace PrivateLink. They serve different purposes. However, you can access PrivateLink Interface Endpoints in one VPC from other VPCs via TGW if the endpoint VPC is a shared hub and proper DNS is configured. <br><br> *Interface Endpoints (PrivateLink) exist in specific VPCs; to access them from other VPCs, those VPCs must either have their own Interface Endpoints or route via TGW to the VPC that has the endpoints.<br> *Centralized Interface Endpoints in a shared services VPC accessed via TGW is a cost optimization: one set of endpoints shared across many VPCs instead of per-VPC endpoints.<br> *Private DNS for centralized endpoints requires custom Route 53 Resolver rules in spoke VPCs forwarding AWS service domains to the endpoint VPC — DNS Private DNS feature only works within the same VPC by default.<br>

149. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the impact of enabling VPN ECMP support on an existing Transit Gateway that already has VPN attachments? ; Enabling ECMP affects future BGP path selection for VPN attachments. Existing active/standby VPN connections with BGP will potentially start using ECMP (both tunnels active) if they advertise the same prefixes. You should test the impact before enabling in production. <br><br> *ECMP changes traffic flow for BGP-based VPN connections that have equal-cost paths — both tunnels become active/active instead of active/standby.<br> *Applications sensitive to packet reordering may experience issues when ECMP distributes packets across different paths with slightly different latencies — test with your specific workloads.<br> *VPN connections using static routing are unaffected by ECMP enablement — only BGP-advertised routes participate in ECMP load balancing.<br>

150. [ Transit Gateway ] How can you test TGW connectivity between two VPCs without deploying EC2 instances? ; Use AWS Reachability Analyzer to test path connectivity between source and destination ENIs. Specify ENIs in both VPCs and it analyzes the route path through TGW, reporting any routing gaps or security group/NACL blocks. <br><br> *Reachability Analyzer is a configuration analysis tool — it doesn't send actual traffic, it analyzes route tables, security groups, and NACLs to determine if a path exists.<br> *It specifically models TGW routing, including TGW route tables, associations, and propagations — it will identify if a TGW route table is missing an entry or if an attachment is not associated.<br> *For actual traffic testing, use VPC Flow Logs enabled on both VPCs and send test traffic — flow logs will show ACCEPT or REJECT status for each packet flow.<br>

151. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the AWS Transit Gateway Network Manager's "global network" concept? ; A global network is a logical container in TGW Network Manager that represents your entire network topology, including TGWs across all regions and accounts, DX connections, VPN connections, and on-premises devices. <br><br> *You register TGWs from multiple regions and accounts into a single global network — Network Manager then builds a topology map of all connections and their states.<br> *SD-WAN devices (registered via Connect attachment) appear in the global network alongside cloud resources, providing a unified view of hybrid network topology.<br> *Network Manager integrates with CloudWatch Events to generate events for network state changes (connection up/down), enabling centralized alerting for the entire global network.<br>

152. [ Transit Gateway ] Can you delete a TGW route table that has active associations or propagations? ; No. You must first remove all associations and propagations from the route table before it can be deleted. Attempting to delete a route table with active associations fails. <br><br> *The dependency order for TGW cleanup: remove associations → remove propagations → delete static routes → delete route table → delete attachments → delete TGW.<br> *This safety mechanism prevents accidental disruption of routing by deleting a route table that VPCs are actively using.<br> *The default TGW route table cannot be deleted even when empty — it is a system-managed table.<br>

153. [ Transit Gateway ] How does TGW handle failover when both a primary DX attachment and a backup VPN attachment exist? ; TGW uses the BGP route from whichever attachment is advertising the prefix. DX attachment (via DX Gateway) typically has lower MED/better BGP attributes → preferred. When DX BGP session fails, routes are withdrawn from TGW route table and VPN BGP routes remain → failover is automatic. <br><br> *BGP preference in TGW route tables follows standard BGP path selection: routes from DX (typically configured with lower MED) are preferred over VPN routes.<br> *The failover time depends on BGP hold-down timer (default 90 seconds in many configurations, but can be tuned with BFD — Bidirectional Forwarding Detection for sub-second failover).<br> *TGW supports BFD on VPN attachments to detect failures faster than standard BGP timers — configure BFD on both the TGW VPN attachment and the customer router for fastest failover.<br>

154. [ Transit Gateway ] What does the TGW feature "Appliance Mode" do on a VPC attachment? ; Appliance Mode ensures that symmetric (same-attachment) routing is maintained for stateful appliances (firewalls, IDS/IPS) deployed in a VPC. Without it, request and response traffic may use different TGW ENIs, breaking stateful inspection. <br><br> *By default, TGW uses ECMP across availability zone ENIs — request traffic may enter via TGW ENI in AZ-a, but response traffic might exit via AZ-b ENI. Stateful firewalls need both flows on the same ENI.<br> *Appliance Mode (enabled per VPC attachment) ensures all flows between a pair of endpoints use the same TGW ENI in the same AZ, preserving flow symmetry required by stateful appliances.<br> *Enable Appliance Mode on the Inspection VPC attachment when using inline security appliances — without it, stateful inspection fails for flows that use different AZ ENIs for request vs response.<br>

155. [ Transit Gateway ] A company wants to use TGW to provide on-premises access to AWS services like S3 and SQS privately (without internet). How should this be architected? ; Use TGW with DX or VPN attachment for on-premises connectivity. Deploy Interface Endpoints (PrivateLink) for S3 and SQS in a central Hub VPC attached to TGW. Configure Route 53 Resolver Outbound Endpoint in Hub VPC to forward AWS service domain queries from on-premises to Interface Endpoint private IPs. <br><br> *On-premises systems access AWS services via: on-prem → DX/VPN → TGW → Hub VPC → Interface Endpoint → AWS service. All traffic stays on private network.<br> *DNS resolution is the key challenge: on-premises DNS must forward queries for service.region.amazonaws.com to Route 53 Resolver Inbound Endpoint IPs in the Hub VPC, which resolve to Interface Endpoint private IPs.<br> *For S3 specifically: on-premises access requires S3 Interface Endpoint (not Gateway Endpoint) because Gateway Endpoints only work from within VPCs via route table, not from on-premises via DX/VPN.<br>

156. [ Transit Gateway ] What routing tables must be updated when a new VPC is attached to an existing TGW (with default flags OFF)? ; 1) TGW: manually associate the new attachment with appropriate TGW route table. 2) TGW: manually configure propagation (which RTs receive new VPC routes). 3) New VPC: add routes for other VPC CIDRs pointing to TGW attachment. 4) Existing VPCs: add routes for new VPC CIDR pointing to their TGW attachment. <br><br> *With default association/propagation OFF, nothing is automatic — forgetting any of these four steps causes partial or complete connectivity failure.<br> *A checklist approach is essential: TGW RT association, TGW RT propagation (in and out), new VPC RT, existing VPC RTs. Automation (CloudFormation, CDK, or Lambda triggered on TGW attachment) is strongly recommended.<br> *With default flags ON, only steps 3 and 4 (VPC-side route tables) require manual action — TGW-side is automatic, but you lose segmentation control.<br>

157. [ Transit Gateway ] How does Transit Gateway support hybrid DNS resolution from on-premises to AWS workloads? ; On-premises DNS forwards queries for AWS domains (.internal, .compute.internal, specific hostnames) to Route 53 Resolver Inbound Endpoints in a VPC attached to TGW. Resolver Inbound Endpoints are ENIs reachable from on-premises via DX or VPN through TGW. <br><br> *Route 53 Resolver Inbound Endpoints are ENIs with private IPs; on-premises DNS servers send UDP/TCP DNS queries to these IPs over the DX/VPN path that traverses TGW.<br> *The Resolver resolves AWS private DNS names (RDS endpoints, EC2 hostnames, etc.) and returns private IP addresses — enabling on-premises systems to reach cloud resources by DNS name.<br> *Reverse: Route 53 Resolver Outbound Endpoints (also in the VPC) enable VPC instances to forward queries for on-premises domains to on-premises DNS servers via TGW.<br>

158. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the AWS recommended way to handle overlapping CIDRs between VPCs that need to communicate via TGW? ; Use Private NAT Gateway to translate the overlapping VPC's addresses before routing via TGW. Deploy Private NAT GW in a transit/proxy VPC, translate source IP to a non-overlapping range, then route via TGW. <br><br> *Private NAT Gateway (no EIP) performs NAT for traffic within the AWS network — it translates a VPC's overlapping CIDR to a unique CIDR before the packet enters TGW routing.<br> *This is the standard workaround for post-merger/acquisition scenarios where two companies have the same RFC1918 CIDR and need to integrate their AWS environments.<br> *The NAT translation adds complexity and statefulness; document the NAT mapping carefully and use consistent port/address mapping for predictable application behavior.<br>

159. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the behavior when you propagate the same attachment's routes into multiple TGW route tables? ; The attachment's CIDRs appear in all specified route tables simultaneously. Attachments associated with any of those route tables can reach the propagating attachment. This is how shared services accessibility is implemented. <br><br> *Shared Services VPC propagates into ALL route tables (Prod RT, Dev RT, Shared RT) → all VPCs can reach Shared Services regardless of which RT they're associated with.<br> *Prod VPC propagates only into Prod RT and Shared RT → only Prod-associated VPCs and Shared Services can reach Prod, not Dev-associated VPCs.<br> *This asymmetric propagation pattern is the foundation of hub-spoke network segmentation: shared resources propagate everywhere, workload VPCs propagate only where needed.<br>

160. [ Transit Gateway ] A security audit requires proof that Prod VPCs cannot communicate with Dev VPCs via TGW. How would you demonstrate this? ; Show the TGW route tables: Prod RT has no routes to Dev CIDR ranges; Dev RT has no routes to Prod CIDR ranges. Confirm Dev VPCs are not propagated into Prod RT and vice versa. Run AWS Reachability Analyzer between a Prod ENI and a Dev ENI to confirm no path exists. <br><br> *Reachability Analyzer provides a definitive "no path exists" analysis that satisfies security audit requirements — it's not just configuration review, it's active path analysis.<br> *Show the TGW route table screenshot: each table should show its associated attachments and propagated routes — absence of Dev CIDR in Prod RT proves isolation.<br> *Complement with VPC Flow Logs showing no accepted traffic between Prod and Dev CIDR ranges over the audit period — provides historical evidence of network isolation.<br>

161. [ Transit Gateway ] What AWS service can be used to automatically provision TGW routes when a new VPC is attached? ; AWS CloudFormation custom resources, AWS Lambda triggered by EventBridge on TGW attachment creation, or AWS Network Firewall rules policies — depending on the automation requirement. <br><br> *EventBridge can trigger a Lambda function on TGW attachment state changes (created, modified, deleted); Lambda can then update TGW route tables and VPC route tables programmatically via the AWS SDK.<br> *AWS CDK provides TGW constructs (via third-party constructs like cdk-aws-transit-gateway) that automate route table management as infrastructure-as-code.<br> *Organizations using AWS Organizations + CloudFormation StackSets can deploy standardized TGW route configurations to all accounts automatically when new accounts are vended.<br>

162. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the AWS Direct Connect + TGW architectural constraint regarding DX Gateway? ; You cannot attach a Transit Gateway directly to a Direct Connect connection. You MUST use a DX Gateway as the intermediary. The chain is: DX Connection → Transit VIF → DX Gateway → TGW attachment. <br><br> *DX Gateway is mandatory in the DX → TGW integration path — there is no direct DX-to-TGW attachment type; Transit VIF always requires DX Gateway as an intermediate step.<br> *This DX Gateway requirement means you need to plan for it in your architecture: create DX Gateway, associate TGW with it, then create Transit VIF on the DX connection pointing to DX Gateway.<br> *DX Gateway supports associating up to 3 Transit Gateways per DX Gateway; if you need more TGWs connected to one DX connection, multiple DX Gateways may be needed.<br>

163. [ Transit Gateway ] How does TGW handle traffic when a route in the TGW route table has a prefix list as the destination? ; AWS Managed Prefix Lists (e.g., for S3, DynamoDB) can be used as destinations in TGW route tables, just like CIDR ranges. Traffic matching any IP in the prefix list follows that route. <br><br> *Managed Prefix Lists are AWS-maintained lists of IP ranges for AWS services (S3, CloudFront, etc.) — using them in route tables means routes auto-update when AWS changes service IPs.<br> *Customer-managed Prefix Lists can also be created and used in TGW route tables, allowing you to group multiple CIDR ranges under one logical name for easier management.<br> *Prefix list entries count toward the route table limit; each prefix in the list counts as one entry, so a large prefix list can consume significant route table capacity.<br>

164. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the TGW maximum number of routes per route table? ; 10,000 routes per TGW route table by default (soft limit, can be increased via Support). <br><br> *10,000 routes is sufficient for most enterprise environments; each VPC CIDR propagated counts as one route, each VPN BGP-advertised prefix counts as one route.<br> *If you have many on-premises subnets advertised via BGP, route summarization at the on-premises router is critical to stay within limits.<br> *Exceeding this limit causes BGP sessions or propagation to fail for the excess routes, potentially causing routing blackholes for affected prefixes.<br>

165. [ Transit Gateway ] A company has TGW with VPN ECMP enabled. Two VPN connections each with 2 tunnels are all active. How many paths can traffic take simultaneously, and what is the maximum aggregate bandwidth? ; 4 paths (2 tunnels × 2 VPN connections). With ECMP across all 4 tunnels = up to 5 Gbps aggregate (4 × 1.25 Gbps), assuming equal BGP path weight and same prefix advertisement. <br><br> *For ECMP to distribute across all 4 tunnels, all 4 must advertise identical prefixes with equal BGP attributes (same MED, same AS path length) — any difference makes some paths preferred over others.<br> *Maximum with VPN ECMP: AWS allows up to 8 equal-cost VPN paths per TGW → 8 tunnels × 1.25 Gbps = 10 Gbps aggregate.<br> *If you need guaranteed 5+ Gbps, DX (10G dedicated connection) is more reliable than VPN ECMP — VPN bandwidth fluctuates with internet conditions even with ECMP.<br>

166. [ Transit Gateway ] What TGW feature should be enabled when using inline inspection appliances to prevent asymmetric routing? ; Appliance Mode on the inspection VPC attachment. This ensures all flows between a source-destination pair always use the same TGW ENI in the same AZ, maintaining flow symmetry required for stateful appliances. <br><br> *Without Appliance Mode, TGW can use different ENIs (different AZs) for the forward and return packets of the same flow — this breaks stateful firewalls that need to see both directions on the same ENI.<br> *Appliance Mode adds a small amount of cross-AZ traffic (when source and destination are in different AZs, packets may cross AZ to reach the correct inspection ENI) but this is necessary for correctness.<br> *Always enable Appliance Mode on VPC attachments that host stateful inspection appliances (firewalls, IDS/IPS, WAF inline); it has no downside for non-stateful scenarios but adds marginal cost if cross-AZ traffic increases.<br>

167. [ Transit Gateway ] How is TGW different from a traditional Layer 3 router? ; TGW is a cloud-native managed routing service. It does not require hardware provisioning, configuration management, or OS patching. It scales automatically, is highly available across multiple AZs by default, and integrates natively with AWS services via attachments. <br><br> *Traditional router: physical/virtual device requiring OS management, interface configuration, BGP tuning, HA clustering (HSRP/VRRP). TGW: API-driven, no OS, auto-HA, auto-scale.<br> *TGW abstracts network complexity — you manage route tables, attachments, and BGP sessions via AWS Console/API/CLI without touching underlying infrastructure.<br> *The equivalent in Linux would be multiple network namespaces with VRFs, FRRouting BGP daemon, and manual VXLAN/GRE tunnels — TGW provides all of this as a managed service.<br>

168. [ Transit Gateway ] What are the implications of not specifying subnets in all AZs when creating a TGW VPC attachment? ; TGW only places ENIs in AZs where subnets are specified. Traffic from EC2 instances in AZs without TGW ENIs must cross AZs to reach the TGW ENI (incurring $0.01/GB cross-AZ charge) and has higher latency. <br><br> *If AZ-c has EC2 instances but no TGW subnet attachment, traffic routes to TGW ENI in AZ-a or AZ-b, crossing AZ boundaries — both a cost and latency concern.<br> *Best practice: attach subnets in ALL AZs used by the VPC for TGW connectivity, even if those AZs have few resources — the cross-AZ charge savings quickly offset any complexity.<br> *Dedicated /28 TGW attachment subnets in each AZ are the minimal investment for optimal TGW routing performance and cost.<br>

169. [ Transit Gateway ] How does TGW inter-region peering encrypt traffic? ; Traffic traversing TGW inter-region peering is automatically encrypted using AES-256 on the AWS backbone network. No additional configuration is required for encryption. <br><br> *This is a key differentiator from regular VPC Peering (inter-region VPC peering is also encrypted) vs DX inter-region (not encrypted by default).<br> *The encryption is transparent to the application — no VPN tunnel configuration or key management is required; AWS handles all encryption and decryption at the TGW level.<br> *While traffic is encrypted at the TGW level, it still travels over AWS-owned backbone infrastructure; for regulatory requirements that mandate customer-managed encryption keys, add application-layer encryption on top.<br>

170. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the recommended TGW architecture for a company that has AWS workloads in 5 regions and on-premises in 2 data centers? ; Per-region TGW connected to respective on-premises DC via DX or VPN. TGW peering between regions for inter-region traffic. DX Gateway spans all regions from a single DX connection in each DC. Network Manager for global visibility. <br><br> *DX Gateway allows a single DX connection from each DC to connect to TGWs in multiple regions — cost-effective vs a DX connection per region per DC.<br> *TGW peering between the 5 regional TGWs (10 peering connections for full mesh, or hub-spoke with a primary region) enables global east-west connectivity.<br> *Network Manager provides a single topology view across all 5 regions, both DCs, all TGWs and their attachments — essential for operations in a complex global network.<br>

171. [ Transit Gateway ] What is TGW's role in an AWS Landing Zone or Control Tower environment? ; TGW is the central network connectivity component in the Network/Connectivity Account of a Landing Zone. It provides connectivity between all account VPCs (via RAM sharing), to on-premises (via DX/VPN), and enables centralized security inspection and egress. <br><br> *AWS Control Tower's Account Factory includes TGW configuration as part of the network baseline — new accounts automatically get VPCs attached to the shared TGW via automation.<br> *The Landing Zone network account owns: TGW, DX/VPN connections, Route 53 Resolver endpoints, centralized egress NAT GW, and security inspection VPC.<br> *Transit Gateway is the technical foundation that makes the Landing Zone network hub-and-spoke model possible at scale — without TGW, each account's VPC networking would be isolated.<br>

172. [ Transit Gateway ] When using TGW with AWS Network Firewall for centralized inspection, what does the traffic flow look like for east-west traffic between two spoke VPCs? ; Spoke-A → TGW (Spoke RT: send all to Inspection VPC) → Inspection VPC (AWS Network Firewall) → TGW (Inspection RT: send to destination) → Spoke-B. Return path mirrors. <br><br> *TGW route tables must be configured so Spoke RT has 0.0.0.0/0 → Inspection VPC attachment (force all traffic through inspection), and Inspection RT has specific spoke CIDR routes → correct spoke attachments.<br> *Network Firewall deployment in the Inspection VPC uses a VPC route table structure: traffic comes from TGW → Network Firewall endpoint → allowed traffic returns to TGW → destination.<br> *Appliance Mode must be enabled on the Inspection VPC attachment to ensure the same TGW ENI handles both directions of a flow, maintaining stateful inspection state.<br>

173. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the implication of using 0.0.0.0/0 as a static route in a TGW route table? ; All traffic with no more-specific route will be sent to the attachment specified in the 0.0.0.0/0 route. This is typically the Egress VPC attachment for centralized internet routing or the Inspection VPC for centralized security. <br><br> *The 0.0.0.0/0 route in TGW RT acts as the default route — any destination not matched by a more specific route (e.g., VPC CIDRs, on-premises CIDRs) hits this default.<br> *Be careful: if on-premises or inter-VPC destinations are missing from the TGW RT, they'll be forwarded to the default route destination (Egress VPC) instead of being dropped — this can create unintended routing paths.<br> *Always ensure more-specific routes for all known networks exist in the TGW RT before relying on a default route; avoid depending on default route as a catch-all for destinations that should be blocked.<br>

174. [ Transit Gateway ] How can you achieve centralized DNS for all VPCs attached to a TGW? ; Deploy Route 53 Resolver endpoints in a shared Shared Services VPC attached to TGW. Use RAM to share Resolver rules across all accounts. Configure each account's VPCs to use the centralized Resolver for DNS forwarding. <br><br> *Shared Resolver rules (via RAM) enable all spoke VPCs to forward DNS queries for specific domains (e.g., .corp.internal) to on-premises DNS servers via the centralized Resolver endpoints in the Shared Services VPC.<br> *This avoids deploying Route 53 Resolver endpoints in every VPC — centralized Resolver endpoints reduce cost and simplify management.<br> *TGW ensures the DNS query traffic path: Spoke VPC → TGW → Shared Services VPC → Resolver Endpoint → on-premises DNS (via DX/VPN also through TGW).<br>

175. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the relationship between TGW and AWS PrivateLink in a multi-account architecture? ; They are complementary. TGW provides network-level connectivity between VPCs (full CIDR routing). PrivateLink provides service-level access (one-way to a specific endpoint). Use PrivateLink for service exposure; use TGW for network connectivity. Centralized Interface Endpoints in a hub VPC accessible via TGW reduces cost. <br><br> *You cannot use TGW as a substitute for PrivateLink when exposing SaaS services to external customers — TGW requires VPC attachment (account relationship), PrivateLink works across any account.<br> *Centralized PrivateLink hubs via TGW: deploy all Interface Endpoints (SSM, EC2, etc.) in one hub VPC; spoke VPCs access via TGW + private DNS forwarding to hub endpoint IPs.<br> *Cost optimization: one set of Interface Endpoints shared across 20 VPCs via TGW costs much less than 20 sets of Interface Endpoints (one per VPC) — the TGW data charges are lower than per-VPC endpoint charges at scale.<br>

176. [ Transit Gateway ] What TGW monitoring is available via AWS Network Manager? ; Network Manager provides: topology diagrams (global network view), event monitoring (connection state changes), CloudWatch metrics integration, route analyzer (similar to Reachability Analyzer), and SD-WAN device status integration. <br><br> *The global topology view shows all TGWs, VPCs, VPN connections, DX connections, and on-premises devices in one map across all regions and accounts.<br> *Route Analyzer in Network Manager can analyze routing paths through TGW without generating actual traffic — useful for validating complex multi-region routing configurations.<br> *Network Manager events (e.g., "VPN connection went down") integrate with EventBridge to trigger automated remediation workflows or send alerts to operations teams.<br>

177. [ Transit Gateway ] A company attaches 100 VPCs to a TGW with default route table association and propagation ON. What are the routing implications? ; All 100 VPC CIDRs propagate into the default route table. All 100 VPCs can route to all other 99 VPCs — completely flat network. Any two VPCs can communicate without any further routing configuration. <br><br> *This is appropriate for simple environments where all VPCs are trusted (same business unit, same environment) and full connectivity is desired.<br> *The risk: a misconfigured workload or compromised instance in any VPC has routing access to all 100 other VPCs — no network-layer isolation.<br> *At 100 VPCs, the default route table has 100+ routes (one per VPC CIDR, potentially more for VPCs with secondary CIDRs) — well within the 10,000 route limit but management becomes unwieldy without tagging/documentation.<br>

178. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the purpose of the TGW "Peering Attachment" compared to other attachment types? ; TGW Peering attachment connects two TGWs (same or different accounts, same or different regions) over AWS backbone. Unlike VPC/VPN/DX attachments that connect external networks, peering connects two TGW instances to enable cross-TGW routing. <br><br> *TGW peering is one-to-one (one attachment per pair of TGWs, per region pairing) and requires acceptance from the other TGW owner — similar to VPC peering request/accept flow.<br> *Traffic between TGWs via peering is NOT transitive beyond the peered TGW — if TGW-A peers with TGW-B, and TGW-B peers with TGW-C, TGW-A cannot route to TGW-C through TGW-B.<br> *This non-transitivity of TGW peering means for full-mesh inter-region connectivity, each TGW pair needs its own peering attachment (O(N^2) for N TGWs) — consider a hub TGW for large multi-region setups.<br>

179. [ Transit Gateway ] How does TGW handle traffic from a VPN attachment that is not associated with any route table? ; A VPN attachment with no route table association cannot route traffic — traffic arrives at TGW but has no route table to look up, resulting in all traffic being dropped. <br><br> *This is a common misconfiguration when default association is turned OFF — a new VPN attachment must be manually associated with a route table before it can route any traffic.<br> *Unlike VPC attachments (where VPC-level route tables still function), TGW attachments with no association are completely black-holed from a TGW routing perspective.<br> *To troubleshoot: check TGW attachment state (should be "available"), check route table association (should show a route table), check route table has a route for the expected destination.<br>

180. [ Transit Gateway ] What is the maximum bandwidth per TGW VPN ECMP configuration and what is the theoretical maximum? ; Maximum with AWS-stated limits: 8 equal-cost VPN paths × 1.25 Gbps = 10 Gbps aggregate. Theoretical maximum: limited by the 50 Gbps TGW aggregate burst limit. <br><br> *AWS supports up to 8 equal-cost BGP paths in ECMP for VPN; beyond 8 paths, additional paths do not participate in load balancing.<br> *For bandwidth needs beyond 10 Gbps via VPN ECMP, Direct Connect (dedicated 10G or 100G) or Connect attachment (up to 20 Gbps per peer, multiple peers possible) are the appropriate alternatives.<br> *ECMP bandwidth is theoretical maximum — actual throughput depends on internet conditions (for VPN) and the overhead of IPSec encryption/decryption at each end.<br>

181. [ Direct Connect ] What is the LOA-CFA and what is its purpose in the DX provisioning process? ; LOA-CFA (Letter of Authorization – Connecting Facility Assignment) is a document AWS issues for Dedicated connections that authorizes the DX colocation facility to install a physical cross-connect between your/partner cage and the AWS router. <br><br> *AWS issues the LOA-CFA within 72 hours of a Dedicated connection request — it contains the AWS router rack/port details needed by the facility's operations team.<br> *Without the LOA-CFA, the colocation facility cannot install the cross-connect — it is the formal authorization that initiates the physical layer work.<br> *Hosted connections do not require an LOA-CFA — the DX Partner already has existing cross-connects with AWS and provisions hosted capacity from their existing port.<br>

182. [ Direct Connect ] What connection type must be used for sub-1Gbps Direct Connect bandwidth requirements? ; Hosted Connection from a DX Partner. Dedicated connections are available only in 1G, 10G, and 100G. For any bandwidth below 1G (e.g., 50 Mbps, 200 Mbps, 500 Mbps), a Hosted Connection via an APN Partner is required. <br><br> *Hosted connections support bandwidths from 50 Mbps up to 10 Gbps, giving customers granular options that Dedicated connections don't offer.<br> *The trade-off: Hosted connections have Partner SLA (not AWS SLA) and support only 1 VIF per connection (vs 50 for Dedicated).<br> *For growing bandwidth needs, start with a Hosted connection and plan migration to a Dedicated connection once requirements exceed the hosted tier — this is a common migration path.<br>

183. [ Direct Connect ] What is MACsec and on which Direct Connect connection types is it supported? ; MACsec (IEEE 802.1AE) is Layer 2 encryption that protects traffic at the Ethernet frame level before it enters the AWS network. It is supported only on Dedicated 10G and 100G connections — not on 1G Dedicated or any Hosted connection. <br><br> *MACsec uses a CAK/CKN (Connectivity Association Key / Key Name) pair configured on both the AWS connection and your router — both sides must support MACsec (IEEE 802.1AE standard).<br> *Unlike IPSec VPN (Layer 3), MACsec operates at Layer 2, is transparent to routing, and provides wire-speed encryption with minimal overhead.<br> *For encryption needs on 1G Dedicated or Hosted connections, the alternative is IPSec VPN over a Public VIF — functionally equivalent encryption but with more routing complexity.<br>

184. [ Direct Connect ] A company needs 50 Mbps Direct Connect bandwidth with AWS SLA. What should they choose? ; This is not possible — AWS SLA is only available for Dedicated connections (minimum 1G). Sub-1G connections require Hosted connections, which carry Partner SLA, not AWS SLA. The company must choose between 1G Dedicated (with AWS SLA, more bandwidth than needed) or 50 Mbps Hosted (meets bandwidth requirement, Partner SLA only). <br><br> *AWS does not offer sub-1G Dedicated connections; the minimum Dedicated bandwidth is 1 Gbps.<br> *For strict AWS SLA requirements at low bandwidth, the only option is to over-provision with a 1G Dedicated connection and use only 50 Mbps of capacity.<br> *In practice, most enterprises in this situation choose Hosted connections because the bandwidth match outweighs the SLA difference — they rely on the partner's SLA and redundancy model instead.<br>

185. [ Direct Connect ] What are the four Direct Connect Resiliency Models and their SLA percentages? ; No Resiliency (no SLA), Development (no SLA), High Resiliency (99.9% SLA), Maximum Resiliency (99.99% SLA). Each model differs in number of connections, number of DX locations, and failure scenarios survived. <br><br> *No Resiliency: 1 connection, 1 location, 1 device — single point of failure at every layer. Use only for testing.<br> *Development: 2 connections at same location, different devices — survives device failure but NOT DX location failure.<br> *High Resiliency: 2 connections at 2 different DX locations — survives complete location outage. Minimum for production workloads.<br>

186. [ Direct Connect ] What is the Maximum Resiliency model and when should it be used? ; Maximum Resiliency: 4 connections — 2 at each of 2 different DX locations, each connection on a different device per location. Survives simultaneous device failure + location failure. Provides 99.99% SLA. Use for mission-critical workloads (financial trading, healthcare, safety systems). <br><br> *Maximum Resiliency is the only DX model that provides AWS's 99.99% connectivity SLA — High Resiliency is 99.9%.<br> *The 4-connection setup (2 locations × 2 devices per location) ensures no single failure — device, rack power, or entire location — can disrupt connectivity.<br> *AWS provides the Resiliency Toolkit in the DX console to validate your configuration meets a specific resiliency model before you rely on it for production traffic.<br>

187. [ Direct Connect ] What is the AWS Direct Connect Resiliency Toolkit? ; A built-in tool in the DX console that guides you through selecting, configuring, and validating a resiliency model. It tests failover by letting you disable specific connections and verify traffic continues to flow, without needing to physically disconnect cables. <br><br> *The Resiliency Toolkit shows a wizard: choose your target resiliency model → toolkit shows required connections → configure them → toolkit validates the configuration.<br> *The failover testing feature lets you simulate connection failure (the toolkit temporarily routes traffic around a connection) to verify BGP failover, application continuity, and latency impact.<br> *Using the Resiliency Toolkit before go-live is a Well-Architected best practice — discovering failover issues in a test is far better than discovering them during an actual outage.<br>

188. [ Direct Connect ] What is a Private VIF and what are its BGP prefix limits? ; A Private VIF connects your on-premises network to a VPC via a Virtual Private Gateway or DX Gateway. BGP limits: 100 prefixes advertised from on-prem to AWS per BGP session; 200 prefixes advertised from AWS to on-prem per BGP session. Exceeding limits drops the BGP session. <br><br> *Private VIF provides access to private IP resources in the VPC — EC2, RDS, ECS, Lambda in VPC, etc. It cannot access public AWS service endpoints like S3 public URLs.<br> *The 100-prefix advertise limit is a hard BGP limit — if your on-premises network has more than 100 routes, you MUST summarize/aggregate them before advertising to AWS, or the BGP session will be terminated.<br> *Each Private VIF has its own VLAN ID (1–4094, unique per connection) and BGP session — multiple Private VIFs on the same physical connection each have independent VLAN and BGP configuration.<br>

189. [ Direct Connect ] What is a Public VIF and what can it access? ; A Public VIF connects to ALL AWS public IP ranges globally — it's a private path to AWS public endpoints. Can access S3, DynamoDB, EC2 public IPs, CloudFront, and every other AWS public service across ALL regions. <br><br> *Public VIF uses public IPs on both sides (your router and AWS) — it's not inside a VPC, it connects directly to the AWS public network.<br> *A common use case: run IPSec VPN over a Public VIF to add encryption to DX — VPN packets travel over the private DX path but are encrypted end-to-end.<br> *AWS advertises a large number of public IP prefixes via Public VIF (thousands of prefixes covering all AWS public ranges globally) — ensure your router's BGP table can handle this volume.<br>

190. [ Direct Connect ] What is a Transit VIF and how does it differ from a Private VIF? ; Transit VIF connects a DX connection to a DX Gateway, which is then associated with a Transit Gateway. This enables access to multiple VPCs across multiple regions via a single DX connection. Private VIF connects to a single VGW (one VPC only). <br><br> *Transit VIF requires a DX Gateway as an intermediary — you cannot attach a Transit VIF directly to a TGW without going through DXGW.<br> *Transit VIF replaces the need for multiple Private VIFs (one per VPC/VGW) — a single Transit VIF through DXGW+TGW can reach hundreds of VPCs.<br> *Dedicated connections support up to 50 VIFs of any type. Hosted connections support exactly 1 VIF — if using a Hosted connection with multi-VPC needs, that 1 VIF should be a Transit VIF via DXGW+TGW.<br>

191. [ Direct Connect ] How many VIFs can a Dedicated connection support vs a Hosted connection? ; Dedicated: up to 50 VIFs (any mix of Private, Public, Transit). Hosted connection: exactly 1 VIF (the VIF IS the hosted connection — you cannot add additional VIFs). <br><br> *This is a critical architectural constraint: if you need multiple VIF types (e.g., a Private VIF for VPC access AND a Public VIF for S3 without VPC), you need a Dedicated connection.<br> *With a Hosted connection for multi-VPC access, the single allowed VIF should be a Transit VIF through DXGW+TGW — this one VIF can reach all your VPCs.<br> *Cross-account VIF allocation: a VIF can be created in one AWS account but allocated/assigned to a different AWS account — useful when a central networking account owns the DX connection but business units have their own VPCs.<br>

192. [ Direct Connect ] What are the LACP requirements for a Direct Connect LAG? ; All connections in a LAG must use identical bandwidth, be at the same DX location, and terminate on the same AWS device. Maximum 4 Dedicated connections per LAG (or 2 Hosted). All links are active/active simultaneously. <br><br> *LACP (802.3ad Link Aggregation Control Protocol) negotiates which ports participate in the bundle — both your router and the AWS device must support LACP.<br> *The same-device requirement is the key limitation: all LAG links terminate on one AWS physical device, which means a device failure takes down the entire LAG — LAG does NOT provide device-level HA.<br> *To achieve both bandwidth aggregation AND device HA: use a LAG at each of two DX locations, then use BGP to load-balance or prefer between the two LAGs.<br>

193. [ Direct Connect ] A company creates a LAG with 4×10G connections. What is the effective bandwidth and what failure scenario is NOT protected against? ; Effective bandwidth: 40 Gbps (4 × 10G, active/active). NOT protected against: DX location failure — all 4 connections are at the same DX location. A full location outage takes down the entire LAG. <br><br> *LAG provides bandwidth aggregation and survives individual link or device failures (if connections are on different devices within the LAG — though same-device is also valid).<br> *The same-location requirement of LAG is a fundamental architectural constraint — LAG cannot span DX locations, so it inherently has a location-level SPOF.<br> *For full HA: deploy a second LAG at a second DX location. Use BGP MED or AS prepending to create primary/backup preference between the two LAGs, or ECMP for active/active across locations.<br>

194. [ Direct Connect ] What is the "minimum links" setting on a LAG and why would you set it? ; Minimum links is the minimum number of active connections required for the LAG to remain operational. If active connections drop below this threshold, the entire LAG goes down rather than operating in a degraded state. <br><br> *Example: 4-link LAG with min-links=2. If 3 links fail, the LAG goes down (preventing operation at 25% capacity, which may cause application issues worse than full failover).<br> *Setting min-links prevents asymmetric routing issues: if half a LAG goes down, traffic may still be routed to the LAG (via BGP) but capacity is halved, potentially causing congestion on surviving links.<br> *When min-links threshold is crossed, BGP withdraws routes for the LAG, forcing traffic to fail over to a backup path (secondary LAG or VPN) — this is the desired automatic failover behavior.<br>

195. [ Direct Connect ] What is a DX Gateway and what is its scope? ; DX Gateway (DXGW) is a globally scoped AWS resource that enables a single Direct Connect connection to reach VPCs in multiple AWS regions. It is not tied to any specific region. <br><br> *DXGW is free — you pay only for the DX connection data transfer. The gateway itself has no per-hour charge.<br> *One DXGW can serve up to 20 DX connections and be associated with up to 10 VGWs or 3 TGWs (but not both — VGW and TGW associations are mutually exclusive).<br> *DXGW can be shared across AWS accounts: the DXGW owner sends a "gateway association proposal" to the VGW/TGW account owner, who accepts it — enabling cross-account DX access without sharing the physical connection.<br>

196. [ Direct Connect ] Can a DX Gateway be associated with both VGWs and TGWs simultaneously? ; No. VGW associations and TGW associations are mutually exclusive on a single DX Gateway. You must choose one model: DXGW+VGW (up to 10 VPCs) OR DXGW+TGW (multi-VPC via TGW). <br><br> *This is an architectural decision made at DXGW configuration time — changing from VGW mode to TGW mode requires removing all VGW associations first (disruptive).<br> *DXGW+TGW is the recommended model for most enterprise environments: Transit VIF → DXGW → TGW gives access to all VPCs attached to TGW across multiple regions.<br> *DXGW+VGW remains appropriate for simple setups: ≤10 VPCs, no need for VPC-to-VPC routing via the DX path, prefer simpler architecture over TGW's added cost.<br>
