197. [ Direct Connect ] Can a DX Gateway route traffic between two VPCs? ; No. DX Gateway only routes traffic between on-premises and VPCs. It does NOT enable VPC-to-VPC communication. To route between VPCs via the DX path, a Transit Gateway must be added behind the DX Gateway. <br><br> *This is a common exam trap: DXGW connects "on-prem to many VPCs" but not "VPC to VPC." Adding TGW behind DXGW enables both patterns simultaneously.<br> *Technically, DXGW enforces this by only advertising allowed prefixes in each direction — it acts as a one-to-many forwarder, not a mesh router.<br> *If you need VPC-to-VPC routing AND on-prem connectivity from a single DX: use Transit VIF → DXGW → TGW. The TGW handles VPC-to-VPC; DXGW handles on-prem-to-TGW.<br>

198. [ Direct Connect ] What are "allowed prefixes" in the context of DX Gateway? ; Allowed prefixes is an explicit list of VPC CIDR ranges that DXGW will advertise to on-premises over the DX connection. Only CIDRs in this list are announced via BGP to your on-prem router. <br><br> *This is a security and routing control mechanism — prevents accidental leakage of VPC CIDRs you don't want on-prem to know about.<br> *If a VPC's CIDR is not in the allowed prefixes list, on-premises cannot route to that VPC even if it's associated with the DXGW — it simply won't receive that BGP route.<br> *Allowed prefixes must be manually updated when new VPCs are added — forgetting to add a new VPC's CIDR to allowed prefixes is a common operational mistake that results in connectivity failure from on-prem to the new VPC.<br>

199. [ Direct Connect ] What is a Virtual Private Gateway (VGW) and what is its ASN behavior? ; VGW is an AWS-managed gateway attached to a single VPC that terminates VPN tunnels and DX Private VIFs. The ASN is set at creation time using a private ASN value and CANNOT be changed after creation. Default ASN is 64512 if not specified. <br><br> *The immutability of VGW ASN means careful planning is required — if you need a specific ASN for BGP compatibility with your on-premises routing policies, set it at creation.<br> *VGW ASN is used in BGP sessions for both Site-to-Site VPN and DX Private VIF connections — your on-premises router establishes BGP with the VGW using this ASN.<br> *To "change" a VGW ASN, you must: create a new VGW with the desired ASN, detach all VPN/DX connections from old VGW, reattach to new VGW, update on-premises BGP config — a significant operational task.<br>

200. [ Direct Connect ] What is VGW Route Propagation and what is the exam trap? ; VGW Route Propagation automatically adds BGP-learned routes (from DX or VPN) into a VPC route table. Must be explicitly enabled per route table. The exam trap: a new custom route table does NOT inherit route propagation from the main route table — it must be enabled separately. <br><br> *Without route propagation enabled, VPC instances don't know how to reach on-prem networks — you'd need to add static routes manually for every on-prem prefix.<br> *Route propagation is enabled in the VPC console under Route Tables → select RT → Route Propagation tab → enable for the VGW.<br> *Common failure scenario: engineer creates a new route table for a new subnet, forgets to enable propagation → instances in that subnet cannot reach on-prem even though VGW/DX is working fine.<br>

201. [ Direct Connect ] How does VGW differ from Transit Gateway for multi-VPC connectivity? ; VGW attaches to ONE VPC only and routes only to/from that VPC. TGW is a regional hub that supports thousands of VPC attachments and routes between all attached VPCs, VPNs, and DX connections. VGW is simpler and free; TGW costs per attachment-hour but scales massively. <br><br> *VGW is appropriate when you have a single VPC needing DX/VPN connectivity — no need for TGW's complexity and cost.<br> *TGW becomes necessary when: you have multiple VPCs that need DX access, VPCs need to route to each other, or you need centralized network architecture (shared services, centralized egress).<br> *A VGW can be "upgraded" to TGW indirectly: create TGW + Transit VIF via DXGW, migrate VPC attachments to TGW, decommission VGW — but there will be a maintenance window for the cutover.<br>

202. [ Direct Connect ] What is an AWS Direct Connect Partner (APN) and what types of connections do they provide? ; DX Partners are AWS Partner Network members with existing physical ports at AWS DX locations. They provide Hosted Connections (sub-1G to 10G) and Hosted VIFs by sub-dividing their existing capacity, and managed last-mile circuits from customer sites to DX locations. <br><br> *Partners enable customers to get DX connectivity without being physically present at a DX colocation facility — the partner handles the DX-location-side infrastructure.<br> *Partner types: telecom carriers (AT&T, Verizon, BT) provide MPLS/WAN circuits; network exchanges (Equinix Fabric, Megaport) provide software-defined connectivity; managed network providers offer end-to-end managed DX services.<br> *Some partners sell DX connectivity via AWS Marketplace — this enables consolidated billing (DX connection charges appear on AWS bill) and simplified procurement.<br>

203. [ Direct Connect ] What is the "last mile" in the context of Direct Connect and who is responsible for it? ; The last mile is the physical network circuit from the customer's data center to the AWS DX colocation facility. The customer (or their DX Partner) is responsible for provisioning and paying for the last mile — AWS does not provide it. <br><br> *Common last-mile options: MPLS circuit from a telecom carrier, leased dark fiber, point-to-point Ethernet WAN, or SD-WAN over broadband (less reliable).<br> *Last-mile latency directly impacts total DX latency — the AWS backbone adds very little latency once traffic reaches the DX PoP. Choose a DX location geographically close to your DC to minimize last-mile distance.<br> *Typical DX provisioning timeline: AWS issues LOA-CFA within 72 hours, but last-mile circuit provisioning takes 2-8 weeks — total time from request to traffic flowing is typically 4-12 weeks.<br>

204. [ Direct Connect ] How can a company achieve multicloud connectivity (AWS + Azure + GCP) using Direct Connect? ; Use a colocation exchange provider (Equinix Fabric, Megaport, PacketFabric). Connect from your DC to the exchange via last-mile circuit. From the exchange, establish AWS DX, Azure ExpressRoute, and GCP Cloud Interconnect — all from a single exchange cross-connect. <br><br> *AWS does not have a native multicloud product — multicloud private connectivity is always mediated through neutral exchange providers or third-party network services.<br> *Exchange providers have pre-established physical ports at AWS DX locations, Azure ER meet-me rooms, and GCP Interconnect facilities — often in the same building (e.g., Equinix colocation) — enabling single-facility multicloud connectivity.<br> *Benefits of exchange-based multicloud: single cross-connect at exchange instead of multiple last-mile circuits, software-defined provisioning (Megaport/PacketFabric allow self-service circuit creation), and consistent private latency to all clouds.<br>

205. [ Direct Connect ] What AWS services provide hybrid cloud connectivity for latency-sensitive workloads as an alternative to DX? ; AWS Outposts (AWS hardware in your DC), AWS Local Zones (AWS infrastructure in metro areas), and AWS Wavelength (AWS at 5G carrier edge). These extend AWS compute/services to locations closer to users, rather than connecting your DC to AWS over DX. <br><br> *AWS Outposts deploys AWS-managed servers in your DC — applications run locally with sub-millisecond latency to on-premises systems while connecting to the AWS region over a dedicated network link.<br> *Local Zones provide a subset of AWS services in major cities — useful for workloads requiring single-digit millisecond latency to end users in specific metro areas, without the full DX setup.<br> *These services are complementary to DX, not replacements — DX handles high-bandwidth DC-to-cloud connectivity; Outposts/Local Zones handle workloads that must physically run near the end users or on-premises systems.<br>

206. [ Direct Connect ] A company wants to encrypt Direct Connect traffic end-to-end without using MACsec. What is the recommended approach? ; Run an IPSec Site-to-Site VPN over a DX Public VIF. The VPN tunnel uses the Public VIF for private physical path while IPSec encrypts the traffic at Layer 3. This combines DX's consistent latency with VPN's encryption. <br><br> *Public VIF provides the physical DX path to AWS public endpoints including VPN endpoints — the VPN tunnel runs over this private circuit rather than over the internet.<br> *This approach works for ALL DX connection types (1G, 10G, 100G Dedicated and Hosted) — unlike MACsec which is Dedicated 10G/100G only.<br> *Bandwidth limitation: each VPN tunnel is limited to 1.25 Gbps — for high-bandwidth DX with encryption, use multiple VPN tunnels (ECMP) or MACsec (where available) to avoid the VPN throughput bottleneck.<br>

207. [ Direct Connect ] What BGP communities does AWS support on Direct Connect and what do they control? ; AWS uses BGP communities to control route propagation scope for Public VIF routes: 7224:9100 (local AWS region), 7224:9200 (all AWS regions in a continent), 7224:9300 (all AWS regions globally). On inbound, use communities to influence AWS's path preference. <br><br> *Setting community 7224:9100 on prefixes you advertise tells AWS to only propagate that route within the local DX region — useful for routing traffic to specific regional endpoints.<br> *Community 7224:9300 (global) means AWS will advertise your prefix globally — useful for anycast-style routing where on-prem wants to appear as a destination from anywhere in AWS.<br> *For inbound traffic shaping (influencing which DX connection AWS prefers for traffic to on-prem), use MED values — lower MED = more preferred path from AWS's perspective.<br>

208. [ Direct Connect ] What is the maximum number of routes AWS will advertise to your router on a BGP session over DX? ; AWS advertises up to 1,000 routes per BGP session (applies to Private VIF and Transit VIF). On Public VIF, AWS advertises all AWS public IP prefixes (thousands of routes). If the 1,000-route limit is reached on Private/Transit VIF, the BGP session is dropped. <br><br> *1,000 routes per BGP session is the DX BGP limit for Private and Transit VIFs — this is the count of VPC CIDRs, secondary CIDRs, and any other prefixes AWS advertises.<br> *In large environments with many VPCs connected via TGW, be careful about how many VPC CIDRs are propagated via DXGW — each VPC CIDR counts against the 1,000-route limit.<br> *To stay within limits: use CIDR summarization at the TGW level, use distinct non-overlapping supernets per environment (prod/dev/staging), and monitor BGP route counts via CloudWatch DX metrics.<br>

209. [ Direct Connect ] What is the difference between DX "Connection" and DX "Virtual Interface" in the AWS console? ; A Connection is the physical (or logical, for Hosted) port at the DX location — it represents the bandwidth capacity. A Virtual Interface (VIF) is the logical 802.1Q VLAN created on top of a connection — it carries actual routing (BGP) and connectivity to AWS resources. <br><br> *One Connection can have multiple VIFs (up to 50 on Dedicated) — each VIF is independent with its own VLAN ID, BGP session, and routing configuration.<br> *Connection state (up/down) represents the physical layer. VIF state (available/down) represents the BGP+VLAN layer — a connection can be up while a VIF is down (BGP not configured yet, or BGP misconfiguration).<br> *Troubleshooting order: check Connection state first (physical layer) → check VIF state (VLAN/BGP layer) → check BGP session details (routing layer) → check VPC route tables (routing application layer).<br>

210. [ Direct Connect ] A company uses a Hosted VIF. What are the key limitations compared to a Hosted Connection or Dedicated Connection? ; Hosted VIF: exactly 1 VIF (shared partner port), bandwidth not guaranteed (best-effort on shared port), Partner SLA, no MACsec, cannot add more VIFs. Hosted Connection: 1 dedicated VIF with guaranteed bandwidth tier. Dedicated: 50 VIFs, guaranteed port, AWS SLA, MACsec on 10G/100G. <br><br> *Hosted VIF is the most constrained option: bandwidth is shared with other customers on the partner's port, so actual throughput may be less than expected during peak partner usage.<br> *Hosted VIF is suitable for initial testing or non-critical workloads where you need DX access quickly but don't have the budget for a Dedicated or Hosted Connection.<br> *For production workloads: use at minimum a Hosted Connection (guaranteed bandwidth tier) or Dedicated Connection (full port ownership, AWS SLA, MACsec option).<br>

211. [ Direct Connect ] What happens to VIFs on a Direct Connect connection when the connection goes to "down" state? ; All VIFs on the connection lose their BGP sessions and go down. Traffic stops flowing through those VIFs. BGP will withdraw all routes learned via those VIFs from your on-prem router, triggering failover to backup paths (second DX connection or VPN). <br><br> *VIF state is dependent on connection state — if the physical port goes down, all logical VIFs on that port are immediately affected.<br> *BGP keepalive timers determine how quickly your router detects the loss of BGP session and withdraws routes — typical BGP timers are 90s hold time (30s keepalive), but BFD (Bidirectional Forwarding Detection) can detect failures in under 1 second.<br> *Enabling BFD on DX BGP sessions is a Well-Architected best practice for fast failure detection — reduces failover time from minutes (BGP timeout) to sub-second (BFD detection).<br>

212. [ Direct Connect ] When should you use DXGW + VGW vs DXGW + TGW? ; Use DXGW+VGW when: ≤10 VPCs need DX access, no VPC-to-VPC routing needed, simplicity preferred, want to avoid TGW cost. Use DXGW+TGW when: >10 VPCs, VPC-to-VPC routing required, need Connect/SD-WAN attachments, centralized architecture, or multi-region scaling. <br><br> *DXGW+VGW is simpler and cheaper — no TGW attachment fees. Each VGW is free and is directly associated with one VPC, making the routing model transparent.<br> *DXGW+TGW scales to thousands of VPCs, supports any attachment type (VPC, VPN, DX, peering, Connect), and enables centralized patterns (shared services, centralized egress, security inspection).<br> *The migration path from DXGW+VGW to DXGW+TGW involves: create TGW, create Transit VIF, update DXGW to associate with TGW (remove VGW associations first), attach VPCs to TGW, update VPC route tables — plan for a maintenance window.<br>

213. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is VPC Flow Logs and what does it capture? ; VPC Flow Logs captures IP traffic metadata flowing through ENIs in a VPC — source/destination IPs and ports, protocol, bytes, packets, timestamps, and action (ACCEPT/REJECT). It does NOT capture packet payloads. <br><br> *Flow Logs is a metadata capture tool, not a packet capture tool — it records connection-level information only, similar to NetFlow in traditional networking.<br> *For full packet capture (pcap), use VPC Traffic Mirroring, which copies actual packet data to a target ENI or NLB — at higher cost and complexity.<br> *Flow Logs has no performance impact on network traffic — capture happens out-of-band on the AWS hypervisor, completely transparent to the instances.<br>

214. [ VPC Flow Logs ] At what three levels can VPC Flow Logs be enabled? ; VPC level (all ENIs in the VPC), Subnet level (all ENIs in a specific subnet), and ENI level (a single network interface). Each level can have multiple flow logs with different destinations or filters. <br><br> *VPC-level flow logs are the broadest — they automatically capture traffic for all existing and future ENIs in the VPC without per-subnet or per-ENI configuration.<br> *ENI-level flow logs are useful for targeted troubleshooting of a specific instance or service without the cost of logging all VPC traffic.<br> *Multiple flow logs per resource are allowed — for example, send REJECT-only traffic to CloudWatch for real-time alerting AND send ALL traffic to S3 for long-term compliance storage, both on the same VPC.<br>

215. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What are the three traffic filter options when creating a VPC Flow Log? ; ALL (both accepted and rejected traffic), ACCEPT (only traffic allowed by security groups and NACLs), and REJECT (only traffic blocked by security groups or NACLs). <br><br> *REJECT-only filter dramatically reduces log volume — in most environments, the vast majority of traffic is accepted, so REJECT-only can cut log volume by 90%+ while still providing security visibility.<br> *ACCEPT-only filter is useful for compliance use cases where you need to prove that only authorized traffic was allowed into a system — reducing noise from rejected port scans.<br> *ALL traffic filter is required for complete forensic investigations or when you need to see both the attack (REJECT) and the lateral movement (ACCEPT) in context.<br>

216. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What does the log-status field "SKIPDATA" mean and when should you be concerned? ; SKIPDATA means the flow log system had an internal capacity issue and dropped some records for that interval — the log is incomplete. NODATA means no traffic occurred in the interval, which is normal. <br><br> *SKIPDATA is a warning sign — if persistent, it means your flow log delivery infrastructure is under capacity stress and you are missing records, which undermines audit and security monitoring.<br> *Occasional SKIPDATA in a high-traffic VPC is less concerning than persistent SKIPDATA — investigate if it appears consistently across multiple aggregation intervals.<br> *Mitigation options: switch from 10-minute to 1-minute aggregation intervals (smaller batches, less likely to hit capacity limits), or split flow logs across multiple log configurations targeting different ENIs.<br>

217. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What traffic is NOT captured by VPC Flow Logs? ; DNS queries to the VPC+2 resolver (169.254.169.253), DHCP traffic, instance metadata requests (169.254.169.254), Amazon Time Sync (169.254.169.123), Windows license activation traffic, and VPC router traffic. <br><br> *DNS queries to the VPC resolver are not logged because DNS is handled internally by AWS infrastructure, not through ENIs — use Route 53 Resolver Query Logs to capture DNS activity.<br> *DHCP traffic is excluded because DHCP is managed by the AWS DHCP service, not routed through ENIs in the normal flow — it's an internal AWS service interaction.<br> *This exclusion list is an exam favorite — "customer needs to log all DNS queries for security" → Route 53 Resolver Query Logs, NOT VPC Flow Logs.<br>

218. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What fields were added in VPC Flow Logs version 3? ; vpc-id, subnet-id, instance-id, tcp-flags, type (IPv4/IPv6), pkt-srcaddr, pkt-dstaddr. Version 3 added instance and subnet context to records, enabling correlation without separate ENI-to-instance lookups. <br><br> *instance-id eliminates the need to maintain an ENI-to-instance mapping table — you can directly see which EC2 instance generated the traffic in each log record.<br> *pkt-srcaddr and pkt-dstaddr capture the original source/destination IPs before NAT translation — critical for tracing true origins when traffic passes through NAT Gateways.<br> *tcp-flags bitmask enables analysis of TCP session patterns: SYN floods, RST storms, incomplete handshakes — useful for detecting DoS attacks or misconfigured applications.<br>

219. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the difference between srcaddr and pkt-srcaddr in VPC Flow Logs? ; srcaddr = the IP address at the ENI being monitored (may be NAT-translated). pkt-srcaddr = the original source IP from the packet header before any NAT translation. For traffic through a NAT Gateway, srcaddr shows the NAT Gateway IP while pkt-srcaddr shows the original EC2 private IP. <br><br> *When an EC2 instance in a private subnet sends traffic outbound through a NAT Gateway, the ENI log on the NAT Gateway shows srcaddr=NAT-GW-IP but pkt-srcaddr=original-EC2-IP — essential for tracing which instance generated internet traffic.<br> *Similarly for inbound traffic, dstaddr shows the IP at the receiving ENI while pkt-dstaddr shows the original destination IP from the packet — useful for tracing traffic through Load Balancers or PrivateLink endpoints.<br> *Always use v3+ format and include pkt-srcaddr/pkt-dstaddr fields in your custom format — they are critical for NAT environments where srcaddr alone is misleading.<br>

220. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What are the three destination options for VPC Flow Logs and their key differences? ; CloudWatch Logs (2-5 min latency, near real-time alerting, metric filters, higher cost at scale), S3 (5-15 min latency, cheapest for long-term storage, query with Athena), and Kinesis Data Firehose (near real-time, ideal for SIEM integration with Splunk/OpenSearch). <br><br> *CloudWatch Logs enables metric filters — you can count REJECT records and create alarms, enabling automated security incident response without any additional tooling.<br> *S3 with Athena is the most cost-effective for large-scale, long-term retention — Athena queries pay per TB scanned, so Parquet format with Hive partitioning dramatically reduces query cost.<br> *Kinesis Firehose is the right choice when you already have a SIEM (Splunk, OpenSearch) — Firehose buffers, batches, and delivers records continuously without the latency of S3 batch files.<br>

221. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the S3 path structure for VPC Flow Log files stored in S3? ; s3://bucket/AWSLogs/{account-id}/vpcflowlogs/{region}/{year}/{month}/{day}/{account-id}_vpcflowlogs_{region}_{flow-log-id}_{date}_{time}_{hash}.log.gz <br><br> *The date-based partitioning in the default path structure means Athena queries must scan all files unless you use Hive-compatible prefix option (year=YYYY/month=MM/day=DD) which enables partition pruning.<br> *Files are gzip-compressed by default — Athena can read gzip natively, but Parquet format provides better compression (~20% smaller than gzip text) AND columnar access for faster selective field queries.<br> *The flow-log-id in the filename identifies which flow log configuration generated the file — useful when multiple flow logs write to the same S3 bucket.<br>

222. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the default aggregation interval for VPC Flow Logs and what are the options? ; Default is 10 minutes. Can be changed to 1 minute for more granular, near-real-time capture. 1-minute interval increases cost (more files, more CloudWatch/S3 PUT operations) but enables faster incident detection. <br><br> *At 10-minute default, a security incident that begins and ends within 10 minutes may appear as a single aggregated record — the 1-minute interval gives better time resolution for forensic analysis.<br> *1-minute aggregation is recommended for high-security VPCs (PCI-DSS, HIPAA workloads) where incident response SLAs require faster detection, accepting the higher cost.<br> *The aggregation interval can be changed on an existing flow log without deleting and recreating it — adjust to 1-minute during an active incident, then revert to 10-minute for cost savings.<br>

223. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the advantage of using Parquet format over text format for VPC Flow Logs in S3? ; Parquet is columnar — Athena reads only the columns needed for a query instead of scanning entire rows. This results in ~20% smaller file sizes, faster query execution, and lower Athena query costs (billed per TB scanned). <br><br> *For a query that only needs srcaddr, dstaddr, and action fields, Parquet reads only those 3 columns from disk while text format reads the entire row — 14+ fields — even when most are irrelevant.<br> *Parquet combined with Hive-compatible partitioning (year/month/day) gives the best Athena performance: partition pruning eliminates entire days of data from the scan scope.<br> *The trade-off: Parquet is less human-readable (requires Athena or a Parquet-aware tool to query) — text format is easier for ad-hoc grep/awk analysis but inefficient at scale.<br>

224. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What IAM permissions are required to create a VPC Flow Log to CloudWatch Logs? ; The IAM role assumed by the flow log service must have: logs:CreateLogGroup, logs:CreateLogStream, logs:PutLogEvents, logs:DescribeLogGroups, logs:DescribeLogStreams. The role trust policy must allow vpc-flow-logs.amazonaws.com to assume it. <br><br> *The trust relationship (who can assume the role) is as important as the permissions — without vpc-flow-logs.amazonaws.com in the trust policy, the flow log service cannot assume the role and flow log creation fails.<br> *For S3 destination, no IAM role is needed — instead, the S3 bucket policy must allow delivery.logs.amazonaws.com to perform s3:PutObject on the bucket.<br> *Common mistake: creating an IAM role with correct permissions but forgetting the trust policy — flow log creation succeeds but records are never written, and you see no errors until you look at CloudWatch and find an empty log group.<br>

225. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What S3 bucket policy is required for VPC Flow Logs to write to S3? ; The bucket policy must grant delivery.logs.amazonaws.com principal s3:PutObject permission on the bucket prefix, plus s3:GetBucketAcl and s3:ListBucket on the bucket itself. The bucket must be in the same region as the VPC (or use a cross-region replication policy). <br><br> *The service principal delivery.logs.amazonaws.com represents the AWS log delivery service — it is NOT the VPC Flow Logs service account, but a shared AWS delivery endpoint.<br> *Without the GetBucketAcl permission, the delivery service cannot verify it has permission to write to the bucket and will fail silently — flow log status shows as "Active" but no files appear in S3.<br> *For cross-account S3 buckets (centralized log archive pattern), add an aws:SourceAccount condition to the bucket policy to restrict which account's flow logs can write to the bucket — prevents log injection from other accounts.<br>

226. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How do VPC Flow Logs work in the context of VPC Peering? ; Each VPC only logs traffic at its own ENIs. For VPC-A → VPC-B traffic: VPC-A logs show the outbound traffic from VPC-A ENIs; VPC-B logs show the inbound traffic arriving at VPC-B ENIs. To get complete bilateral visibility, enable flow logs on BOTH VPCs. <br><br> *Neither VPC "sees through" the peering connection — the peering is transparent at the network layer, and each ENI logs traffic as if it were direct IP communication between the two VPC CIDRs.<br> *The srcaddr in both VPCs will show the original source IP (no NAT in VPC peering) — srcaddr and pkt-srcaddr will be identical for peered traffic (unlike NAT Gateway traffic where they differ).<br> *For compliance audits requiring proof of communication between VPC-A and VPC-B, security teams often require flow logs on both sides — one side showing ACCEPT outbound, other showing ACCEPT inbound, with matching srcaddr/dstaddr pairs.<br>

227. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What are Transit Gateway Flow Logs and how do they differ from VPC Flow Logs? ; TGW Flow Logs capture traffic flowing THROUGH the Transit Gateway between attachments, at the TGW level. VPC Flow Logs capture traffic at individual ENIs within a VPC. TGW flow logs include TGW-specific fields like tgw-id, tgw-attachment-id, tgw-src-vpc-id, tgw-dst-vpc-id. <br><br> *TGW flow logs provide a "central view" of all east-west traffic in the network — one log shows traffic between any pair of VPCs attached to the TGW, without needing logs in every VPC.<br> *VPC flow logs show what entered/exited a VPC's ENIs; TGW flow logs show what transited through the TGW switching fabric — they are complementary, not redundant.<br> *TGW flow log destinations are S3 and CloudWatch Logs only — Kinesis Data Firehose is NOT supported for TGW flow logs, unlike VPC flow logs which support all three destinations.<br>

228. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What TGW-specific fields are available in Transit Gateway Flow Logs? ; tgw-id, tgw-attachment-id, tgw-src-vpc-account-id, tgw-dst-vpc-account-id, tgw-src-vpc-id, tgw-dst-vpc-id, tgw-src-subnet-id, tgw-dst-subnet-id, tgw-pair-attachment-id. <br><br> *tgw-src-vpc-account-id and tgw-dst-vpc-account-id are critical in multi-account Organizations — they let you trace traffic origin to a specific AWS account without knowing which VPC CIDRs belong to which account.<br> *tgw-pair-attachment-id identifies the return-path attachment, enabling correlation of request and response flows for stateful traffic analysis.<br> *These fields are only available in TGW flow logs — standard VPC flow logs on the spoke VPCs will NOT show these TGW-specific identifiers, making TGW flow logs essential for cross-account traffic auditing.<br>

229. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What does the traffic-path field (v5) value of 5 indicate? ; traffic-path=5 means the traffic exited the VPC through a Transit Gateway attachment. Values: 1=IGW, 2=VGW (DX/VPN), 3=Interface VPC Endpoint, 4=NAT Gateway, 5=TGW, 6=VPC Peering, 7=Egress-Only IGW, 8=Gateway Endpoint (S3/DynamoDB). <br><br> *traffic-path field is only populated for EGRESS traffic — it shows how traffic left the VPC. For ingress traffic, the field is not set (shows as `-`).<br> *Using traffic-path=4 (NAT Gateway), you can identify all instances sending traffic through NAT GW and their destinations — useful for cost optimization (identify high-volume NAT traffic) and security (detect unexpected internet communications).<br> *traffic-path=8 (Gateway Endpoint) helps confirm that S3 or DynamoDB traffic is using the free Gateway Endpoint path rather than the internet — validate cost savings after deploying Gateway Endpoints.<br>

230. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A security team needs to detect port scanning activity targeting their EC2 instances. How can VPC Flow Logs help? ; Enable flow logs at VPC level with ALL filter. Use CloudWatch Logs metric filter to count REJECT records where a single srcaddr has many distinct dstport values in a short time window. Create alarm and SNS notification when the count exceeds threshold. <br><br> *Port scanning generates many REJECT records from one source IP to many destination ports — this pattern is distinct from normal traffic and can be detected with a metric filter counting unique dstport values per srcaddr.<br> *Athena query approach (for S3 destination): `SELECT srcaddr, COUNT(DISTINCT dstport) as ports_scanned FROM vpc_flow_logs WHERE action='REJECT' AND start > UNIX_TIMESTAMP(NOW()-INTERVAL 1 HOUR) GROUP BY srcaddr HAVING ports_scanned > 100`.<br> *VPC Flow Logs detect scanning ATTEMPTS (REJECT records) — if an SG rule is permissive, actual connections will show as ACCEPT, not REJECT, making them harder to distinguish from legitimate traffic. Defense in depth: use both flow logs and GuardDuty (which has built-in port scan detection using flow log data).<br>

231. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How can you use VPC Flow Logs to troubleshoot connectivity issues between two EC2 instances? ; Enable flow logs on both source and destination ENIs (or their VPC). Check if traffic appears with ACCEPT (connectivity exists at network layer but may have app issue) or REJECT (blocked by SG or NACL). If no record appears, check route tables. <br><br> *ACCEPT + no application response = network path is open but application is not responding — check application health, listening port, process running.<br> *REJECT = traffic is reaching the destination ENI but being blocked by security group (stateless REJECT) or NACL (stateless REJECT) — check both SG and NACL rules for the destination.<br> *No record at all = traffic never reached the destination ENI — likely a routing issue (missing route in route table, wrong CIDR, traffic going to wrong subnet) or traffic is being dropped before the ENI (upstream NACL on a different subnet).<br>

232. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the Hive-compatible partition prefix option in S3 VPC Flow Logs? ; It formats the S3 path as key=value pairs (year=YYYY/month=MM/day=DD/hour=HH/) instead of the default path. This enables Athena automatic partition discovery and partition pruning — Athena only scans the date ranges relevant to a query, dramatically reducing cost. <br><br> *Without Hive partitioning, Athena must scan ALL files in the S3 prefix to find records matching a date filter — `WHERE start > ...` doesn't help Athena skip old files stored in chronological path structure.<br> *With Hive partitioning and `MSCK REPAIR TABLE` (or AWS Glue Crawler), Athena knows exactly which S3 prefixes map to which date ranges and skips irrelevant partitions entirely.<br> *Best practice: always enable Hive-compatible prefix when creating flow logs destined for S3+Athena — it's a one-time setting that cannot be changed on an existing flow log, requiring recreation if missed.<br>

233. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A company needs to centralize VPC Flow Logs from 50 AWS accounts into a single S3 bucket for security analysis. What is the recommended approach? ; Create a centralized S3 bucket in the security/log archive account. Configure the bucket policy to allow delivery.logs.amazonaws.com from all member accounts (using aws:SourceAccount or aws:PrincipalOrgID condition). Each account configures VPC flow logs pointing to the central S3 bucket. <br><br> *Using aws:PrincipalOrgID condition in the bucket policy automatically allows all current and future accounts in the AWS Organization to write flow logs — no per-account bucket policy updates needed when new accounts join.<br> *Centralized S3 bucket enables organization-wide Athena queries across all accounts in one table — security analysts query the entire organization's network traffic without switching accounts.<br> *AWS Security Lake is the managed alternative — it automatically normalizes flow logs to OCSF (Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework) format across all accounts, making cross-account, cross-service security analysis easier.<br>

234. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the relationship between VPC Flow Logs and AWS GuardDuty? ; GuardDuty automatically analyzes VPC Flow Logs (along with DNS logs and CloudTrail) to detect threats. GuardDuty ingests flow logs directly from AWS internal sources — you do NOT need to separately enable VPC Flow Logs for GuardDuty to function. <br><br> *GuardDuty's threat detection uses flow log data internally without you needing to enable or pay for VPC Flow Logs separately — it reads from AWS's internal flow telemetry at no additional charge for flow log access.<br> *However, your own VPC Flow Logs (CloudWatch/S3 destination) are still valuable for detailed forensic investigation after GuardDuty generates a finding — GuardDuty tells you WHAT happened, flow logs help you reconstruct HOW and WHEN in detail.<br> *Example: GuardDuty finding "UnauthorizedAccess:EC2/SSHBruteForce" → use your flow logs to see all srcaddr values that sent traffic to port 22, identify scope of the attack, and confirm which instances were targeted.<br>

235. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How can you use VPC Flow Logs to identify unused or overly permissive security group rules? ; Enable flow logs with ACCEPT filter. Over a 30-day period, query for which ports/protocols had ACCEPT traffic. Security group rules for ports with no ACCEPT traffic in 30 days are likely unused. Rules with ACCEPT from 0.0.0.0/0 should be reviewed for over-permissiveness. <br><br> *This "dead rule detection" approach provides evidence-based security group cleanup — instead of guessing which rules are used, you have 30 days of actual traffic data.<br> *AWS Network Access Analyzer and AWS Config rules (vpc-sg-open-only-to-authorized-ports) complement flow log analysis — they check configuration, while flow logs check actual usage.<br> *Be cautious: a rule with no ACCEPT traffic in 30 days might still be needed for a disaster recovery scenario that hasn't triggered — document the business reason before removing any rule.<br>

236. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is VPC Traffic Mirroring and when should it be used instead of VPC Flow Logs? ; Traffic Mirroring copies actual packet data (full payload) from an ENI to a target (another ENI or NLB). Use when you need: full packet inspection, IDS/IPS integration, forensic analysis of packet contents, application-layer visibility. Flow Logs = metadata only, Mirroring = full packets. <br><br> *Traffic Mirroring requires a target instance (e.g., EC2 running Wireshark, Zeek, Suricata) or NLB with appliances to process the mirrored traffic — significantly higher cost and operational complexity than flow logs.<br> *Use cases where Mirroring is required: capturing HTTP request bodies for WAF rule tuning, forensic investigation of a compromised instance, IDS signature matching, reconstructing application transactions.<br> *Traffic Mirroring supports filters (mirror only specific ports, protocols, or src/dst IPs) to reduce the volume sent to the target — without filters, mirroring ALL traffic from a busy instance would overwhelm the target.<br>

237. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A company wants real-time alerting when any REJECT traffic is detected on their production VPC. What is the architecture? ; Enable VPC Flow Logs → CloudWatch Logs (REJECT filter only). Create CloudWatch Metric Filter on the log group to count records matching action=REJECT. Create CloudWatch Alarm (threshold > 0 per 1 minute) → SNS topic → email/Lambda/PagerDuty. <br><br> *Using REJECT-only filter reduces CloudWatch ingestion cost significantly — in most production environments, legitimate REJECT traffic (e.g., internet scanners probing AWS IPs) would create high volume if ALL traffic is logged.<br> *The CloudWatch Metric Filter pattern for REJECT records: `[version, account, eni, src, dst, srcport, dstport, proto, packets, bytes, start, end, action="REJECT", logstatus]` — the action="REJECT" pattern extracts only rejection records.<br> *For more sophisticated alerting (e.g., alert only on REJECT from specific CIDR ranges, or only on specific ports), use CloudWatch Logs Insights subscription filter → Lambda → custom alert logic → SNS.<br>

238. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the difference between a REJECT record in VPC Flow Logs and a NODATA record? ; REJECT = a packet was received at the ENI but blocked by a Security Group or NACL rule — real traffic was denied. NODATA = no traffic whatsoever passed through the ENI in that aggregation interval — absence of traffic, not a block. <br><br> *REJECT records always indicate a security group or NACL block — they prove traffic arrived at the ENI but was denied. This is actionable data for security investigation.<br> *NODATA records appear for every ENI every aggregation interval when there's no traffic — in a VPC with many ENIs and ACCEPT-only filter, NODATA records can dominate the log volume if you're not filtering them out in queries.<br> *SKIPDATA (third possible status) is the problematic one — it means records were dropped due to internal capacity issues, potentially creating gaps in your security audit trail.<br>

239. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How can you use the tcp-flags field (v3+) to detect SYN flood attacks? ; tcp-flags=2 means SYN flag set (new connection initiation). A high volume of ACCEPT or REJECT records with tcp-flags=2 from many source IPs to one destination IP indicates a SYN flood. Monitor for srcaddr diversity + tcp-flags=2 + single dstaddr. <br><br> *TCP flags bitmask: 1=FIN, 2=SYN, 4=RST, 8=PSH, 16=ACK, 32=URG — values combine (e.g., 18=SYN+ACK, 17=FIN+ACK). A record with tcp-flags=2 is a SYN (connection initiation).<br> *SYN flood pattern: thousands of records per minute with tcp-flags=2, many distinct srcaddr values, single dstaddr+dstport combination — look for asymmetric SYN (many SYNs, few or no SYN-ACK responses).<br> *Flow Logs detect the pattern but cannot block it — for automated response, pair with AWS Shield Advanced (automatic SYN flood mitigation) or WAF rate limiting, using GuardDuty to trigger Lambda-based automatic NACL blocking of source IPs.<br>

240. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What are the flow-direction values in VPC Flow Logs v5 and what do they represent? ; flow-direction=ingress means traffic entering the ENI (incoming to the instance). flow-direction=egress means traffic leaving the ENI (outgoing from the instance). Only available in v5+ format. <br><br> *Before v5, determining traffic direction required inferring from srcaddr/dstaddr relative to the monitored ENI's IP — error-prone and complex in multi-NIC instances.<br> *With flow-direction, you can easily filter all outbound traffic from a specific ENI without knowing its IP address: `WHERE flow-direction='egress'` — simplifies analysis in environments with dynamic IPs.<br> *flow-direction is particularly useful for egress filtering use cases: prove that a sensitive instance is not initiating any outbound internet connections (egress=0 to public IPs) despite having internet route in route table.<br>

241. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A company needs to retain VPC Flow Logs for 7 years for regulatory compliance. What is the most cost-effective architecture? ; Flow Logs → S3 (text or Parquet format). Apply S3 lifecycle policy: current → S3 Intelligent-Tiering or Standard-IA after 30 days → S3 Glacier after 90 days → S3 Glacier Deep Archive after 1 year. Enable S3 Object Lock (WORM) for immutability. <br><br> *S3 Glacier Deep Archive is the cheapest AWS storage tier at ~$0.00099/GB/month — for 7-year retention of large log volumes, this is 10-20x cheaper than S3 Standard or CloudWatch Logs.<br> *S3 Object Lock in Compliance mode prevents ANY deletion or modification for the specified retention period — even the root account cannot delete locked objects, satisfying strict regulatory requirements (SOX, HIPAA, PCI-DSS).<br> *CloudWatch Logs is NOT suitable for 7-year retention at scale — it charges for both ingestion and storage, making long-term retention extremely expensive compared to S3 lifecycle policies.<br>

242. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How do VPC Flow Logs integrate with Amazon Athena for analysis? ; Create an Athena external table pointing to the S3 bucket/prefix where flow logs are stored. Define the schema matching the flow log fields (space-delimited or Parquet). Run SQL queries against the table — Athena charges per TB scanned, so Parquet + partitioning minimizes cost. <br><br> *AWS provides a pre-built CloudFormation template that creates the Athena table, partitioning, and some sample queries — use this as a starting point rather than hand-writing the CREATE TABLE statement.<br> *For Parquet format flow logs, Athena reads only the columns referenced in the query rather than entire rows — a query on just srcaddr, dstaddr, action fields reads ~3/14 of the data, reducing both cost and execution time.<br> *Use Athena workgroups to control query costs — set per-query and per-workgroup data scan limits to prevent runaway queries from scanning years of flow log data accidentally.<br>

243. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the impact of enabling VPC Flow Logs on existing network performance? ; Zero performance impact. Flow Logs capture is performed out-of-band on the AWS hypervisor infrastructure — the capture process is completely transparent to the EC2 instances and does not consume instance CPU, memory, or network bandwidth. <br><br> *This is a common exam distractor — candidates assume logging adds overhead. Flow Logs is a passive monitoring capability on the AWS side; instances have no awareness of it.<br> *The only indirect impact is on your operations: flow log data volume can be very large for busy VPCs, and querying/storing that data has its own cost and complexity — but the network itself is unaffected.<br> *Contrast with VPC Traffic Mirroring: mirroring DOES consume network bandwidth on the source ENI (mirrored traffic is additional copies sent to the target) — this is why Traffic Mirroring has a performance consideration that Flow Logs does not.<br>

244. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A company uses NAT Gateway and wants to identify which private EC2 instances are generating the most internet traffic. How? ; Enable VPC flow logs (v3+) on the VPC or NAT Gateway ENI with ALL filter, S3 destination. Use pkt-srcaddr field to identify original EC2 IP (srcaddr shows NAT Gateway IP). Query Athena: GROUP BY pkt-srcaddr ORDER BY SUM(bytes) DESC. <br><br> *Without v3+ format, srcaddr shows the NAT Gateway's IP for all outbound internet traffic — you cannot distinguish which instance behind NAT sent how much data using only srcaddr.<br> *pkt-srcaddr preserves the original pre-NAT IP through the flow log record at the NAT Gateway ENI — this is the field specifically designed to solve the NAT tracing problem.<br> *Combine with instance-id field (v3+) or maintain an IP-to-instance mapping in a lookup table (AWS Config records historical ENI→instance associations) to translate private IPs to instance names/IDs for operational reporting.<br>

245. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What happens to VPC Flow Logs when you delete the IAM role used by the flow log? ; The flow log enters a degraded state and stops delivering records to CloudWatch Logs. The flow log still exists and shows status "Active" in the console, but no new records are written. You must update the flow log with a valid IAM role to restore delivery. <br><br> *The flow log service checks IAM permissions at delivery time, not at creation time — deleting the role doesn't immediately delete the flow log configuration, but breaks delivery silently.<br> *Monitor CloudWatch Logs log streams for gaps in timestamps — if new records stop appearing for more than the aggregation interval, check the flow log IAM role and status.<br> *For S3 destinations, if the bucket policy is removed or modified, the same silent failure occurs — S3 PUT operations fail but the flow log shows "Active." Use S3 server access logging to detect delivery failures.<br>

246. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the difference between enabling flow logs at VPC level vs creating individual ENI-level flow logs for every ENI in the VPC? ; VPC-level flow log: single configuration, automatically covers all current AND future ENIs in the VPC, simpler management. Individual ENI-level: must create per ENI, doesn't cover new ENIs automatically, but allows different filters/destinations per ENI. <br><br> *VPC-level flow logs are operationally much simpler — as new EC2 instances launch and new ENIs are created, they are automatically covered without any additional configuration.<br> *The only reason to use ENI-level flow logs is when different ENIs need different configurations: different destinations, different filters, or different retention settings — for example, a web-facing ENI might need ALL-traffic logging while backend ENIs only need REJECT logging.<br> *You can combine both: a VPC-level REJECT-only log to CloudWatch for alerting, PLUS an ENI-level ALL-traffic log to S3 for specific high-value instances that require complete audit trails.<br>

247. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What AWS service can automatically create Athena tables and run queries on VPC Flow Logs stored in S3? ; Amazon Athena with AWS Glue Data Catalog. AWS Glue Crawler can automatically discover the flow log schema and create/update the Athena table. Alternatively, AWS VPC Flow Logs can be directly queried through the CloudWatch Logs Insights query interface (for CW destination). <br><br> *AWS Glue Crawler configured to run daily will automatically detect new Hive partitions (year/month/day) added to the S3 bucket and update the Athena partition table — eliminating the need to manually run MSCK REPAIR TABLE after each day's logs arrive.<br> *CloudWatch Logs Insights provides a purpose-built query interface for flow logs in CloudWatch destination — faster to set up than Athena/S3 for teams that don't need long-term retention or complex multi-table analysis.<br> *For large organizations, Amazon Security Lake integrates with Athena through a pre-built subscriber model — flow logs from all accounts are normalized to OCSF schema and queryable through Athena with pre-built security analytics queries.<br>

248. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How can you use VPC Flow Logs to validate that traffic to S3 is going through a Gateway Endpoint and not the internet? ; Enable v5 flow logs. Filter records for traffic to S3 IP ranges. Check traffic-path field: value 8 = Gateway Endpoint (correct). Value 1 = IGW (traffic going via internet = Gateway Endpoint not being used correctly or route table issue). <br><br> *traffic-path=8 confirms the traffic is using the Gateway VPC Endpoint to S3 or DynamoDB — this is the free path and never traverses the internet or incurs NAT Gateway data processing fees.<br> *If traffic-path=4 (NAT Gateway) appears for S3 traffic, the instance is routing S3 requests through NAT GW → IGW → internet instead of through the Gateway Endpoint — this incurs NAT GW data processing fees and unnecessary cost.<br> *Root cause when traffic-path≠8 for S3: the route table for that subnet doesn't have the S3 Gateway Endpoint route (pl-xxxxxxxx → vpce-xxxxxxxx), or the Gateway Endpoint policy is restricting the bucket access, causing fallback to internet routing.<br>

249. [ VPC Flow Logs ] Can VPC Flow Logs capture IPv6 traffic? ; Yes. VPC Flow Logs captures both IPv4 and IPv6 traffic. The type field (v3+) indicates whether the record is IPv4 or IPv6. IPv6 addresses appear in full expanded or compressed notation in srcaddr/dstaddr fields. <br><br> *IPv6 addresses in flow logs use standard IPv6 notation — queries need to account for address format differences (IPv6 uses colons, longer strings) when filtering by address in Athena or CloudWatch Insights.<br> *Egress-Only Internet Gateway (for IPv6 outbound) traffic appears with traffic-path=7 in v5 flow logs — distinguishing it from regular IGW (traffic-path=1) for IPv4 traffic.<br> *The type field values are IPv4 and IPv6 — filtering `WHERE type='IPv6'` in Athena isolates all IPv6 traffic for analysis of your dual-stack VPC's IPv6 usage patterns.<br>

250. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is the maximum retention period for VPC Flow Logs in CloudWatch Logs? ; CloudWatch Logs retention can be set from 1 day to 10 years (3653 days), or Never Expire. However, for long-term retention, S3 with lifecycle policies to Glacier/Glacier Deep Archive is significantly more cost-effective than CloudWatch Logs storage. <br><br> *CloudWatch Logs storage costs ~$0.03/GB/month — for years of flow log retention at scale, this becomes very expensive compared to S3 Glacier Deep Archive at ~$0.00099/GB/month (30x cheaper).<br> *Best practice: CloudWatch Logs with short retention (7-30 days) for operational use and real-time alerting, plus S3 with lifecycle policies for long-term compliance retention — export logs from CloudWatch to S3 periodically using CreateExportTask API or subscription filter → Firehose → S3.<br> *Never Expire in CloudWatch accumulates costs indefinitely — always set an explicit retention period and use S3 for anything beyond 90 days to control costs.<br>

251. [ VPC Flow Logs ] How can you send VPC Flow Logs to both CloudWatch Logs AND S3 simultaneously? ; Create two separate flow log configurations on the same VPC/subnet/ENI — one with CloudWatch Logs as destination and one with S3 as destination. Multiple flow logs per resource are supported. Each can have different filters and aggregation intervals. <br><br> *Example setup: Flow Log 1 → CloudWatch Logs, REJECT filter, 1-minute interval (for real-time security alerting). Flow Log 2 → S3, ALL filter, 10-minute interval (for compliance archive and long-term analysis).<br> *There is no limit on the number of flow log configurations per resource — you could have 3 or more (CW + S3 + Firehose), though cost adds up with each additional log stream.<br> *This dual-destination pattern is a Well-Architected best practice: operational visibility in CloudWatch, long-term evidence in S3 — each optimized for its purpose without compromising either.<br>

252. [ VPC Flow Logs ] A company's Athena queries against VPC Flow Logs in S3 are running slowly and costing too much. What optimizations should be applied? ; 1) Enable Hive-compatible partitioning (year/month/day) and use partition filters in queries. 2) Switch to Parquet format (columnar, 20% smaller). 3) Use AWS Glue Crawler for automatic partition updates. 4) Add WHERE clauses on partition columns in every query. <br><br> *The most impactful change is Hive partitioning — without it, Athena scans ALL files in the bucket regardless of date filters in the query. With partitioning, a query for one day scans only that day's partition (~1/365 of the total data).<br> *Parquet vs text: a query reading 3 columns from a 14-column text record still reads all 14 fields' worth of data. Parquet reads only the 3 columns needed — typically 60-80% less data scanned = 60-80% cost reduction.<br> *To apply these optimizations to existing flow logs: create NEW flow logs with Parquet format and Hive prefix enabled (can't change format on existing flow logs), transition old logs to Glacier, and create a new Athena table for the Parquet data going forward.<br>

253. [ VPC Flow Logs ] What is AWS Security Lake and how does it relate to VPC Flow Logs? ; AWS Security Lake is a managed service that automatically centralizes security logs (including VPC Flow Logs, CloudTrail, Route 53 logs, Security Hub findings) from all AWS accounts, normalizes them to OCSF (Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework), and stores them in S3. Subscribers (SIEM tools, Athena) query the normalized data. <br><br> *Security Lake automatically enables and collects VPC Flow Logs from all accounts in an AWS Organization — you don't need to individually configure flow logs in each account; Security Lake manages that automatically.<br> *OCSF normalization means all log types share a common schema, enabling cross-service correlation queries: find GuardDuty findings AND the associated flow log records AND CloudTrail API calls in a single Athena query.<br> *Security Lake is the recommended approach for organizations that need centralized multi-account security data lake — it replaces custom solutions of "S3 bucket + cross-account bucket policy + Glue Crawler + Athena" with a managed, maintained service.<br>

254. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] How many IPSec tunnels does a single AWS Site-to-Site VPN connection provide, and why? ; Two tunnels per VPN connection. Each tunnel terminates in a different Availability Zone on the AWS side, providing redundancy against AZ-level failures. <br><br> *The two tunnels are both active simultaneously — AWS recommends configuring your on-prem device to use both tunnels to maximize availability and bandwidth.<br> *If one tunnel fails (AZ issue, planned maintenance), traffic automatically routes through the surviving tunnel — BGP routing handles this automatically when using dynamic routing.<br> *Tunnel endpoints are automatically distributed across different AZs by AWS; you cannot choose which AZs the tunnels use — this is handled by the AWS managed infrastructure.<br>

255. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is the maximum bandwidth of a single AWS Site-to-Site VPN connection? ; 1.25 Gbps per tunnel, so 2.5 Gbps theoretical maximum using both tunnels with ECMP. In practice, single-tunnel bandwidth is limited to 1.25 Gbps. <br><br> *The 1.25 Gbps limit is per VPN tunnel — it is a hard limit of the AWS VPN endpoint infrastructure and cannot be increased by choosing a different configuration.<br> *To exceed 2.5 Gbps, use TGW with VPN ECMP enabled and multiple VPN connections — each connection adds up to 2.5 Gbps to the aggregate (subject to TGW bandwidth limits).<br> *For consistently high bandwidth requirements above 2.5 Gbps, Direct Connect is the appropriate solution — it supports dedicated connections of 1/10/100 Gbps with consistent, uncontended bandwidth.<br>

256. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is the difference between IKEv1 and IKEv2 in AWS Site-to-Site VPN? ; IKEv2 is newer, more efficient, and recommended. IKEv2 differences: faster negotiation (fewer round trips), built-in NAT-T, better reliability, supports MOBIKE (connection migration), EAP authentication. IKEv1 is legacy but still supported. <br><br> *IKEv2 reduces tunnel establishment time and is more resilient to network interruptions — it supports "reconnect" semantics where IKEv1 requires full re-negotiation after interruption.<br> *AWS defaults to IKEv2 when creating new VPN connections; you can specify IKEv1 if your on-prem device is legacy and doesn't support IKEv2.<br> *For new deployments, always choose IKEv2 unless constrained by legacy hardware — it is the industry standard and supported by all modern VPN devices including StrongSwan, Cisco, Juniper, Palo Alto.<br>

257. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is Dead Peer Detection (DPD) in VPN context and why is it important? ; DPD is a keepalive mechanism that detects when a VPN peer becomes unreachable. When DPD detects a dead peer, the VPN endpoint clears the IKE/IPSec state and attempts to re-establish. Without DPD, a failed peer may not be detected until the IKE lifetime expires (hours). <br><br> *AWS enables DPD by default on S2S VPN connections; DPD timeout is configurable. When DPD fails (no response to DPD probes), AWS clears the tunnel state and re-initiates IKE negotiation.<br> *DPD works by sending periodic R-U-THERE messages and expecting ACK responses — if no response after the timeout, the peer is declared dead and the SA is deleted.<br> *Configure matching DPD settings on your on-prem device — mismatched DPD settings can cause unnecessary tunnel drops or prevent quick detection of failures.<br>

258. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is NAT-Traversal (NAT-T) and when is it required for Site-to-Site VPN? ; NAT-T encapsulates IPSec ESP packets inside UDP port 4500, allowing IPSec traffic to pass through NAT devices. Required when your on-prem VPN device or AWS endpoint is behind a NAT. <br><br> *Standard IPSec ESP (protocol 50) packets do not contain port numbers, making it impossible for NAT devices to track connections — NAT-T solves this by wrapping ESP in UDP (which has ports).<br> *AWS Site-to-Site VPN supports NAT-T automatically — if NAT is detected during IKE negotiation, both sides switch to UDP 4500 for the data plane automatically.<br> *If your on-prem device is behind NAT, ensure your firewall/NAT device allows UDP 500 (IKE) and UDP 4500 (NAT-T) outbound and UDP 4500 inbound for the data plane.<br>

259. [ VPN CloudHub ] What is AWS VPN CloudHub and what type of AWS resource enables it? ; CloudHub is a hub-and-spoke VPN pattern (not a separate service) enabled by attaching multiple Customer Gateways (CGWs) to a single Virtual Private Gateway (VGW). Branches communicate with AWS and with each other through the VGW hub. <br><br> *CloudHub requires BGP dynamic routing — each CGW must have a unique BGP ASN so the VGW can distinguish routes from different branches and re-advertise them appropriately.<br> *Static routing cannot support CloudHub because static routes don't carry AS information needed for the VGW to re-advertise between branches — dynamic BGP route exchange is the mechanism that enables branch-to-branch routing.<br> *The VGW acts as a BGP route reflector: it learns Branch-A's prefixes via BGP and advertises them to Branch-B, and vice versa — branches never have a direct VPN tunnel between them.<br>

260. [ VPN CloudHub ] What is the maximum number of Customer Gateways per VGW in a CloudHub configuration? ; 10 CGWs per Virtual Private Gateway. Each CGW requires its own unique BGP ASN and VPN connection. <br><br> *The 10 CGW limit per VGW is a hard limit — if you need more than 10 branch sites, use Transit Gateway which supports up to 5000 attachments.<br> *Each CGW must have a unique public IP address — if a branch is behind a NAT device and multiple branches share the same public IP, they must use different pre-shared keys and are treated as separate NAT-T sessions.<br> *For enterprise-scale multi-branch connectivity (>10 sites), TGW with multiple VPN attachments is the correct architecture — it also adds multi-VPC access and ECMP capabilities not available with VGW CloudHub.<br>

261. [ VPN CloudHub ] Can VPN CloudHub work with static routing? ; No. CloudHub requires BGP dynamic routing. Each CGW must have a unique BGP ASN. Static routing cannot enable branch-to-branch communication through the VGW because static routes lack the AS information needed for route re-advertisement. <br><br> *With static routing, the VGW knows which CIDRs to reach via each VPN connection, but it does NOT re-advertise those routes to other VPN connections — static routes are not re-distributed between VPN attachments.<br> *BGP is the mechanism that enables the VGW to act as a route reflector: it learns Branch-A's BGP-advertised prefixes and re-advertises them to Branch-B as reachable via the VGW.<br> *If you need branch-to-branch connectivity and cannot use BGP (e.g., legacy device), the workaround is to use TGW with static routes — TGW can route between VPN attachments using static routes in TGW route tables, unlike VGW.<br>

262. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] A company wants to connect 3 branch offices to AWS and have the branches communicate with each other. They have a VGW. Branch traffic is low (<100 Mbps each). What is the simplest solution? ; Use VPN CloudHub: attach 3 CGWs to the existing VGW, each with a unique BGP ASN and VPN connection. Enable BGP. Branches communicate with each other and with the VPC through the VGW hub. <br><br> *CloudHub is the simplest multi-branch solution because it uses the existing VGW — no additional AWS resources (TGW, etc.) needed. Total additional cost is 2 more VPN connections + data transfer.<br> *BGP ASNs can be private (64512–65534) — assign one unique private ASN per branch. The VGW's ASN should differ from all branch ASNs.<br> *The main limitation of this approach: all branch-to-branch traffic transits AWS (adds latency vs direct WAN), and maximum 10 branches — acceptable for this small-scale use case.<br>

263. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is AWS Accelerated Site-to-Site VPN and what infrastructure does it use? ; Accelerated VPN uses AWS Global Accelerator anycast IP addresses as VPN tunnel endpoints. Traffic from the remote site enters the AWS global network at the nearest PoP (edge location) and rides the AWS backbone to the TGW region — reducing internet traversal and improving latency. <br><br> *Standard VPN: remote site → full internet path → VPN endpoint in the AWS region. Accelerated: remote site → nearest Global Accelerator PoP (~nearby) → AWS backbone → TGW region. Only the last-mile portion uses internet.<br> *Accelerated VPN is only available when the VPN connection is attached to a TGW (not VGW). The TGW creates the Global Accelerator automatically when you enable acceleration on the VPN connection.<br> *Additional cost: ~$0.025/hr per Global Accelerator + $0.01–0.08/GB data transfer premium — justified for latency-sensitive applications connecting from distant geographies (APAC to us-east-1, etc.).<br>

264. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] How does enabling VPN ECMP on Transit Gateway affect Site-to-Site VPN bandwidth? ; With ECMP enabled on TGW and BGP dynamic routing on the VPN connections, TGW distributes traffic across multiple tunnels (even across multiple VPN connections) using equal-cost paths. Each additional VPN connection adds up to 2.5 Gbps. <br><br> *Without ECMP: even if two VPN connections exist, BGP selects one best path — the other connection is standby. Active bandwidth = 1.25 Gbps per active tunnel.<br> *With ECMP: TGW hashes flows across all equal-cost tunnels. Two VPN connections (4 tunnels total) = up to 5 Gbps aggregate. Three connections = up to 7.5 Gbps. Scales linearly.<br> *ECMP requires both TGW ECMP enabled AND BGP routing on the VPN connections — static VPN connections cannot participate in ECMP because static routes don't carry the AS-path information used for ECMP hash calculations.<br>

265. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What are the Security Group rules required on an EC2 instance running a Software VPN (IPSec)? ; Allow: UDP 500 inbound/outbound (IKE phase 1), UDP 4500 inbound/outbound (NAT-T / IKE phase 2), Protocol 50 inbound/outbound (ESP — encrypted data). If using OpenVPN: UDP 1194 or TCP 443. <br><br> *Protocol 50 (ESP) is the actual encrypted data payload — without allowing it, IKE negotiates successfully (UDP 500) but no data flows (ESP blocked).<br> *UDP 4500 is NAT-T — if either end is behind NAT (common for on-prem), the VPN will switch to UDP 4500 for both IKE continuation and ESP encapsulation. Must allow both 500 and 4500.<br> *Security Groups are stateful — allowing inbound UDP 500 automatically allows the response outbound; you need explicit outbound rules only if your SG has a restrictive outbound policy (default allows all outbound).<br>

266. [ Software VPN ] Why must source/destination check be disabled on an EC2 instance acting as a VPN gateway or router? ; By default, EC2 drops packets where it is not the source or destination. A VPN/NAT/router EC2 forwards packets on behalf of other hosts — it receives packets destined for remote CIDRs and sends packets with source IPs of internal hosts. Both cases would be dropped without disabling this check. <br><br> *Source/destination check is an AWS hypervisor-level check — it drops packets at the virtualization layer before they reach the OS. It cannot be bypassed by iptables rules or kernel settings alone; you must disable it via the EC2 console/CLI.<br> *Disable via CLI: `aws ec2 modify-instance-attribute --instance-id i-xxx --no-source-dest-check` or via Console: EC2 → Actions → Networking → Change source/destination check.<br> *This same requirement applies to: NAT Instances, Software VPN, custom routing appliances, any EC2 that forwards traffic it didn't originate — a key exam scenario often tested alongside NAT Instance questions.<br>

267. [ StrongSwan ] What Linux kernel subsystem does StrongSwan use for IPSec data plane processing and why is this significant? ; StrongSwan uses the Linux kernel XFRM framework for IPSec. The keying daemon (charon) handles IKE negotiation in userspace, then programs the kernel XFRM policy/state tables. The actual ESP encryption/decryption happens in the kernel, not userspace — this gives near-wire-speed performance. <br><br> *Kernel-level IPSec processing avoids the performance penalty of copying packets between kernel and userspace for encryption — this is why StrongSwan can saturate a 10 Gbps NIC on modern hardware.<br> *This contrasts with OpenVPN, which runs entirely in userspace and copies every packet through a TUN/TAP device — OpenVPN has higher CPU overhead per GB transferred.<br> *On AWS EC2, choose network-optimized instances (c5n, m5n, r5n) for StrongSwan in high-throughput scenarios — these instances have enhanced networking (ENA + SR-IOV) that reduces CPU overhead for packet processing.<br>

268. [ StrongSwan ] A StrongSwan EC2 VPN is set up but traffic flows one way only (on-prem can reach VPC, VPC cannot reach on-prem). What are the likely causes? ; Three common causes: 1) VPC route table missing a route: remote CIDR → StrongSwan ENI. 2) IP forwarding not enabled on EC2 (sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward=1). 3) Source/destination check not disabled on EC2 ENI. <br><br> *The VPC route table tells the AWS router where to send traffic destined for the on-prem CIDR — without this route, traffic from VPC instances has no path to reach StrongSwan. Add: `192.168.0.0/24 → eni-xxx` (StrongSwan's ENI).<br> *IP forwarding in the Linux kernel allows the StrongSwan EC2 to forward packets it receives (destined for on-prem) — without it, the kernel receives the packet from the IPSec decrypt but silently drops it instead of routing it out.<br> *Source/destination check is the AWS hypervisor check — even if the kernel has ip_forward enabled, AWS will drop packets at the hypervisor if the EC2 ENI is not the packet's source/destination and the check is enabled.<br>

269. [ OpenVPN ] What is the difference between OpenVPN Community Edition and OpenVPN Access Server? ; Community Edition: open source, CLI-configured, no web UI, free, supports any number of clients but requires manual management. Access Server: commercial product, web-based admin UI, user authentication (LDAP/SAML/MFA), client provisioning portal, limited free tier (2 connections), paid for more. <br><br> *Community Edition is the better choice for site-to-site VPN (no user management needed) — it's free and has all necessary routing features; the web UI of Access Server adds no value for site-to-site use.<br> *Access Server is designed for client-to-site use cases (remote workers) — the web portal lets users download their .ovpn profile and the admin UI manages user access without editing config files.<br> *On AWS Marketplace, OpenVPN Access Server is available as a pre-configured AMI — useful for quick deployment but has licensing costs per concurrent connection beyond the free 2-connection limit.<br>

270. [ OpenVPN ] How does OpenVPN differ from IPSec VPN at the protocol level? ; OpenVPN uses TLS (SSL) to create an encrypted tunnel, running over UDP or TCP. IPSec uses IKE for key exchange and ESP for data encryption — these are IP-layer protocols. OpenVPN is application-layer; IPSec is network-layer. <br><br> *OpenVPN can traverse firewalls more easily than IPSec — it runs on TCP 443 (looks like HTTPS) or UDP 1194, while IPSec uses protocol 50 (ESP) which some firewalls block or don't understand.<br> *IPSec (StrongSwan) is faster because encryption/decryption happens in the kernel; OpenVPN runs in userspace and copies every packet through a TUN device, adding CPU overhead.<br> *IPSec is the industry standard for site-to-site VPN (all hardware VPN appliances speak IPSec); OpenVPN is more common for client-to-site. AWS Managed S2S VPN uses IPSec — it cannot interoperate with OpenVPN directly.<br>

271. [ Remote Connectivity ] A company needs to connect its on-premises network to 50 VPCs across 3 AWS accounts. They currently use Site-to-Site VPN with VGW on each VPC. What is wrong with this architecture and how should it be redesigned? ; Problem: 50 VGW-based VPN connections requires 50 VPN endpoints on-prem (50 CGW configs, 50 × 2 tunnels = 100 tunnels to manage). Redesign: use TGW shared via RAM across 3 accounts. Single or few VPN connections to TGW reach all 50 VPCs via TGW routing. <br><br> *VGW-based VPN is 1:1 — one VPN connection per VPC. With 50 VPCs, you need 50 VPN connections and 50 separate tunnel configurations on your on-prem router, which is operationally unmanageable and expensive.<br> *TGW centralization: create one TGW in a network account, share via RAM to all 3 accounts, attach all 50 VPCs. Configure 1-2 VPN connections (with ECMP for redundancy) to the TGW. On-prem router has 2-4 tunnel configs instead of 100.<br> *This also enables east-west traffic between VPCs via TGW route tables, centralized BGP management, and a single point for network policy enforcement — all impossible with 50 individual VGW VPN connections.<br>

272. [ Remote Connectivity ] When should you choose AWS Direct Connect over Site-to-Site VPN? ; Choose DX when: bandwidth requirement exceeds 2.5 Gbps consistently, latency SLA is critical (VPN latency varies with internet), compliance requires private connectivity (not over internet), or data transfer costs at high volume favor DX (DX GB rate < VPN over internet GB rate). <br><br> *DX provides consistent, dedicated bandwidth not shared with internet traffic — internet-based VPN bandwidth fluctuates based on internet congestion, especially during peak hours.<br> *Cost crossover: DX data transfer is ~$0.02/GB vs internet ~$0.09/GB for data out. At high sustained transfer (TB/month), DX port cost is quickly offset by transfer savings. Rough break-even: 1-3 TB/month depending on region.<br> *Timeline matters: DX takes 4-12 weeks to provision; VPN can be up in minutes. For urgent hybrid requirements, start with VPN, then migrate to DX once provisioned — using DX primary + VPN backup is a common final architecture.<br>

273. [ Remote Connectivity ] What is the recommended architecture for a company that has Direct Connect but needs encrypted connectivity for compliance? ; Run IPSec Site-to-Site VPN OVER the Direct Connect connection using a Public VIF. Traffic takes the private DX path (low latency, dedicated bandwidth) AND is encrypted by IPSec (compliance). <br><br> *DX alone does not encrypt traffic — it's a private connection but not encrypted. Some compliance frameworks (PCI-DSS, HIPAA) require encryption in transit even on private circuits.<br> *Using a Public VIF: establish a Public VIF on the DX connection (accesses AWS public IPs including VPN endpoints), then create a Site-to-Site VPN connection. VPN tunnel travels over the DX private path to reach the AWS VPN endpoint public IP.<br> *This combination gives: DX benefits (low latency, consistent bandwidth, lower GB cost) + IPSec encryption. MACsec (available on Dedicated DX 10G/100G) is an alternative — L2 encryption before traffic even enters AWS network, but requires compatible hardware.<br>

274. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What CloudWatch metric indicates that a VPN tunnel is down? ; TunnelState metric with value 0 = tunnel down, value 1 = tunnel up. Set a CloudWatch alarm: TunnelState = 0 for > 1 minute → alert operations. <br><br> *Additional useful metrics: TunnelDataIn/TunnelDataOut (bytes) — if TunnelState=1 but no data flowing, suspect routing or SG issue not a tunnel issue. TunnelPacketDropCountNoSA = packets dropped because no matching IPSec SA (indicates IKE negotiation issue).<br> *AWS also publishes Personal Health Dashboard events for planned VPN maintenance — subscribe to these via EventBridge to get advance notice and prepare for temporary tunnel interruptions.<br> *For Software VPN (EC2-based), CloudWatch metrics are not automatic — you need CloudWatch Agent + custom metrics or use a health check script that pushes tunnel state as a custom metric.<br>

275. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] A company uses static routing for their Site-to-Site VPN. They add a new subnet on-prem. What must they do in AWS to ensure traffic to the new subnet is routed via VPN? ; Add the new subnet CIDR as a static route in the VPN connection configuration AND add the CIDR to the VPC route table pointing to the VGW. Two places to update: VPN static routes + VPC route tables. <br><br> *Static routing requires manual updates on both sides whenever network topology changes — this is a key operational burden vs BGP which auto-propagates new routes.<br> *VPC route tables must also be updated: add `192.168.2.0/24 → vgw-xxx` (new on-prem subnet). If route propagation is enabled on the route table, VGW-learned routes appear automatically — but only BGP routes, not static VPN routes, are propagated this way.<br> *For production environments with frequently changing on-prem networks, static routing becomes operationally expensive — each CIDR change requires coordinated updates. Migrating to BGP eliminates this operational overhead.<br>

276. [ VPN CloudHub ] A branch office fails to communicate with another branch office through VPN CloudHub. BGP sessions are up on both sides. What should you check? ; Check that each branch has a UNIQUE BGP ASN. CloudHub requires unique ASNs for each CGW — duplicate ASNs cause BGP loop prevention to drop re-advertised routes. Also verify that VGW route propagation is enabled on the VPC route table. <br><br> *BGP loop prevention rule: a BGP speaker will not accept a route that contains its own ASN in the AS-path — this prevents routing loops. If two branches use the same ASN, Branch-A's routes (with ASN 65001) are re-advertised to Branch-B, but Branch-B (also ASN 65001) will see its own ASN in the path and DROP the route.<br> *Also check: VGW route propagation is enabled on all relevant VPC route tables. Without propagation, the VGW doesn't insert learned routes into the route table even if BGP is working correctly.<br> *Use `aws ec2 describe-vpn-connections` to review BGP status and advertised/received prefix counts — if a branch receives 0 routes, the issue is on the VGW re-advertisement side.<br>

277. [ Software VPN ] A company wants to deploy a Software VPN (StrongSwan) on EC2 in high availability configuration. What architecture should they use? ; Two StrongSwan EC2 instances in different AZs, behind a Network Load Balancer (NLB) for UDP 500 and UDP 4500. VPC route tables point to ENI of active instance; use health check script or Lambda to update route table on failover. <br><br> *NLB handles UDP traffic natively (starting from 2018) — configure NLB listener on UDP 500 and UDP 4500, target group with both StrongSwan EC2s using health checks.<br> *Challenge: IPSec is stateful — IKE and ESP sessions must stick to the same EC2 for the duration of the connection. Use NLB source IP hash (flow hash) for session stickiness — same source IP always routes to same target.<br> *Route table update for failover: when StrongSwan-A fails, Lambda triggered by CloudWatch alarm updates VPC route tables to point `on-prem-CIDR → StrongSwan-B ENI`. This ENI-based failover has ~30-60 second convergence time.<br>

278. [ Remote Connectivity ] What is the AWS Network Manager and how does it relate to VPN connectivity? ; AWS Network Manager is a centralized dashboard for monitoring and managing global network resources including TGWs, VPN connections, DX connections, SD-WAN devices, and on-premises routers. It provides topology visualization, event monitoring, and route analysis. <br><br> *Network Manager uses a "global network" construct — you register TGWs, VPN CGWs, DX connections, and on-premises devices (via SD-WAN integration) to get a unified view of your hybrid network.<br> *It integrates with CloudWatch Events/EventBridge: changes in VPN tunnel state, BGP session state, or DX connection state generate events you can act on with Lambda automations.<br> *Route Analyzer in Network Manager lets you trace the routing path between two points in your global network (including through TGW) without sending actual traffic — useful for validating VPN routing configurations before cutover.<br>

279. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What happens to a VPN tunnel when the on-prem router's public IP changes? ; The VPN tunnel goes down. The Customer Gateway (CGW) resource in AWS stores the on-prem device's public IP — it must be updated to reflect the new IP, and the VPN connection re-established. <br><br> *The CGW is an immutable record of the on-prem device's public IP — you cannot update the IP on an existing CGW. You must create a NEW CGW with the new IP, then modify or recreate the VPN connection to use the new CGW.<br> *This is why NAT-T (UDP 4500) is important — if the on-prem device is behind NAT (dynamic public IP), the VPN may drop and reconnect when the NAT mapping changes. Using a static NAT (fixed public IP from ISP) for the VPN device avoids this.<br> *Best practice: use a static, dedicated public IP for your on-prem VPN device — shared or dynamic IPs cause tunnel instability and operational complexity when IPs change.<br>

280. [ Remote Connectivity ] A company has Site-to-Site VPN to AWS and wants to add Direct Connect for higher bandwidth. How should they configure BGP to use DX as primary and VPN as backup? ; Set lower MED (Multi-Exit Discriminator) on DX-advertised routes compared to VPN routes.
