From AWS side, use BGP communities to prefer DX for return traffic. AWS advertises DX routes with better attributes (shorter AS path) by default. <br><br> *MED lower = more preferred. Set MED 100 on DX BGP session, MED 200 on VPN BGP session. Your on-prem router selects DX as best path. When DX fails, BGP withdraws DX routes → VPN routes (MED 200) become active → automatic failover.<br> *For traffic FROM AWS back to on-prem: AWS prefers DX over VPN automatically (DX has shorter AS path). To influence this further, use AS path prepending on the VPN side — prepend your ASN 2-3 times on VPN advertisements to make AWS strongly prefer DX.<br> *Test failover: use DX Resiliency Toolkit to simulate DX failure and verify traffic switches to VPN. Measure BGP convergence time — default BGP timer (90s hold) can be reduced using BFD for sub-second failure detection.<br>

281. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What pre-shared key (PSK) best practices should be followed for AWS Site-to-Site VPN? ; Use strong random PSKs (minimum 8 chars, max 64 chars, alphanumeric + special characters). Store PSKs in AWS Secrets Manager. Rotate PSKs periodically. Use different PSKs for Tunnel 1 and Tunnel 2. Certificate-based auth (IKEv2) is more secure than PSK for production. <br><br> *AWS automatically generates a random PSK when you create a VPN connection — use this rather than setting your own weak PSK. The generated PSK is available in the VPN configuration download.<br> *PSKs are symmetric secrets — if your on-prem VPN device is compromised, the PSK can be extracted, allowing an attacker to impersonate either VPN endpoint. Certificate-based IKEv2 avoids this by using asymmetric keys.<br> *Store the PSK in Secrets Manager (not in code, config files, or Confluence) — rotate it by updating both the AWS VPN tunnel options and the on-prem device simultaneously during a maintenance window.<br>

282. [ Remote Connectivity ] When would you use a third-party VPN appliance from AWS Marketplace (e.g., Cisco CSR) instead of AWS Managed Site-to-Site VPN? ; Use third-party appliances when: existing vendor standardization (all sites run Cisco, maintaining one platform), advanced SD-WAN features needed, specific compliance requiring certified hardware/software, protocol support beyond IPSec (DMVPN, GET VPN, GRE+IPSec), or existing BYOL license investment. <br><br> *Cisco CSR 1000v on EC2 speaks full Cisco IOS-XE — companies running Cisco everywhere can manage the AWS endpoint with the same tools, configs, and policies as their on-prem routers. Operational familiarity reduces errors.<br> *AWS Managed VPN doesn't support DMVPN (Dynamic Multipoint VPN), Cisco GET VPN (group encryption), or other Cisco-proprietary protocols that large enterprise WANs use — a Cisco CSR on EC2 does.<br> *Cost consideration: Cisco CSR licensing + EC2 cost is higher than AWS Managed VPN for simple use cases. Only justified when the advanced features or vendor standardization provide tangible operational benefits.<br>

283. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is the difference between "route propagation" on a VPC route table and static route entries for VPN? ; Route propagation (VGW propagation enabled on RT): VGW automatically injects BGP-learned routes into the route table as they change. Static route entries: manually added CIDR → VGW entries that never change automatically. BGP propagation = dynamic; static entries = manual. <br><br> *Enable route propagation per-route-table: in VPC console → Route Tables → select RT → Route Propagation tab → enable for the VGW. BGP routes from VGW are then automatically added/removed as BGP sessions change.<br> *Static entries override propagated routes if they have the same prefix — more specific routes always win (longest prefix match). If you have both a static entry and a propagated route for the same CIDR, the static entry doesn't necessarily win; longest prefix wins.<br> *If using static routing on the VPN connection (not BGP), route propagation on the RT has no effect — static VPN routes are NOT automatically propagated. You must manually add static routes to VPC route tables.<br>

284. [ Remote Connectivity ] A company's VPN tunnel shows TunnelState=1 (up) in CloudWatch but applications cannot connect to on-prem servers. What layers should be investigated? ; Check in order: 1) VPC route tables (route to on-prem CIDR exists pointing to VGW?), 2) Security Groups on EC2 instances (allow traffic from on-prem CIDR?), 3) NACLs on subnets (stateless — check both inbound and outbound rules), 4) On-prem firewall (allows traffic from VPC CIDR?), 5) Application layer (listening port, firewall on OS). <br><br> *TunnelState=1 means IPSec/IKE is established — the tunnel layer is fine. Connectivity issues with tunnel up are almost always routing or firewall/SG issues, not VPN issues.<br> *VPC Flow Logs are the fastest diagnostic tool: enable flow logs on the subnet, send test traffic, check if action=ACCEPT (routing/SG OK → app layer issue) or action=REJECT (routing or SG blocking). REJECT in flow logs narrows it to AWS-side network layer.<br> *Common mistake: SG allows traffic from VPC CIDR but not from on-prem CIDR. On-prem traffic arrives at EC2 with source IP in on-prem range (e.g., 192.168.x.x) — SG rules must explicitly allow that source CIDR.<br>

285. [ Software VPN ] What is WireGuard and how does it compare to OpenVPN and IPSec/StrongSwan for AWS Software VPN use? ; WireGuard is a modern, minimal VPN protocol built into Linux kernel 5.6+. Faster than OpenVPN (kernel-based like IPSec), simpler config than StrongSwan, smaller codebase (auditable). Uses ChaCha20 + Poly1305 encryption. Not supported by AWS Managed VPN. <br><br> *WireGuard performance: kernel-based like IPSec (no userspace copy overhead), uses ChaCha20 which is hardware-accelerated on ARM (good for Graviton EC2) and fast on CPUs without AES-NI.<br> *Simplicity: WireGuard config is 10 lines vs hundreds for StrongSwan ipsec.conf. No IKE state machine, no phase 1/2 — just static keys and routes. Easier to audit and less attack surface.<br> *Limitation: WireGuard doesn't support dynamic routing (BGP) natively — best for static site-to-site with known subnets. For dynamic environments needing BGP, use StrongSwan. AWS Managed VPN cannot interoperate with WireGuard (AWS speaks IPSec/IKE only).<br>

286. [ Remote Connectivity ] Explain the Transit Gateway Connect attachment and when to use it instead of Site-to-Site VPN. ; TGW Connect attachment uses GRE (Generic Routing Encapsulation) tunnel over existing VPC or DX attachments, with BGP for dynamic routing. Purpose: connect SD-WAN appliances in a VPC to TGW without IPSec overhead. Bandwidth: up to 5 Gbps per Connect peer (vs 1.25 Gbps per VPN tunnel). <br><br> *Connect is designed for SD-WAN integration: deploy an SD-WAN appliance (Cisco, VMware, Fortinet) in a VPC, create a Connect attachment to TGW, configure GRE tunnel + BGP. The SD-WAN manages connectivity to branch offices outside AWS.<br> *GRE has lower overhead than IPSec — no encryption key negotiation, no ESP overhead. Useful when encryption is handled at another layer (MACsec on DX, SD-WAN encryption) and you need maximum throughput.<br> *Each Connect attachment supports up to 4 Connect peers, each with up to 5 Gbps — total 20 Gbps per Connect attachment, far exceeding VPN's 2.5 Gbps per connection. Use Connect for high-bandwidth SD-WAN hub-in-VPC scenarios.<br>

287. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What AWS service can be used to test if a proposed VPN routing configuration will work before deploying? ; AWS Network Manager Route Analyzer and AWS Reachability Analyzer. Route Analyzer (Network Manager) traces routing through TGW global networks. Reachability Analyzer analyzes path between two ENIs including TGW, SG, NACLs. <br><br> *Reachability Analyzer is particularly useful for pre-deployment testing: define source ENI (on-prem-side representation) and destination ENI, run analysis — it reports whether a path exists and exactly where it's blocked (missing route, SG deny, NACL deny).<br> *Network Manager Route Analyzer works at the TGW route table level — input source attachment and destination CIDR, it traces which route table entry would be used and which attachment the traffic would exit.<br> *Neither tool sends actual traffic — they perform configuration analysis. For actual traffic testing, use VPC Flow Logs + a test EC2 sending traffic to the on-prem destination, checking flow logs for ACCEPT/REJECT.<br>

288. [ Remote Connectivity ] A startup needs temporary VPN connectivity to AWS for a 3-month project. They have no hardware VPN appliance. What is the fastest and cheapest option? ; Use AWS Site-to-Site VPN with a free open-source software gateway on an existing Linux server (StrongSwan or OpenVPN). AWS side: create CGW + VPN connection ($0.05/hr = ~$36/month). On-prem side: StrongSwan on any Linux machine. Total cost: ~$36/month + data transfer. <br><br> *No hardware purchase needed — StrongSwan runs on any Linux VM, including an existing server, a cloud VM, or even a Raspberry Pi. The AWS-generated VPN config file provides all settings needed for StrongSwan configuration.<br> *If no Linux server is available, use the OpenVPN Community Edition — it also runs on Windows and macOS, enabling a laptop or workstation to act as a site-to-site VPN endpoint temporarily.<br> *Faster alternative: AWS Client VPN can be set up in 15 minutes and allows individual users to access VPC resources — if only a few developers need access (not full network-to-network), Client VPN may be simpler and cheaper for this use case.<br>

289. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is the Customer Gateway (CGW) resource in AWS and what information does it store? ; CGW is an AWS resource that represents your on-premises VPN device. It stores: the public IP address of your on-prem VPN device, the BGP ASN (for dynamic routing), and optionally a certificate ARN (for certificate-based auth). CGW does not represent the VPN connection itself — it's just a record of the peer device. <br><br> *You can create multiple VPN connections using the same CGW (same on-prem device connecting to multiple VGWs or TGWs). The CGW is reusable across connections.<br> *The CGW is a configuration record only — creating a CGW doesn't establish any connection or incur cost. Cost is incurred only when you create a VPN Connection using the CGW.<br> *If your on-prem device's public IP changes (ISP change, failover to backup ISP), you cannot edit the CGW's IP. You must create a new CGW with the new IP and update the VPN connection to use it — a key operational consideration for sites with dynamic public IPs.<br>

290. [ Remote Connectivity ] Compare the costs of AWS Managed Site-to-Site VPN, Software VPN on EC2 (t3.small), and AWS Direct Connect (1Gbps hosted) for a scenario transferring 10 TB/month outbound. ; S2S VPN: $0.05/hr × 720hr = $36 + $0.09/GB × 10,000GB = $900 + $36 = ~$936/month. EC2 t3.small: ~$15/month + NAT/data $0.09/GB = $900 + $15 = ~$915/month. DX 1G Hosted: ~$100-200/month port + $0.02/GB × 10,000GB = $200 + $100 = ~$300/month. <br><br> *At 10 TB/month outbound, DX is significantly cheaper — the $0.02/GB DX transfer rate vs $0.09/GB internet rate saves ~$700/month, quickly justifying the DX port cost.<br> *Software VPN on EC2 saves the $36/month VPN connection fee but doesn't save on data transfer costs (internet egress is still $0.09/GB regardless of whether you use AWS Managed VPN or your own EC2) — the savings are small for data-heavy workloads.<br> *The main advantage of Software VPN cost-wise is for low-traffic scenarios: if you transfer <500 GB/month, the EC2 cost (t3.micro ~$8/month) may be less than the $36/month VPN connection fee. Above ~500 GB the data transfer dominates and the difference is negligible.<br>

291. [ Site-to-Site VPN ] What is the purpose of the IKE Phase 1 and Phase 2 negotiations in a VPN connection? ; Phase 1 (IKE SA): establishes a secure authenticated channel between VPN peers. Negotiates: encryption algo, hash, DH group, lifetime, auth method (PSK or cert). Creates the IKE SA. Phase 2 (IPSec SA / Child SA): uses the Phase 1 channel to negotiate the actual IPSec tunnel parameters (ESP encryption, hash, PFS). Creates the IPSec SA for data traffic. <br><br> *Phase 1 exists solely to protect Phase 2 negotiation — it creates a temporary encrypted channel for key exchange. Traffic does NOT flow through Phase 1; it flows through the Phase 2 IPSec SA.<br> *AWS VPN default Phase 1 lifetime: 28800 seconds (8 hours). Phase 2 lifetime: 3600 seconds (1 hour). Both are configurable. SAs are renegotiated before expiry (rekey) to maintain continuous connectivity.<br> *Mismatched Phase 1 or Phase 2 parameters (e.g., on-prem uses AES128 but AWS configured AES256) will prevent tunnel establishment. The AWS VPN configuration download file shows exactly what parameters AWS is configured with — use these as reference for on-prem config.<br>

292. [ Remote Connectivity ] A company needs to connect its AWS VPCs to Azure VNets with low latency and high bandwidth. What options exist? ; Options: 1) Both clouds connected to a colocation exchange (Equinix/Megaport) — DX from AWS + ExpressRoute from Azure to same exchange. 2) VPN over internet: AWS S2S VPN endpoint + Azure VPN Gateway, both internet-facing. 3) Third-party SD-WAN connecting both. Exchange-based is best for production. <br><br> *Exchange-based multicloud: your traffic AWS → DX → exchange PoP → ExpressRoute → Azure never touches public internet. Lowest latency, private connectivity, providers like Megaport provide point-and-click circuit provisioning.<br> *VPN over internet: easy to set up (minutes vs weeks for exchange) but subject to internet latency/variability. Use for dev/test or as backup while exchange connectivity is provisioned.<br> *AWS does not have a native Azure interconnect product — multicloud connectivity is always through an intermediary (exchange/colocation provider, SD-WAN, or internet). Plan for this dependency when designing multicloud architectures.<br>

293. [ StrongSwan ] How would you configure StrongSwan on EC2 to interoperate with AWS Managed Site-to-Site VPN (where StrongSwan is the on-prem side)? ; Download the AWS VPN connection config file (select "Generic" vendor), extract the IKE/ESP settings, tunnel IPs, pre-shared keys, and BGP peer IPs. Configure /etc/ipsec.conf with those values. Configure /etc/ipsec.secrets with PSKs. Enable BGP (FRRouting/quagga) for dynamic routing. <br><br> *AWS generates a configuration for both tunnels — configure both in ipsec.conf as separate conn blocks (conn tunnel1, conn tunnel2) with the respective tunnel outside IPs and pre-shared keys. Both tunnels can be active simultaneously.<br> *For BGP: after IPSec tunnels are up, configure FRRouting with BGP: neighbor the AWS BGP peer IPs (inside tunnel IPs provided in the config download), advertise on-prem CIDRs, receive VPC CIDRs. The tunnel inside IPs use /30 subnets from the 169.254.x.x range.<br> *Test the tunnel: `ipsec statusall` shows IKE and IPSec SA state. `ipsec status` shows short summary. Once tunnel is up, `ping 169.254.x.x` (AWS BGP peer IP) should succeed. Then check BGP: `vtysh -c "show bgp summary"` in FRRouting.<br>

294. [ Remote Connectivity ] What are the BGP inside tunnel IP addresses used for in AWS Site-to-Site VPN? ; Inside tunnel IPs are the BGP peering addresses — /30 subnets from the 169.254.x.x range (link-local). AWS assigns one IP, your device gets the other. These are used for BGP sessions inside the tunnel, not for routing actual VPC or on-prem traffic. <br><br> *Example: AWS tunnel inside IP: 169.254.12.1/30, your device: 169.254.12.2/30. BGP neighbor statement: `neighbor 169.254.12.1 remote-as 7224` (AWS's BGP ASN). BGP session runs over these link-local IPs inside the IPSec tunnel.<br> *The 169.254.x.x range is used specifically because it's link-local (not routed externally) — these IPs are only reachable through the tunnel itself. You cannot ping them from outside the tunnel.<br> *AWS default BGP ASN for VGW: 7224 (public ASN). For TGW, you configure the ASN (default 64512). Verify the BGP ASN in your VPN connection details before configuring your router.<br>

295. [ Resource Scope ] Which AWS services are global (not regional) in scope? ; IAM (users, roles, policies), Route 53, CloudFront distributions, AWS Organizations, Direct Connect Gateway, Global Accelerator, AWS WAF Classic, S3 bucket namespace, AWS Artifact, AWS Support plans. <br><br> *Global means the resource exists once per AWS account, is accessible from all regions, and is not tied to any specific region — creating an IAM role in the us-east-1 console makes it usable in every region.<br> *S3 is a common exam confusion: the bucket NAME is globally unique (global namespace), but the bucket DATA resides in the specific region you chose at creation — data is regional, name is global.<br> *Direct Connect Gateway is global: one DXGW can connect a DX connection to VPCs in multiple regions, unlike VGW which is regional and limited to one VPC.<br>

296. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of an IAM role, and what are the implications for cross-region access? ; IAM is global — roles, users, and policies exist once per account and are usable in every AWS region simultaneously. Creating a role in the us-east-1 console creates a global resource — no per-region recreation needed. <br><br> *The IAM console in us-east-1 shows the same roles as the IAM console in eu-west-1 — they are the same global resources. The regional console selector doesn't affect IAM.<br> *Implication for security: a compromised IAM credential works in ALL regions — there is no region-level IAM isolation. Use SCPs in AWS Organizations to restrict which regions can be accessed by specific accounts.<br> *Global IAM resources are exempt from region-based SCP conditions like `aws:RequestedRegion` — SCPs with region restrictions only affect regional service API calls, not IAM itself.<br>

297. [ Resource Scope ] A developer created an ACM certificate in eu-west-1 for their domain and tries to attach it to a CloudFront distribution. The certificate does not appear in the CloudFront console. Why? ; CloudFront is a global service and only reads ACM certificates from the us-east-1 region. The certificate must be created (or re-requested) in us-east-1 to be available for CloudFront. <br><br> *This is one of the most common ACM/CloudFront exam traps: even if your origin and users are in Europe, the CloudFront certificate MUST exist in us-east-1 — CloudFront pulls cert data from that specific region.<br> *For ALB, NLB, and API Gateway, use the certificate in the same region as the resource — those services read ACM from their own region, not us-east-1.<br> *Practical implication for multi-region architectures: you may need two certificates — one in us-east-1 for CloudFront, and one in each region for regional ALBs — even if they cover the same domain name.<br>

298. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of a VPC, and what does that mean for subnets and security groups? ; VPC is regional — it spans all AZs in a region. Subnets are AZ-scoped (each subnet lives in exactly one AZ). Security Groups are regional (scoped to the VPC). NACLs are regional (scoped to the VPC). <br><br> *A VPC with CIDR 10.0.0.0/16 in us-east-1 automatically spans us-east-1a, us-east-1b, us-east-1c, etc. — you then create per-AZ subnets within it.<br> *Security groups are regional (you can reference SG IDs in the same VPC/region in rules) but cannot reference SGs from another region or VPC without TGW SG referencing enabled.<br> *Each subnet belongs to exactly one AZ — this is why HA requires multiple subnets in multiple AZs, each with its own resources (EC2, RDS, etc.).<br>

299. [ Resource Scope ] An EBS volume exists in us-east-1a. An EC2 instance in us-east-1b needs access to this data. What is the correct approach? ; EBS volumes are AZ-scoped and cannot be attached to instances in a different AZ. Create a snapshot of the volume (regional resource), then create a new EBS volume from the snapshot in us-east-1b, and attach it to the EC2 instance. <br><br> *EBS snapshot is a regional resource — once created, you can create a new volume from it in any AZ within the same region, or copy it to another region for cross-region use.<br> *Alternative: use EFS (Elastic File System) which is regional and accessible from any AZ via mount targets — EFS is the right choice when multiple instances across AZs need shared storage.<br> *For cross-region EBS data movement: copy the snapshot to the target region (snapshot copy operation), then create a volume from it in the target region's desired AZ.<br>

300. [ Resource Scope ] What is the difference between a DynamoDB standard table and a DynamoDB Global Table in terms of scope? ; Standard DynamoDB table: regional — data lives in one region. Global Table: multi-region active-active — data is automatically replicated to all configured regions, with sub-second replication. <br><br> *Global Tables enable active-active writes: applications in any configured region can read AND write locally, and DynamoDB replicates changes to all other regions using last-write-wins conflict resolution.<br> *Converting a standard table to a Global Table requires enabling DynamoDB Streams (streams are used under the hood for replication) and then adding replica regions in the console/CLI.<br> *For KMS encryption with Global Tables: use a KMS Multi-Region Key (MRK) so the same key material is available in all replica regions — standard regional CMKs cannot decrypt data replicated to another region.<br>

301. [ Resource Scope ] What is a KMS Multi-Region Key (MRK) and when is it required? ; KMS MRK is a set of KMS keys with the same key ID and key material, replicated across multiple AWS regions. Required when data encrypted in one region must be decrypted in another — e.g., DynamoDB Global Tables, S3 CRR with SSE-KMS, cross-region Lambda, cross-region backup. <br><br> *Standard KMS CMK is regional — a key in us-east-1 cannot decrypt ciphertext in eu-west-1. MRK solves this by sharing key material: mrk-abc123 in us-east-1 = same key as mrk-abc123 in eu-west-1.<br> *MRKs have the same key ID prefix `mrk-` in all regions. They are interoperable: data encrypted with the us-east-1 MRK can be decrypted with the eu-west-1 replica — no re-encryption needed during replication.<br> *MRKs are not automatically replicated everywhere — you explicitly add replica regions. Deleting the primary MRK requires deleting all replicas first; there is a 7-30 day waiting period before deletion takes effect.<br>

302. [ Resource Scope ] Why must NAT Gateways be deployed one per AZ rather than one per region? ; NAT Gateway is an AZ-scoped resource. A private subnet in AZ-b routing traffic to a NAT GW in AZ-a incurs cross-AZ data transfer charges (~$0.01/GB each way) AND if AZ-a fails, all outbound internet connectivity from AZ-b and AZ-c also fails. <br><br> *Best practice: create one NAT GW in each AZ, and configure each AZ's private route table to point 0.0.0.0/0 to the NAT GW in the SAME AZ — this eliminates both the cross-AZ cost and the single-AZ SPOF.<br> *Cross-AZ data transfer to NAT GW is often overlooked in cost optimization reviews — in high-traffic environments, the cross-AZ fees from a single NAT GW can exceed the cost of additional NAT GWs.<br> *NAT Gateway is described as a "regional service" in AWS docs but physically deployed per-AZ — the confusion arises because you manage it via regional APIs but deploy it into a specific subnet (and therefore AZ).<br>

303. [ Resource Scope ] What is an AWS Local Zone and how does it differ from a standard AZ? ; Local Zone is an AWS infrastructure extension in a metro area (e.g., Los Angeles, Chicago). It is part of the parent region (same VPC, same API) but physically located closer to end users in that city. Unlike standard AZs, Local Zones are opt-in and offer a subset of services. <br><br> *A subnet in a Local Zone (e.g., us-west-2-lax-1a) is still part of the us-west-2 VPC — you can attach it to the same VPC, same security groups, same TGW as your main AZ subnets.<br> *Not all services are available in Local Zones — primarily EC2, EBS, ELB, VPC subnets, and RDS. Services like Lambda, SQS, and complex managed services are not in Local Zones.<br> *Local Zones provide single-digit millisecond latency to users in that metro area — use cases include media production, game streaming, real-time collaboration tools where 30-50ms from a distant region is unacceptable.<br>

304. [ Resource Scope ] How does AWS Wavelength differ from Local Zones? ; Wavelength embeds AWS compute at 5G carrier network edges (Verizon, Vodafone, KDDI), providing <10ms latency to 5G devices without traffic crossing the internet. Local Zones provide sub-10ms latency to metro-area users via the internet. Wavelength traffic stays on the carrier network. <br><br> *Wavelength is co-located at the carrier's 5G mobile edge — traffic from a 5G device to a Wavelength endpoint stays entirely on the carrier network (no internet traversal), enabling ultra-low latency for mobile use cases.<br> *Local Zones extend AWS to city locations — traffic from users reaches the Local Zone via the internet (still faster than going to the parent region, but not carrier-native).<br> *Services in Wavelength are more limited (EC2, EBS, VPC only); Local Zones support more services including ELB and RDS. Use Wavelength for 5G mobile apps; use Local Zones for general metro-area latency reduction.<br>

305. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of CloudWatch metrics and alarms, and how do you get cross-region visibility? ; CloudWatch is regional — metrics, alarms, dashboards, and log groups are scoped to the region they're created in. Cross-region visibility options: CloudWatch cross-region dashboards, CloudWatch metric streams to Kinesis Firehose → central S3/OpenSearch, or AWS Observability Access Manager. <br><br> *A CloudWatch alarm in us-east-1 cannot trigger based on a metric from eu-west-1 — alarms only reference metrics in the same region. For cross-region alarming, replicate metrics or use a central monitoring account with metric streams.<br> *CloudWatch cross-region dashboards allow adding metric widgets from any region — you get a single dashboard view but the underlying alarms and actions remain regional.<br> *For centralized monitoring across accounts and regions: use CloudWatch cross-account observability (share metrics/logs from source accounts to a monitoring account) combined with metric streams for Firehose delivery.<br>

306. [ Resource Scope ] A company uses AWS Organizations and needs to prevent any AWS resources from being created outside eu-west-1 and eu-central-1 for data sovereignty. What is the correct mechanism? ; Apply a Service Control Policy (SCP) to the root OU (or specific OUs/accounts) with a Deny on all actions where aws:RequestedRegion is not in [eu-west-1, eu-central-1]. Note: global services (IAM, Route 53, CloudFront) must be explicitly exempted. <br><br> *The SCP condition: `"StringNotEquals": {"aws:RequestedRegion": ["eu-west-1","eu-central-1"]}` — but you must add a `NotAction` or separate allow for global services, otherwise the SCP blocks IAM console access and other essential global APIs.<br> *Global services like IAM, Route 53, STS, and CloudFront do not have a region in their API calls — `aws:RequestedRegion` is empty for them, so the region deny condition won't match and they are not restricted by this SCP pattern.<br> *AWS provides a sample "deny all outside approved regions" SCP in the AWS documentation that includes the correct global service exemption list — always use that as a template rather than writing from scratch.<br>

307. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Transit Gateway, and how do you connect TGWs across regions? ; TGW is regional. To connect VPCs across regions, create a TGW Peering attachment between two regional TGWs. Traffic travels over the AWS backbone (encrypted by default). TGW peering uses static routes only — no BGP between TGWs. <br><br> *Each TGW peering is one-to-one between a specific pair of TGWs. For full mesh across N regions, you need N*(N-1)/2 peering attachments — consider a hub TGW design instead.<br> *TGW peering is non-transitive: if TGW-A peers with TGW-B, and TGW-B peers with TGW-C, traffic from TGW-A cannot reach TGW-C through TGW-B. You need a direct peering or accept the hub design.<br> *Unlike VPC peering (also non-transitive), TGW at least provides transitive routing within a region — all TGW-attached VPCs can communicate via the hub. Cross-region transitivity requires explicit peering.<br>

308. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of an S3 bucket, and what is the difference between the bucket name scope and the data scope? ; S3 bucket names are globally unique (global namespace — no two buckets in all of AWS can share a name). Bucket data is regional — stored in the region selected at creation. The global name uniqueness does not mean data leaves the region. <br><br> *Bucket name globally unique means: you cannot create a bucket named "my-data" if anyone else in AWS already has it. This is purely a naming namespace — not a data replication mechanism.<br> *Data residency: objects in a bucket remain in the chosen region unless explicitly replicated (S3 CRR, S3 Replication Rules) or accessed via CloudFront (which may cache at edge — use Origin Shield to control caching geography).<br> *For GDPR/data sovereignty: choose eu-west-1 (Ireland) or eu-central-1 (Frankfurt) as the bucket region, and data stays in the EU. The global name doesn't move the data — only replication does.<br>

309. [ Resource Scope ] What ARN characteristic distinguishes a global resource from a regional resource? ; Global resources have an empty region field in their ARN. Regional resources include a region code. Example: IAM role ARN = `arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/MyRole` (no region). Lambda ARN = `arn:aws:lambda:us-east-1:123456789012:function:MyFn` (has region). <br><br> *The ARN format is: arn:partition:service:region:account-id:resource. For global services, the region segment is empty (two consecutive colons: `iam::`). This is not a bug — it reflects that the resource has no region.<br> *Exam tip: if you see an ARN with an empty region field, the resource is global. If you see a region code, it's regional. This is useful for quickly identifying service scope in policy condition examples.<br> *Some services like CloudFront use `arn:aws:cloudfront::123456789012:distribution/ABCDE` — no region, confirming CloudFront distributions are global resources.<br>

310. [ Resource Scope ] A company is designing a multi-region active-active application. Which AWS services can natively replicate data across regions, and which require manual replication setup? ; Natively multi-region (active-active): DynamoDB Global Tables, Route 53 (global DNS). Require explicit cross-region setup: S3 (CRR), RDS (cross-region read replica), Aurora Global Database, Secrets Manager (cross-region replication), ElastiCache Global Datastore. <br><br> *DynamoDB Global Tables is the closest to "automatic" — once enabled, writes in any region replicate to all configured regions within ~1 second. No application changes needed for reads; writes use last-writer-wins.<br> *S3 CRR must be configured with a replication rule specifying source/destination buckets — it's not automatic. CRR only replicates NEW objects after the rule is created; existing objects need S3 Batch Replication.<br> *RDS cross-region read replica requires a separate RDS instance in the target region, asynchronous replication, and manual promotion during failover. Aurora Global Database provides faster replication (<1s) and automated failover compared to standard RDS cross-region replicas.<br>

311. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS GuardDuty, and what must an organization do to ensure complete threat detection coverage? ; GuardDuty is regional — it must be enabled in each region separately. In multi-account organizations, use AWS Organizations integration with GuardDuty delegated administrator to automatically enable GuardDuty in all accounts and all regions. <br><br> *GuardDuty disabled in a region = no threat detection in that region. An attacker who creates resources in ap-southeast-2 (not monitored) can operate undetected. Enable GuardDuty in ALL regions, even those not actively used.<br> *AWS Organizations + GuardDuty delegated admin enables "auto-enable" for new accounts and existing accounts — one configuration propagates to all member accounts across all regions automatically.<br> *Similar pattern applies to Security Hub, Config, Macie, Inspector, IAM Access Analyzer — all regional services that should be enabled org-wide using Organizations integration and delegated administrator accounts.<br>

312. [ Resource Scope ] What is Global Accelerator, what is its scope, and how does it differ from CloudFront? ; Global Accelerator is a global networking service that routes user traffic to the nearest AWS edge PoP, then across the AWS backbone to the regional endpoint (ALB, NLB, EC2, Elastic IP). Scope: global (two static anycast IPs per accelerator). CloudFront caches content; Global Accelerator accelerates dynamic/real-time traffic without caching. <br><br> *Global Accelerator provides two static anycast IPv4 addresses that route to the nearest edge PoP globally — traffic enters AWS at the closest PoP and traverses the AWS private backbone to the origin, bypassing internet congestion.<br> *CloudFront caches objects at edge — great for static content, images, video. Global Accelerator does NOT cache — it's for dynamic APIs, gaming, IoT, real-time applications where content cannot be cached.<br> *Both use the AWS edge network but for different purposes: CloudFront = CDN (cache at edge), Global Accelerator = network acceleration (no cache, just route optimization). CloudFront supports HTTP/HTTPS; Global Accelerator supports TCP/UDP.<br>

313. [ Resource Scope ] An application uses EFS for shared storage. The application is deployed in 3 AZs in us-east-1. How does EFS support this multi-AZ architecture? ; EFS is a regional service — one file system serves all AZs. EFS creates a mount target in each AZ (subnet-scoped ENI). Each AZ's EC2 instances mount EFS using the mount target IP in their AZ. Data is replicated across AZs by EFS automatically. <br><br> *EFS mount targets are AZ-specific: us-east-1a has its own mount target IP, us-east-1b has another. Instances always mount from their local AZ's mount target for lowest latency — but all see the same data.<br> *EFS automatically replicates data across multiple AZs within the region, providing 11 nines of durability without any configuration. Unlike EBS (single-AZ), EFS survives AZ failure — instances in surviving AZs continue accessing the file system.<br> *For cross-region EFS access: EFS Replication creates a copy in a second region (read-only). For failover, promote the replica in the target region. Unlike S3 CRR, EFS Replication is one-way by default.<br>

314. [ Resource Scope ] What happens to regional resources in a region during an AWS regional outage? ; Regional resources (EC2, RDS, Lambda, etc.) are unavailable during the outage. Global resources (IAM, Route 53) remain accessible. Proper DR design uses Route 53 health checks to detect the regional failure and shift traffic to a standby region via failover routing policy. <br><br> *Route 53 health checks continuously probe your regional endpoints. When us-east-1 ALB stops responding, Route 53 removes it from DNS and starts serving the eu-west-1 ALB IP — DNS TTL determines how fast clients see the change (use short TTL for faster failover).<br> *IAM remains available during a regional outage because it's global — your applications and operators can still authenticate and authorize. However, regional services they would normally use are down.<br> *Complete regional outages are rare but have occurred (us-east-1 in 2021, us-east-2 in 2023). SAP-C02 tests your ability to design systems that survive them — multi-region active-passive or active-active with Route 53 failover is the expected answer.<br>

315. [ Resource Scope ] How does AWS Config differ from AWS CloudTrail in terms of scope, and what does each capture? ; CloudTrail: regional (with global service events option) — captures API calls (who did what when). Config: regional — captures resource configuration state and changes (what a resource looks like at any point in time). Both should be enabled in all regions. <br><br> *CloudTrail records API activity: `RunInstances`, `CreateBucket`, `AssumeRole` — the audit log of actions. Enable "Include global service events" in CloudTrail to capture IAM, Route 53, CloudFront API calls (which are global).<br> *Config records configuration snapshots and changes: "EC2 instance i-abc had security group sg-123 attached at 14:32 UTC." It answers "what did this resource look like?" not "who changed it?" — both together give the full picture.<br> *For organization-wide coverage: use CloudTrail organizational trail (one trail captures all accounts, all regions) and Config organizational aggregator. Without explicit multi-region/multi-account setup, gaps in coverage exist.<br>

316. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Secrets Manager, and how can secrets be made available across regions? ; Secrets Manager is regional — secrets are created in and scoped to a specific region. Cross-region replication is available (built-in): replicate a secret to other regions. Replica secrets are read-only in target regions; write to the primary. <br><br> *Cross-region replication in Secrets Manager creates a linked copy of the secret in target regions — applications in those regions read from the local replica (lower latency, no cross-region API call needed).<br> *Replicas are managed copies: when you rotate the primary, the rotation is automatically propagated to all replicas. You cannot write directly to a replica — writes go to the primary and replicate.<br> *For KMS encryption of replicated secrets: use a KMS MRK so the same key can decrypt the secret in all replica regions. Using a regional CMK in the primary means replicas need their own KMS key in each region.<br>

317. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Lambda, and what must be done to run Lambda in multiple regions for high availability? ; Lambda is regional — each function deployment is scoped to a region. For multi-region HA: deploy the Lambda code to each target region (via CloudFormation StackSets or CI/CD pipeline), use Route 53 failover or weighted routing to direct triggers to the appropriate region. <br><br> *Lambda does not automatically replicate across regions — the function code, environment variables, IAM role references, VPC config, and event source mappings must all be recreated in each region.<br> *For Lambda@Edge (CloudFront): the function is deployed to us-east-1 and CloudFront replicates it to edge PoPs globally — this is the only Lambda variant with automatic global distribution.<br> *Infrastructure-as-code is critical for multi-region Lambda deployments: CloudFormation StackSets deploy the same template to multiple accounts/regions simultaneously, maintaining consistency and enabling quick region adds or failover rebuilds.<br>

318. [ Resource Scope ] A company needs to enforce that no S3 bucket can be created outside of eu-west-1. What is the correct approach? ; Apply an SCP to the relevant OUs with a Deny on `s3:CreateBucket` where `aws:RequestedRegion` is not `eu-west-1`. This prevents bucket creation in any other region. Note: this only prevents new bucket creation — existing buckets in other regions are not deleted. <br><br> *SCP with `Deny` and `StringNotEquals` on `aws:RequestedRegion` is the standard pattern. It applies to ALL IAM principals (including root users of member accounts) in the targeted OUs — SCPs override IAM permissions when they deny.<br> *Important limitation: SCPs do not affect existing resources — they only prevent future API calls. Existing S3 buckets in us-east-1 created before the SCP remain. Use Config rules to detect and flag existing non-compliant resources.<br> *The SCP should also deny `s3:PutBucketPolicy`, `s3:PutObject`, and other S3 write operations restricted by region — just denying CreateBucket prevents new buckets but not writes to existing non-compliant buckets.<br>

319. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of VPC Endpoints (both Gateway and Interface types), and can they be shared across regions? ; VPC Endpoints are regional — they exist within a VPC (regional) and cannot be used from another region. Gateway Endpoints (S3, DynamoDB): added to route tables in the VPC. Interface Endpoints: ENIs in specific subnets (AZ-scoped deployment, regional service). <br><br> *Interface Endpoints deploy ENIs in chosen subnets (one per AZ for HA) — the ENI is AZ-scoped, but the endpoint itself is a regional resource. Traffic to the endpoint stays within the VPC and AWS network — no internet traversal.<br> *Sharing across accounts is possible via AWS PrivateLink endpoint services — one account creates an endpoint service backed by NLB, another account creates an Interface Endpoint to consume it. Both must be in the same region.<br> *Cross-region VPC endpoint sharing is NOT natively supported — an Interface Endpoint in us-east-1 cannot be accessed from eu-west-1. For cross-region private access, use TGW peering + route to the endpoint, or deploy separate endpoints in each region.<br>

320. [ Resource Scope ] What is Route 53, what is its scope, and what types of routing policies does it support? ; Route 53 is a global DNS service — hosted zones and records exist globally, served from 400+ edge PoPs via anycast. Routing policies: Simple, Weighted, Latency-based, Failover, Geolocation, Geoproximity, Multivalue Answer, IP-based. <br><br> *Route 53 is global, so there is no "region" for a hosted zone — it automatically serves from the nearest edge PoP to the resolver worldwide. No per-region deployment needed.<br> *Health checks enable failover: Route 53 monitors endpoints and removes unhealthy records from DNS responses. Failover policy: primary record served normally; if unhealthy, Route 53 serves the secondary (DR) record automatically.<br> *Latency-based routing directs users to the region with lowest measured latency — NOT necessarily the geographically closest region. Route 53 measures actual latency from the user's resolver to each configured region and picks the best.<br>

321. [ Resource Scope ] What are the data transfer cost implications of a single NAT Gateway serving multiple AZs vs one NAT Gateway per AZ? ; Single NAT GW in AZ-a: instances in AZ-b and AZ-c pay cross-AZ data transfer (~$0.01/GB each way) for all outbound internet traffic. One NAT GW per AZ: instances only pay NAT GW data processing fees ($0.045/GB) with no cross-AZ charges. At high traffic volumes, multiple NAT GWs are cheaper. <br><br> *Cross-AZ cost is $0.01/GB each way = $0.02/GB round trip. NAT GW data processing is $0.045/GB. For every GB of internet traffic from AZ-b through AZ-a's NAT GW: $0.045 (NAT processing) + $0.01 (cross-AZ to NAT) + $0.01 (cross-AZ return) = $0.065/GB vs $0.045/GB with local NAT GW. That's 44% more expensive per GB.<br> *For small deployments (< few hundred GB/month), the savings may not justify 3x NAT GW hourly cost ($0.045/hr each = $99/month for 3 vs $33/month for 1). Calculate break-even based on your traffic volume.<br> *Best practice for production: always one NAT GW per AZ for both cost and HA reasons. For dev/test environments with minimal traffic, a single NAT GW is acceptable to save costs.<br>

322. [ Resource Scope ] Can a Security Group in us-east-1 be referenced in a security group rule in eu-west-1? ; No. Security Groups are scoped to a VPC (regional). A security group ID can only be referenced in rules within the same VPC (or VPCs in the same region with TGW Security Group referencing enabled). Cross-region SG referencing is not supported. <br><br> *Security Group referencing across VPCs (same region) requires Transit Gateway with the "Security Groups referencing" feature enabled — this allows SG-A in VPC-1 to reference SG-B in VPC-2 in the same region.<br> *For cross-region security: use IP CIDR ranges in security group rules (e.g., allow 10.1.0.0/16 from eu-west-1 VPC) rather than SG ID references, since cross-region SG referencing is not supported.<br> *This limitation means complex microservice SG-mesh architectures (where services reference each other's SGs) must operate within a single region — expanding to multi-region requires redesigning the security model to use CIDR-based rules or PrivateLink.<br>

323. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS CloudFront, and how does Origin Shield work to improve data residency control? ; CloudFront distributions are global (resource is global, content served from 400+ edge PoPs worldwide). Origin Shield is an optional caching layer in a specific AWS region that all edge PoPs pull from before going to origin — reducing origin load and limiting the geographic regions that access origin data. <br><br> *Without Origin Shield: any edge PoP might fetch from origin independently, potentially causing many origin requests and distributing cached copies globally. With Origin Shield in eu-west-1: all European and potentially global PoPs cache-miss to Origin Shield (eu-west-1), which fetches from origin — data stays in eu-west-1 until served to the PoP.<br> *For GDPR: Origin Shield helps but doesn't fully prevent data from reaching edge PoPs (which may be outside EU). For strict data sovereignty, use CloudFront geo-restriction to deny requests from specific countries/regions, keeping data from being served to non-compliant locations.<br> *CloudFront Functions and Lambda@Edge execute at edge PoPs — if they log or process personal data, that processing happens outside the origin region. Consider this for compliance and data residency assessments.<br>

324. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store, and how do you make parameters available across regions? ; Parameter Store is regional — parameters are scoped to the region where they're created. For cross-region availability: replicate parameters using SSM automation, EventBridge rules + Lambda cross-region writes, or use AWS AppConfig (also regional) with cross-region deployment strategies. <br><br> *Unlike Secrets Manager (which has built-in cross-region replication), Parameter Store has no native replication feature — you must build or use third-party solutions to keep parameters in sync across regions.<br> *For multi-region deployments using IaC: have CloudFormation or Terraform create the same parameters in each region as part of the deployment pipeline — treat parameters as code-deployed config, not manually managed.<br> *AWS Secrets Manager is the recommended alternative when cross-region replication is needed — it has native built-in replication, automatic rotation propagation, and is only slightly more expensive than Parameter Store advanced tier.<br>

325. [ Resource Scope ] What is the difference in scope between EFS and FSx, and when would you choose each? ; EFS: regional, NFS protocol, Linux only, serverless auto-scaling storage. FSx: several variants — FSx for Windows (AZ or Multi-AZ), FSx for Lustre (AZ, high performance), FSx for NetApp ONTAP (Multi-AZ), FSx for OpenZFS (AZ). Each FSx type has its own scope. <br><br> *EFS spans all AZs in a region with automatic replication — mount targets in each AZ provide local access. Single shared namespace, pay-per-use, auto-scales. Best for Linux workloads needing shared NFS storage.<br> *FSx for Windows (Multi-AZ) deploys in two AZs with synchronous replication and automatic failover — SMB protocol, Active Directory integration, Windows features. Required when Windows workloads need shared file storage.<br> *FSx for Lustre is AZ-scoped (no multi-AZ option) — it's optimized for HPC/ML workloads needing sub-millisecond latency. Integration with S3 (import/export) allows using S3 as the permanent backing store.<br>

326. [ Resource Scope ] How does AWS Config aggregator work across regions and accounts? ; Config aggregator collects configuration and compliance data from multiple source regions and accounts into a central aggregator account. The aggregator is a regional resource in the aggregator account — but it pulls data from all configured source regions/accounts. <br><br> *Source accounts must authorize the aggregator account to collect their Config data — this is done via Config in each source account or automatically through AWS Organizations (Organizations integration auto-authorizes all member accounts).<br> *Config rules themselves are regional — you must deploy Config rules in each region where you want compliance evaluation. The aggregator shows you results from all regions/accounts in one place but doesn't evaluate rules centrally.<br> *For complete coverage: use CloudFormation StackSets to deploy a consistent set of Config rules across all accounts and regions, then use an aggregator to view consolidated compliance status from a central security account.<br>

327. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS WAF, and how does it differ between WAF Classic and WAF v2? ; WAF Classic: regional for ALB, API Gateway; global for CloudFront (must use WAF Classic, not v2, for original CloudFront). WAF v2 (current): regional for ALB, API Gateway, AppSync, Cognito; global for CloudFront (created in us-east-1). <br><br> *WAF v2 for CloudFront must be created in us-east-1 — this is because CloudFront is a global service and reads WAF web ACLs from us-east-1, similar to ACM certificates. Creating a WAF v2 web ACL in eu-west-1 cannot be attached to CloudFront.<br> *WAF v2 for regional resources (ALB, API GW): create the web ACL in the same region as the resource. A WAF ACL in us-east-1 cannot be attached to an ALB in eu-west-1.<br> *Managed rule groups (AWS managed or third-party) are available in both the regional and us-east-1 scopes — you don't need to configure rules differently, just deploy the web ACL in the correct scope for your resource.<br>

328. [ Resource Scope ] An Aurora database is deployed as Aurora Global Database. What is the scope of each component? ; Aurora Global Database primary cluster: regional (one AWS region). Read-only secondary clusters: regional (different regions). The "global database" itself is a meta-resource spanning regions. Replication: <1 second from primary to secondaries. Failover: promote a secondary to primary in <1 minute (Managed Planned Failover) or <3 min (Unmanaged). <br><br> *Aurora Global Database is not truly global — it's a coordinated set of regional clusters with automated cross-region replication. The primary accepts writes; secondaries are read-only replicas in other regions.<br> *Unlike DynamoDB Global Tables (active-active writes in all regions), Aurora Global Database is active-passive: writes go to one primary region, reads can be served from any region. Promoting a secondary to primary is a manual operation (though can be automated).<br> *For RTO/RPO requirements: Aurora Global Database offers RPO <1 second and RTO <1 minute for planned failover. Compare with RDS cross-region read replica: similar RPO but longer RTO due to manual promotion steps.<br>

329. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Transit Gateway Network Manager, and what does it provide? ; TGW Network Manager is a global service that provides a centralized view of your entire global network — including TGWs across regions, VPN connections, DX connections, SD-WAN devices, and on-premises networks. Scope: global console, but underlying TGWs are regional. <br><br> *Network Manager creates a global network object and you register your regional TGWs, VPN connections, and DX connections into it — giving a single topology map across all regions and accounts.<br> *Route Analyzer in Network Manager can analyze routing paths through TGW networks without sending actual traffic — identifies missing routes, misconfigured route tables, attachment issues across the global topology.<br> *CloudWatch metrics for the global network are aggregated in Network Manager — you can see which VPN or DX connections are up/down globally from one console, rather than checking each region's CloudWatch independently.<br>

330. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of AWS Resource Access Manager (RAM), and what resources can be shared? ; RAM is regional — resource shares are created per region. Shared resources must be in the same region as the RAM share. Resources shareable via RAM (selected examples): VPC subnets, TGW, Route 53 Resolver rules, Aurora clusters, CodeBuild projects, EC2 Dedicated Hosts, AWS Network Firewall policies. <br><br> *RAM shares resources with specific AWS accounts or entire AWS Organizations OUs — the recipient uses the shared resource in their own account without owning or managing it. Useful for centralized VPC architecture where a network account owns the TGW and shares it with spoke accounts.<br> *TGW sharing via RAM: the network account creates a TGW and shares it; spoke accounts attach their VPCs to the shared TGW. This is the recommended pattern for AWS Organizations multi-account networking — all TGW management in one account.<br> *RAM shares are regional — you cannot share a TGW in us-east-1 with accounts that need it in eu-west-1. Each region's TGW must be separately shared. This is consistent with TGW being a regional resource.<br>

331. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of an Elastic IP (EIP), and what happens to it when associated EC2 is stopped or terminated? ; EIP is regional — scoped to an AWS region (tied to a specific AZ when associated with a resource). When EC2 is stopped: EIP remains associated (no charge if associated, even when stopped). When EC2 is terminated: EIP is disassociated but retained in your account (you pay $0.005/hr for unassociated EIP). <br><br> *EIP is an AZ-independent regional resource — it can be reassociated to any EC2/ENI in any AZ within the same region. This is different from EBS (AZ-locked) — EIP can "move" between AZs by disassociating and reassociating.<br> *For HA patterns: EIP + ENI failover — if primary EC2 fails, the standby EC2 detaches the EIP from the primary (or it's already detached due to termination) and associates it to itself. DNS record pointing to the EIP continues to resolve correctly.<br> *Charges: No charge for EIP associated with running instance. $0.005/hr for unassociated EIP or EIP associated with stopped instance. Always release EIPs you're not using — they are a limited regional resource (default quota: 5 per region).<br>

332. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of Amazon SNS and SQS, and how do you implement cross-region messaging? ; SNS topics and SQS queues are regional. Cross-region messaging options: SNS → subscribe an SQS queue in another region (SNS can deliver cross-region); EventBridge event buses support cross-region rules; Kinesis Data Streams can replicate cross-region via Kinesis Data Firehose. <br><br> *SNS cross-region: an SNS topic in us-east-1 can have an SQS queue in eu-west-1 as a subscriber — SNS sends the message cross-region to the SQS endpoint. This enables fan-out patterns that span regions.<br> *SQS itself cannot subscribe across regions natively — messages in an SQS queue in us-east-1 don't automatically appear in eu-west-1. SNS fan-out is the bridge for cross-region SQS delivery.<br> *EventBridge event buses: you can configure EventBridge in us-east-1 to forward matching events to an event bus in eu-west-1 using a cross-region event rule — this is the recommended modern pattern for cross-region event routing and fan-out.<br>

333. [ Resource Scope ] What is the difference in scope between VPC peering and Transit Gateway for connecting multiple VPCs? ; VPC Peering: regional (or inter-region with different cost/latency) — each peering is a direct 1:1 connection, not transitive. TGW: regional hub-and-spoke — transitive routing between all attached VPCs. TGW peering extends to other regions. VPC peering: N*(N-1)/2 connections for N VPCs. TGW: N connections for N VPCs. <br><br> *VPC peering inter-region is supported but uses public IPv4/IPv6 addresses over the AWS backbone — traffic is encrypted and stays on AWS network. Inter-region TGW peering is also over AWS backbone, same security characteristics.<br> *VPC peering has no bandwidth limit and no hourly cost (pay data transfer only). TGW has per-attachment hourly cost but provides transitive routing and operational simplicity for large numbers of VPCs. Break-even is roughly 3-4 VPCs.<br> *Critical architectural difference: VPC peering cannot overlap CIDRs between any two peered VPCs (even indirectly). TGW also cannot route overlapping CIDRs — but the constraint is the same. Plan CIDR space carefully regardless of connectivity method.<br>

334. [ Resource Scope ] What is the scope of Amazon ElastiCache, and what HA options are available? ; ElastiCache clusters are regional — you choose the region at creation. Individual cache nodes are AZ-scoped. HA options: Multi-AZ with automatic failover (Redis) — primary in one AZ, replica in another; automatic promotion on primary failure. Memcached: multi-node across AZs but no replication/failover. <br><br> *ElastiCache Redis (now Amazon ElastiCache for Redis or Valkey): supports Multi-AZ with automatic failover — primary node in AZ-a, read replicas in AZ-b and AZ-c. On primary failure, Redis promotes a replica and updates the configuration endpoint automatically.<br> *The configuration endpoint (for cluster mode) or primary endpoint (for non-cluster) is what applications use — it automatically points to the current primary after failover. No DNS change needed in application code.<br> *Cross-region ElastiCache: AWS does not provide native cross-region replication for ElastiCache. For cross-region caching: deploy separate ElastiCache clusters in each region, use DynamoDB Global Tables (with DAX accelerator) instead of ElastiCache if cross-region consistency is needed.<br>

335. [ Resource Scope ] How does AWS Backup handle resource scope for cross-region and cross-account backup? ; AWS Backup is regional — backup vaults are created per region. Cross-region copy: configure backup plan with a copy action to copy backups to a vault in another region. Cross-account backup: configure backup plan to copy to a vault in a different account. Both require IAM permissions and KMS key in target region/account. <br><br> *Cross-region backup copy is a key DR pattern: EBS snapshots, RDS snapshots, DynamoDB backups, EFS backups, FSx backups can all be copied cross-region via AWS Backup — providing an off-site copy without manual snapshot management.<br> *Cross-account backup isolation: storing backups in a separate account (backup account) means ransomware or accidental deletion in the production account cannot affect the backups — IAM permissions are separate, and the backup account has its own controls.<br> *AWS Organizations + AWS Backup: use Backup Policies (similar to SCPs) to enforce backup plans across all accounts in the org — ensuring every account has compliant backup schedules without manual per-account configuration.<br>


336. [ Aurora ] What is the maximum number of Aurora Replicas you can have in a single Aurora cluster, and how does this compare to RDS? ; Aurora supports up to 15 Aurora Replicas per cluster. RDS MySQL/PG/MariaDB supports up to 5 read replicas. <br><br> *Aurora Replicas share the same distributed storage volume as the writer — they don't store their own copy of data, only the compute layer differs. This allows 15 replicas without 15× the storage cost.<br> *RDS read replicas use async log-shipping — each replica stores a full independent copy of the data, which is why fewer are supported (storage and replication overhead).<br> *The exam frequently asks this comparison: 15 (Aurora) vs 5 (RDS). When you see a question about needing more than 5 read replicas, the answer is Aurora.<br>

337. [ Aurora ] What happens to Aurora storage when you delete rows from a table? Does storage shrink? ; Aurora storage does NOT automatically shrink when data is deleted. Storage only grows — it uses a high-water-mark model. Once Aurora has allocated storage for data, that allocated space is retained even after deletes. <br><br> *Aurora storage is billed at the high-water-mark — if you had 100 GB of data, deleted half, you still pay for 100 GB of allocated storage until the cluster is replaced or a restore is done.<br> *This is different from RDS where you can reduce EBS volume size (with some effort). Aurora's storage is managed by AWS and does not shrink.<br> *To reclaim storage: export data → drop table → restore from snapshot (new cluster starts at actual data size). Alternatively, accept the cost as Aurora storage is relatively inexpensive ($0.10/GB-month).<br>

338. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora Backtrack, and what are its limitations? ; Aurora Backtrack rewinds an Aurora MySQL cluster to a specific point in time (up to 72 hours) IN PLACE — no new cluster needed. The cluster pauses briefly (~1-5 min) during backtrack. Limitations: MySQL only, not PostgreSQL; max 72-hour window; brief outage; does not create a separate cluster. <br><br> *Backtrack is ideal for "oops" scenarios — accidental DELETE, schema change gone wrong, bad data load. Instead of 20-40 min restore to a new cluster, you rewind the existing cluster in minutes.<br> *Backtrack is NOT a replacement for PITR — PITR creates a new cluster for parallel investigation; Backtrack overwrites current state. Use both: Backtrack for quick recovery, PITR for forensics.<br> *The backtrack window has a cost — you pay for the undo log storage. Longer window = higher cost. Assess the tradeoff: 72-hour window vs cost.<br>

339. [ Aurora ] How does Aurora Global Database replication work at the storage level, and why is it faster than log-based replication? ; Aurora Global Database uses storage-level replication — it replicates the storage I/O operations (not database logs) directly from the primary region's storage layer to secondary regions. This bypasses the database engine entirely, achieving <1 second typical replication lag. <br><br> *Traditional cross-region replication (RDS cross-region read replicas) ships binary logs or WAL records through the DB engine — the log must be generated, transmitted, and applied to the secondary DB. This adds latency at every step.<br> *Aurora Global Database ships raw storage operations — the source storage nodes directly replicate to destination storage nodes over dedicated AWS network paths, bypassing the DB compute layer. Faster and more efficient.<br> *The consequence: Global Database failover is fast (~1 min) because the secondary already has all storage data up to <1 sec lag. Promoting it only requires starting the compute layer and updating DNS.<br>

340. [ Aurora ] A company requires RPO of 1 second and RTO of 1 minute for a database failure. Which Aurora configuration achieves this? ; Aurora Global Database with Managed Planned Failover. RPO: storage-level replication at <1 sec lag. RTO: ~1 minute for promoted secondary to become writable. Standard Aurora Multi-AZ (same-region replica) gives ~0 RPO and ~30 sec RTO but doesn't cover regional failures. <br><br> *For RPO=1s + RTO=1min and regional DR scope: Aurora Global Database is the only AWS-native option. The secondary region's cluster is already populated; failover promotes it and updates DNS.<br> *Managed Planned Failover (MPF) synchronizes the secondary with the primary before initiating failover — achieving near-zero RPO even for unplanned failover scenarios. Unmanaged failover accepts the <1 sec replication lag as RPO.<br> *Compare with RDS cross-region read replica: RPO is seconds (async replication lag), RTO is 5-10+ minutes (manual promotion + DNS change + app reconnect). Much worse for critical workloads.<br>

341. [ Aurora ] What is the difference between Aurora Serverless v1 and v2, and which should you use for new workloads? ; Serverless v1: scales by doubling ACUs (coarse steps), cold-start gap when scaling from zero, limited engine versions (MySQL 5.6/5.7, PG 10/11), one scaling cooldown period at a time. v2: scales in 0.5 ACU increments near-instantly, no cold start, supports MySQL 8.0 and PG 13-16, writer AND readers scale independently. Use v2 for all new workloads. <br><br> *Serverless v2 is the current recommendation from AWS — it eliminates the v1 cold-start problem (v1 could pause for seconds when scaling from zero ACUs). v2 maintains a minimum ACU floor (0.5 ACU) to avoid cold starts.<br> *v2 supports provisioned replicas alongside Serverless v2 instances in the same cluster — you can have a Serverless v2 writer with a provisioned reader for predictable read workloads.<br> *v1 is being deprecated — AWS has announced end-of-support timeline. Migrate v1 workloads to v2. The v1 → v2 migration path: create v2 cluster, use DMS or mysqldump to migrate data.<br>

342. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora Database Cloning, and how does it work internally? ; Aurora cloning creates a copy of a cluster using copy-on-write semantics — the clone initially shares all storage pages with the source. Only when either the source or clone modifies a page is a separate copy made. This means clone creation is near-instant and costs nothing initially. <br><br> *Copy-on-write means: both source and clone point to the same 6-copy distributed storage initially. When source modifies page A, the clone still sees the original page A. When clone modifies page B, source still sees original page B. Only diverged pages are stored separately.<br> *Use cases: create dev/staging environments from production data instantly; test schema migrations before running on production; run analytics queries without impacting production.<br> *Cloning is within the same region and account. For cross-account cloning: share the snapshot cross-account and restore. For cross-region: copy snapshot to target region and restore.<br>

343. [ Aurora ] What is an Aurora Custom Endpoint, and when would you use it? ; A Custom Endpoint is a user-defined endpoint that routes connections to a specific subset of Aurora Replicas in a cluster. Unlike the Reader Endpoint (which load-balances across ALL readers), a Custom Endpoint targets only the instances you assign to it. <br><br> *Use case: tiered reader pools. Example: 3 large (r6g.4xlarge) readers handle heavy analytics queries via a custom endpoint; 2 small (r6g.xlarge) readers handle OLTP reads via the Reader Endpoint. This prevents analytics queries from stealing IOPS from OLTP readers.<br> *Another use case: testing new instance types — add 1 upgraded reader, assign it to a custom endpoint, route test traffic there, validate before applying to all readers.<br> *Custom Endpoints do not automatically exclude the writer — if you assign the writer to a custom endpoint, writes from that endpoint execute normally. Be careful about read-only vs read-write custom endpoints.<br>

344. [ Aurora ] How does IAM Database Authentication work in Aurora, and what are the benefits and limitations? ; IAM DB Authentication replaces traditional database passwords with short-lived IAM-generated tokens (valid 15 minutes). The application generates an auth token using AWS credentials (via `generate-db-auth-token` API call), then connects with that token as the password. Benefits: no passwords to manage, centralized IAM control. <br><br> *The authentication token is cryptographically signed using the IAM credentials — Aurora verifies it by calling IAM internally. No password stored in the application or in Secrets Manager (though Secrets Manager is still recommended for token generation credentials).<br> *Limitations: slight additional latency for token generation (~100ms); the IAM user/role must have `rds-db:connect` IAM permission; the DB user must be created with `IDENTIFIED WITH AWSAuthenticationPlugin`; only supported for MySQL and PostgreSQL engines.<br> *Combine with Secrets Manager for the IAM credentials used to generate tokens — even though tokens are short-lived, the underlying IAM credentials that generate them should still be rotated and managed securely.<br>

345. [ Aurora ] A company is migrating from Oracle to Aurora PostgreSQL. What tools are required, and what are the key migration steps? ; Tools: AWS Schema Conversion Tool (SCT) to convert Oracle schema/SQL/stored procedures to PostgreSQL; AWS DMS to migrate data with CDC for minimal downtime. Steps: 1) SCT assessment report, 2) convert schema with SCT, 3) manually fix SCT-flagged items, 4) deploy schema to Aurora PG, 5) DMS full load, 6) DMS CDC, 7) validate, 8) cutover. <br><br> *SCT handles DDL conversion (tables, views, indexes → PG equivalents) and most PL/SQL → PL/pgSQL conversion. The assessment report shows a conversion rate — typically 60-80% auto-converted; remainder needs manual effort.<br> *Oracle-specific features requiring manual work: Oracle sequences → PG sequences (different syntax), ROWNUM → LIMIT, Oracle DATE (timestamp) → PG TIMESTAMP, Oracle packages → PG schemas + functions.<br> *DMS for the data move: create source endpoint (Oracle), target endpoint (Aurora PG), replication instance (sized for data volume), task (Full Load + CDC). CDC uses Oracle LogMiner — ensure supplemental logging enabled on source.<br>

346. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora Activity Streams, and when is it required? ; Aurora Activity Streams provides a near-real-time stream of database activity — SQL statements, authentication events, session changes — in a format suitable for compliance auditing. Required for: PCI DSS compliance, HIPAA, SOX, or any regulation requiring a tamper-resistant database activity log. <br><br> *Activity Streams writes to a Kinesis Data Stream — you can consume with Lambda, Kinesis Firehose → S3, or third-party tools (Splunk, IBM Guardium, etc.). The stream is encrypted with a KMS key separate from the DB encryption key.<br> *Unlike CloudTrail (which captures API calls to AWS), Activity Streams captures what happens INSIDE the database — SQL queries, results, user sessions. This is the audit layer regulators require.<br> *Activity Streams has two modes: Synchronous (writes activity log before returning result to client — no gaps, slight latency increase) and Asynchronous (writes activity log after returning result — lower latency, possible small gap under crash scenario). For strict compliance: Synchronous.<br>

347. [ Aurora ] What is the RDS Proxy, and why is it especially important for Lambda functions connecting to Aurora? ; RDS Proxy is a managed database proxy that sits between the application and Aurora/RDS. It maintains a persistent connection pool to the database. Lambda functions spin up thousands of concurrent instances, each wanting its own database connection — this exhausts Aurora's connection limit quickly. RDS Proxy absorbs those connections into a pool. <br><br> *Lambda cold starts create new TCP connections to Aurora on every invocation — with 1000 concurrent Lambdas, that's 1000 database connections which Aurora can't handle (Aurora max connections scales with instance size, typically hundreds to low thousands).<br> *RDS Proxy maintains ~10-20 persistent connections to Aurora and multiplexes thousands of Lambda connections through them. The proxy handles connection pooling, idle connection management, and reconnection after failover.<br> *RDS Proxy also improves failover: applications connecting via proxy see minimal disruption during failover (proxy handles reconnection to the new primary transparently; typical extra failover time reduced by ~66%).<br>

348. [ Aurora ] What is the difference between Aurora MySQL and Aurora MySQL with the parallel query feature? ; Parallel Query pushes query processing down to the Aurora storage layer — multiple storage nodes process parts of a query scan in parallel, dramatically reducing data transferred to the compute layer. Supported for Aurora MySQL 8.0 on specific instance types. <br><br> *Without Parallel Query: a full-table scan reads all data from storage to the compute instance, then filters. With Parallel Query: storage nodes filter and pre-aggregate data before sending to compute — less network I/O, less compute load.<br> *Parallel Query is most beneficial for: analytics queries on large tables (full scans, aggregations, range scans). It does NOT benefit: OLTP point queries (already fast), queries hitting indexes (already optimized).<br> *Enabling Parallel Query: use `aurora_parallel_query` parameter and `/*+ PARALLEL_QUERY */` hint, or set `aurora_disable_hash_join=0`. Requires specific instance types (r4, r5) and Aurora MySQL 3.x.<br>

349. [ Aurora ] What encryption options exist for an Aurora snapshot being shared with another AWS account? ; To share an encrypted Aurora snapshot cross-account: 1) the snapshot must be encrypted with a Customer Managed Key (CMK) — NOT the AWS-managed `aws/rds` key; 2) share the CMK with the target account (key policy); 3) share the snapshot with the target account; 4) target account copies the snapshot (re-encrypting with their own CMK if desired), then restores. <br><br> *The AWS-managed key (`aws/rds`) cannot be shared with another account — it's account-specific. Only CMKs can have cross-account key policies. This is a critical exam gotcha: always use CMK for snapshots that need to be shared.<br> *In the target account: the receiving account can use the shared CMK OR re-encrypt the snapshot copy with their own CMK. Re-encrypting is recommended for ownership clarity and to avoid dependency on the source account's key.<br> *This same pattern applies to RDS snapshots, not just Aurora — any encrypted RDS/Aurora snapshot shared cross-account requires a CMK, not the AWS-managed key.<br>

350. [ Aurora ] What happens during an Aurora Multi-AZ failover, and what is the expected impact on application connections? ; When an Aurora primary fails: 1) Aurora detects failure (typically within 30 sec); 2) promotes the highest-priority replica (by failover priority tier); 3) updates DNS CNAME of the cluster endpoint to point to the new primary; 4) applications reconnect to the same cluster endpoint DNS name. Impact: ~30 seconds of connection interruption; some in-flight transactions lost. <br><br> *Applications MUST use the cluster endpoint DNS name, not the IP address — the DNS CNAME changes during failover. Hardcoding an IP address means the application never discovers the new primary.<br> *RDS Proxy further reduces failover impact: Proxy maintains the connection pool and reconnects to the new primary faster than applications can detect the change — reduces effective application failover time from ~30 sec to ~10-15 sec.<br> *Failover priority: you can set tier priority (0-15) on each replica. Tier 0 is highest priority — if multiple replicas exist, Aurora promotes the tier-0 replica first. Same tier = Aurora picks the one with least replication lag.<br>

351. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Storage I/O-Optimized configuration, and when should you use it? ; Aurora I/O-Optimized is a cluster configuration that includes storage I/O cost in the instance price — instead of paying separately per I/O operation, you pay a higher instance price with no I/O charges. Beneficial when I/O costs exceed 25% of total Aurora bill. <br><br> *Standard Aurora pricing: instance + storage ($0.10/GB-month) + I/O ($0.20 per 1M requests). For I/O-intensive workloads (heavy writes, frequent reads), I/O can dominate cost.<br> *I/O-Optimized pricing: higher instance cost (~30% premium) + storage ($0.225/GB-month), but $0 per I/O. Break-even when I/O bill > ~25-30% of total Aurora cost — above that, I/O-Optimized is cheaper.<br> *Use case: OLTP with many small reads/writes (e-commerce transactions), MySQL with binary log enabled (high I/O), frequent small writes (IoT, logging). Calculate based on actual I/O metrics in CloudWatch before switching.<br>

352. [ Aurora ] What is the aurora_replica_read_consistency parameter in Aurora PostgreSQL, and how does it affect read replica behavior? ; `aurora_replica_read_consistency` controls whether Aurora PostgreSQL replicas honor the session's read consistency level. Options: EVENTUAL (default — might return stale data), SESSION (reads on the replica reflect all writes from the same session — waits for replica to catch up if needed), GLOBAL (replica waits to be consistent with all writes before responding). <br><br> *EVENTUAL consistency is fastest (reads from replica immediately, might be milliseconds stale). SESSION consistency ensures your own writes are visible on reads in the same session. GLOBAL consistency ensures all writes globally are reflected (slowest, highest latency).<br> *For most read-replica use cases (reporting, dashboards), EVENTUAL is acceptable and provides best performance. For cases where a user writes then immediately reads from a replica expecting to see their write: SESSION.<br> *This is more nuanced than MySQL's simpler eventual consistency model — PostgreSQL Aurora offers finer control matching ANSI isolation levels. Exam typically tests EVENTUAL vs SESSION distinction.<br>

353. [ Aurora ] A developer accidentally dropped a production Aurora table. What is the fastest recovery method? ; If Aurora MySQL with Backtrack enabled: use Backtrack to rewind the cluster to just before the DROP TABLE — typically completes in 1-5 minutes. If Backtrack not enabled or Aurora PostgreSQL: restore to a new cluster via PITR, then export the table from the restored cluster and import into production. <br><br> *Backtrack (MySQL only) is the fastest option — no new cluster creation, just a cluster pause and rewind. Must have been enabled before the incident.<br> *PITR path (no Backtrack): restore to new cluster (20-40 min depending on DB size) → connect to new cluster → `mysqldump` or `pg_dump` the affected table → import into production. Total time: ~30-60 min.<br> *Database cloning can speed this up: instead of restoring from scratch, clone the production cluster at the pre-drop time point (using a snapshot from that time). Clone creation is near-instant; then extract the table data.<br>

354. [ Aurora ] What are Aurora Serverless v2 Aurora Capacity Units (ACUs), and how do min/max ACU settings affect cost and performance? ; ACUs are units of Aurora compute capacity (1 ACU ≈ 2 GB RAM + proportional CPU/network). Min ACU: floor scaling limit (0.5 ACU minimum to avoid cold start). Max ACU: ceiling to prevent runaway scaling cost. Cost scales proportionally with ACU-hours consumed. <br><br> *Setting Min ACU = 0.5: Aurora keeps the cluster warm (no cold start) but at minimal cost (~$0.06/ACU-hour). Setting Min = 0 in Serverless v1 caused cold starts — v2 minimum is 0.5 ACU.<br> *Setting Max ACU too low caps performance — if your workload needs 32 ACUs at peak but max is set to 8, queries queue or fail. Monitor `ServerlessDatabaseCapacity` CloudWatch metric to see if you're hitting the max.<br> *For production: set Max ACU to the expected peak × 1.5 buffer. For dev/test: set Max ACU to 2-4 ACU to control costs. Min ACU should be >0 for production (avoid cold start).<br>

355.
