[ Aurora ] What is Babelfish for Aurora PostgreSQL, and what migration use case does it address? ; Babelfish for Aurora PostgreSQL allows Aurora PG to understand T-SQL (Microsoft SQL Server's dialect) on port 1433 and TDS wire protocol — SQL Server applications can connect to Aurora PG using SQL Server drivers without code changes. Addresses migration from SQL Server to Aurora PG with minimal application refactoring. <br><br> *Babelfish adds a T-SQL endpoint to Aurora PG alongside the standard PG endpoint (port 5432 still works). Applications using SQL Server JDBC/ODBC drivers can connect to port 1433 and issue T-SQL queries.<br> *Not 100% compatible — complex T-SQL features, vendor-specific functions, SQL Server Agent jobs, and some DDL are not supported. Run the Babelfish Compass tool (offline analysis) to assess compatibility before committing to this path.<br> *For simple SQL Server applications (CRUD, basic stored procs): Babelfish often achieves 80-95% compatibility with no code changes. For complex applications with heavy T-SQL: may require more refactoring than a full SCT migration would.<br>

356. [ Aurora ] How does Aurora handle the case where 2 of 6 storage copies are unavailable? ; Aurora can still service reads and writes with only 4/6 copies available. Write quorum requires 4/6 (acknowledged after 4 confirm). Read quorum requires 3/6. So losing 2 copies: writes still work (4 ≥ quorum of 4), reads still work (4 ≥ quorum of 3). Aurora operates normally. <br><br> *This is the key HA advantage of Aurora's 6-copy quorum model — you can lose an entire AZ (2 copies) and Aurora continues to read AND write normally. RDS Multi-AZ loses its standby in an AZ failure but the primary continues.<br> *Losing 3 copies: read quorum (3/6) is met marginally; write quorum (4/6) is NOT met — Aurora enters read-only mode. AWS is typically alerted and replaces the failed storage nodes before 3 copies are lost.<br> *This is why Aurora's SLA and durability guarantees are higher than RDS — the distributed storage model tolerates multiple simultaneous failures that would cause RDS to failover or become unavailable.<br>

357. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's cluster parameter group vs instance parameter group, and when does each apply? ; Cluster parameter group: applies to the entire Aurora cluster (all instances); controls storage-level and engine-wide settings (e.g., binlog format, character set, backtrack window). Instance parameter group: applies to a specific DB instance; controls instance-level settings (e.g., max_connections, sort_buffer_size, query cache). <br><br> *Aurora has TWO levels of parameter groups, unlike RDS (which only has instance parameter groups). Aurora inherits cluster-level params from the cluster group; instance-level params from instance group.<br> *Most Aurora-specific parameters (binlog_format, aurora_parallel_query, backtrack) are cluster parameters. Performance-tuning parameters (innodb_buffer_pool_size, work_mem in PG) are instance parameters.<br> *Dynamic parameters apply without reboot; static parameters require reboot. A common exam scenario: you changed a parameter but the change didn't apply — check if it's static (requires reboot) and whether you modified cluster vs instance parameter group.<br>

358. [ Aurora ] What CloudWatch metrics should you monitor for Aurora, and what do each indicate? ; Key metrics: DatabaseConnections (current open connections — alert on approaching max_connections), ReadLatency/WriteLatency (query performance), AuroraReplicaLag (replica lag — alert if >100ms), BufferCacheHitRatio (target >99% — low = too small instance), CPUUtilization (high = workload growth or missing index). <br><br> *AuroraReplicaLag: measures lag between primary and replica. If consistently >100ms, the replica is under-provisioned or receiving too many reads. Under very high write load, replica lag can grow — consider larger replica instance.<br> *BufferCacheHitRatio: percentage of reads served from in-memory buffer pool vs disk storage. Target >99% for OLTP. If <95%, the working set doesn't fit in memory — consider larger instance class or add read replicas.<br> *Deadlocks metric: count of deadlocks per second. Rising deadlocks indicate application-level lock contention — investigate transaction ordering, reduce transaction scope, or use optimistic locking.<br>

359. [ Aurora ] What is the maximum Aurora cluster storage, and what happens when you approach this limit? ; Aurora storage maximum is 128 TiB. If storage approaches 128 TiB, Aurora auto-grows up to the limit. Beyond 128 TiB, new writes will fail. There is no automatic split or archiving. <br><br> *128 TiB is the hard limit per Aurora cluster. For larger datasets: use Redshift (data warehouse, not relational OLTP), implement data archiving (move old data to S3 + query with Athena), partition data across multiple Aurora clusters, or use Aurora's native `PARTITION BY` table partitioning.<br> *Monitor FreeLocalStorage CloudWatch metric — though Aurora storage is distributed, this metric tracks available capacity. Set alarms well before hitting 128 TiB.<br> *Practical consideration: most Aurora deployments never approach 128 TiB for OLTP. This limit is more relevant for log-heavy or analytics-adjacent workloads where data accumulates rapidly.<br>

360. [ Aurora ] A company needs its Aurora database to remain available during an entire AWS region failure. What is the recommended architecture? ; Deploy Aurora Global Database with a secondary cluster in a second region. Monitor replication lag (<1 sec). Create automation (Lambda + Route 53 health checks) to detect regional failure and trigger managed planned failover (promotes secondary to primary + updates DNS). RTO ~1 minute, RPO <1 second. <br><br> *Global Database failover is not fully automatic by default — AWS recommends automation: Route 53 health check monitors primary endpoint, CloudWatch alarm triggers Lambda, Lambda calls `failover-global-cluster` API, Route 53 record updates to secondary endpoint.<br> *After failover, the original primary region is detached from the Global Database. Once the region recovers, it can be re-added as a new secondary and data re-synced (takes time based on divergence).<br> *Alternative for even lower RTO: pre-warm application in the secondary region (DNS weighted routing, blue/green) so failover is just a DNS weight change, not a cold start.<br>


361. [ Aurora ] What is the difference between a Global Database Managed Planned Failover and an unmanaged failover? ; Managed Planned Failover (MPF): AWS synchronously ensures the secondary is fully caught up with the primary before initiating failover — achieves RPO ~0. Unmanaged failover: detaches the secondary and promotes it immediately without waiting for full sync — accepts up to <1 sec RPO (async replication lag). <br><br> *MPF is used for scheduled maintenance or DR drills — you control the timing and AWS ensures no data loss. The primary stops accepting writes, waits for secondary to fully sync, then promotes secondary. Downtime: ~1 minute.<br> *Unmanaged failover is used when the primary region is inaccessible (actual disaster) — you cannot wait for sync. The secondary is promoted with whatever data it has (up to <1 sec of uncommitted writes lost).<br> *Exam question pattern: "RPO must be 0 for regional DR" → MPF + pre-arranged primary shutdown. "Region is completely down, must recover immediately" → unmanaged failover accepting <1 sec RPO.<br>

362. [ Aurora ] Can you enable encryption on an existing unencrypted Aurora cluster? If not, what is the workaround? ; No — encryption cannot be enabled on an existing Aurora cluster after creation. Workaround: take a snapshot of the unencrypted cluster → copy the snapshot with encryption enabled (specify KMS key during copy) → restore from the encrypted snapshot to a new cluster → update application connection strings → delete old cluster. <br><br> *The snapshot copy step is the key: `copy-db-cluster-snapshot` with `--kms-key-id` parameter creates an encrypted copy. The original snapshot remains unencrypted; only the copy is encrypted.<br> *During the migration: maintain the old unencrypted cluster until the new encrypted cluster is validated. Use DMS or log shipping to sync any writes that occur between snapshot time and cutover.<br> *This same workaround applies to RDS instances, not just Aurora. Always enable encryption at creation in production — retrofitting is a significant operational event.<br>

363. [ Aurora ] What is the purpose of the Aurora writer endpoint and the reader endpoint, and what happens if you write to the reader endpoint? ; Writer (Cluster) Endpoint: DNS name that always resolves to the current primary instance — use for all writes. Reader Endpoint: load-balances connections across all available Aurora Replicas — use for reads. If you write to the reader endpoint: the connection is routed to a replica, which is read-only — the write fails with a "read-only" error. <br><br> *Replicas are read-only at the storage level — no writes allowed. Even if you connect to the cluster (writer) endpoint and the writer fails mid-failover, Aurora redirects the endpoint to the new primary; the application doesn't need to change its connection string.<br> *Reader endpoint load-balances at the connection level (not query level) — each new connection is routed to a different replica. Within a connection, all queries go to the same replica. For per-query routing, use connection pooling with round-robin.<br> *Application best practice: use two connection strings — one pointing to the writer endpoint (for write path), one to the reader endpoint (for read path). Separate your read and write traffic explicitly.<br>

364. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's zero-downtime patching (ZDP) feature? ; ZDP allows Aurora to apply minor engine patches without dropping active database connections or causing application errors. During ZDP: Aurora waits for a low-traffic moment, pauses application sessions briefly (seconds), applies the patch to the engine in memory, then resumes sessions — applications see a brief stall but no disconnection. <br><br> *ZDP is available for Aurora MySQL and Aurora PostgreSQL. It applies to minor version patches only — major version upgrades (e.g., PG 14 → PG 15) require a restart and brief downtime.<br> *ZDP does not guarantee zero downtime in all cases — if there are long-running transactions or locks at patch time, ZDP may fall back to a standard restart. Monitor CloudWatch for patch events.<br> *Combined with Multi-AZ (replicas): ZDP patches the primary; replicas are patched separately. The cluster remains available throughout — replicas serve reads while the primary is patching.<br>

365. [ Aurora ] How does Aurora storage pricing differ from RDS EBS storage pricing? ; Aurora: pay for actual storage consumed (high-water-mark) at ~$0.10/GB-month, plus I/O operations (~$0.20 per 1M requests) — no pre-provisioning. RDS: pay for provisioned EBS volume size regardless of actual usage; gp3: $0.115/GB-month + optional extra IOPS cost. <br><br> *Aurora is better for variable-growth DBs — you don't overprovision. RDS gp3 can be more predictable in cost for fixed-size DBs. Aurora's auto-grow means you never run out of space; RDS requires monitoring free storage and manual expansion.<br> *Aurora I/O cost can add up for write-heavy workloads — consider Aurora I/O-Optimized (higher instance price, no I/O charge) when I/O > 25% of total cost.<br> *Aurora storage never shrinks — the high-water-mark billing means you pay for max storage ever used. RDS EBS: you can snapshot, modify volume size, and restore to a smaller volume (with downtime).<br>

366. [ Aurora ] What is the purpose of Aurora's Performance Insights, and what does the DB Load metric represent? ; Performance Insights shows DB Load — the average number of active sessions × their wait state. DB Load > number of vCPUs = DB is CPU-bound or waiting; DB Load > 1 but < vCPUs = single bottleneck (lock wait, I/O wait). Used to identify which SQL queries, wait events, users, or hosts are causing the most load. <br><br> *DB Load is normalized — a value of 1 means 1 session is always active. DB Load = 4 on a 4-vCPU instance means all CPUs are fully utilized. DB Load = 8 on a 4-vCPU instance means the DB is overloaded — queries are queuing.<br> *Wait events in Performance Insights: `io/file/*` = I/O bound, `lock/table/*` = lock contention, `CPU` = CPU bound, `synch/mutex/*` = mutex contention. Drill into the top SQL by load to identify the problematic queries.<br> *Performance Insights retains data for 7 days (free) or up to 2 years (paid). Use the "Top SQL" and "Top Wait Events" tabs to focus investigation quickly.<br>

367. [ Aurora ] A company needs to replicate Aurora MySQL changes to S3 for data lake analytics. What is the recommended approach? ; Use Aurora MySQL binary log → AWS DMS (CDC mode with S3 target), OR use Aurora MySQL zero-ETL integration with Amazon Redshift, OR use Amazon EventBridge Pipes with Aurora Streams. The simplest for S3: enable DMS CDC task with S3 target in Parquet format. <br><br> *DMS CDC approach: enable binary logging on Aurora (binlog_format=ROW), create DMS task with Aurora MySQL source + S3 target, choose Parquet format. DMS writes changes as Parquet files to S3 — queryable by Athena immediately.<br> *Aurora zero-ETL with Redshift: Aurora natively replicates changes to a Redshift database with near-zero latency and no DMS required. AWS launched this in 2023 — simplest path for Aurora MySQL → Redshift analytics.<br> *For S3 data lake specifically: DMS + S3 is well-understood, mature, and supports full load + CDC. Lambda/Kinesis can also consume Aurora Streams for custom transformation before writing to S3.<br>

368. [ Aurora ] What are the key differences between Aurora Global Database and Aurora cross-region read replicas? ; Aurora Global Database: storage-level replication (<1 sec lag), managed failover, secondary clusters can have multiple read replicas, dedicated cross-region replication path. Cross-region read replicas: engine log-based replication (seconds to minutes lag), separate RDS-style read replica per region, no coordinated failover, higher latency. <br><br> *Global Database is the recommended approach for Aurora cross-region — it's faster, more reliable, and has managed failover support. Cross-region read replicas are the RDS pattern and also available for Aurora but considered legacy for multi-region Aurora deployments.<br> *Failover comparison: Global Database managed failover takes ~1 min. Cross-region replica promotion: manual process, longer (5-10 min minimum) — you must manually promote the replica, then update application DNS. No "one API call" failover.<br> *Cost: Global Database has a replication cost (I/O and network); cross-region read replicas also have network transfer costs. Global Database is generally worth the slightly higher cost for the managed failover capability.<br>

369. [ Aurora ] What is the impact of enabling binary logging (binlog) on Aurora MySQL, and why might you enable or disable it? ; Enabling binary logging increases I/O (every write generates a binlog event) and slightly increases write latency. Enable when: using DMS CDC replication, external replication to on-premises MySQL, Aurora Streams requires it. Disable when: no external replication needed and you want to minimize I/O cost/overhead. <br><br> *Binlog retention on Aurora: set `binlog_retention_hours` — default 0 (disabled) means binlogs are purged immediately. DMS CDC requires retention > 0. Minimum: retention period > maximum DMS task restart time (typically 24-72 hours).<br> *Performance impact: binlog adds ~5-15% write latency overhead and proportional I/O increase. For I/O-sensitive Aurora workloads, binlog is a meaningful overhead to consider.<br> *Aurora native replication (for Aurora replicas in the same cluster) does NOT use binary logs — it uses the shared storage redo log. External replication (DMS, external MySQL) requires binlog. Global Database also does NOT use binlog — it's storage-level.<br>

370. [ Aurora ] How do you perform a major version upgrade on Aurora (e.g., Aurora PostgreSQL 14 → 15)? ; Major version upgrades require using the in-place upgrade (Modify Cluster → Engine Version) or snapshot restore method. In-place: Aurora restarts with the new engine version — brief downtime (minutes). Snapshot restore: take snapshot → restore to new cluster with new engine version → validate → cutover. <br><br> *In-place major version upgrade is supported for Aurora PostgreSQL (requires pg_upgrade internally) and Aurora MySQL. The cluster is unavailable for 5-15 minutes during the upgrade. Test in a cloned environment first.<br> *Before upgrading: check AWS RDS documentation for the specific version upgrade path (some versions require intermediate upgrades — e.g., PG 12 → 13 → 14, not directly 12 → 14), check extension compatibility, run pg_upgrade compatibility check.<br> *After upgrading: monitor for query plan regressions (query planner may choose different plans on new version), check deprecated features, validate application behavior. Blue/green deployments (RDS Blue/Green feature) allow testing the new version alongside the old before final cutover.<br>


371. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS Multi-AZ DB Instance and Multi-AZ DB Cluster? ; Multi-AZ DB Instance: 1 primary + 1 standby (passive, not readable) in a different AZ; failover time 60-120 sec; all RDS engines. Multi-AZ DB Cluster: 1 primary + 2 readable standbys across 3 AZs; failover time ~35 sec; MySQL 8.0 and PostgreSQL 13+ only. <br><br> *Multi-AZ DB Cluster is newer (2022) and provides better RTO (~35 sec vs 60-120 sec) AND readable standbys — eliminating the main drawback of Multi-AZ Instance (standby is idle).<br> *Multi-AZ Cluster uses local NVMe SSD storage for the primary and synchronous replication — the transaction commits only when at least 1 standby confirms. This is a quorum write model similar to Aurora.<br> *Cost: Multi-AZ Cluster costs ~3× a single instance (3 nodes). Multi-AZ Instance ~2×. For MySQL/PG in production requiring readable standbys + fast failover: Multi-AZ Cluster is the best non-Aurora option.<br>

372. [ RDS ] What is RDS Blue/Green Deployments, and when would you use it? ; RDS Blue/Green creates a staging copy (green) of your production RDS instance (blue) with replication, allowing you to test changes on the green environment. When ready, switch over with <1 minute cutover. Green is promoted to production; blue becomes the old environment (retained for rollback). <br><br> *Use cases: major version upgrades (test on green before cutover), schema changes (validate on green with prod-like traffic), parameter group changes, instance class changes — any change requiring validation before production impact.<br> *Blue/Green is supported for RDS MySQL, Aurora MySQL, RDS MariaDB. The switch-over (cutover) blocks connections briefly (~35 sec), ensures both environments are in sync, then flips DNS. This is much less risky than in-place upgrades.<br> *After switch: both environments exist for a period. Blue (old) can be deleted after validation. Blue/Green also works with Aurora — test Aurora version upgrades before committing to production.<br>

373. [ RDS ] An RDS MySQL instance has backup retention set to 0. What is the impact? ; Setting backup retention to 0 disables ALL automated backups and disables Point-In-Time Recovery (PITR). No automated snapshots are taken. Manual snapshots remain unaffected. The instance cannot be restored to a specific time — only manual snapshots (if taken) can be restored. <br><br> *Retention = 0 is dangerous for production — it means you have no automated safety net. If the instance is accidentally deleted (and no final snapshot taken) or corrupted, data is unrecoverable via backups.<br> *Use case for retention = 0: dev/test environments where you want to minimize storage costs and don't care about PITR. Setting retention = 1 (minimum for PITR) is strongly recommended for any environment with valuable data.<br> *When you change retention from 0 to >0, AWS starts taking automated backups immediately. PITR is available from the moment the first automated backup completes (the first backup covers the entire DB; subsequent backups are incremental + transaction logs).<br>

374. [ RDS ] What is the RDS Proxy, and how does it handle database failover? ; RDS Proxy is a managed connection pooler that maintains persistent connections to the DB and multiplexes application connections. During failover: the proxy detects the new primary, redirects pooled connections to it, and application connections experience minimal interruption (the proxy absorbs the reconnection). Effective failover time is reduced by ~66%. <br><br> *Without RDS Proxy: during a 60-120 sec RDS failover, ALL application connections are dropped and must reconnect individually — if 1000 connections reconnect simultaneously, this creates a connection storm and can overwhelm the new primary.<br> *With RDS Proxy: the proxy maintains ~10-50 persistent connections to RDS. Application connections are multiplexed through the proxy. During failover, the proxy reconnects its small pool to the new primary; application connections continue through the proxy.<br> *RDS Proxy also enforces max connection limits — configurable via proxy max_connections_percent. This prevents Lambda or container workloads from creating thousands of direct DB connections.<br>

375. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS Read Replicas and Multi-AZ standbys in terms of purpose and access? ; Multi-AZ standby: HA purpose only — synchronous replication, same region, NOT accessible for reads, automatic failover to primary on failure. Read Replica: performance purpose — async replication, readable, can be same or cross-region, manual promotion to standalone DB. <br><br> *This is one of the most common RDS exam distinctions: Multi-AZ = HA (not readable). Read Replica = performance/scale (readable). They serve different purposes and can be used together.<br> *You can have both: Multi-AZ instance (for HA) AND read replicas (for read scaling). The standby handles HA; the read replicas handle read traffic. These are independent features.<br> *Cross-region read replica: useful for DR (promote in another region) OR serving reads closer to users globally. Cross-region standby: not possible for RDS (Multi-AZ is same-region only).<br>

376. [ RDS ] A company's RDS PostgreSQL instance is experiencing high CPU usage. How would you investigate and resolve? ; Investigate: enable Performance Insights (DB Load metric, top SQL, wait events). Check CloudWatch for CPUUtilization, ReadIOPS, WriteIOPS, DatabaseConnections. Resolution options: upgrade instance class (vertical scale), add read replicas for read-heavy queries, add missing indexes, tune slow queries identified in Performance Insights, use RDS Proxy to reduce connection overhead. <br><br> *Performance Insights Top SQL tab shows which queries consume most DB Load. Sort by "Load by Wait States" — CPU wait means compute-bound queries (need indexes or query optimization). I/O wait means disk-bound (bigger instance class or gp3 with more IOPS).<br> *RDS Enhanced Monitoring: shows per-process CPU breakdown on the OS — identify if it's the DB process consuming CPU or other system processes (replication, maintenance).<br> *Read replicas for read-heavy workloads: if 70%+ of queries are reads, offloading reads to a replica reduces CPU on primary by a proportional amount. Application must use separate read/write endpoints.<br>

377. [ RDS ] What is the difference between static and dynamic RDS parameter group parameters, and how do you apply each? ; Dynamic parameters: apply immediately without requiring a DB instance reboot. Static parameters: require a DB instance reboot to take effect. After changing a static parameter in a parameter group, the instance shows "pending-reboot" status. <br><br> *The parameter group change itself is instant — it's the parameter application that may require reboot. Dynamic parameters are picked up on the next connection or immediately (engine-dependent). Static parameters are only read at DB startup.<br> *Common dynamic parameters: `max_connections` (takes effect immediately for new connections), `log_min_duration_statement`, query cache settings. Common static parameters: `innodb_buffer_pool_size` (MySQL — requires restart), `shared_buffers` (PostgreSQL).<br> *Exam trap: "I changed the parameter but it didn't take effect" → check if it's static (reboot required) AND if the instance is in "pending-reboot" state. For production, schedule the reboot during maintenance window.<br>

378. [ RDS ] What happens to an RDS instance during an AWS-managed maintenance window? ; During the maintenance window: AWS applies pending OS patches, DB engine minor version patches, and other maintenance tasks. For Multi-AZ: failover to standby first → patch primary → failover back → patch old primary (now standby). Total downtime: ~60 sec per failover × 2. For Single-AZ: instance reboots, brief downtime (1-5 min). <br><br> *Multi-AZ instances experience two brief failovers during patching (not zero downtime). Applications must handle reconnections gracefully. The maintenance window is the reason to keep maintenance window at low-traffic hours.<br> *You can defer maintenance by moving the maintenance window (7-day maximum defer). After 7 days, AWS applies the maintenance regardless. Critical security patches may override the window.<br> *Check pending maintenance via Console or AWS CLI: `describe-pending-maintenance-actions`. Apply immediately if the patch is critical and you want to control timing rather than wait for the window.<br>

379. [ RDS ] What is RDS Storage Auto Scaling, and what conditions trigger it? ; RDS Storage Auto Scaling automatically expands EBS storage when free space drops below a threshold. Trigger conditions (ALL three must be true): free storage < 10% of allocated storage AND free storage < 5 GiB AND low storage has persisted for ≥5 minutes. Expansion: adds 5 GiB or 10% of current size, whichever is greater. <br><br> *Storage Auto Scaling requires setting a Maximum Storage Threshold — RDS will not expand beyond this. Without a max threshold, you must enable auto-scaling explicitly (it's not enabled by default).<br> *Storage expansion is live — no downtime required for EBS volume expansion. However, the inode/filesystem resizing happens in the background and can cause brief I/O degradation.<br> *Storage only grows — RDS cannot automatically shrink EBS storage. To reduce storage: take snapshot → restore to new instance with smaller storage. This requires downtime and careful planning.<br>

380. [ RDS ] A company wants to migrate from Microsoft SQL Server on-premises to Amazon RDS for SQL Server with minimal downtime. What approach is recommended? ; Use AWS DMS with Full Load + CDC: 1) set up DMS replication instance, 2) DMS Full Load migrates existing data to RDS SQL Server, 3) DMS CDC captures ongoing changes via SQL Server transaction log, 4) validate data, 5) cutover (switch app connection strings). For schema-identical migration: no SCT needed. <br><br> *SQL Server → RDS SQL Server is a homogeneous migration — same engine, so AWS SCT is not required. DMS handles it with native SQL Server CDC (using the transaction log).<br> *Pre-requisites on source: enable SQL Server Agent for DMS CDC, ensure `sysadmin` or `db_owner` rights for DMS user, ensure sufficient transaction log retention (DMS needs to read log during migration).<br> *Downtime window: stop application → let DMS CDC drain remaining transactions → validate row counts → update DNS/connection string → start application against RDS. Typical downtime: <5 minutes with this approach.<br>

381. [ RDS ] What Oracle features are NOT available in Amazon RDS for Oracle, and what alternatives exist? ; NOT available in RDS Oracle: Oracle RAC (Real Application Clusters), Oracle Data Guard (separate managed instance), Oracle GoldenGate (need to run on EC2), Oracle ASM, Oracle Management Packs requiring extra licensing. Alternatives: Multi-AZ RDS (no RAC but provides similar HA), AWS DMS for replication (not GoldenGate), Aurora PostgreSQL for non-Oracle workloads. <br><br> *Oracle RAC (active-active clustered writes) is not possible on RDS — Oracle RAC requires shared storage at the OS level, which AWS managed infrastructure doesn't expose. If RAC is required: run Oracle on EC2 (self-managed) or evaluate Aurora PostgreSQL as a migration target.<br> *Oracle Data Guard: RDS Oracle supports Multi-AZ (which uses synchronous replication similar to Data Guard), but not the full Data Guard feature set. RDS Oracle read replicas are not available either.<br> *BYOL vs License Included: RDS Oracle supports both. BYOL is cost-effective if you already have Oracle licenses with "mobility rights." License Included is simpler but more expensive per hour.<br>

382. [ RDS ] What is Amazon RDS for SQL Server's maximum storage limit, and how does SQL Server licensing work on RDS? ; RDS SQL Server: max storage 16 TiB. Licensing: License Included (SQL Server Standard or Enterprise included in hourly price — ~3-5× more expensive per hour than BYOL but no separate Oracle/SQL Server license contract needed). BYOL: bring your own SQL Server license — lower hourly cost, requires active SA (Software Assurance) coverage. <br><br> *License Included is convenient but expensive for large instances. At scale (multiple large instances), BYOL with existing enterprise agreements is much cheaper. Calculate break-even based on your instance hours and existing license coverage.<br> *RDS SQL Server editions: Express (free tier, 10 GB limit), Web (limited features), Standard, Enterprise. Web edition cannot be used for commercial hosting. Express is limited to 10 GB DB size and 1 GB RAM.<br> *SQL Server read replicas: NOT supported on RDS SQL Server. For read scaling: use Always On Availability Groups (only on RDS SQL Server Enterprise in Multi-AZ DB Cluster) or add a separate reporting instance with DMS replication.<br>

383. [ RDS ] What is the purpose of RDS Enhanced Monitoring, and how does it differ from standard CloudWatch metrics? ; Standard CloudWatch metrics: hypervisor-level metrics (CPU, network, disk at the VM level), sampled every minute. Enhanced Monitoring: OS-level metrics (per-process CPU, memory breakdown, filesystem, I/O per OS process), sampled every 1-60 seconds. Requires `AmazonRDSEnhancedMonitoringRole` IAM role. <br><br> *Standard CloudWatch CPU is the hypervisor's view — it includes all processes on the VM. Enhanced Monitoring breaks it down: database process, monitoring agent, OS processes separately. Helps distinguish DB-specific CPU from infrastructure overhead.<br> *Enhanced Monitoring runs a CloudWatch agent on the DB host — the agent metrics go to CloudWatch Logs (not standard CloudWatch metrics), viewable in the RDS console under "Monitoring" tab.<br> *For deep OS-level analysis (memory pressure, swap usage, I/O wait per process): Enhanced Monitoring is essential. Standard CloudWatch is sufficient for high-level alerting. Use both together for comprehensive observability.<br>

384. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS Automated Backups and manual snapshots in terms of lifecycle and portability? ; Automated backups: exist only within backup retention period (1-35 days); deleted when DB instance is deleted (unless "Final Snapshot" is taken); can be enabled per instance; support PITR. Manual snapshots: retained indefinitely until explicitly deleted; survive DB deletion; can be shared cross-account; can be copied cross-region; no PITR from manual snapshots alone. <br><br> *Final snapshot: when deleting an RDS instance, AWS prompts for a final manual snapshot. If you select "No" and have no recent manual snapshot, data is unrecoverable after deletion. Always take a final snapshot before deleting production instances.<br> *PITR from automated backups: the automated backup + transaction logs enable restoration to any second within the retention window. Manual snapshot alone only restores to the moment the snapshot was taken.<br> *Cross-region DR: copy manual snapshots to the DR region periodically (or use AWS Backup to automate). Automated backups are regional only — they don't automatically replicate cross-region.<br>

385. [ RDS ] A company needs to query RDS data from a Lambda function that runs 5000 concurrent invocations. What architecture prevents connection exhaustion? ; Use RDS Proxy between Lambda and RDS. RDS Proxy maintains a persistent connection pool (typically 10-50 connections to RDS), multiplexing the 5000 Lambda connections through the pool. Without Proxy, 5000 Lambda functions × 1 connection each = 5000 DB connections → exceeds RDS max_connections → connection refused errors. <br><br> *RDS max_connections scales with instance class: db.t3.micro ~66, db.r6g.large ~1500, db.r6g.4xlarge ~5000. Lambda at scale easily exceeds smaller instances. RDS Proxy allows a small instance to handle Lambda-scale workloads.<br> *RDS Proxy also supports IAM authentication — Lambda can get DB credentials from IAM tokens instead of Secrets Manager at every invocation. The proxy handles the native DB authentication, reducing Lambda init overhead.<br> *Additional benefit: RDS Proxy reduces the "connection storm" after an RDS failover — instead of 5000 Lambdas all reconnecting simultaneously to the new primary, the proxy reconnects its small pool and routes Lambda connections through.<br>

386. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS for MySQL and RDS for MariaDB? When would you choose one over the other? ; MariaDB is a MySQL fork that started as a drop-in replacement (same wire protocol, same SQL syntax, compatible drivers). Differences: MariaDB has additional storage engines (Aria, ColumnStore), different optimizer in some versions, some MariaDB-specific functions, and slightly different minor feature set. Choose MySQL: if you specifically need MySQL 8.0 features, AWS Aurora MySQL compatibility, or team familiarity. Choose MariaDB: if you're migrating an existing MariaDB workload or want MariaDB-specific features. <br><br> *Both use MySQL wire protocol — most applications work with either without code changes. The choice is usually driven by what the application was developed against.<br> *Aurora is MySQL-compatible (not MariaDB-compatible) — if you plan to migrate to Aurora for better scaling, start with MySQL on RDS. MariaDB on RDS cannot directly migrate to Aurora.<br> *RDS MariaDB supports up to 5 read replicas (same as MySQL). Multi-AZ DB Cluster is available for MariaDB (same as MySQL 8.0 and PG 13+). Feature parity is very close for typical web application workloads.<br>

387. [ RDS ] What does it mean when an RDS instance is in "storage-full" state? How do you recover? ; "Storage-full" means the EBS volume has no free space — RDS automatically sets the instance to read-only (to prevent data corruption). Recovery: immediately connect and delete or archive large tables/logs; OR increase EBS storage (Modify → Increase Allocated Storage). Increasing storage is live (no downtime for EBS expansion). <br><br> *First action in an emergency: increase allocated storage via Console/CLI. EBS expansion is live — no reboot needed for the storage change itself. The instance returns to read-write mode once free space is above threshold.<br> *To prevent recurrence: enable RDS Storage Auto Scaling with a Maximum Storage Threshold. Set CloudWatch alarms on FreeStorageSpace metric (alert at 20%, critical at 10%).<br> *Common cause: MySQL binary logs accumulating (set `expire_logs_days` or `binlog_expire_logs_seconds`), large temp table spills to disk, runaway query with huge result set, data growth without monitoring.<br>

388. [ RDS ] What is RDS Custom, and what use case does it address? ; RDS Custom is a managed RDS option that gives you OS-level and database engine access — unlike standard RDS where you cannot access the underlying OS. It supports Oracle and SQL Server. Use case: applications requiring custom Oracle patches, third-party agents on the DB host, custom DB configurations not possible via parameter groups, Oracle Multimedia or legacy Oracle features requiring OS-level configuration. <br><br> *Standard RDS: no OS access, no custom software installation. RDS Custom: SSH access, ability to install agents, custom file system configuration — but you take responsibility for patches and customizations (shared responsibility model shifts).<br> *RDS Custom still provides: automated backups, Multi-AZ, performance monitoring. But it does not automatically apply OS or DB patches — you manage patching when you need OS access.<br> *Use RDS Custom when: migrating an Oracle application that requires a specific Oracle patch or third-party monitoring agent on the DB host. If standard RDS suffices (most CRUD workloads), prefer standard RDS for lower operational overhead.<br>

389. [ RDS ] How does AWS DMS handle a large-table migration where the source has 500 GB of data? What is recommended to speed it up? ; DMS Full Load for large tables: use parallel full load (set LOB mode appropriately, increase `MaxFullLoadSubTasks`). Enable parallel table loading across multiple tables simultaneously. Use a larger DMS replication instance (C5.4xlarge or R5.4xlarge for heavy workloads). Pre-create indexes on target only after full load (not during — indexes slow inserts). <br><br> *DMS parallel full load: configure `ParallelLoadQueuesPerThread`, `ParallelLoadThreads` settings. For very large tables (500 GB), consider splitting the migration into segments (range partitions by PK) and running multiple DMS tasks in parallel.<br> *Target optimization: disable/drop indexes before full load, re-create after. Disable foreign key checks during load. Use batched bulk inserts (increase `CommitRate` parameter). For RDS MySQL target: `innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit=2` during migration (re-enable after).<br> *LOB (Large Object) handling: always set LOB mode appropriate for your data. "Full LOB Mode" is slow (fetches LOBs row-by-row). "Limited LOB Mode" with appropriate LOB max size is much faster if LOBs are within the size limit.<br>

390. [ RDS ] What is the AWS Schema Conversion Tool (SCT), and what does its assessment report contain? ; SCT is a desktop tool (runs on your machine) that analyzes a source database schema and converts it to a target engine format. The assessment report shows: conversion summary (% auto-converted), list of manually-required items with effort estimates, database objects (tables, views, procedures, functions, triggers) with conversion status and issues. <br><br> *SCT assessment report categories: "Auto Converted" (green — no action needed), "Requires Attention" (yellow — converted with caveats, review), "Cannot Convert" (red — manual conversion required, highest effort).<br> *"Action items" in SCT are database objects with conversion issues — each has a description of why it couldn't be auto-converted and a recommended fix. Complex PL/SQL packages with Oracle-specific features (UTL_FILE, DBMS_SCHEDULER) are commonly flagged red.<br> *SCT also converts application SQL (embedded in Java, .NET, C++ code): SCT can scan code files and convert SQL strings. This helps estimate total migration effort including application-side changes, not just the database schema.<br>

391. [ DynamoDB ] What is a hot partition in DynamoDB, and how do you prevent it? ; A hot partition occurs when a disproportionate amount of traffic goes to a single DynamoDB partition (determined by the partition key). This causes throttling on that partition even if total table capacity is sufficient. Prevention: choose high-cardinality partition keys (user ID, UUID, random suffix), avoid low-cardinality keys (status, country with few values). <br><br> *DynamoDB splits data across partitions by hashing the partition key. If 80% of requests use the same partition key value (e.g., "status=active"), 80% of traffic hits one partition — causing throttling even if table-level RCU/WCU is provisioned high enough.<br> *Common anti-patterns: date-based keys ("2026-10-30" — all today's writes on one partition), sequential IDs (1,2,3... — writes cluster at the top), status codes (few distinct values).<br> *Fix for existing hot partition: write sharding — append a random suffix (1-N) to the partition key, spread writes across N virtual partitions, use scatter-gather pattern on reads (query all N partitions, merge results).<br>

392. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB's item size limit, and what should you do if an item exceeds it? ; DynamoDB items have a maximum size of 400 KB (item + attribute names). If data exceeds 400 KB: store the large data in S3, store only the S3 object key in the DynamoDB item, retrieve large data from S3 when needed. <br><br> *400 KB includes attribute names in the size calculation — long attribute names reduce available space for values. Use short attribute names (e.g., "u" instead of "user_id") for storage efficiency, especially in large tables.<br> *For JSON documents or binary blobs >400 KB: S3 + DynamoDB pointer pattern is the standard solution. DynamoDB holds the index/metadata; S3 holds the blob. Access pattern: query DynamoDB → get S3 key → GET from S3.<br> *If items are commonly 350-400 KB: reconsider data model — DynamoDB is not optimal for large document storage. Amazon DocumentDB or S3 with Athena may be more appropriate for large document patterns.<br>

393. [ DynamoDB ] What is the difference between a Global Secondary Index (GSI) and a Local Secondary Index (LSI) in DynamoDB? ; LSI: same partition key as base table, different sort key; must be created at table creation; max 5 per table; uses table's RCU/WCU; supports strong consistency. GSI: different partition key (and optional sort key); can be added after table creation; max 20 per table; has its own provisioned capacity; only eventually consistent. <br><br> *LSI key constraint: same partition key as the base table — you can only query LSI records within a specific partition. If you need to query across all partitions on a non-PK attribute: use GSI.<br> *GSI write throttle impact: when writing to the base table, DynamoDB also writes to all GSIs. If a GSI is under-provisioned (throttled), the base table write is also throttled — GSI capacity affects base table write throughput.<br> *GSI sparse index: if a GSI's key attribute doesn't exist in an item, that item is NOT included in the GSI. This is a powerful optimization — create a sparse GSI on a rarely-populated attribute to index only the relevant items (e.g., "isAdminUser" attribute only on admin records).<br>

394. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB TTL, and how does it work? ; TTL (Time To Live) automatically deletes items after a specified timestamp expires. You designate an attribute to hold the Unix epoch expiry timestamp. DynamoDB scans for expired items and deletes them (typically within 48 hours of expiry). Deletions are free (no WCU consumed). <br><br> *TTL is eventually consistent for deletions — items may persist for up to 48 hours past expiry. If your application needs to hide expired items immediately, add a TTL check in the application read path.<br> *TTL deletes appear in DynamoDB Streams with a "system" user origin — useful for triggering cleanup workflows when items expire (Lambda on Streams processes TTL deletes).<br> *Use cases: session data (expire sessions after 24 hours), temporary items (OTP codes, cart items), log records (expire after 90 days for cost management), rate limiting (sliding window counters with TTL).<br>

395. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB's Scan operation, and why should it be avoided in production? ; Scan reads every item in the table (or GSI) and then filters. It consumes RCU proportional to ALL data scanned, not just items returned. On a 100 GB table, a Scan with a filter returning 10 items still consumes RCU for all 100 GB. This is slow, expensive, and can throttle other operations. <br><br> *Use Query instead of Scan whenever possible — Query uses the partition key to target a specific partition, consuming only the RCU for matching items. Design data model so all access patterns can be served by Query operations.<br> *Parallel Scan: if a full-table scan is unavoidable (e.g., one-time migration), use parallel Scan to split the table across multiple segments and scan simultaneously — reduces wall clock time but consumes proportionally more RCU.<br> *For analytics on DynamoDB data: export to S3 (no RCU consumed) → query with Athena or Spark. Do NOT run analytical Scans on production DynamoDB tables.<br>

396. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX), and when should you NOT use it? ; DAX is an in-memory cache cluster for DynamoDB providing microsecond latency. Do NOT use DAX when: (1) workload is write-heavy (DAX doesn't help writes — it write-through, adding latency), (2) you need strongly consistent reads (DAX cache returns eventually consistent data), (3) queries are predominantly Scans or complex queries (DAX caches item-level and query results, not useful for constantly-changing scan results), (4) data fits poorly in RAM (DAX evicts items under memory pressure). <br><br> *DAX sits in your VPC — Lambda and EC2 in the same VPC can access it. It is NOT accessible from outside the VPC. Applications must use the DAX SDK (not standard AWS SDK) — but DAX SDK is a drop-in replacement (same API structure).<br> *DAX has its own Multi-AZ cluster: primary + replica nodes across AZs. If primary fails, replica is promoted. Configuration endpoint resolves to the current primary. Total cache failure falls back to DynamoDB.<br> *Cost: DAX node costs are additive to DynamoDB costs. Calculate whether cache hit rate justifies the cost. For read-heavy workloads with >80% cache hit rate: DAX often saves more in RCU costs than it adds in node costs.<br>

397. [ DynamoDB ] How do DynamoDB Global Tables handle write conflicts when two regions write to the same item simultaneously? ; DynamoDB Global Tables uses last-writer-wins conflict resolution based on timestamp. The write with the most recent timestamp (per item, per attribute) wins and is propagated to all regions. The losing write is discarded — no merge, no error, no manual resolution. <br><br> *Last-writer-wins is a simple but sometimes lossy strategy. If two regions write different values to the same attribute within milliseconds, one write is silently lost. For applications requiring conflict detection: implement application-level versioning (condition expressions + version counter).<br> *Global Tables replication is asynchronous (~<1 sec typical). During this window, different regions may have different versions of the same item. This is eventual consistency — acceptable for many use cases (user preferences, product catalogs) but not for financial transactions.<br> *For financial/inventory workloads requiring strict consistency across regions: use a single primary region for writes (accept higher write latency for non-primary regions) rather than active-active Global Tables.<br>

398. [ DynamoDB ] What is a DynamoDB Conditional Write, and how does it prevent race conditions? ; A Conditional Write adds a condition expression to a PutItem, UpdateItem, or DeleteItem — the operation only succeeds if the condition is true at the time of execution. If the condition fails, DynamoDB returns ConditionalCheckFailedException (no partial write). Used to implement optimistic locking and prevent overwrites. <br><br> *Example: `ConditionExpression: "attribute_not_exists(PK)"` — write only if item doesn't already exist (prevents duplicate creation in concurrent insert scenarios). Or `"version = :expected_version"` — update only if version hasn't changed (optimistic locking).<br> *Conditional writes are atomic — the condition check and write are a single atomic operation at DynamoDB. No external locking mechanism needed. This is the recommended pattern for concurrent DynamoDB updates.<br> *Conditional writes consume WCU even if the condition fails (you're charged for the write attempt). Design applications to retry with backoff on ConditionalCheckFailedException when appropriate.<br>

399. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB Transactions, and what are its limitations? ; DynamoDB Transactions (`TransactWriteItems`, `TransactGetItems`) provide ACID guarantees across up to 100 items in up to 100 tables within a single region in a single request. Cost: 2× normal WCU/RCU. Limitations: single region only, max 100 items per transaction, max 4 MB total per transaction. <br><br> *TransactWriteItems: atomically apply up to 100 writes (Put, Update, Delete, ConditionCheck) across multiple tables — either all succeed or all fail. No partial application. Used for financial transfers (debit account A + credit account B atomically).<br> *Cross-region transactions: NOT supported. Global Tables replicate eventually — transactional guarantees exist only within a single region. For cross-region ACID: use Aurora Global Database (SQL transactions within a region, async replication cross-region).<br> *2× cost is a common exam distractor. "I need transactions across 200 items" — not possible in a single DynamoDB transaction (max 100). Solution: redesign data model to batch into ≤100 items, or use multiple transactions with compensation logic.<br>

400. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB single-table design pattern, and why is it recommended? ; Single-table design puts all application entities (users, orders, products) in one DynamoDB table, distinguished by the PK/SK pattern. Benefits: fewer DynamoDB API calls (fetch user + their orders in one Query), better partition key distribution, no JOIN latency. Drawback: complex schema design; less intuitive; harder to maintain. <br><br> *In relational DBs: separate tables, JOINs. In DynamoDB: all entities in one table, access patterns determine the key schema. Query "get user and their recent orders": PK=USER#123, SK=ORDER#* returns all orders for that user in one partition (one Query call).<br> *Overloaded attribute names: "PK" and "SK" are generic names — user items set PK=USER#123, SK=METADATA; order items set PK=USER#123, SK=ORDER#2024-01-15. Same table, same attribute names, different semantic meaning per entity type.<br> *Not always right: for independent entities with no access pattern overlap, multiple tables can be simpler. Single-table shines when related entities are frequently fetched together. Start with the access patterns, design the table schema around them.<br>

401. [ DynamoDB ] How does DynamoDB On-Demand capacity mode handle traffic spikes compared to Provisioned + Auto Scaling? ; On-Demand: instantly absorbs any traffic spike up to 2× the table's previous peak throughput (doubles within a 24-hour window). No throttling for spikes within this range. Provisioned + Auto Scaling: scaling is reactive and takes 5-15 minutes — spikes before scale-out causes throttling. <br><br> *On-Demand "previous peak" rule: if your table has never seen more than 10,000 WCU/second, On-Demand will instantly handle up to 20,000 WCU/second. Beyond 2× previous peak: throttling (AWS raises the limit over minutes).<br> *Switching between modes: you can switch between On-Demand and Provisioned once per table per 24 hours. Use this to handle known upcoming spikes: switch to On-Demand before a flash sale, switch back after for cost savings.<br> *Cost comparison: On-Demand is ~6-7× more expensive per WCU/RCU than Provisioned. For predictable workloads: Provisioned + auto-scaling + Reserved Capacity is 60-80% cheaper. On-Demand is worth the premium for truly unpredictable workloads.<br>

402. [ DynamoDB ] What is DynamoDB Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR), and what does "restore to new table" mean? ; PITR continuously backs up DynamoDB table data and allows restore to any second within the last 35 days. Restore always creates a NEW table — you cannot restore in-place to the existing table. The new table must be validated and then either replace the existing table or be used as a source for data recovery. <br><br> *PITR is per-table — enable on each table individually. It uses DynamoDB's continuous backup mechanism (not periodic snapshots), so any second in the 35-day window is available.<br> *After PITR restore: the new table has no GSIs, TTL, streams, or other settings from the original — you must re-configure these manually. Table name is different. Applications must be pointed to the new table.<br> *PITR does NOT restore to the same table because DynamoDB needs to ensure data integrity — the existing table may have received writes since the restore point. Creating a new table avoids overwriting newer (valid) data.<br>

403. [ DynamoDB ] What is the purpose of DynamoDB Streams, and what is the data retention period? ; DynamoDB Streams captures a time-ordered log of item-level changes (INSERT, MODIFY, REMOVE) for a table. Retention: 24 hours. After 24 hours, records expire from the stream. Use for: event-driven processing (Lambda), cross-region replication (Global Tables), search indexing (→ OpenSearch), audit trail (→ S3). <br><br> *Stream record view types: KEYS_ONLY (only PK/SK of changed item), NEW_IMAGE (item as it appears after change), OLD_IMAGE (item as it appeared before change), NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES (both — highest overhead, needed for full audit). Choose view type based on what downstream consumers need.<br> *Lambda integration: Lambda polls the stream shard and invokes the function with a batch of records. Configure batch size (1-10,000) and bisect-on-error behavior. Lambda retries failed batches — ensure idempotent processing.<br> *24-hour retention is the key limitation — if your Lambda consumer falls behind by >24 hours (extended outage), records are lost. Design with DLQ (Dead Letter Queue) and monitoring for stream lag (IteratorAge CloudWatch metric).<br>

404. [ DynamoDB ] A company has a DynamoDB table with 1 TB of data. They want to run analytical queries without consuming table read capacity. What is the recommended approach? ; Use DynamoDB Export to S3 (enabled with PITR). DynamoDB exports table data to S3 in JSON or ION format without consuming read capacity (uses backup storage). Query the exported data with Amazon Athena or EMR. For ongoing analytics: Kinesis Data Firehose via DynamoDB Streams → S3 → Athena. <br><br> *DynamoDB Export to S3: near-instant for metadata (export job runs asynchronously); data lands in S3 in typically hours for large tables. No RCU consumed. Exported data is in ION format by default — Athena can query it with the appropriate SerDe.<br> *For real-time analytics (not point-in-time): DynamoDB Streams → Kinesis Data Firehose → S3 → Athena. This captures changes as they happen; Athena queries accumulate data continuously.<br> *Avoid running Scan operations for analytics — even with parallel Scan, a 1 TB table Scan would consume enormous RCU and potentially throttle production workloads. Always export to S3 for analytical workloads.<br>

405. [ DynamoDB ] What are the DynamoDB Reserved Capacity options, and how do they interact with On-Demand mode? ; DynamoDB Reserved Capacity applies to Provisioned mode only — you purchase a fixed number of RCU and WCU at a discount (1- or 3-year term, up to 77% savings). Reserved Capacity cannot be used with On-Demand mode. If you switch to On-Demand, reserved capacity dollars are effectively wasted (you still pay for the reservation). <br><br> *Reserved Capacity is applied automatically against your provisioned RCU/WCU usage in the same region. No configuration needed — AWS applies the discount to matching usage. Unused reservation is lost (no rollover).<br> *Strategy: maintain baseline provisioned capacity with Reserved Capacity for cost savings; use On-Demand mode only for tables with truly unpredictable traffic. Don't buy Reserved Capacity for tables you plan to switch to On-Demand.<br> *Exam trap: "company wants to save costs AND handle traffic spikes" → use Provisioned + auto-scaling + Reserved Capacity (not On-Demand). On-Demand is more expensive; reserved capacity requires provisioned mode.<br>


406. [ Redshift ] What is the difference between Redshift Elastic Resize and Classic Resize? ; Elastic Resize: adds or removes nodes of the same type in ~15 minutes; cluster remains online (queries paused briefly, then resumed); recommended for regular scaling. Classic Resize: can change node type or make large size changes; cluster goes read-only for hours; use only when Elastic Resize is not supported. <br><br> *Elastic Resize works within the same node type (add more dc2.8xlarge nodes). If you need to change from dc2 to ra3 (node type change), you must use Classic Resize or snapshot + restore to a new cluster with different node type.<br> *During Elastic Resize: data redistribution happens in the background while the cluster is online. Queries may be slower during redistribution as Redshift reshuffles data across the new node count.<br> *Alternative to Classic Resize: snapshot the cluster → restore to a new cluster with the desired configuration → update DNS/connections. This "snapshot-restore" approach avoids the read-only period — the new cluster is fully operational from snapshot restore completion.<br>

407. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Spectrum, and when would you use it instead of loading data into Redshift? ; Redshift Spectrum queries data directly in S3 without loading it into Redshift. Use Spectrum when: data is in S3 and rarely queried (no need to load), query performance on S3 is acceptable for the use case, data exceeds Redshift cluster storage, cold/historical data that's expensive to load. <br><br> *Spectrum uses massively parallel Redshift compute infrastructure to scan S3 data — it's not as fast as querying loaded Redshift tables (no local cache, no sort keys), but dramatically cheaper for cold data.<br> *Pricing: $5/TB scanned in S3 (Spectrum) vs storing data in Redshift (~$0.024/GB-month for RA3 managed storage). For data queried <2-3× per month: Spectrum is cheaper. For hot data queried daily: load into Redshift for performance and cost.<br> *Spectrum requires an external schema pointing to Glue Data Catalog (or Hive Metastore). Use Parquet or ORC columnar formats in S3 for best Spectrum performance — columnar formats reduce data scanned (pay less, query faster).<br>

408. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Data Sharing, and how does it work across accounts? ; Data Sharing allows a Redshift producer cluster to share live data (not copies) with consumer clusters — same account or cross-account — without data movement. Producer creates a datashare and grants access; consumer creates a database from the datashare and queries it read-only. <br><br> *Within an account: producer cluster shares with consumer cluster in same or different region (cross-region Data Sharing on RA3). No data copied — consumer queries always see the latest producer data.<br> *Cross-account: producer adds consumer account ID to the datashare; consumer account administrator associates the datashare to a cluster. Requires both clusters on RA3 nodes.<br> *Use cases: data mesh (each team owns their Redshift cluster, shares relevant data); analytics isolation (analytics team has read-only consumer, doesn't impact production producer); department-level access control without data copies.<br>

409. [ Redshift ] What distribution styles does Redshift support, and how do you choose the right one? ; Distribution styles: EVEN (round-robin across slices — balanced distribution, no co-location), KEY (rows with same KEY value on same slice — enables co-location for joins), ALL (copy entire table to every node — good for small dimension tables), AUTO (Redshift chooses based on table size). <br><br> *EVEN: use for tables with no common join key, or when key skew is a concern. Even distribution prevents hot slices but requires data movement (shuffling) during joins.<br> *KEY: use for large fact tables that are frequently joined with dimension tables on the same column. If ORDERS and CUSTOMERS both distributed on CUSTOMER_ID: the join is local (co-located), no shuffling needed — fast.<br> *ALL: use for small dimension tables (typically <10M rows) that are joined frequently. Every slice has a full copy — any join to this table is co-located. Drawback: writes are replicated to all nodes (slow for write-heavy dimension updates).<br>

410. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Workload Management (WLM), and what problem does it solve? ; WLM creates queues with dedicated concurrency and memory allocations for different query types. Prevents long-running analytics queries from blocking short OLTP-style queries. Configure: create separate queues for ETL (high memory, low concurrency), interactive queries (moderate memory, high concurrency), and reports (medium memory).<br><br> *Without WLM: all queries compete for the same resources — a 10-minute complex query can prevent simple 1-second queries from running. With WLM: short queries route to a fast queue, long queries to a separate queue; neither blocks the other.<br> *Automatic WLM (recommended): Redshift dynamically adjusts memory and concurrency based on query complexity — simpler queries get more concurrency, complex queries get more memory. Reduces manual tuning.<br> *Short Query Acceleration (SQA): automatically detects queries predicted to complete quickly and routes them to a dedicated short-query slot, bypassing the main WLM queue. Significantly reduces p99 latency for mixed workloads.<br>

411. [ Redshift ] What is a sort key in Redshift, and how does it improve query performance? ; A sort key defines the physical sort order of data on disk in each Redshift slice. When a query filters on the sort key column, Redshift uses zone maps (min/max values per 1 MB block) to skip blocks that don't contain matching values — reducing I/O dramatically. <br><br> *Example: table sorted by `event_date`. Query `WHERE event_date BETWEEN '2026-01-01' AND '2026-01-31'`: Redshift reads only the blocks containing January 2026 dates, skipping all other blocks. Without sort key: full table scan.<br> *Compound sort key: multiple columns specified in order (col1, col2, col3). Most effective when queries filter on leading columns in order. If queries skip col1 and only filter on col2: compound sort key is ineffective.<br> *Interleaved sort key: each sort column given equal weight — useful when queries filter on different subsets of columns with no dominant order. More expensive to maintain (VACUUM REINDEX required). Generally, compound sort key + good column ordering is preferred.<br>

412. [ Redshift ] What is VACUUM in Redshift, and when should it be run? ; VACUUM reclaims disk space from deleted or updated rows (Redshift marks rows as deleted but doesn't remove them immediately) and re-sorts unsorted rows in sort key order. Two types: VACUUM DELETE ONLY (removes deleted rows), VACUUM SORT ONLY (re-sorts unsorted rows), VACUUM FULL (both). <br><br> *Automatic VACUUM: Redshift runs VACUUM automatically during off-peak hours (since ~2019). Manual VACUUM is needed after very large deletes or bulk loads that leave significant unsorted data.<br> *ANALYZE: updates query optimizer statistics after large data changes. Run ANALYZE after bulk loads. Automatic ANALYZE also runs — but for very large tables after a big load, manual ANALYZE ensures fresh statistics for query planning.<br> *Signs you need VACUUM: queries slower after large DELETEs or UPDATEs (deleted rows not reclaimed), sort key range queries slower after many out-of-order inserts (rows unsorted). Check SVV_TABLE_INFO for `pct_unsorted` and `diststyle` details.<br>

413. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift's Concurrency Scaling, and what is the cost model? ; Concurrency Scaling automatically adds read-only cluster capacity when query queue depth grows (burst reads beyond main cluster capacity). The added capacity is billed per second while active. First 1 hour of Concurrency Scaling per day is free per cluster; beyond that: $0.008/second on the same node type as the main cluster. <br><br> *Concurrency Scaling only handles SELECT queries — not writes or DDL. Enable per WLM queue (set max_concurrency_scaling_clusters). Queries automatically routed to Concurrency Scaling when the queue is full.<br> *When to use: workloads with predictable daily peaks (end-of-day reports, morning dashboards). Concurrency Scaling handles the peak without permanent cluster expansion. For continuous high concurrency: better to resize the cluster.<br> *Cost calculation: 3600 seconds/day free = 1 hour. If peak lasts 2 hours: pay for the second hour (~$29/day for a 2-node dc2.8xlarge add-on). Compare with always-on extra nodes (~$600/month for the same). Concurrency Scaling is cost-effective for short, predictable bursts.<br>

414. [ Redshift ] A company uses Redshift to run reports on data that is 7 years old. Most of this old data is never queried. How can they reduce cost while maintaining query capability? ; Use Redshift Spectrum: keep hot data (recent 1-2 years) in Redshift tables; move cold data (5-7 year old) to S3 in Parquet format; query cold data via Spectrum (external tables pointing to S3). Redshift nodes are not needed for cold data storage — S3 is 97% cheaper than Redshift storage. <br><br> *Implementation: use Redshift's `UNLOAD` command to export old data partitioned by date to S3 in Parquet format → delete from Redshift → create Spectrum external table. Queries can JOIN Redshift tables and Spectrum tables seamlessly.<br> *Glue Data Catalog: register the S3 data in Glue Catalog as external tables → Redshift Spectrum queries via the Glue Catalog schema. Parquet + partitioning (by year/month) reduces data scanned per query.<br> *Cost math: 1 TB in Redshift RA3 managed storage: ~$23/month. 1 TB in S3 Standard: ~$23/month (same!). 1 TB in S3 Infrequent Access: ~$12.5/month. BUT Spectrum adds $5/TB scanned — for rarely queried data, the total cost (storage + occasional Spectrum scans) is still much less than keeping it in Redshift and having it consume cluster memory/cache.<br>

415. [ Redshift ] What is the difference between Redshift Serverless and a provisioned Redshift cluster? ; Redshift Serverless: no cluster management, auto-scales RPU based on workload, pay per RPU-second active, no idle cost. Provisioned: dedicated nodes always running, fixed cost regardless of query activity, more predictable performance, reserved instances for cost savings. <br><br> *Redshift Serverless is best for: sporadic or unpredictable analytical workloads, development environments, multi-tenant analytics where workload is variable. Idle Serverless = zero cost (no queries, no charge).<br> *Provisioned is best for: predictable high-throughput analytical workloads, when reserved instances make it cost-competitive, when you need specific node configurations for performance. Serverless has a "cold start" (compute allocation delay) if completely idle for extended periods.<br> *Migration: you can restore a provisioned cluster snapshot to Serverless (and vice versa). Serverless max 512 RPU; provisioned scales to hundreds of nodes. For petabyte-scale workloads with sustained high concurrency: provisioned (with RA3) is likely better.<br>

416. [ Redshift ] How does Redshift handle encryption and what are the key management options? ; Redshift supports encryption at-rest using AES-256. Options: AWS-managed KMS key (default — simple, no extra cost), Customer Managed Key (CMK) via KMS (full control, audit), CloudHSM (FIPS 140-2 Level 3 — highest security, needed for some compliance regimes). Encryption must be enabled at cluster creation and cannot be changed afterward. <br><br> *To change encryption on existing cluster: create new encrypted cluster → UNLOAD all data to S3 → COPY to new cluster. This requires significant downtime and operational planning for large clusters.<br> *CloudHSM for Redshift: Redshift uses a two-tier key hierarchy — a master key (in HSM), a cluster encryption key (CEK), a database encryption key (DEK). All data encrypted with DEK; DEK encrypted with CEK; CEK encrypted with master key in HSM.<br> *Column-level encryption: Redshift has built-in encryption functions (`encrypt()`, `decrypt()`, `fn_encrypt()`) for applying client-managed encryption to specific columns. Use when only certain columns (SSN, credit card) need encryption with different access control than the full database encryption.<br>


417. [ Neptune ] What query languages does Amazon Neptune support, and when would you choose each? ; Neptune supports: Gremlin (Apache TinkerPop — property graph traversals), SPARQL (W3C standard — RDF triple stores), and openCypher (Cypher language — property graph, similar to Neo4j). Gremlin: imperative traversal style for property graphs. SPARQL: for semantic web, ontologies, linked data. openCypher: declarative SQL-like style for property graphs, familiar to Neo4j users. <br><br> *Choose Gremlin: existing TinkerPop-based application, procedural graph traversal logic, large Apache ecosystem. Choose SPARQL: RDF data model (triples), W3C standards compliance, semantic web use cases, linked data publishing. Choose openCypher: migrating from Neo4j, SQL-background developers preferring declarative style.<br> *Gremlin and openCypher work with Neptune's Property Graph model (vertices + edges + properties). SPARQL works with Neptune's RDF model (subject-predicate-object triples). You cannot mix models — choose one at cluster creation time (or use separate clusters).<br> *Exam tip: if the question mentions "social network graph" or "fraud detection" → Property Graph → Gremlin or openCypher. If it mentions "semantic web," "ontology," or "linked data" → RDF → SPARQL.<br>

418. [ Neptune ] What are the key use cases for Amazon Neptune vs DynamoDB vs RDS? ; Neptune: highly connected data where relationships are the primary query (social graphs, fraud detection, knowledge graphs, recommendation engines, network topology). DynamoDB: key-value lookups, flexible schema, massive scale, simple access patterns. RDS: structured relational data, complex SQL joins, ACID transactions, existing relational applications. <br><br> *Rule of thumb: if your data is naturally graph-shaped (entities are connected to other entities in complex ways), and you frequently ask "who is connected to whom?" or "find the shortest path" → Neptune. If you have simple entity relationships representable in a relational model → RDS. If you need KV/document at scale → DynamoDB.<br> *Fraud detection is a classic Neptune use case: detect rings of fraudulent accounts by traversing relationships (shared device, shared address, shared phone number). In relational DB: this requires many self-JOINs that become exponentially expensive. In Neptune: a few graph traversal steps.<br> *Knowledge graphs: ontologies representing domain knowledge (e.g., medical conditions → symptoms → treatments → drugs). RDF triples naturally represent this; SPARQL queries traverse the ontology. Neptune handles billions of triples efficiently.<br>

419. [ Neptune ] Neptune does not have native cross-region replication. What is the recommended DR strategy for a Neptune cluster? ; Neptune DR strategy: 1) Enable automated snapshots (PITR up to 35 days); 2) Copy snapshots to a secondary region using `copy-db-cluster-snapshot` with cross-region option; 3) In case of region failure, restore from the cross-region snapshot to a new cluster in the secondary region. RTO: 30-60 minutes. RPO: hours (last snapshot). <br><br> *Automated daily snapshots provide the baseline. For lower RPO: use Neptune Streams → Kinesis → Lambda → replay events in secondary region's Neptune cluster. This is a custom CDC approach similar to building a replica manually.<br> *Neptune Streams + cross-region replay: export changes as they happen, replay them in secondary region. This approaches near-real-time replication. However, it adds operational complexity and must handle ordering and idempotency.<br> *Compare with Aurora Global Database: Aurora has managed <1 sec cross-region replication. Neptune lacks this native capability. For graph workloads requiring strict cross-region DR with low RPO: evaluate whether Neptune is the right choice, or consider modeling the graph in a different store with better cross-region support.<br>

420. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune Bulk Loader, and what format does it require? ; Neptune Bulk Loader is a REST API that loads graph data from S3 into Neptune in bulk — much faster than inserting individual vertices/edges via Gremlin/SPARQL. Requires: S3 bucket in same region, IAM role for Neptune to access S3, data in Neptune CSV format (separate vertex file and edge file). <br><br> *Neptune CSV vertex file format: header row `~id,~label,property1:type,property2:type,...` then rows for each vertex. Neptune CSV edge file: `~id,~from,~to,~label,property1:type,...`. The `~` prefix is Neptune-specific metadata.<br> *Bulk Loader status: it's asynchronous — initiate with a POST, then poll the status endpoint to check progress. Large graphs (hundreds of millions of vertices/edges) can take hours to load.<br> *Prerequisite: Neptune must have an IAM role with `s3:GetObject` permission attached. Enable enhanced VPC routing if the S3 bucket is in a VPC. Bulk Loader errors are logged in S3 — check the error log file if load fails.<br>

421. [ Neptune ] What monitoring should you set up for a Neptune cluster? ; Key CloudWatch metrics: GremlinRequestsPerSec (query throughput), GremlinError4xx/5xx (client/server errors), CPUUtilization (high = query optimization needed), NetworkThroughput, FreeLocalStorage. Set alarms on: CPUUtilization >80%, AuroraReplicaLag >100ms (replica lag), FreeLocalStorage <10%. <br><br> *Neptune audit logs: enable via cluster parameter group (`neptune_enable_audit_log=1`). Logs all Gremlin/SPARQL queries to CloudWatch Logs. Essential for security auditing and query analysis.<br> *Neptune profiler: similar to RDS Slow Query Log — enable to capture queries exceeding a threshold duration. Use to identify expensive traversals that need optimization (index additions, query rewrite).<br> *CloudTrail for API-level audit: captures all Neptune API calls (cluster creation, modification, snapshot operations). Neptune queries themselves go to audit logs, not CloudTrail.<br>

422. [ Neptune ] What are Neptune property graph indexes, and how do they improve query performance? ; Neptune automatically creates indexes for Gremlin and openCypher queries. Property indexes: Neptune can index vertex and edge properties for faster lookups. Without index: a traversal filtering by property value requires scanning all vertices/edges. With index: Neptune jumps directly to matching vertices/edges. <br><br> *Neptune creates 4 indexes by default for SPARQL (SPOG, POGS, GPSO, GOPS — different permutations of Subject-Predicate-Object-Graph) to optimize different query patterns. These cannot be disabled.<br> *For property graph: Neptune creates indexes on properties when you declare them. Use `neptune-ml:enableIndexing` annotation or create indexes explicitly. Check Gremlin explain output (`explain().step()`) to see if queries use indexes.<br> *Index tradeoff: indexes speed up reads but slow down writes (index must be updated on every vertex/edge insert or property update). For write-heavy ingest: consider disabling some indexes during bulk load, then re-enable. Neptune Bulk Loader has a `queueRequest` mode that processes after load.<br>

423. [ Neptune ] Neptune is deployed in a private VPC. How does a Lambda function in the same VPC query Neptune? ; Lambda must be configured to run inside the same VPC as Neptune (VPC configuration on Lambda function: select the VPC + subnets + security group). Lambda SG must be allowed inbound access to Neptune SG on port 8182. Lambda calls Neptune via HTTPS using the cluster or reader endpoint. <br><br> *Neptune endpoint format: `https://your-cluster.cluster-XXXXX.region.neptune.amazonaws.com:8182/gremlin`. Use the Gremlin WebSocket URL for traversals, or HTTPS for SPARQL queries and REST endpoints.<br> *Lambda cold starts in VPC: VPC-attached Lambda has slightly longer cold starts (ENI allocation). Use Provisioned Concurrency for latency-sensitive Neptune queries.<br> *If Lambda is in a different VPC: use VPC peering between Lambda VPC and Neptune VPC, or PrivateLink (Neptune doesn't support PrivateLink natively — use NLB + PrivateLink pattern in front of Neptune). Simpler: put Lambda and Neptune in the same VPC.<br>


424. [ ElastiCache ] What is the difference between Redis Cluster Mode Enabled and Cluster Mode Disabled in ElastiCache? ; Cluster Mode Disabled (CMD): single shard, all data on each node; max data = one node's RAM; supports up to 5 replicas; simple configuration. Cluster Mode Enabled (CME): data sharded across 1-500 shards; each shard has primary + up to 5 replicas; total capacity = sum of all node RAM across shards. <br><br> *CMD is simpler but RAM-limited — if your working set exceeds a single node's memory (e.g., r6g.12xlarge = ~384 GB), you must use CME to scale horizontally.<br> *CME online resharding: add or remove shards without downtime. Data rebalanced automatically. This is the key operational advantage — scale the cache without taking it offline.<br> *CME application requirement: application must use the configuration endpoint (not individual node endpoints) and use a Redis Cluster client (e.g., Jedis cluster mode, redis-py cluster). Regular Redis clients that don't understand cluster routing will fail with MOVED errors.<br>

425. [ ElastiCache ] What is the difference between ElastiCache Redis AUTH and RBAC? ; AUTH: single password for the entire Redis instance — one token, all users get same access. RBAC (Redis 6+): multiple user accounts, each with specific permissions (which commands they can run, which key patterns they can access). RBAC is fine-grained; AUTH is coarse-grained. <br><br> *RBAC use case: application uses a read-only user (only GET/LRANGE/SMEMBERS commands); administrator uses an admin user (all commands including CONFIG/DEBUG). Principle of least privilege applied to Redis.<br> *RBAC configuration: create user group → attach to ElastiCache cluster; create users with username + password + permissions → add to group. Users authenticate with `AUTH username password`.<br> *Exam: "company wants to restrict access to certain Redis commands for the application tier" → RBAC. "Simple password protection" → AUTH. "No authentication" → not recommended for production.<br>

426. [ ElastiCache ] A company uses ElastiCache Memcached. Their application requires high availability with automatic failover. Is this possible? ; No — Memcached does NOT support replication or automatic failover. Each Memcached node is independent. If a node fails, its cached data is lost — the application must re-fetch from the backend database (cache miss). For HA with automatic failover: switch to ElastiCache Redis, which supports Multi-AZ replication and automatic primary failover. <br><br> *Memcached nodes across AZs provide limited fault tolerance — if AZ-a node fails, AZ-b and AZ-c nodes still serve their portion of the cache. But the AZ-a node's data is gone (cache miss for those keys).<br> *The "consistent hashing" in Memcached distributes keys across nodes — when a node fails, ~1/N of cache keys are affected (N = number of nodes).
