Applications see increased cache miss rate for affected keys only.<br> *Migration path: Memcached → Redis is not automatic (different data models, different protocols). Cold migration: stop using Memcached, warm up Redis cache by allowing cache misses to populate from DB. Application connection string change to Redis endpoint.<br>

427. [ ElastiCache ] How does ElastiCache Redis handle a primary node failure in a Multi-AZ cluster? ; ElastiCache detects primary failure (typically within 10-30 seconds), promotes the replica with the lowest replication lag to primary, updates the primary endpoint DNS to the new primary's IP, and deletes the old primary. Application connections to the primary endpoint are briefly interrupted (~30-60 sec total). <br><br> *Applications must use the primary endpoint DNS hostname (not hardcoded IP) to benefit from automatic failover. The DNS TTL is set low (~10 sec) to ensure fast propagation.<br> *If primary failure during a write: writes in flight may be lost (async replication means replicas may be slightly behind). Set `min-replicas-to-write` and `min-replicas-max-lag` to require acknowledgment from replicas before confirming writes — at the cost of write latency.<br> *RDS Proxy equivalent for Redis: no managed proxy exists for ElastiCache. Application connection retry logic must handle the ~30-60 sec failover window. Use exponential backoff in the application retry loop.<br>

428. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache for Redis Online Migration feature, and when would you use it? ; Online Migration migrates data from a self-managed Redis server (on-premises or EC2) to ElastiCache Redis with minimal downtime. It uses Redis replication — ElastiCache cluster acts as a replica of the source, syncs all data, then you cut over by redirecting application traffic. <br><br> *Migration steps: 1) source Redis must allow ElastiCache to connect (security group/firewall), 2) start Online Migration (ElastiCache becomes a slave of source), 3) allow full sync to complete, 4) monitor replication lag drops to ~0, 5) redirect application to ElastiCache endpoint, 6) source can then be decommissioned.<br> *Limitations: source Redis version must be compatible with target ElastiCache version. Source must have `requirepass` disabled or ElastiCache must be configured with the correct password. Source must allow inbound from ElastiCache replication IPs.<br> *Downtime: near-zero — the cutover step (update DNS/connection string) is the only interruption. Clients in the same VPC reconnect within milliseconds. External clients: depends on DNS TTL.<br>

429. [ ElastiCache ] What is the caching pattern "Write-Through" and when does it differ from "Cache-Aside" (Lazy Loading)? ; Cache-Aside (Lazy Loading): app checks cache first; on miss, loads from DB and writes to cache. Cache is only populated on cache misses. Write-Through: every DB write simultaneously writes to cache — cache is always up to date, no stale data, but adds latency to writes. <br><br> *Cache-Aside pros: cache only contains requested data (no wasteful pre-loading of unused data). Cache-Aside cons: first request always hits DB (cold cache on restart); potential stale data if DB is updated without invalidating cache.<br> *Write-Through pros: cache always fresh (no stale data window). Write-Through cons: write latency increases (two writes per operation — DB + cache); cache may hold data never read (wasted memory for write-heavy but not read-heavy data).<br> *Hybrid strategy (most production systems): Write-Through for critical frequently-read data (user sessions, product prices); Cache-Aside for long-tail data (old articles, historical records). Set appropriate TTL for all cache entries regardless of strategy to prevent stale data accumulation.<br>

430. [ ElastiCache ] What ElastiCache metrics should you monitor for a Redis cluster serving a read-heavy application? ; Key metrics: CacheHits / CacheMisses → CacheHitRate (target >85-90%); Evictions (non-zero = cache is full, items being evicted before TTL expiry → add memory); CurrConnections (monitor for connection leaks); ReplicationLag (replica lag); CPUUtilization (high engine CPU = CPU bottleneck, not just memory). <br><br> *Evictions: when Redis hits `maxmemory`, it evicts items based on `maxmemory-policy` (allkeys-lru, volatile-lru, etc.). Evictions mean valuable data is being removed before TTL — either increase node size (more RAM) or review TTL settings (shorter TTL = faster eviction before memory pressure).<br> *EngineCPUUtilization vs CPUUtilization: Redis is single-threaded for commands — EngineCPUUtilization shows the Redis process CPU. Regular CPUUtilization includes background processes. For CPU bottleneck diagnosis, use EngineCPUUtilization. If >80%: optimize client-side (pipelining, reduce round trips) or shard more aggressively (CME with more shards).<br> *SwapUsage: if Redis is using swap memory, it's severely under-provisioned — performance degrades dramatically when data spills to disk. SwapUsage should always be 0 for in-memory cache. Alert at any non-zero value.<br>

431. [ ElastiCache ] What is the purpose of the ElastiCache `maxmemory-policy` setting, and what are the options? ; `maxmemory-policy` determines what Redis does when it runs out of memory and needs to evict items. Options: `noeviction` (return error on write — prevents data loss but application gets errors), `allkeys-lru` (evict least-recently-used key regardless of TTL), `volatile-lru` (evict LRU key only from keys with TTL set), `allkeys-random` (random eviction), `volatile-random`, `allkeys-lfu` (least-frequently-used). <br><br> *For pure cache (where all data is re-fetchable from DB): use `allkeys-lru` — the cache evicts least-recently-used items to make room, keeping hot data. This is the most common setting for session caches and data caches.<br> *For mixed cache+durable data (some keys must never be evicted): set `volatile-lru` — only keys WITH TTL are eligible for eviction. Keys without TTL (your durable data) are never evicted. But: if all keys have TTL, this behaves like allkeys-lru.<br> *`noeviction` for Redis as a message queue or primary store: you want errors when full, not silent data loss. Application must handle the error and take action (scale up, purge old data explicitly).<br>


432. [ DocumentDB ] What MongoDB features are NOT supported in Amazon DocumentDB? ; Not supported: MongoDB Atlas Search, Change Streams (limited support — DocumentDB Streams instead), MongoDB 5.0+ features (aggregation operators added in v5), certain aggregation pipeline stages, `$text` full-text search (use Atlas Search on MongoDB Atlas instead), multi-document distributed transactions across collections. <br><br> *MongoDB version compatibility: DocumentDB supports MongoDB 3.6, 4.0, and 5.0 API levels. Applications using MongoDB 6.0+ features will fail on DocumentDB.<br> *Text search workaround: use OpenSearch (Amazon OpenSearch Service) alongside DocumentDB — write to DocumentDB via Streams → Lambda → index in OpenSearch for full-text search. This is the AWS recommended pattern for DocumentDB + full-text search.<br> *Before migrating to DocumentDB: run the compatibility script against your application code and MongoDB queries. AWS provides a `mtools` compatibility checker and the official [DocumentDB compatibility guide](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/documentdb/latest/developerguide/mongo-apis.html).<br>

433. [ DocumentDB ] How does AWS DMS support migration from MongoDB to DocumentDB? ; DMS supports MongoDB as a source (Full Load + CDC) and DocumentDB as a target. Full Load: DMS reads MongoDB collections and writes to DocumentDB. CDC: DMS reads MongoDB Change Streams (v4.0+ required for CDC) to capture ongoing changes. For minimal downtime: Full Load + CDC → validate → cutover. <br><br> *MongoDB CDC requires MongoDB 4.0+ (Change Streams support) or MongoDB replica set/sharded cluster (Change Streams require a replica set). Standalone MongoDB cannot do CDC with DMS.<br> *Document structure: DMS migrates BSON documents as-is to DocumentDB (same data model). No schema conversion needed. However, MongoDB indexes must be recreated manually on DocumentDB after migration.<br> *Large migrations: use parallel Full Load (configure `ParallelLoadQueuesPerThread` and collection segmentation). For migrations >500 GB, consider using `mongodump`/`mongorestore` for faster initial load, then DMS CDC for ongoing changes.<br>

434. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB Global Clusters feature, and how does it compare to Aurora Global Database? ; DocumentDB Global Clusters (preview/GA depending on region): primary cluster in one region + read-only secondary clusters in up to 5 regions; storage-level replication (<1 sec lag); managed failover. Very similar architecture to Aurora Global Database. <br><br> *DocumentDB Global Clusters use the same distributed storage architecture as Aurora — storage-level replication is inherently fast. Secondary clusters are read-only; failover promotes a secondary to primary in ~1 minute.<br> *Not available in all regions — check current AWS documentation for regional availability. Where available, Global Clusters is the recommended approach for DocumentDB cross-region DR, replacing the old snapshot-restore approach.<br> *Where Global Clusters is NOT available: use DocumentDB Streams → Kinesis → Lambda → write to secondary DocumentDB cluster as a custom replication solution, similar to the Neptune cross-region approach.<br>

435. [ DocumentDB ] What is the maximum document size in DocumentDB, and how does it compare to MongoDB? ; DocumentDB maximum document size: 16 MB per document (same as MongoDB). Both MongoDB and DocumentDB enforce the 16 MB BSON document limit. This is a well-known limitation for both systems — store large files in S3 and reference them in DocumentDB. <br><br> *16 MB is actually quite large for a document database — a typical MongoDB/DocumentDB document is 1-100 KB. The 16 MB limit is rarely hit in practice unless storing embedded binary data (which should be in S3 anyway).<br> *DocumentDB throughput: measured in RCU/WCU (similar to Aurora, not DynamoDB). Performance depends on instance class and storage I/O. Monitor `DatabaseConnections`, `WriteLatency`, `ReadLatency` in CloudWatch.<br> *For very large documents (images, PDFs, video metadata): S3 + DocumentDB pointer pattern — store the file in S3, store metadata + S3 key in DocumentDB. DocumentDB handles fast queries; S3 handles large binary storage efficiently and cheaply.<br>

436. [ DocumentDB ] How do you enable and use DocumentDB change streams? ; DocumentDB Change Streams (different from MongoDB Change Streams) is DocumentDB's native CDC feature. Enable per collection: `db.runCommand({modifyChangeStreams: 1, database: "myDB", collection: "myCollection", enable: true})`. Change stream records retained up to 7 days. Consume via: DocumentDB change stream API (pull-based) OR emit to Lambda/Kinesis. <br><br> *DocumentDB Change Streams ≠ MongoDB Change Streams at the API level — DocumentDB Change Streams uses a different API (`describe_events` endpoint style). Applications built for MongoDB Change Streams may need modification to use DocumentDB Change Streams.<br> *Common pattern: DocumentDB Change Streams → Lambda (polls the stream) → OpenSearch for search indexing, or → Kinesis Data Firehose → S3 for analytics. This enables the "event sourcing" pattern with DocumentDB as the write store.<br> *Limitation: Change Streams only available for DocumentDB 4.0+ API compatibility clusters. Streams are per-collection, not per-database. Must be explicitly enabled per collection.<br>

437. [ DocumentDB ] A company is deciding between DocumentDB and DynamoDB for a product catalog. What factors determine the choice? ; Choose DocumentDB when: rich document queries (complex filter by nested attributes, arrays), MongoDB migration (existing MongoDB code), complex aggregation pipelines, documents have variable-depth nested structures. Choose DynamoDB when: access patterns are key-value (get by product ID), massive scale (millions of products), serverless/unpredictable load, global multi-region active-active writes. <br><br> *Product catalog typical access: "get product by ID" (DynamoDB wins — single-key lookup), "find all products in category with price<100 sorted by rating" (DocumentDB wins — requires index on category + filter + sort, DocumentDB handles naturally with compound index).<br> *Schema flexibility: DynamoDB is schemaless at the table level (each item can have different attributes) but limited query flexibility (must query by PK/SK or GSI). DocumentDB is schemaless at the collection level and allows querying on any nested attribute with indexes.<br> *Migration consideration: if product catalog data is already in MongoDB: DocumentDB is the natural choice (compatible API, DMS migration). If building from scratch on AWS with simple access patterns: DynamoDB is cheaper and more scalable.<br>

438. [ DocumentDB ] What are DocumentDB instance-based clusters vs elastic clusters? ; Instance-based cluster: fixed instance classes (r6g, r5, t3); you provision specific instances; scale by changing instance type or adding replicas. Elastic cluster (newer): serverless-like — auto-scales underlying compute; uses sharding to distribute data; no specific instance management; pay per shard and storage. <br><br> *Elastic clusters: designed for workloads needing massive horizontal scaling (millions of documents/second). Uses multi-master sharded architecture — write to any shard. Good for high-throughput document writes.<br> *Instance-based clusters: better for workloads with predictable load, existing MongoDB applications (easier compatibility testing), Reserved Instance discounts. Elastic clusters: better for massive scale, variable load, or when sharded MongoDB architecture is already expected.<br> *Feature parity: elastic clusters don't support all features of instance-based clusters (check AWS docs for current feature matrix). Instance-based is the more mature option with broader feature support as of late 2026.<br>


439. [ QLDB ] What is the fundamental difference between QLDB and a traditional database with audit logging? ; Traditional audit logging is mutable — a database administrator can modify or delete audit logs. QLDB's journal is cryptographically immutable — each block contains a hash of the previous block; modifying any historical record breaks the hash chain and is mathematically detectable. QLDB's integrity is self-verifiable. <br><br> *In a traditional DB: you write a trigger or CDC log to an "audit" table. But anyone with DB admin access can `DELETE FROM audit_log WHERE ...` — the audit trail is not tamper-proof. QLDB's journal cannot be deleted (not even by the account owner).<br> *Verification: QLDB provides a `getDigest` API (SHA-256 hash of the entire journal up to a point in time) and `getRevision` API (Merkle tree proof for a specific document revision). Third-party auditors can independently verify that QLDB data has not been altered.<br> *Regulatory use case: financial services regulators may require auditable, tamper-resistant records. QLDB satisfies this requirement at the database level — no additional audit infrastructure needed. Compare with BlockChain: QLDB is centralized (trusted single owner), blockchain is decentralized (multiple untrusted parties).<br>

440. [ QLDB ] What PartiQL operations are supported in QLDB, and what is NOT supported? ; Supported: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE (on current state tables). History function: `SELECT * FROM history(tableName)` (read history of a document). NOT supported: stored procedures, triggers, complex joins across tables, GROUP BY in early versions (check current docs), DDL beyond CREATE/DROP table. <br><br> *QLDB is not a full-featured relational DB — PartiQL in QLDB is a subset optimized for document-oriented queries and ledger operations. Complex analytical queries should be done by exporting the journal to S3 and querying with Athena.<br> *`history()` function is QLDB-specific — it queries the journal for all revisions of a document. Returns metadata including `txId`, `txTime`, `hash` for each revision. This is the primary way to audit changes to a specific record.<br> *Transactions: QLDB supports ACID transactions (atomicity for multi-table operations within a session). All operations in a QLDB transaction are committed atomically to the journal — either all succeed or none appear in the journal.<br>

441. [ QLDB ] What is a QLDB Ledger Digest, and how is it used for verification? ; A QLDB Digest is a cryptographic hash (SHA-256) of the entire journal contents up to a specific block sequence number and time. You call `getDigest` → QLDB returns the digest hash + the tip address (last block). Store this digest externally (email, cold storage). Later, to verify: call `getDigest` again → if any historical record was modified, the new digest will not match the stored one. <br><br> *Digest verification process: `getDigest` → save the {digest, tipAddress}. To verify a specific document revision: `getRevision` for that revision → QLDB returns the revision hash + a Merkle tree proof → verify the Merkle proof against the stored digest. This mathematically proves the revision is part of the unaltered journal.<br> *Practical use: regulators or auditors store digests at month-end. At audit time, they verify current digest against stored digest — instant confirmation that no records were altered. No need to read every record, just verify the hash.<br> *Third-party verification: because QLDB's cryptographic proof is mathematical, a third party who doesn't trust the account owner can independently verify that QLDB data was not altered — without AWS providing any additional trust. This is the core value proposition.<br>

442. [ QLDB ] QLDB is a public endpoint service, not deployed in a VPC. What are the security implications, and how do you mitigate them? ; QLDB is accessed via HTTPS public endpoints — traffic is encrypted in transit (TLS) and authenticated via IAM. Mitigation: (1) IAM resource policies restrict which principals can call QLDB APIs, (2) VPC Interface Endpoint for QLDB (available in some regions) routes traffic through AWS PrivateLink (no internet), (3) use IAM policies with `aws:SourceVpc` condition to restrict calls from specific VPCs only. <br><br> *Even without VPC deployment, QLDB traffic is always TLS-encrypted — the concern is not encryption but network exposure. With IAM auth required (no DB-level credentials), there's no password to steal; only IAM credentials grant access.<br> *`aws:SourceVpc` condition in IAM policy: denies QLDB API calls unless they originate from a specified VPC (requires VPC endpoint). This effectively restricts QLDB access to your VPC even though QLDB itself isn't in the VPC.<br> *CloudTrail logs all QLDB API calls — `SendCommand` (PartiQL operations), `CreateLedger`, `GetDigest`. These are the primary operations to monitor for unauthorized access. Enable CloudTrail in all regions.<br>

443. [ QLDB ] What is the difference between QLDB and Amazon Managed Blockchain? When would you use each? ; QLDB: centralized, single-owner, AWS-managed immutable ledger with cryptographic verification. Fast (<10ms latency). No consensus mechanism. Managed Blockchain: distributed ledger for multiple organizations that don't trust each other; uses Hyperledger Fabric or Ethereum consensus; slower (hundreds of ms); multi-party governance. <br><br> *Key question: "Who do you trust?" If you trust yourself (single company maintaining a ledger for their customers): QLDB. If multiple companies need to share and verify a ledger without trusting each other: Managed Blockchain.<br> *Use QLDB: bank ledger (bank trusts itself; customers trust the bank), supply chain tracking within one company, medical records within one health system, car title registry (government-owned). QLDB is much simpler to operate and 10-100× faster than blockchain.<br> *Use Managed Blockchain: cross-company supply chain (multiple manufacturers + distributors + retailers, none trusts the others), consortium banking (multiple banks settling transactions), pharmaceutical track-and-trace (manufacturer + distributor + pharmacy each verify).<br>

444. [ QLDB ] How do you implement cross-region DR for QLDB since it has no native cross-region replication? ; Export QLDB journal to S3 periodically → copy S3 to secondary region → in disaster: restore from S3 data by replaying journal blocks into a new QLDB ledger. For near-real-time: use QLDB Streams → Kinesis Data Streams → Lambda → write to secondary region QLDB ledger. RPO: depends on stream lag (seconds); RTO: minutes (Lambda replay speed). <br><br> *Journal export: `ExportJournalToS3` API exports journal blocks in ION format to S3. This is a complete, verifiable copy of all transactions. In a DR scenario: create new QLDB ledger → replay exported journal blocks → new ledger reflects all historical transactions.<br> *QLDB Streams approach: real-time stream of journal changes → Kinesis → Lambda writes each transaction to secondary region QLDB. The secondary QLDB is a continuously updated replica. Cutover: redirect application to secondary region endpoint.<br> *Note: replaying journal into a secondary QLDB creates a NEW ledger with its own hash chain — the cryptographic chain from the primary is not preserved in the replica. For forensic purposes, preserve the original S3 journal export as the canonical tamper-proof copy.<br>

445. [ QLDB ] What document revisions does QLDB store, and how do you access historical data? ; QLDB stores ALL revisions of every document in the journal, forever (the journal is immutable and never purged). Current state table shows only the latest revision. To access historical data: use `history()` function in PartiQL. To access a specific block/revision: use `GetBlock` or `GetRevision` API. <br><br> *`SELECT * FROM history(Vehicles, `2026-01-01`, `2026-10-30`) AS h WHERE h.data.VIN = 'ABC123'` — returns all revisions of VIN ABC123 between those dates, with metadata (txId, txTime, hash).<br> *Metadata on each document: `metadata.id` (document ID), `metadata.version` (revision number starting at 0), `metadata.txTime` (transaction commit time), `metadata.txId` (transaction ID). Use `metadata.id` to query history for a specific document across all its revisions.<br> *Journal retention: QLDB never deletes journal entries — there is no retention policy for the journal (unlike CloudTrail which has max 90 days by default). The journal grows indefinitely. Export to S3 for long-term archiving if QLDB cost becomes a concern for very old data.<br>

446. [ QLDB ] What is the difference between a QLDB table's "current state" and the "journal"? ; Current state: a queryable view of the LATEST revision of each document in each table. Supports standard PartiQL SELECT/UPDATE/DELETE. Journal: append-only, immutable log of ALL committed transactions (all revisions of all documents). The current state is DERIVED from the journal — if the journal is the source of truth, the current state is a materialized view of it. <br><br> *If the current state table is dropped: the journal entries remain. You could theoretically rebuild the current state by replaying the journal. The journal is the canonical record; the current state is a convenience view for queries.<br> *INSERT: creates revision 0 in journal, adds document to current state. UPDATE: creates new revision in journal, updates current state to show new values. DELETE: creates a "tombstone" revision in journal, removes document from current state query results (but history() still shows the deleted document).<br> *This is why QLDB supports "show me this account's balance at any point in history" — the journal has all balance-change transactions; the current state shows today's balance; history() shows every intermediate state.<br>

447. [ QLDB ] What are the QLDB pricing dimensions? ; QLDB pricing: (1) Read I/O (per million read requests), (2) Write I/O (per million write requests — journal writes), (3) Storage (per GB-month for journal + current state). No compute cost (serverless). Data transfer: standard AWS rates for cross-region or internet. Journal export to S3: standard S3 PUT costs. <br><br> *QLDB is serverless — no instance cost, no provisioned capacity, no idle cost. Cost only accrues for actual reads/writes and stored data.<br> *Journal storage is the largest cost driver for long-running ledgers — every revision of every document is stored permanently. A ledger with 10 years of financial transactions may have significant storage cost. Export old journal blocks to S3 (cheaper) and drop from QLDB for cost management.<br> *Write I/O cost: each QLDB write operation writes to the journal AND updates the current state — counted as 2 writes. Plan for 2× write I/O relative to application-visible writes. Read I/O includes both current state reads and history() queries.<br>

448. [ QLDB ] What compliance certifications and regulatory requirements does QLDB help satisfy? ; QLDB helps with: SOX (financial record immutability), HIPAA (medical record audit trail), PCI DSS (cardholder data audit trail), GDPR (right to audit, right to be forgotten — note tension with immutability), FDA 21 CFR Part 11 (electronic record integrity). QLDB is HIPAA eligible and SOC 1/2/3 certified. <br><br> *GDPR tension: GDPR's "right to erasure" conflicts with QLDB's immutability. Mitigation: store only non-PII in QLDB journal; store PII in a separate system that supports deletion; QLDB references the PII by an anonymized ID. On erasure request: delete PII from the separate system; QLDB journal shows the anonymized transaction record only.<br> *FDA 21 CFR Part 11: electronic records must be attributable, legible, contemporaneous, original, accurate. QLDB's immutable journal with timestamps and cryptographic proof satisfies the "original and accurate" requirements. Attributable: combine with IAM authentication logging (CloudTrail shows which IAM principal issued each QLDB operation).<br> *For compliance: always enable CloudTrail for QLDB API calls + application-level logging of who performed what operation (QLDB stores WHAT changed, but not WHO at the application user level — you must combine with your application's user context).<br>


449. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora storage replication quorum model, and what does it mean when Aurora reports "degraded" storage? ; Aurora writes require 4/6 copy acknowledgments (write quorum). If 2 storage nodes fail (AZ failure), only 4/6 remain — writes still succeed (4 ≥ quorum). Aurora "degraded" status means 1 or more storage nodes are unhealthy — Aurora continues operating but AWS is working to replace the failed node(s). Reads still serve with 3/6 quorum. <br><br> *Aurora "degraded" does NOT mean the DB is unavailable — it means the storage safety margin has reduced. AWS monitors and automatically replaces failed storage nodes (typically within minutes).<br> *If 3 storage nodes fail (worst case, across multiple AZs): write quorum is lost (only 3/6 remain, need 4). Aurora enters read-only mode. AWS alerts and replaces nodes rapidly. This scenario is extremely rare.<br> *This quorum model is why Aurora has higher availability SLA than RDS — RDS Multi-AZ has 1 standby; Aurora has 6-copy distributed storage inherently tolerant to multi-node failures.<br>

450. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's maximum connection limit, and how is it calculated? ; Aurora's max_connections is determined by the instance class RAM: approximately `(DBInstanceClassMemory / 12582880)`. For r6g.large (16 GB RAM): ~1312 connections. For r6g.4xlarge (128 GB): ~10500 connections. RDS Proxy can pool many application connections to a smaller Aurora instance. <br><br> *The formula varies by engine version and instance type — check the AWS documentation for exact values. The general principle: larger instance = more memory = more connections supported.<br> *For Lambda workloads needing >5000 concurrent connections: use RDS Proxy (pools connections) or an r6g.4xlarge+ instance. Lambda at 5000 concurrent executions × 1 connection each = 5000 connections needed — exceeds smaller instances.<br> *Aurora Serverless v2 dynamically adjusts max connections based on current ACU (memory allocation). As v2 scales up ACUs, max_connections scales proportionally — good alignment of connection capacity with compute capacity.<br>

451. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS "maintenance window" and "backup window"? ; Backup window: daily time window during which RDS takes an automated snapshot. Duration 30 minutes minimum. I/O is briefly suspended during snapshot (for single-AZ; Multi-AZ takes snapshot from standby — no I/O impact on primary). Maintenance window: weekly 30-minute window for OS/DB patches and AWS-initiated maintenance tasks. <br><br> *Backup window and maintenance window should not overlap — if they do, AWS defers the backup to after maintenance. Set them both in low-traffic hours but at different times (e.g., backup at 3:00 AM, maintenance at 4:00 AM).<br> *For Multi-AZ: backup is taken from the standby — no I/O suspension on primary. For Single-AZ: brief I/O suspension during snapshot — set backup window during low-traffic to minimize impact.<br> *Both windows are configurable. Default: AWS selects a random maintenance window and a random backup window within your region's off-peak hours. Change both to ensure control over when maintenance and backups occur.<br>

452. [ RDS ] How does RDS handle minor version upgrades vs major version upgrades? ; Minor version upgrades (e.g., PostgreSQL 14.8 → 14.9): can be applied automatically with "Auto Minor Version Upgrade" enabled; applied during maintenance window; brief restart. Major version upgrades (e.g., PG 14 → 15): never automatic; require manual initiation via Modify; tested for compatibility first; use Blue/Green for safe rollout. <br><br> *Auto Minor Version Upgrade: enabled by default for new RDS instances. Best practice: enable for non-production, disable for production (test minor versions first). AWS tests minor versions before release but surprises can still occur.<br> *Major version upgrade path: not all versions can be upgraded directly. Some require intermediate steps (e.g., MySQL 5.7 → 8.0 directly is supported; PG 12 → 15 may require 12 → 13 → 14 → 15). Check the upgrade path in AWS RDS console before initiating.<br> *RDS Blue/Green for major upgrades: create a green environment with the new major version, DMS or native replication keeps it current, test on green, then switch. This is the safest upgrade path — zero-data-risk approach.<br>

453. [ DynamoDB ] What is the maximum number of items per DynamoDB transaction, and what happens when you exceed it? ; DynamoDB `TransactWriteItems` and `TransactGetItems` support a maximum of 100 items per transaction. If you try to include 101+ items: AWS returns a `ValidationException` ("Too many items requested for the BatchWriteItem call" or "Transaction request cannot include multiple operations on one item"). <br><br> *100-item limit is per transaction API call — not per session. You can run multiple transactions sequentially, but each must be ≤100 items. For >100-item atomic operations: redesign the data model (aggregate multiple items into one, use a saga pattern with compensation).<br> *4 MB total size limit: even if you have <100 items, the total serialized size cannot exceed 4 MB. Large items (say, 100 KB each × 40 items = 4 MB) will hit this before the 100-item limit.<br> *For financial systems needing >100-item atomicity: consider Aurora (SQL transactions can span thousands of rows) or design the DynamoDB model so each transaction touches fewer items (denormalization, event sourcing with aggregates).<br>

454. [ DynamoDB ] What is adaptive capacity in DynamoDB, and how does it help with uneven traffic patterns? ; Adaptive Capacity automatically shifts the capacity allocation within a table to "hot" partitions that are consuming more throughput than their fair share. Instead of each partition getting exactly 1/N of total capacity, adaptive capacity loans excess capacity from under-used partitions to busy ones in real-time. <br><br> *Before adaptive capacity: if partition A is hot (using 100% of its share) and partitions B, C are idle, partition A gets throttled even though aggregate table capacity is available. With adaptive capacity: B and C's idle capacity flows to A automatically.<br> *Adaptive capacity is automatic — no configuration needed. It was introduced to handle temporary hot-spot scenarios without requiring table redesign. However, it cannot solve persistent hot partitions (a key that is ALWAYS hot).<br> *For persistent hot partitions: redesign the partition key to distribute traffic (add random suffix, use a composite key). Adaptive capacity is for transient/temporary hot spots — it's not a substitute for good partition key design.<br>

455. [ Redshift ] What is the purpose of the Redshift ANALYZE command, and when should it be run? ; ANALYZE updates the query optimizer's statistical metadata (column histograms, row counts, data distribution) for a table. After bulk loads or significant data changes: run ANALYZE so the query planner has accurate statistics and chooses optimal query plans. Automatic ANALYZE runs in the background but may lag for very large loads. <br><br> *Stale statistics symptom: after a large data load, queries suddenly use sub-optimal plans (wrong join order, wrong hash vs sort join). Running ANALYZE typically fixes this by refreshing statistics.<br> *`ANALYZE tablename` (specific table) is faster than `ANALYZE` (entire database). Run for tables that received large changes. Include ANALYZE in your ETL pipeline after bulk `COPY` operations.<br> *ANALYZE does NOT modify data — it only reads data to compute statistics. Safe to run on a live cluster. High-frequency ANALYZE (after every small insert) is unnecessary — the cost is CPU/I/O for statistics computation.<br>

456. [ Redshift ] What is the `COPY` command in Redshift, and why is it preferred over `INSERT` for bulk loading? ; `COPY` loads data from S3 (or DynamoDB, EMR) in parallel across all Redshift compute nodes — each node reads a subset of S3 files directly. Massively faster than serial `INSERT` (which goes through the leader node). For 100 GB load: COPY (minutes) vs INSERT (hours). <br><br> *COPY parallelism: Redshift processes S3 files in parallel — optimal when the number of S3 files equals or exceeds the number of slices (number of nodes × slices per node). File size recommendation: 1 MB – 1 GB per file. Too few files = bottleneck; too many tiny files = overhead.<br> *File format recommendation: Parquet or ORC (columnar formats) are fastest for COPY — Redshift can read only needed columns during load. CSV/JSON also work but are row-based (read all columns even if inserting into fewer columns).<br> *COPY with MANIFEST: use a manifest file to specify exactly which S3 files to load. Ensures idempotent loads (same manifest = same files loaded). Use with S3 versioning to prevent loading modified files unexpectedly.<br>

457. [ Neptune ] What is Neptune ML, and how does it integrate graph data with machine learning? ; Neptune ML uses the Deep Graph Library (DGL) and SageMaker to train machine learning models that incorporate graph structure features (node connectivity, neighborhood properties). Accessed via Gremlin/SPARQL with `ml.predict()` queries. Example use: predict whether a user in a fraud network is fraudulent based on graph topology. <br><br> *Traditional ML ignores graph structure — it treats entities as independent samples. Neptune ML incorporates graph neural networks (GNNs) that use connections between nodes as features. Two connected fraud accounts → both scored higher by the GNN.<br> *Neptune ML workflow: 1) export graph data to S3, 2) SageMaker trains GNN model, 3) model endpoints deployed, 4) query Neptune with `ml.predict()` → Neptune calls SageMaker endpoint → returns prediction alongside graph query.<br> *Use cases: fraud detection (predict fraud probability for new account based on connections), recommendation systems (predict user-item ratings from user-item bipartite graph), knowledge graph completion (predict missing relationships).<br>

458. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache Global Datastore feature? ; Global Datastore for Redis enables cross-region replication — one primary cluster in one region, up to 2 secondary read-only clusters in other regions. Replication is async (~1 sec lag). Secondary clusters can be promoted to primary on regional failure. <br><br> *Global Datastore is the ElastiCache answer to Aurora Global Database — cross-region Redis replication with managed failover. Secondary clusters serve local reads (low latency for globally distributed applications).<br> *Failover: promote a secondary to primary using the console/CLI. Applications must update their connection string to the new primary endpoint. There is no automatic failover (unlike Aurora Global Database managed failover) — promotion is a manual step.<br> *Use case: globally distributed session stores (users in US, EU, APAC each hit their regional Redis replica for session reads; writes go to primary region), global leaderboards with local read performance.<br>

459. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB parameter group, and what key parameters should be configured for production? ; DocumentDB uses parameter groups (cluster parameter group + instance parameter group) similar to Aurora. Key production parameters: `tls` (enabled — always require TLS), `profiler` (enabled for slow query capture), `profiler_threshold_ms` (threshold for slow query log), `audit_logs` (enabled for CloudWatch Logs audit). <br><br> *TLS parameter: set `tls=enabled` (default). Setting `tls=disabled` allows unencrypted connections — never do this in production. All MongoDB drivers support TLS; download the Amazon DocumentDB CA certificate bundle for driver configuration.<br> *Profiler: `profiler=enabled` + `profiler_threshold_ms=100` logs queries taking >100ms. Review these logs to identify indexes needed or queries requiring optimization. Similar to MongoDB Profiler / MySQL slow query log.<br> *Audit logs in CloudWatch: `audit_logs=enabled` — sends connection events, authentication, and DDL operations to CloudWatch Logs. Essential for compliance. Be aware: enabling audit logs may increase CloudWatch Logs costs significantly for high-connection-rate clusters.<br>

460. [ QLDB ] How does QLDB pricing differ from RDS pricing, and what workloads is QLDB most cost-effective for? ; QLDB: per I/O operation + storage (no instance cost, serverless). RDS: per instance-hour + storage. QLDB is cost-effective for: low-to-moderate transaction volumes with long-term retention requirements (pay per transaction, not idle capacity), regulatory workloads where the immutability feature justifies the I/O cost. <br><br> *QLDB write I/O: charged per write operation (approximately $0.28 per million write requests). For a ledger doing 100 write transactions/second: 8.64 million writes/day × $0.28 = ~$2.40/day = ~$73/month just for writes. Compare: RDS t3.medium at $0.068/hr = ~$49/month (much cheaper but no immutability).<br> *Storage cost: QLDB journal grows forever (immutable). At $0.30/GB-month for journal storage: a system producing 100 MB/day of journal = ~3 GB/month = ~$0.90/month storage. This grows linearly over years.<br> *QLDB is NOT cost-effective for high-throughput transactional systems (thousands of transactions/second) — RDS/Aurora is cheaper. QLDB is purpose-built for compliance ledger workloads where the immutability + verification feature has business/regulatory value that justifies the premium.<br>

461. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Storage bottleneck and how can you monitor it? ; Aurora storage I/O is shared across all instances in a cluster (writer + all readers). Under very high aggregate I/O load, storage can become the bottleneck. Monitor: `AuroraVolumeBytesLeftTotal` (available storage), `VolumeReadIOPS` and `VolumeWriteIOPS` (aggregate I/O), `StorageNetworkThroughput`. High `BlockedTransactions` or high write latency indicates storage throughput saturation. <br><br> *Unlike RDS (where each instance has its own EBS), Aurora shares storage across all instances. This means readers and writers compete for the same storage bandwidth — a heavy analytics query on a replica can impact write latency on the primary.<br> *Mitigation: dedicated analytics replicas (custom endpoint for analytics, different subnet/AZ from OLTP readers), Aurora I/O-Optimized storage (removes per-I/O billing, same throughput), scale reader instances to distribute read I/O load.<br> *Aurora I/O-Optimized is particularly valuable under storage-bottleneck scenarios — predictable cost regardless of I/O volume, allowing workloads to push I/O aggressively without cost spikes.<br>

462. [ RDS ] What is an RDS DB Subnet Group, and what are the requirements? ; A DB Subnet Group is a collection of subnets (in different AZs) within a VPC that RDS can use when creating DB instances. Requirements: must contain subnets in at least 2 AZs (for Multi-AZ support), subnets should be private (no direct internet route), must be in the same VPC as the application. <br><br> *RDS places the primary instance in one AZ subnet and the standby (Multi-AZ) in another AZ subnet from the group. You don't control which AZ — RDS chooses. To force a specific AZ: use single-AZ deployment and specify the preferred AZ.<br> *The subnet group is shared across all RDS instances in a VPC — you can have one subnet group with all private subnets and use it for all RDS instances. Or create separate groups per environment (prod/dev) for isolation.<br> *Subnets in a DB Subnet Group should NOT have auto-assign public IP enabled — RDS instances in these subnets should be private. The `Publicly Accessible` parameter on the RDS instance separately controls whether a public IP is assigned (set to false for production).<br>

463. [ DynamoDB ] What is the `aws:dynamodb:RequestItems` condition key, and when is it used in IAM policies? ; `aws:dynamodb:RequestItems` is NOT a real IAM condition key. The correct fine-grained condition key for DynamoDB is `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` — restricts access to items whose partition key value matches the IAM principal's identity. Example: `{"dynamodb:LeadingKeys": ["${aws:userId}"]}` — user can only access items where PK = their IAM user ID. <br><br> *`dynamodb:LeadingKeys` enables row-level security in DynamoDB — without it, any authorized user with `dynamodb:GetItem` can access any item in the table. With `LeadingKeys`, they can only access their own items.<br> *Implementation pattern: user_id as partition key, condition: `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` = `${aws:userid}` or `${cognito-identity.amazonaws.com:sub}` (for Cognito). Application users each have their own "partition" in the table — physically isolated at the API call level.<br> *Works with: GetItem, BatchGetItem, PutItem, UpdateItem, DeleteItem, Query. Does NOT work with: Scan (no partition key filter) — Scan should be restricted or blocked via IAM for user-level access.<br>

464. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Serverless RPU, and how should you configure the base RPU setting? ; RPU (Redshift Processing Unit) is the compute unit for Redshift Serverless. Base RPU: minimum compute allocated even when no queries are running. Max RPU: maximum allowed (controls cost ceiling). Each RPU ≈ 2 vCPUs + 16 GB RAM. Recommended starting base: 32-64 RPU for production workloads; 8-16 for dev/test. <br><br> *Setting base RPU too low: queries take longer to start (compute allocated up to base first, then scales). Setting base too high: idle cost increases (you pay for base RPU even when no queries run).<br> *Billing: $0.36/RPU-hour (approximate). 64 RPU × 24 hours × 30 days = $15,552/month if always at 64 RPU. Serverless only charges for actual usage — if queries run 2 hours/day, cost is 64 RPU × 2 hrs × 30 days = $1,382/month.<br> *For intermittent analytics (dashboard queries burst for 30 min morning, 30 min evening): base RPU = 32 (quick start), max RPU = 128 (allow burst). Total monthly cost: ~$700-1500 depending on actual usage. Compare with provisioned: 4-node RA3 cluster = ~$3,400/month (always on).<br>

465. [ Neptune ] What is Neptune openCypher, and how does it compare to Gremlin? ; openCypher is a declarative, SQL-like graph query language developed by Neo4j and open-sourced. Neptune added openCypher support alongside Gremlin. Gremlin: imperative (step-by-step traversal), more flexible for complex traversals, TinkerPop ecosystem. openCypher: declarative (describe WHAT you want, not HOW to traverse), easier for SQL-background developers, MATCH/WHERE/RETURN syntax. <br><br> *openCypher example: `MATCH (u:User)-[:PURCHASED]->(p:Product) WHERE u.city = 'Berlin' RETURN p.name, count(*) ORDER BY count(*) DESC` — find top products purchased by Berlin users. Natural to read for SQL developers.<br> *Gremlin equivalent is more verbose: `g.V().hasLabel('User').has('city','Berlin').out('PURCHASED').groupCount().by('name').order(local).by(values, desc).limit(local,10)` — same query but imperative step-by-step.<br> *Exam context: when a question mentions "Neo4j migration to Neptune" → openCypher (familiar syntax, lower migration effort for Cypher-based applications). When question mentions "Apache TinkerPop" → Gremlin.<br>

466. [ ElastiCache ] What is the difference between ElastiCache for Redis and Amazon MemoryDB for Redis? ; ElastiCache Redis: in-memory cache with optional persistence (RDB/AOF); persistence is best-effort, not primary purpose; designed as a cache. MemoryDB for Redis: durable, Redis-compatible database with a transactional log stored in a Multi-AZ transaction log; designed as a primary database (not just cache); strong consistency + durability guarantees. <br><br> *MemoryDB stores all writes to a distributed, Multi-AZ transaction log before acknowledging — Redis data is the in-memory view, but durability is guaranteed even on total cluster failure (data replayed from the log).<br> *Use ElastiCache Redis when: caching RDS/Aurora data, session storage where re-authentication on cache miss is acceptable, pub/sub messaging. Use MemoryDB when: Redis is the primary database (no backend DB to fall back to), microservices data store, financial/gaming leaderboards requiring durability.<br> *MemoryDB is ~2-3× more expensive than ElastiCache Redis for similar node counts — the durable transaction log has a cost. Only pay for MemoryDB if you truly need Redis as a primary durable database.<br>

467. [ DocumentDB ] What indexes does DocumentDB support, and how do you create a compound index? ; DocumentDB supports: single-field indexes (ascending/descending), compound indexes (multiple fields), multikey indexes (on array fields), text indexes (limited), 2dsphere (geospatial). Create compound: `db.collection.createIndex({fieldA: 1, fieldB: -1})`. Index on nested field: `db.collection.createIndex({"address.city": 1})`. <br><br> *Compound index prefix rule: a compound index on (A, B, C) supports queries on (A), (A,B), (A,B,C) but NOT (B), (C), or (B,C) — queries must use the index prefix. Design compound indexes to match the most common query filters.<br> *DocumentDB auto-creates index on `_id` field (primary key). Additional indexes are optional but essential for performance — without indexes, DocumentDB performs collection scans (reads every document). Monitor with `db.collection.explain()` to check if indexes are used.<br> *Background vs foreground index builds: DocumentDB supports background index creation (does not block reads/writes). Always use background builds in production. Foreground builds are faster but lock the collection.<br>

468. [ QLDB ] A financial services company needs to prove to regulators that no financial transaction has been altered. What QLDB feature enables this? ; QLDB cryptographic verification: use `GetDigest` API to get a hash of the entire journal at a point in time → store the digest externally → use `GetRevision` API with Merkle proof to prove any specific transaction is part of the unaltered journal. Present the digest + proof to regulators as cryptographic evidence. <br><br> *The Merkle tree proof: QLDB returns a path of hashes from the specific transaction to the journal digest. Any third party can independently verify the proof using SHA-256 — no need to trust AWS or the company's claims.<br> *For automated compliance reporting: call `GetDigest` at end of each business day → store in immutable external store (S3 with Object Lock, or email to regulator) → monthly audit compares stored digests with current digest → any discrepancy proves tampering.<br> *AWS provides QLDB SDK with verification utilities (Python, Java, Node.js) — you don't need to implement the Merkle verification manually. Use the provided `verify_document()` function with the stored digest and revision proof.<br>

469. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora PostgreSQL logical replication, and what is it used for? ; Aurora PostgreSQL logical replication uses the PostgreSQL logical replication protocol (via publication/subscription model or pglogical extension) to replicate changes to external targets. Use cases: replicate to external data warehouse, migrate data to another DB with CDC, replicate specific tables cross-region. <br><br> *Logical replication vs physical (Aurora's storage replication): logical = table-level, SQL-visible changes (INSERTs, UPDATEs, DELETEs). Physical = block-level storage replication (Aurora's internal storage). Logical replication goes through the PG engine; physical bypasses it.<br> *Enable logical replication: set `rds.logical_replication = 1` in parameter group, set `wal_level = logical`. This enables Aurora to publish changes that external subscribers can consume.<br> *DMS uses logical replication under the hood for Aurora PostgreSQL CDC (reads WAL via logical replication slot). A misconfigured replication slot left active can cause WAL accumulation and storage growth — monitor `replication_slot_active` and clean up unused slots.<br>

470. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS for PostgreSQL's max_connections and Aurora PostgreSQL's max_connections? ; RDS PostgreSQL max_connections: fixed formula based on RAM. Default: `LEAST({DBInstanceClassMemory/9531392}, 5000)`. Aurora PostgreSQL: `LEAST({DBInstanceClassMemory/9531392}, 5000)` but Aurora's formula uses a slightly higher divisor for larger instances. In practice: same formula, similar values. Both can be increased with RDS Proxy. <br><br> *Important difference: Aurora PostgreSQL connections include both direct connections and connections from Aurora Replicas (replicas share storage but have their own connection pools). Each replica's connections count against its own instance limit, not the primary's.<br> *Connection management is a key exam topic because Lambda × Aurora = connection exhaustion scenario. Solution: RDS Proxy. For RDS/Aurora, the proxy is a first-class managed service. For self-managed databases (EC2): use pgBouncer (PG connection pooler) or similar.<br> *`max_connections` parameter is static on RDS — requires reboot. Changing it on Aurora is also static (cluster-level parameter). Plan connection limits before deployment; retrofitting requires downtime.<br>


471. [ Aurora ] What happens to Aurora Read Replicas during a writer upgrade (instance class change)? ; Changing the writer's instance class triggers a failover: Aurora promotes the highest-priority reader to writer (during the brief failover), upgrades the original writer in the background, then either keeps it as a reader or it becomes the new writer at the next opportunity. Multi-AZ failover sequence: ~30 sec downtime on writer. <br><br> *All readers continue serving reads during the writer upgrade — no reader downtime. Only the writer has ~30 sec interruption (failover). With RDS Proxy, the application may not even notice the 30-sec window.<br> *If you want to upgrade readers: upgrade one reader at a time, never all at once. Upgrade the reader, validate it's healthy, then upgrade the next. Upgrading all readers simultaneously leaves no read capacity during upgrade.<br> *For major Aurora engine upgrades: the entire cluster is taken offline briefly (even with replicas). Plan this during a maintenance window. Blue/Green deployments can avoid downtime for Aurora major version upgrades (new cluster, then traffic switch).<br>

472. [ RDS ] What is the purpose of IAM database authentication in RDS, and what does the auth token contain? ; IAM DB authentication replaces static passwords with short-lived (15-minute) tokens generated using AWS Signature Version 4. The token is a signed URL containing: the region, DB hostname, port, DB user name, and an expiry timestamp. The token is NOT an IAM session token — it's a signed request used as a DB password. <br><br> *To use: `aws rds generate-db-auth-token --hostname mydb.xyz.rds.amazonaws.com --port 5432 --username mydbuser --region us-east-1`. The returned string is used as the PostgreSQL or MySQL password. It expires in 15 minutes.<br> *IAM policy requirement: the IAM entity (Lambda role, EC2 role) must have `rds-db:connect` permission on the DB resource ARN (`arn:aws:rds-db:region:account:dbuser:db-resource-id/db-username`).<br> *No static credentials: applications don't store DB passwords. The IAM role provides temporary credentials. Rotation is automatic (new token generated every 15 min). Audit: CloudTrail shows which IAM principal generated a token; DB logs show which DB user connected.<br>

473. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB PartiQL interface, and how does it compare to the standard DynamoDB API? ; PartiQL for DynamoDB provides an SQL-compatible query syntax (`SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`) as an alternative to the DynamoDB-native API (`GetItem`, `PutItem`, `Query`, etc.). PartiQL is NOT full SQL — it cannot do JOINs, complex subqueries, or aggregations across tables. <br><br> *PartiQL SELECT example: `SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE OrderId = '123'` — equivalent to `GetItem` with PK=123. Useful for teams familiar with SQL who find DynamoDB's API verbose.<br> *PartiQL does NOT change DynamoDB's underlying capabilities — it's a syntax layer. You still need a partition key in queries (`WHERE` clause must include PK for efficient access). A PartiQL query without a PK filter becomes a full table Scan internally.<br> *Executive Summary Comparisons table for the exam: PartiQL adds SQL-like syntax to DynamoDB (read convenience), does NOT add relational capabilities (no JOINs, no cross-table aggregation, no GROUP BY without GSI). For analytics requiring SQL: use Redshift or Athena on DynamoDB export.<br>

474. [ Redshift ] What are Redshift table design best practices for a star schema data warehouse? ; Best practices: (1) fact table: KEY distribution on the most common join key (e.g., customer_id), COMPOUND sort key on the most common filter columns (e.g., order_date, region). (2) dimension tables: ALL distribution (copy to every node — small tables, frequently joined). (3) avoid EVEN distribution for join keys — causes data shuffling. <br><br> *Star schema in Redshift: central fact table (large, billions of rows) surrounded by dimension tables (smaller, millions of rows). Fact table: KEY distribution so it co-locates with dimensions. Dimensions: ALL distribution so every join is local to each slice.<br> *Sort key for fact table: first column should be the most common filter (date range queries → order_date first). Second column can be another high-cardinality filter. ANALYZE COMPRESSION before load to identify optimal compression encodings per column.<br> *Avoid VARCHAR(MAX) for dimension columns — Redshift uses fixed-size buffers; VARCHAR(MAX) wastes memory and slows queries. Use the actual maximum length for each column. CHAR vs VARCHAR: CHAR pads to fixed length (faster for fixed-size values like country codes); VARCHAR for variable-length strings.<br>

475. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune T-family (burstable) vs R-family (memory-optimized) instance choice, and when would you choose each? ; T-family (t3, t4g): burstable CPU — cheap for small/intermittent graph workloads; suitable for dev/test, small graphs. R-family (r5, r6g): memory-optimized — designed for large graph datasets that must fit in RAM for traversal performance; suitable for production graph workloads. <br><br> *Graph traversals are memory-intensive — traversing millions of vertices and edges requires the active working set to be in memory. On R-family instances, more graph data fits in RAM cache; on T-family, cache overflows to storage (slow).<br> *T-family CPU credits: burst performance is limited by credit balance. If CPU credits deplete (sustained high-traversal load), performance drops to baseline (~10-20% of peak). Production graph workloads should use R-family to avoid performance cliffs.<br> *Memory sizing for Neptune: rule of thumb — aim to fit the "hot" portion of your graph (actively traversed vertices/edges) in RAM. Use `FreeLocalStorage` and `CPUUtilization` as indicators. High CPU with high I/O wait = graph exceeds RAM → upgrade to larger R-family instance.<br>

476. [ ElastiCache ] What is Redis Sentinel, and how does it differ from ElastiCache's built-in Multi-AZ? ; Redis Sentinel (open-source Redis HA): external set of "sentinel" processes that monitor Redis, detect primary failure, orchestrate replica promotion, and notify clients. ElastiCache Multi-AZ: AWS manages all of this automatically — no sentinel processes needed; ElastiCache detects failure, promotes replica, updates DNS. <br><br> *For ElastiCache: you don't need Sentinel — AWS built the same functionality into the service. This is a key "managed service" benefit: operational tooling is included.<br> *Sentinel comparison with ElastiCache: Sentinel needs 3+ processes for quorum; DNS-based failover; client must be Sentinel-aware. ElastiCache: AWS detects via health checks, promotes in ~30-60 sec, DNS flip; standard Redis client works.<br> *For self-managed Redis on EC2 needing HA: use Sentinel or Redis Cluster. Migrating to ElastiCache eliminates Sentinel management overhead. This is a common "migration to managed services" exam scenario.<br>

477. [ DocumentDB ] Can Amazon DocumentDB be used as an event source for AWS Lambda? ; DocumentDB Change Streams can be consumed by Lambda as an event source using the Lambda Streams integration. Enable Change Streams on the DocumentDB collection → Lambda polls the stream (similar to DynamoDB Streams → Lambda). Lambda is invoked with batches of change events from DocumentDB. <br><br> *Lambda DocumentDB integration requires: Lambda in the same VPC as DocumentDB (VPC configuration), Lambda execution role with DocumentDB stream read permissions, Change Streams enabled on the collection.<br> *Filter patterns: Lambda supports event filtering for DocumentDB streams — configure filter to only invoke Lambda on specific operation types (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE) or document attribute conditions. Reduces unnecessary Lambda invocations.<br> *Use cases: search indexing (DocumentDB change → Lambda → OpenSearch), notifications (new document → Lambda → SNS), analytics pipeline (DocumentDB change → Lambda → Kinesis Firehose → S3).<br>

478. [ QLDB ] What is the difference between a QLDB committed transaction and an abandoned transaction? ; QLDB transactions are committed only when `commit_transaction` is called — all writes in the session since `start_transaction` are atomically written to the journal. If the session is closed without committing (abandon), all changes in that transaction are discarded — no partial writes appear in the journal. <br><br> *QLDB transactions must explicitly call `commit_transaction` (or use the QLDB SDK's automatic transaction wrapper). If an error occurs mid-transaction: catch the error, call `abort_transaction` explicitly (or let the session close — same effect), handle the error, and optionally retry.<br> *Session limit: a single QLDB session can have only one active transaction at a time. To run concurrent transactions: use multiple sessions. The QLDB driver manages a session pool automatically.<br> *OCC (Optimistic Concurrency Control): QLDB uses OCC for conflict detection. Two transactions attempting to modify the same document: the second one gets `OccConflictException` — retry logic must handle this. OCC is appropriate because QLDB assumes conflicts are rare.<br>

479. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Read Replica promotion process, and what should you verify before promoting? ; Promotion makes a read replica the new standalone writer instance (Aurora MySQL) or standalone cluster (Aurora PostgreSQL). Steps: verify replica lag is minimal (ideally 0), promote via `promote-read-replica-db-cluster`, update application connection string to the new promoted cluster endpoint. The original cluster is no longer the primary. <br><br> *Before promoting: check `AuroraReplicaLag` metric — if replica is significantly behind, data loss may occur after promotion (writes to the old primary since the lag point). Wait for lag to approach 0 before promoting for minimal data loss.<br> *After promotion: the promoted cluster is FULLY INDEPENDENT — it no longer replicates from the original. The original cluster continues operating. You now have two independent Aurora clusters. Delete or decommission the old one as appropriate.<br> *Use case: DR drill (promote cross-region replica, validate, then reverse). Read-only workload isolation (promote a replica to a read-write cluster for a reporting database). Major version upgrade path (promote, upgrade separately, cut over).<br>

480. [ RDS ] What is the RDS "apply immediately" vs "apply during next maintenance window" choice, and when does each cause downtime? ; "Apply immediately": changes applied right now — may cause an immediate reboot (for static parameters, instance class changes). "Apply during next maintenance window": changes queued for the next scheduled maintenance window (default: once per week). For production changes requiring a reboot: always use maintenance window to control timing. <br><br> *Some modifications don't require reboot regardless of timing choice: storage increase, adding a read replica, enabling/disabling Multi-AZ (Multi-AZ enablement is live for some engines). Modifications requiring reboot: instance class change, static parameter changes, enabling encryption.<br> *Multi-AZ failover path: for instance class change with Multi-AZ: AWS fails over to standby (new class), upgrades original, fails back. Two failovers (~60 sec each) = ~2 min total downtime. Controllable if done in maintenance window.<br> *When to use "apply immediately": emergency changes (security patches, critical bug fixes). Always test emergency changes in non-production first. "Apply during maintenance window" is the safe default for production.<br>


481. [ Aurora ] An Aurora cluster has 1 writer (r6g.4xlarge) and 3 readers (r6g.2xlarge). The application is 90% reads. What is the recommended endpoint configuration? ; Use the Reader Endpoint for 90% of application reads (load-balanced across 3 readers), Cluster Endpoint for writes. Optionally, create Custom Endpoints to separate heavy analytics reads (route to 1 reader) from OLTP reads (route to 2 readers), preventing analytics queries from starving OLTP read capacity. <br><br> *Reader Endpoint load-balances at connection time (each new connection to a different reader round-robin). For 90% read traffic: 3 r6g.2xlarge readers handle read scaling; r6g.4xlarge writer handles writes without read pressure.<br> *Custom Endpoint value: if some analytical queries are multi-second scans, they'd degrade OLTP read latency if routed through the same readers. Custom Endpoint separates them: analytics → dedicated reader (accepts higher latency), OLTP → remaining readers (fast, uncontested).<br> *If readers are bottlenecked: scale vertically (increase reader instance size) or add more readers (up to 15). If writer is bottlenecked: only one writer is allowed — scale vertically or consider sharding at the application layer.<br>

482. [ RDS ] A company is running Oracle 19c with Oracle Advanced Security (TDE) on-premises. They want to migrate to RDS Oracle. What should they know about TDE? ; RDS Oracle supports Oracle TDE (Transparent Data Encryption). On RDS Oracle: TDE can be enabled on the DB itself (Oracle-managed TDE) while RDS also applies AWS-level AES-256 KMS encryption. Both can coexist (double encryption). Oracle Wallet (TDE key management) must be managed within RDS constraints. <br><br> *Oracle TDE on RDS uses Oracle's native TDE with the Oracle wallet stored on the RDS instance. Unlike on-premises where you manage the wallet file, on RDS the wallet management is constrained — you cannot directly access the OS-level wallet file.<br> *Migration with TDE: on-premises Oracle with TDE → RDS Oracle: decrypt data first (use Oracle Data Pump export with decryption), then import to RDS Oracle, then re-enable TDE on RDS. Alternatively, use AWS DMS which handles TDE-encrypted source transparently.<br> *RDS Encryption vs Oracle TDE: they're independent. RDS encryption (KMS) encrypts EBS storage at the hypervisor level. Oracle TDE encrypts at the tablespace/column level. For compliance requiring application-level encryption (e.g., specific columns): Oracle TDE provides that; RDS encryption alone does not.<br>

483. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB table class "Standard" vs "Standard-Infrequent Access (IA)", and when would you switch? ; Standard: default; optimized for frequently accessed data; lower RCU/WCU cost, higher storage cost. Standard-IA: optimized for infrequently accessed data; lower storage cost (~60% cheaper), higher RCU/WCU cost (~25% more). Switch to Standard-IA when storage cost > throughput cost in your access pattern. <br><br> *Break-even: if your table has large amounts of data rarely read (compliance data, historical logs, archived records), Standard-IA reduces the storage bill substantially. If your table is actively queried, the higher per-RCU cost of Standard-IA exceeds the storage savings.<br> *Switching table class: can be changed via console or API (`UpdateTable` with `TableClass=STANDARD_INFREQUENT_ACCESS`). The change is asynchronous. Only one class change per 30 days allowed.<br> *Standard-IA is NOT a different tier of DynamoDB service — same durability, same API, same features. Only the cost model changes (storage cheaper, I/O more expensive). Use AWS Cost Explorer to identify tables where Standard-IA would save money.<br>

484. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift column compression encoding, and why is it important? ; Compression encodings reduce the amount of data stored per column, improving query performance (less I/O to read) and reducing storage cost. Redshift-specific encodings: RAW (no compression), AZ64 (AWS-proprietary — default for integers), ZSTD (high compression ratio), LZO, BYTEDICT (for low-cardinality columns), DELTA (for sequential numbers/dates), RUNLENGTH (for columns with many consecutive identical values). <br><br> *ANALYZE COMPRESSION command: automatically recommends the best encoding per column based on sample data. Run on populated tables to get recommendations. Apply encodings during table creation (not retroactively without recreating).<br> *AZ64 default: AWS introduced AZ64 as the default compression for numeric and date/time types. It provides good compression with fast decompression for SIMD-optimized query execution. Generally: use AZ64 for numbers/dates, ZSTD for text columns, BYTEDICT for low-cardinality strings (country, status codes).<br> *Compression effect on query performance: a well-compressed table can reduce I/O by 3-5× compared to uncompressed. On a 1 TB table: 200 GB compressed → 1/5 the I/O per query scan → proportionally faster queries and cheaper Spectrum costs.<br>

485. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune Workbench (Jupyter), and how is it accessed? ; Neptune Workbench is a managed Jupyter notebook environment (SageMaker Notebooks) integrated with Neptune for interactive graph exploration. Pre-loaded with Neptune-specific magic commands (`%gremlin`, `%sparql`, `%opencypher`), visualization libraries (neptune-python-utils), and sample code. Access: create via Neptune console → hosted in SageMaker → opens in browser. <br><br> *Neptune Workbench magic commands: `%%gremlin` (execute Gremlin in a cell), `%%sparql` (execute SPARQL), `%%opencypher` (execute Cypher). Results rendered as tables, graph visualizations, or raw JSON in the notebook.<br> *No extra Neptune cost for Workbench queries — queries go through the Neptune endpoint normally (billed as normal Neptune I/O). The SageMaker notebook instance has its own cost ($0.046/hr for ml.t3.medium).<br> *Use case: data scientists exploring graph data interactively, developers testing graph queries during development, operations teams investigating specific graph patterns (e.g., "show me all nodes connected to this suspected fraud account").<br>

486. [ ElastiCache ] What is the Redis `SCAN` command, and why should it be preferred over `KEYS` in production? ; `KEYS pattern` returns all matching keys — blocks the Redis server for the entire scan (O(N) blocking operation). `SCAN cursor pattern COUNT hint` is an iterative cursor-based scan — returns a small batch of keys per call, non-blocking. Production: always use `SCAN` (iterative, non-blocking) never `KEYS` (blocks Redis for large keyspaces). <br><br> *Redis is single-threaded for commands. `KEYS *` on a 10-million-key Redis instance blocks ALL other commands for potentially seconds. This causes connection timeouts and latency spikes across the entire application using that Redis instance.<br> *SCAN approach: call `SCAN 0 MATCH prefix:* COUNT 100` → get cursor + batch of keys → call `SCAN cursor` again → repeat until cursor = 0 (full scan complete). Each call returns quickly; other commands execute between calls.<br> *ElastiCache limitation: `KEYS` is technically allowed but will generate CloudWatch latency alerts and may trigger cluster health issues. Best practice: disable `KEYS` via Redis RBAC (Redis 6+ ACL): set ACL to deny the `KEYS` command for non-admin users.<br>

487. [ DocumentDB ] What is Amazon DocumentDB's read concern and write concern, and how do they affect consistency? ; Write concern: `{w: 1}` (acknowledged by primary only — default), `{w: "majority"}` (acknowledged by majority of replica set — stronger durability). Read concern: `"local"` (default — reads from primary, might return recently written data not yet replicated), `"majority"` (reads data acknowledged by majority — more consistent). <br><br> *DocumentDB supports a subset of MongoDB's read/write concern options. `w: majority` write concern ensures writes survive primary failure (replica has the data). Use for financial operations where write durability is critical.<br> *Read concern `"majority"`: waits for data to be acknowledged by majority of replicas before returning it on read. Prevents reading data that could be rolled back. At the cost of slightly higher read latency.<br> *Default behavior (`w:1`, `rc: "local"`): writes acknowledged by primary only, reads from primary may include writes not yet replicated. For most OLTP applications: acceptable. For financial ledger applications: use `w: majority` for writes + `rc: majority` for reads — strong consistency guarantees.<br>

488. [ QLDB ] How does QLDB handle the "right to be forgotten" (GDPR Article 17) given that journal data cannot be deleted? ; QLDB's immutable journal conflicts with GDPR right to erasure. Recommended approach: store personally identifiable information (PII) OUTSIDE QLDB — keep only an anonymous identifier (hash or UUID) in the QLDB record. The PII lives in a separate system (RDS, DynamoDB) that supports deletion. On erasure request: delete PII from the separate system; QLDB journal shows only the anonymized identifier. <br><br> *"Pseudonymization" strategy: QLDB record contains `customerId: "uuid-abc-123"`, financial data. Separate DynamoDB table maps `uuid-abc-123` → personal details. On erasure: delete the DynamoDB record (personal details gone), QLDB journal still records the financial transaction with the UUID. The financial record remains (required by law for 7+ years), PII is erased.<br> *QLDB stores whatever data you INSERT — if you put PII in the journal, you cannot delete it. Design data models to keep QLDB lean (IDs, amounts, timestamps) and sensitive attributes in systems that support deletion.<br> *GDPR technical compliance note: the regulation focuses on personal data being unrecoverable. If the mapping between UUID and person is deleted, the UUID in QLDB is anonymized data — no longer "personal data" under GDPR. Legal counsel should validate this interpretation for your specific jurisdiction.<br>

489. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora zero-downtime restart (ZDR), and in what scenarios does it NOT work? ; ZDR (same as Zero-Downtime Patching/ZDP) allows Aurora to restart the DB engine without dropping application connections — applications see a brief stall (seconds) but no disconnection errors. ZDR works for minor engine patches and restarts. ZDR does NOT work for: major version upgrades, operating system reboots, instance class changes (these require failover or full restart). <br><br> *ZDR mechanism: Aurora sends a "quiesce" signal to the engine → open transactions either commit or are rolled back → engine restarts with new version → resumes sessions. Applications using persistent connections see a ~1-5 second pause but no TCP disconnect.<br> *ZDR limitations: long-running transactions prevent ZDR from completing (engine can't safely quiesce). Aurora will attempt ZDR; if transactions don't complete within the timeout window, it falls back to a standard restart (brief disconnect).<br> *Best practice: set appropriate `wait_timeout` and `interactive_timeout` (MySQL) or `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` (PostgreSQL) to terminate idle-in-transaction connections. These long-idle connections would prevent ZDR from completing.<br>

490. [ RDS ] What is the maximum number of Read Replicas you can create for an RDS MySQL instance, and what determines this limit? ; RDS MySQL Read Replicas: up to 5 per source instance. The limit is per-source (not per-region). You can chain replicas: replica of a replica, up to 4 levels deep (source → replica1 → replica2 → replica3 → replica4). Total chained replicas per source: 5 at each level = up to 3125 theoretically (5^4), but practical limit is lower. <br><br> *Primary concern with Read Replicas: each replica adds replication overhead on the primary (binary log shipping). 5 replicas shipping binary logs simultaneously increases primary CPU and I/O by proportional amounts. Monitor `ReplicaLag` and primary `CPUUtilization` as replicas are added.<br> *Cross-region replica: each cross-region replica counts as 1 of the 5 allowed replicas. Cross-region replicas add network latency in the replication lag. For read scaling across regions: each region gets its own replica. For low-latency reads: replicas should be in the same region as the readers.<br> *Recommendation: if you need >5 read replicas → migrate to Aurora (15 replicas, near-zero lag, no binary log overhead since shared storage). Aurora is the upgrade path from RDS MySQL/PG when Read Replica limits are hit.<br>


491. [ DynamoDB ] What is the maximum write throughput for a single DynamoDB partition, and what happens when this limit is reached? ; Each DynamoDB partition can handle up to 1,000 WCU/second and 3,000 RCU/second. If a single partition key value receives traffic exceeding these limits: throttling occurs on that partition even if aggregate table WCU is sufficient. Adaptive capacity partially mitigates this for burst scenarios. <br><br> *Partition throughput limit is a hard architectural constraint: no amount of provisioned table capacity allows a single partition key to exceed 1000 WCU/second sustained. For workloads requiring >1000 WPS on a single item: redesign the access pattern (write sharding, aggregation outside DynamoDB).<br> *For time-series data (heavy write on "current minute" partition key): use random suffix to distribute writes. Write to "sensor:2026-10-30T10:15#shard1" through "#shard4" — 4× write capacity for that time bucket.<br> *Burst capacity: DynamoDB allows short bursts above the partition limit using accumulated unused capacity (up to 5 minutes of unused throughput). After the burst, throttling resumes. Do not architect around burst — it's for occasional spikes, not sustained load.<br>

492. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift Table Inspector and how do you use it to optimize a table? ; Redshift Table Inspector (in the Redshift Query Editor v2) shows table details: distribution style, sort key, encoding, row count, unsorted percentage. Also check `SVV_TABLE_INFO` system view. Use findings to: change distribution key (requires table rebuild), add/change sort key, apply compression. <br><br> *`SVV_TABLE_INFO`: columns include `table_id`, `schema`, `table`, `diststyle`, `sortkey1`, `sortkey_num`, `size` (blocks), `pct_unsorted` (% unsorted rows — high = needs VACUUM). Query this view regularly for optimization opportunities.<br> *`SVL_QUERY_REPORT` and `EXPLAIN` plan: use `EXPLAIN SELECT...` to see the query plan including: DS_DIST_NONE (good — no redistribution), DS_DIST_INNER (inner table shuffled — consider changing distkey), DS_BROADCAST (small table broadcast — acceptable for ALL distribution), DS_DIST_ALL_INNER (large table broadcast — bad, consider KEY distribution).<br> *Redistribution (DS_DIST_*) in query plans indicates data is being moved between slices during joins — this is expensive (network I/O). Eliminate by matching distribution keys between joined tables or using ALL distribution for small tables.<br>

493. [ Neptune ] What is the maximum property size in a Neptune vertex or edge property? ; Neptune property values: individual property values can be up to 128 KB per value. Vertices and edges can have multiple properties. There is no documented limit on total properties per vertex/edge, but very large numbers of properties per element may impact query performance. <br><br> *128 KB per property value: sufficient for most text properties (descriptions, embedded JSON, short documents). For larger content (full-text articles, binary data): store in S3, reference the S3 key as a property in Neptune.<br> *Numeric property types: Neptune supports integers (long), floats (doubles), dates, and strings. For precision decimal arithmetic (financial values): consider storing as string or use 64-bit integer with implicit decimal (cents instead of dollars).<br> *Property cardinality: Neptune supports single-cardinality (one value per property) and multi-cardinality (list of values per property). Multi-cardinality properties allow `g.V().has('color', 'red').has('color', 'blue')` — vertex has both colors.<br>

494.
