[ ElastiCache ] What are Redis data structures beyond simple key-value, and what are their ElastiCache use cases? ; Redis structures: Strings (cache values), Hashes (user profiles — field:value maps), Lists (queues, activity feeds — ordered), Sets (unique membership — tag lists, friend lists), Sorted Sets (leaderboards — scored rank), Bitmaps (presence tracking), HyperLogLog (count distinct), Streams (append-only log with consumer groups), Geospatial (location-based queries). <br><br> *Leaderboard with Sorted Set: `ZADD leaderboard 9500 user:alice 8800 user:bob` → `ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 9` returns top 10. O(log N) updates and O(log N + M) range queries — ideal for real-time gaming leaderboards with millions of players.<br> *Session storage with Hash: `HSET session:abc123 user_id 456 cart_items 3 expires 1735689600` → `HGETALL session:abc123` fetches all session fields atomically. More efficient than storing JSON string (individual field reads/updates without full serialization).<br> *Pub/Sub for real-time notifications: `SUBSCRIBE channel:user:123` in consumer, `PUBLISH channel:user:123 "new_message"` in producer. Real-time delivery to all subscribers. Note: no persistence — messages lost if no active subscriber when published. For durable messaging: use Redis Streams or SQS.<br>

495. [ DocumentDB ] What is the Amazon DocumentDB Global Clusters feature, and when was it made generally available? ; Amazon DocumentDB Elastic Clusters with Global Database replication was announced in late 2023. Instance-based DocumentDB clusters received Global Clusters capability in 2024. Storage-level cross-region replication (<1 sec lag), secondary clusters are read-only, managed failover. Same architectural pattern as Aurora Global Database. <br><br> *Before Global Clusters: DocumentDB cross-region DR required periodic snapshot copies and manual restore — RTO hours, RPO hours. With Global Clusters: RPO <1 sec, RTO ~1 minute (similar to Aurora Global Database).<br> *Current limitation (as of mid-2026): DocumentDB Global Clusters are not available in all regions. Check the AWS Regional Services page before designing a multi-region DocumentDB architecture. If unavailable: use the Streams + Lambda + cross-region write pattern for custom replication.<br> *Migration to Global Clusters from standalone clusters: create the Global Cluster, then add existing clusters as primary/secondary members. No data migration needed — existing data becomes part of the global cluster's storage layer.<br>

496. [ QLDB ] How many tables can a QLDB Ledger contain, and what is the recommended design for a financial ledger? ; QLDB ledger limit: 20 tables per ledger (default). Recommended financial ledger design: separate tables by entity type (Accounts, Transactions, BillingAddresses) within a single ledger — all in the same ledger share the same journal for consistent cryptographic verification. <br><br> *All tables in a QLDB ledger share the same journal hash chain — QLDB Digest covers the entire ledger, providing one proof for all tables. Separating accounts and transactions into different ledgers would require separate verification proofs.<br> *Table naming: QLDB table names are case-sensitive. PartiQL queries must use exact case. Convention: PascalCase for table names matches document-oriented style (Accounts, Transactions, Events).<br> *Scale per ledger: QLDB is serverless — the 20-table limit is the primary constraint, not throughput. For ledgers requiring >20 tables: consider using multiple ledgers (separate verification domains) or consolidate tables using document type discriminator fields.<br>

497. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Global Database write forwarding feature? ; Write Forwarding allows applications connected to a secondary (read-only) Aurora Global Database cluster to issue write statements — the secondary cluster transparently forwards the write to the primary region. This eliminates the need for applications to manage separate write endpoints per region. <br><br> *Without write forwarding: applications in secondary regions must connect to the primary region's endpoint for writes (higher latency cross-region). With write forwarding: application in eu-west-1 connects to eu-west-1's secondary cluster, sends INSERT — the INSERT is forwarded to us-east-1 primary transparently.<br> *Latency impact: write forwarding adds round-trip latency (secondary → primary → secondary acknowledgment). For latency-sensitive writes: still better to connect directly to primary. Write forwarding is a convenience feature for applications where write latency is tolerable.<br> *Consistency: reads in the secondary region after a forwarded write may return stale data (replica lag). Use `--wait-for-write` option or strongly consistent reads via primary endpoint for read-after-write consistency in secondary regions.<br>

498. [ RDS ] What is the RDS option group, and how does it differ from a parameter group? ; Parameter group: engine configuration parameters (memory, connections, query settings, log settings). Option group: plugin-style features added to the DB engine (Oracle APEX, SQL Server TDE, Oracle OEM, MySQL memcached plugin). Some features require an option group to activate them — parameters can't enable them. <br><br> *Option groups are specific to RDS (not Aurora). Aurora uses cluster/instance parameter groups and doesn't have option groups — Aurora's features are controlled by parameters or are built-in.<br> *Oracle option groups: required for Oracle Advanced Security (TDE), Oracle Enterprise Manager, Oracle Application Express (APEX), Oracle Statspack. These are separate Oracle software components activated via RDS option groups.<br> *SQL Server option group: enables "Transparent Data Encryption" (option: `TRANSPARENT_DATA_ENCRYPTION`), "SQL Server Agent," "SQL Server Audit" features via option group. Without the option group, these features are not available even if the instance class supports them.<br>

499. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB capacity for a Global Secondary Index (GSI) in relation to the base table? ; A GSI has INDEPENDENT provisioned capacity from the base table in Provisioned mode. You set separate RCU and WCU for each GSI. In On-Demand mode: GSI capacity scales automatically with the table. Critical: if a GSI is under-provisioned, writes to the base table that also update the GSI will be THROTTLED at the GSI level, causing base table write throttling. <br><br> *GSI provisioning recommendation: provision GSI WCU >= base table WCU (GSI receives the same write load as the base table, since every write that touches the GSI key attribute triggers a GSI write). Under-provisioning GSIs is a common operational mistake.<br> *GSI RCU: provision based on expected read queries that use this GSI. If the GSI is used for 20% of read traffic: provision 20% of your read requirements as GSI RCU. GSI RCU is independent from base table RCU — no sharing.<br> *GSI throttling visibility: monitor `ConsumedWriteCapacityUnits` per GSI separately from the base table. CloudWatch has per-GSI metrics. Set alarms on `WriteThrottleEvents` for each GSI — GSI throttle often goes unnoticed until users complain.<br>

500. [ All DBs ] A company is choosing between Aurora MySQL, RDS MySQL, and DynamoDB for a new e-commerce application with a product catalog (5M products) and order management (500 orders/second peak). What is the recommended architecture? ; Orders (high write throughput, structured, transactional): Aurora MySQL (handle 500 writes/sec with write endpoint + connection pooling via RDS Proxy; ACID for inventory/payment transactions; up to 15 read replicas for order history queries). Product catalog (read-heavy, flexible schema): DynamoDB (scale to millions of reads/sec, schema flexibility for varied product attributes, DAX for sub-ms reads). <br><br> *Split the workload by access pattern: financial/transactional data (orders, payments, inventory) → relational DB with ACID (Aurora MySQL). Product metadata (catalog, search, varied attributes) → DynamoDB (flexible schema, high read throughput).<br> *Aurora for orders: 500 writes/sec is well within Aurora's capability (thousands of WPS on r6g.2xlarge+). Add RDS Proxy for Lambda-based order processing. Aurora Serverless v2 for the write path handles variable load gracefully.<br> *DynamoDB for catalog: 5M products × 1000 reads/second = DynamoDB On-Demand easily handles this. Add DAX layer for hot products (top 10,000 most-viewed = 99% of traffic) with microsecond reads. Product catalog reads from DAX, writes go to DynamoDB (write-through).<br>


501. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's default connection timeout when connecting to the cluster endpoint? ; Aurora cluster endpoint default TCP connection timeout: 30 seconds. Application-level query timeout is configurable per connection. RDS Proxy adds its own connection timeout (default 120 seconds for the proxy idle connection). <br><br> *Cluster endpoint DNS TTL: 30 seconds for Aurora. After a failover, the cluster endpoint DNS updates within 30 seconds to point to the new primary. Application connection pools must honor DNS TTL (avoid caching DNS beyond 30s). Java apps: set `networkaddress.cache.ttl=0` or use RDS Proxy to eliminate DNS caching issues.<br> *Reader endpoint DNS: load-balances across read replicas using 5-second TTL. DNS-level round-robin distributes connections. If you need session-level affinity: use custom endpoints or direct instance endpoints.<br> *Connection timeout vs socket timeout: TCP `connect()` timeout (reaching the host) differs from socket timeout (waiting for data after connected). Set both in JDBC: `connectTimeout=30000&socketTimeout=60000`.<br>

502. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora Serverless v1 vs v2 pause behavior? ; v1: pauses after configurable idle period (min 5 minutes), cold start 30-60+ seconds. v2: NO pause — always has at least minimum capacity active, no cold starts. v1 also scales in larger ACU steps; v2 scales in 0.5 ACU increments. <br><br> *Aurora Serverless v1 use case: dev/test environments that can tolerate cold starts, variable or intermittent workloads with long idle periods where pause saves cost. Not for production APIs (cold start = user-facing latency spike).<br> *Aurora Serverless v2 use case: production workloads needing automatic scale without managing instance sizes. Scales from min ACU (0.5–1) to max ACU (128) within seconds. Scales per-replica independently. Compatible with Multi-AZ (each AZ replica scales independently).<br> *ACU pricing: pay per ACU-hour for v2 (even at minimum capacity, no pause). v1 charges per ACU-second with pause savings. For workloads active <4 hours/day: v1 pause may be more economical. For continuous workloads: v2.<br>

503. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's Backtrack snapshot-based backup difference, and which engines support each? ; Backtrack (MySQL only): rewinds the LIVE cluster to a past time using a special storage log — no snapshot restore needed, no downtime (just brief pause). Snapshot restore: creates a NEW cluster from any snapshot. Automated backups: point-in-time restore creates new cluster. Backtrack window: up to 72 hours back. <br><br> *Backtrack vs PITR: Backtrack rewinds the existing cluster in place (seconds to minutes to rewind 24 hours). PITR creates a new cluster (takes 10-30+ minutes). If you need to undo an accidental `DELETE` without creating a new cluster: Backtrack is much faster.<br> *PostgreSQL: no Backtrack support. PostgreSQL-compatible Aurora uses PITR only (creates new cluster from backup). When designing Aurora PG applications: plan for new-cluster restore latency in DR runbooks.<br> *Backtrack does not protect against AZ failure: Backtrack is a time-travel feature, not HA. For HA: Aurora's multi-copy storage + Multi-AZ writer provide that independently.<br>

504. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora local write forwarding feature for Global Database vs write forwarding for Multi-AZ clusters? ; Two distinct features. (1) Aurora Global Database Write Forwarding: secondary region clusters (read-only) forward writes to primary region writer over inter-region network. (2) Aurora Multi-AZ DB Cluster write forwarding: NOT a feature — all reader instances in a Multi-AZ cluster are read-only; application must use separate endpoints. <br><br> *Global Database write forwarding latency: adds the inter-region round-trip (e.g., us-east-1 → eu-west-1 → back = 100-150ms). Only worthwhile when the application tier is in eu-west-1 and needs occasional writes without changing its connection endpoint. High-throughput write workloads: always connect directly to the primary region writer.<br> *Aurora Multi-AZ (multi-writer): Aurora MySQL Parallel Query clusters can use multiple writer instances for I/O parallelism, but this is Parallel Query — not a general multi-master feature. Aurora Multi-Master (2019) was deprecated in 2023.<br> *Exam: distinguish Aurora Global Database write forwarding (secondary REGION → primary REGION) from any misconception about multi-master within a single cluster. Aurora is single-writer per cluster (except the deprecated Multi-Master).<br>

505. [ Aurora ] How does Aurora handle a write quorum failure (fewer than 4 of 6 nodes available)? ; If fewer than 4 of 6 storage nodes are available for writes (or fewer than 3 for reads), Aurora marks the cluster as "storage failure" and the cluster becomes unavailable. This is extremely rare — requires simultaneous failure of 3+ storage nodes across 3 AZs. <br><br> *6 copies across 3 AZs = 2 copies per AZ. Losing 1 AZ = lose 2 copies → 4 remain → write quorum maintained. Losing 2 AZs = lose 4 copies → 2 remain → write quorum lost (need 4). So Aurora tolerates loss of 1 full AZ for writes, and 2 full AZs for reads.<br> *Practical meaning: a single AZ outage (most common AWS failure type) never takes down Aurora. Regional-scale outages affecting 2 AZs simultaneously are extremely rare. For those scenarios: Aurora Global Database provides RPO <1 second cross-region.<br> *Quorum repair: when nodes come back online, Aurora automatically re-peers them. The storage layer self-heals by copying from surviving quorum nodes to the recovering node.<br>

506. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Performance Insights retention period and cost model? ; Default: 7 days free. Extended: up to 2 years, charged per vCPU per month. Performance Insights captures wait events, SQL statements, execution plans. Free tier: 7 days. Extended retention: useful for trend analysis, seasonal comparisons. <br><br> *Performance Insights vs Enhanced Monitoring: PI focuses on SQL-level waits (which queries are slow and why — lock waits, I/O waits, CPU). Enhanced Monitoring focuses on OS metrics (CPU, memory, disk I/O, network per-process). Both complement each other. PI for query tuning; EM for resource saturation investigation.<br> *Top SQL in PI: shows the top SQL statements by load (DBLoad = average active sessions × time). A query at 0.5 DBLoad means it averages 0.5 active executions — 50% of the time 1 session is blocked there. DBLoad > vCPU count = CPU saturation.<br> *PI dimensions: load can be sliced by host (which app server generates load), SQL statement, wait event, user, or database. Use wait event breakdown to diagnose: high `io/aurora:AuroraStorageIoRead` = I/O bound; `cpu` = CPU bound; `lock/row/wait` = lock contention.<br>

507. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora cluster cloning and what is the storage model that makes it instantaneous? ; Aurora cloning uses copy-on-write (CoW) semantics at the storage layer. Clone is instantaneous regardless of database size — the clone shares the same storage blocks as the source. Writes to either the source or clone cause that block to be copied and written independently. <br><br> *Cost of a clone: zero additional storage cost at creation. As source and clone diverge (each writes different data), storage increases. Initially: near-zero extra cost. After significant writes: clone stores its own changed blocks.<br> *Use case: production clone for testing a migration. Clone 2 TB production Aurora in seconds. Run the migration script on the clone. If migration fails: delete clone. No wait, minimal cost. The alternative (restore from snapshot) takes hours.<br> *Clones stay within an AWS account and region. You cannot clone across accounts (use snapshot copy + restore) or across regions. Clones are full Aurora clusters — same security, same failover, same scale capabilities.<br>

508. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's zero-downtime patching (ZDP) feature? ; ZDP (Zero-Downtime Patching): allows minor version patch application to Aurora MySQL while maintaining connections — the patch applies without requiring a connection drop. Available for Aurora MySQL. Mechanism: Aurora waits for a quiescent point (no active transactions), performs in-memory engine swap, connections survive. <br><br> *ZDP success conditions: no long-running transactions at the patch window, no active temporary tables, no locks. If Aurora cannot find a quiescent point within the maintenance window: falls back to a standard restart (brief connection drop, 10-30 seconds).<br> *ZDP vs failover patching: for Aurora Multi-AZ, another patching approach is "failover-and-patch" — patch the reader, failover to it, patch the old writer. Achieves 20-30 second downtime. ZDP achieves 0-second downtime when successful.<br> *Aurora PostgreSQL: no ZDP. Minor version upgrades require a restart. For PG: use the Multi-AZ failover approach or Blue/Green Deployments (RDS feature also available for Aurora PG via RDS Blue/Green).<br>

509. [ Aurora ] What is the maximum Aurora cluster storage size? ; Aurora storage auto-scales in 10 GB increments up to 128 TiB maximum. You do not pre-provision storage; it grows automatically. There is no throttle or performance penalty during growth. <br><br> *128 TiB limit: sufficient for most databases. For datasets approaching this: consider Aurora Limitless (2024), which shards across multiple Aurora clusters to support petabyte-scale. Aurora Limitless is a separate product — standard Aurora tops at 128 TiB.<br> *Storage billing: billed for the allocated and used space (not pre-provisioned). As data grows to 50 TiB → billed for ~50 TiB + some overhead. Deleting data frees space for reuse in Aurora storage layer but the allocated high watermark does not automatically shrink (similar to PostgreSQL bloat — VACUUM reclaims logically but not necessarily physically for billing).<br> *For exam: "customer needs 200 TB database on Aurora" → Aurora Limitless or consider Aurora + S3 external tables, or migrate to Redshift if it's analytics. Standard Aurora max = 128 TiB.<br>

510. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora custom endpoint feature, and how is it different from the reader endpoint? ; Reader endpoint: DNS-level round-robin across ALL reader instances. Custom endpoint: manually specify a SUBSET of instances (e.g., high-memory instances for analytics queries, or instances running specific engine version). Up to 5 custom endpoints per Aurora cluster. <br><br> *Custom endpoint use case: "analytics team runs long-running queries that saturate an instance — route them to dedicated instances." Create custom endpoint with only the 2 large-instance readers. OLTP traffic uses reader endpoint (all instances). Analytics traffic uses custom endpoint (large instances only).<br> *Custom endpoints and Auto Scaling: if Aurora auto-adds readers via Replica Auto Scaling, the new replicas are NOT automatically added to existing custom endpoints. Manage custom endpoint membership manually, or design workloads to use the reader endpoint if auto-scaled instances should serve the workload.<br> *Failover behavior for custom endpoints: if all instances in a custom endpoint fail, the endpoint returns connection errors. Design custom endpoint groups with multiple instances for HA. Do not assign only 1 instance to a production custom endpoint.<br>


511. [ RDS ] What is the RDS Blue/Green Deployments feature, and what is its primary use case? ; Blue/Green Deployments: creates a staging environment (green) synchronized with production (blue) via logical replication. Schema changes and engine upgrades are applied to green. After validation, switchover is performed in 60-300 seconds with minimal downtime. Supported: MySQL, MariaDB, Aurora MySQL. <br><br> *Blue/Green switchover process: (1) Create green environment (copy of blue). (2) Apply changes to green (schema migration, engine upgrade, parameter changes). (3) Validate green. (4) Switch: RDS reroutes connections, rename instances, minimal downtime. (5) Old blue becomes available for rollback window. (6) Delete blue when confident.<br> *Logical replication lag: green environment uses logical replication from blue (binlog-based for MySQL). Monitor replication lag before switching — switchover has a lag threshold (default 0 seconds; set to up to 1 second). If lag exceeds threshold: switchover blocked until caught up.<br> *Not available for: RDS Oracle, RDS SQL Server, RDS PostgreSQL (PostgreSQL: use standard Multi-AZ failover or manual blue/green). Check exam questions specifically — "Blue/Green for PostgreSQL upgrade" is a distractor if the service is RDS PG (not Aurora PG).<br>

512. [ RDS ] What are the RDS Multi-AZ Instance vs Multi-AZ DB Cluster failover times? ; Multi-AZ Instance (1 standby): 60-120 seconds failover. Multi-AZ DB Cluster (2 readable standbys): 35 seconds or less. The DB Cluster model uses local hot-standby replicas with quorum writes — faster detection and promotion. <br><br> *Multi-AZ Instance mechanism: synchronous replication to standby. On failure: DNS flip to standby. Standby requires promotion and DNS propagation. Typical: 60-120s but can be longer if crash recovery needed.<br> *Multi-AZ DB Cluster mechanism: 2 reader instances in separate AZs, synchronously replicated. On failure: one reader promoted in <35s. Similar to Aurora's fast failover but for RDS. Available for: MySQL 8.0.28+, PostgreSQL 13.4+.<br> *RPO comparison: both Multi-AZ models have RPO = 0 (synchronous replication). RTO difference: Multi-AZ DB Cluster is 2× faster failover. For RPO=0 + minimize RTO on RDS: choose Multi-AZ DB Cluster over Multi-AZ Instance.<br>

513. [ RDS ] What is RDS Storage Auto Scaling and what triggers it? ; Storage Auto Scaling: automatically increases storage when: free storage < 10% of allocated, AND the low-free condition lasts > 5 minutes, AND 6 hours have passed since last scaling. Maximum autoscaled storage cap: set by you (max storage threshold). Never scales down. <br><br> *Minimum 6-hour interval between auto-scalings: if database grows rapidly (e.g., 500 GB → 550 GB in 2 hours), auto-scaling fires once → waits 6 hours → fires again if still needed. Plan for this lag — don't rely on auto-scaling for sudden large data ingestion.<br> *Storage auto-scaling does NOT scale down: if you grew from 1 TB to 2 TB and then archived data, RDS does not shrink to 1 TB. To reclaim: snapshot, restore to a new smaller instance (with the old data removed). This is a manual process.<br> *gp3 vs io1: gp3 supports independent throughput/IOPS configuration (up to 16,000 IOPS, 1,000 MB/s). io1 allows 1,000-256,000 IOPS. Storage auto-scaling applies to both types. For IOPS-intensive workloads (OLTP, high-concurrency): io1/io2 with provisioned IOPS.<br>

514. [ RDS ] What is the RDS read replica promotion process and what happens to replication after promotion? ; Promote: `PromoteReadReplica` API call. The replica stops receiving replication from the source. It becomes an independent standalone DB instance. Promotion takes a few minutes (replica must apply all pending replication events first). After promotion: no replication link — fully independent. <br><br> *Promotion use cases: (1) Disaster recovery — source region fails, promote cross-region replica in DR region. (2) Migration — replicate to new engine version read replica, promote, cut over. (3) Create a dev/test instance from production data.<br> *Promoting a read replica does NOT delete the source: source continues running normally. If source is deleted first: replica was already independent (no cascade). Read replicas of read replicas (chain replication): the leaf replicas also become independent if the intermediate one is promoted.<br> *Cross-region replica promotion latency: cross-region replicas have async lag (seconds to minutes depending on write volume). Before promoting for DR: monitor `ReplicaLag` metric. For near-zero RPO DR: keep replica lag < 5 minutes; accept that some transactions may not have replicated at time of failure.<br>

515. [ RDS ] What is Enhanced Monitoring and how does it differ from CloudWatch RDS metrics? ; Enhanced Monitoring: agent-based OS-level metrics (CPU per process, memory breakdown, disk I/O per disk, network per interface) at 1-60 second granularity. CloudWatch RDS metrics: virtualization-level aggregates at 1-minute minimum granularity, no per-process detail. <br><br> *Why both exist: CloudWatch RDS metrics come from the hypervisor — they see aggregate CPU but not which process is consuming it. Enhanced Monitoring runs an agent INSIDE the OS — sees `mysqld` consuming 80% CPU specifically vs OS processes.<br> *Enhanced Monitoring storage: metrics go to CloudWatch Logs (not CloudWatch Metrics), under `/RDSOSMetrics` log group. Retention: set your own CloudWatch Logs retention. Parse with Logs Insights queries or export to OpenSearch for dashboards.<br> *Cost: Enhanced Monitoring has an additional cost per instance (based on granularity selected). At 1-second granularity on many instances: significant cost. Standard 60-second granularity is cheaper. For dev/test: disable. For production troubled instances: enable 1-second temporarily for diagnosis.<br>

516. [ RDS ] What is the maximum number of RDS read replicas for each engine? ; MySQL/MariaDB: 5 replicas (from source or chain). PostgreSQL: 5 replicas. Oracle: 5 replicas (Oracle Active Data Guard license required for >1). SQL Server: 5 replicas (Enterprise Edition only, Always On Availability Groups). Aurora: 15 replicas (Aurora-specific, not counted as RDS replicas). <br><br> *Oracle read replicas require Oracle EE license (Enterprise Edition) with Active Data Guard option. RDS does NOT include this license — you must BYOL (Bring Your Own License) or use Oracle EE license-included (LI) with the additional cost.<br> *SQL Server read replicas require Enterprise Edition + AlwaysOn Availability Groups. Not available on SQL Server Standard Edition in RDS. For SQL Server HA with read scale: upgrade to Enterprise or use RDS Multi-AZ DB Cluster (available for SQL Server EE).<br> *Read replica chain depth: MySQL allows replica-of-replica (source → replica → replica). Max chain depth is not documented but each hop increases lag. PostgreSQL: no chain replication in standard RDS PG. Aurora: all replicas read from shared storage directly — no replication chain concept.<br>

517. [ RDS ] What is RDS Custom and what are its two supported engines? ; RDS Custom: Oracle and SQL Server. Allows OS access (SSH/SSM), custom software installation, custom scripts, DBA tools. Trade-off: customer responsibility for OS patching (RDS Custom pauses automated patching when you enter automation-pause mode). Runs on dedicated EC2. <br><br> *RDS Custom use case: applications requiring Oracle Forms, Oracle E-Business Suite, SQL Server Integration Services (SSIS), or custom agents that need OS access. Standard RDS does not allow SSH access to the underlying OS.<br> *Automation-pause: before making OS-level changes in RDS Custom, enter automation-pause mode. RDS stops automatic patching, backup, and monitoring during pause. Resume automation after changes. Forgetting to resume: automated backups stop accruing, monitoring gaps occur.<br> *RDS Custom vs EC2 self-managed: RDS Custom still provides: automated backups to S3, Multi-AZ, read replicas, CloudWatch metrics. EC2 self-managed: full control but you manage everything. Use RDS Custom for compliance requiring OS access WITH managed database infrastructure.<br>

518. [ RDS ] What is the difference between RDS Oracle TDE (Transparent Data Encryption) vs KMS encryption? ; KMS encryption: AWS-managed at the EBS volume layer. Encrypts storage files, snapshots, backups. Managed by AWS KMS. TDE: Oracle-native encryption within the Oracle engine — encrypts individual tablespaces or columns within Oracle. Both can be used simultaneously (TDE inside KMS-encrypted storage = double encryption). <br><br> *TDE use case: Oracle applications that require Oracle-level encryption (e.g., Oracle Advanced Security license requirement, compliance mandating Oracle TDE specifically, Oracle Wallet management). Standard RDS KMS encryption does not satisfy Oracle TDE requirements.<br> *Oracle Wallet (TDE key store): RDS manages the Oracle Wallet automatically. You cannot export the wallet. If you move the database off RDS: you must use datapump export (re-encrypted) — cannot physically copy wallet to self-managed Oracle.<br> *Enabling TDE on RDS Oracle: add TDE option to an RDS option group, apply option group to the instance. TDE activation requires instance reboot. Once enabled: cannot be disabled. All existing tablespaces must be encrypted individually with `ALTER TABLESPACE ... ENCRYPTION ENCRYPT`.<br>

519. [ RDS ] What is the "storage full" state in RDS and how is it prevented? ; Storage full: RDS instance stops accepting writes. For MySQL: sets to read-only. For PostgreSQL: shuts down to prevent corruption. Prevention: enable Storage Auto Scaling (automatically grows storage) or set CloudWatch alarm on FreeStorageSpace < threshold. <br><br> *PostgreSQL storage full behavior: PostgreSQL REQUIRES free storage for WAL (Write Ahead Log) segments. When storage fills: Postgres can crash to prevent corruption. RDS detects this and may restart. This is more severe than MySQL's read-only mode.<br> *CloudWatch alarm for storage: `FreeStorageSpace` in bytes. Alarm threshold: 10% of allocated storage. For 1 TB instance: alarm at 100 GB free. Use the alarm to trigger manual review or SNS notification to the on-call engineer.<br> *Recovery from storage full: increase allocated storage (takes minutes, minimal downtime, can be done online). Delete old data/logs. For MySQL: `SET GLOBAL read_only=0` after freeing space (RDS automatically restores this if storage is freed). Do not wait for auto-scaling — if auto-scaling is not enabled or hasn't fired: instance is down.<br>

520. [ RDS ] What is the minimum and maximum backup retention period for RDS automated backups? ; Minimum: 0 days (disables automated backups). Maximum: 35 days. Default: 7 days. Setting to 0 disables: no automated backups, no PITR, no backup storage costs. Production: always set to at least 7 days. <br><br> *Disabling automated backups (retention=0): deletes all existing automated backups for that instance. Snapshots created manually are unaffected — manual snapshots persist until explicitly deleted. Retention=0 on production: catastrophic if an incident occurs — no PITR capability.<br> *Backup window: 30-minute daily window (set during create or modify). If you don't specify: RDS chooses a random window in the maintenance timezone. Best practice: set explicitly to a known low-traffic window. Backup I/O impact: on single-AZ instances, brief I/O pause. On Multi-AZ: backup taken from standby — no I/O impact on primary.<br> *Cross-region backup replication (separate from read replicas): available via "Automated backup replication" — automatically copies daily automated backups to a second region. Retention in secondary region: same as primary (up to 35 days). Enables cross-region PITR. Cost: standard S3 cross-region transfer + destination backup storage.<br>


521. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB transactional read/write API and what are its limits? ; TransactGetItems: up to 100 items or 4 MB in one atomic all-or-nothing read. TransactWriteItems: up to 100 operations (Put, Update, Delete, ConditionCheck) or 4 MB across items. Idempotent with `ClientRequestToken`. Costs 2× the normal read/write units. <br><br> *TransactWriteItems conditions: each operation can include a `ConditionExpression`. Additionally, include `ConditionCheck` operations that verify conditions on items NOT being modified (optimistic locking across multiple items). All conditions must pass — any failure = entire transaction fails.<br> *2× WCU cost: a transactional write to 5 items each consuming 1 WCU = 10 WCUs total (2× for transaction overhead). In On-Demand mode: 2× the request units. For high-transaction workloads: evaluate if all operations truly need atomic guarantees or if conditional writes (`ConditionExpression`) on individual items suffice (1× cost).<br> *Idempotency: `ClientRequestToken` (up to 36 chars, valid for 10 minutes) ensures the same token produces idempotent results. On retry: if the original succeeded → returns the original result without re-executing. If original failed → re-executes. Use for retried Lambda invocations sending payment transactions.<br>

522. [ DynamoDB ] What are the DynamoDB Global Tables conflict resolution rules? ; Last-writer-wins (LWW) based on timestamp. Each write carries an internal timestamp. On conflict (same item updated in two regions simultaneously): the write with the latest timestamp survives. Design consequence: avoid simultaneous writes to the same item from multiple regions (use sharding or region-affinity routing). <br><br> *Conflict scenario: user in us-east-1 updates `quantity=5`, simultaneously user in eu-west-1 updates `quantity=3`. Both writes propagate globally. Final state: whichever write had a later internal timestamp wins (potentially overwriting the other). This is "last-writer-wins" — no merge logic.<br> *Design to avoid conflicts: route all writes for a specific "shard key" (e.g., user_id) to one region. Route user:alice always to us-east-1, user:bob always to eu-west-1. Each item has a "home region" — writes from other regions still work but increase conflict probability.<br> *Version attribute pattern: if conflict resolution requires knowing which value "should" win, add a `version` attribute and use `ConditionExpression: version = :expected`. Failed conditions are NOT propagated to other regions — local failure, no conflict propagation. Monitor `ReplicationConflicts` CloudWatch metric.<br>

523. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB item collection and what is the 10 GB limit? ; An item collection = all items sharing the same partition key value (table + all its LSIs). LSI storage limit: 10 GB per partition key value across the table and all its LSIs. Exceeding: `ItemCollectionSizeLimitExceededException`. GSIs: NO per-collection limit. <br><br> *10 GB LSI limit is a hard constraint: if you store all orders for customer_id "1001" and use LSI, and that customer has more than 10 GB of order data → `ItemCollectionSizeLimitExceededException` on writes. Fix: redesign schema to distribute large customer data across multiple partition key values (add suffix sharding to customer_id).<br> *GSIs have no per-collection limit: GSIs project onto a different partition key, distributing data independently. This is one reason to prefer GSIs over LSIs — no collection size limit. LSI trade-off: LSI enables strongly consistent reads on the alternate key; GSI does not.<br> *Monitoring: no built-in CloudWatch metric for item collection size. You must query `DESCRIBE TABLE` and look at `ItemCollectionMetrics` in `BatchWriteItem` response, or use CloudWatch Contributor Insights to monitor large partition key values.<br>

524. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB PartiQL and how does it differ from native DynamoDB operations? ; PartiQL: SQL-compatible query language for DynamoDB. Supports SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE with SQL syntax. Does not change DynamoDB's underlying semantics (still key-value, filter expressions still scan). No JOINs. Performance implications identical to equivalent native operations — PartiQL is syntax sugar, not a new query engine. <br><br> *PartiQL does NOT enable joins: `SELECT * FROM Orders JOIN Customers ON Orders.customer_id = Customers.pk` — NOT SUPPORTED. DynamoDB has no join capability. Multi-table queries require application-side joins or use single-table design patterns.<br> *SELECT with no WHERE on partition key: same as `Scan` — reads every item. PartiQL `SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE status = 'pending'` = Scan + filter. All the cost and capacity implications of Scan apply. For query efficiency: always filter on partition key in WHERE.<br> *ExecuteTransaction in PartiQL: transactional PartiQL supported (`ExecuteTransaction` API). Mix SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE in one PartiQL transaction. Same 100-item, 4 MB limits as native `TransactWriteItems`. Useful for migrating relational code that uses SQL transactions.<br>

525. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB Streams event format and how long are records retained? ; DynamoDB Streams events: each stream record contains: table name, event type (INSERT/MODIFY/REMOVE), old image, new image, sequence number, timestamp. Retention: 24 hours. StreamViewType options: KEYS_ONLY, NEW_IMAGE, OLD_IMAGE, NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES. <br><br> *StreamViewType NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES: required if you need to compare before/after values (e.g., "what changed?"). Doubles stream record size vs NEW_IMAGE alone. For audit logging: NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES. For CDC pipelines that only need new state: NEW_IMAGE.<br> *Exactly-once semantics: DynamoDB Streams does NOT guarantee exactly-once delivery. Each stream record is delivered at-least-once to Lambda (duplicate delivery possible during retries). Make stream consumers idempotent. Lambda processor: check if item already processed (write processed sequence numbers to DynamoDB or SQS deduplication).<br> *24-hour retention: if your Lambda consumer fails for >24 hours, stream records are lost. Set Lambda event source mapping error handling: `BisectBatchOnFunctionError`, `DestinationConfig` (failed records → SQS DLQ). Monitor `IteratorAgeMilliseconds` — if > 3,600,000 (1 hour), consumer is falling behind.<br>

526. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB DAX (DynamoDB Accelerator) cache hit vs miss behavior? ; Cache hit: DAX returns response in microseconds from in-memory cache. Cache miss: DAX performs the DynamoDB operation (reads item from DynamoDB, caches result, returns to application). Write operations: write-through (DAX writes to DynamoDB first, then updates cache). DAX TTL: item-level cache TTL (default 5 minutes for reads). <br><br> *DAX cluster consistency: DAX provides eventually consistent reads only. Strongly consistent reads bypass DAX and go directly to DynamoDB. If your application uses `ConsistentRead=True`: those requests skip DAX entirely — no acceleration. If <10% of reads need consistency: DAX still provides significant benefit for the 90%.<br> *DAX and write patterns: every write goes through DynamoDB (DAX does not buffer writes). DAX write-through: after DynamoDB acknowledges the write, DAX updates the cache. If the DAX node fails mid-write: DynamoDB has the authoritative data; cache inconsistency is resolved by the TTL expiry + next miss.<br> *DAX cluster sizing: dax.r6g.xlarge (32 GB RAM) to dax.r6g.8xlarge (256 GB RAM). Size based on working set (hot items fitting in memory). If DAX hit rate < 80%: either working set > cluster memory (increase node size) or access pattern doesn't repeat (DAX provides less benefit — reconsider).<br>

527. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB On-Demand vs Provisioned capacity recommendation framework? ; On-Demand: no capacity planning, pay per request, auto-scales instantly. Best for: unpredictable traffic, new tables, spiky workloads, dev/test. Provisioned + Auto Scaling: plan capacity, lower cost at predictable high throughput. Best for: steady or predictable traffic with occasional spikes, production tables at scale. <br><br> *Cost crossover: On-Demand costs ~7× more per WCU/RCU compared to Provisioned at full utilization. If your table runs at >15% utilization of provisioned capacity: Provisioned + Auto Scaling is cheaper. At <15% utilization: On-Demand (no waste from idle provisioned capacity).<br> *Auto Scaling lag: Provisioned + Auto Scaling scales up within 1-3 minutes. Short spikes (< 1 minute of burst): burst capacity absorbs them. Medium spikes (2-5 minutes): may cause throttling while scaling fires. On-Demand: no throttling lag — instant scale. For traffic patterns with sharp spikes (marketing campaigns): On-Demand or increase provisioned buffer.<br> *Switching: can switch from Provisioned to On-Demand and back. Switch has a cooldown (1 switch per table per 24 hours). Do not switch On-Demand → Provisioned right before a major traffic event — you need time to set correct provisioned values.<br>

528. [ DynamoDB ] What are the scan vs query capacity unit consumption differences? ; Query: reads only items matching the partition key. Consumes RCUs based on returned item sizes. Scan: reads EVERY item in the table or index. Consumes RCUs for all items scanned, not just returned. A Scan with filter expression still consumes RCUs for all scanned items — filter reduces returned data, not capacity consumed. <br><br> *Parallel Scan: splits the table into N logical segments (up to N workers scan in parallel). Finishes faster but N× the RCU consumption vs sequential scan. For one-time data migrations: acceptable. For production patterns: never use Scan for regular data access — always convert to Query by adjusting partition key design.<br> *Scan Limit parameter: limits the number of items EVALUATED (not returned). `Limit=100` on a Scan: reads 100 items, applies filter, returns matching subset. Consumes RCUs for 100 items regardless of how many pass the filter. For large table scans in Lambda: use Limit=100 with pagination (LastEvaluatedKey) to avoid timeout and smooth RCU consumption.<br> *Table size estimation: `DescribeTable` returns approximate `TableSizeBytes` and `ItemCount` (updated every 6 hours). For recent large imports: actual size may differ. For capacity planning: use CloudWatch `SuccessfulRequestLatency` and `ConsumedReadCapacityUnits` from actual workload — more accurate than estimates.<br>

529. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB Conditional Write and why is it important for distributed systems? ; Conditional Write: `PutItem`/`UpdateItem`/`DeleteItem` with `ConditionExpression`. DynamoDB evaluates condition atomically before applying the write. If condition fails: `ConditionalCheckFailedException` (no write). Enables optimistic concurrency control without locks. <br><br> *Optimistic locking pattern: read item (get `version=5`), compute new values, write with `ConditionExpression: version = 5`, increment version to 6. If another process wrote version=6 before you: your write fails (`ConditionalCheckFailedException`). Retry by re-reading. No pessimistic lock needed.<br> *`attribute_not_exists(pk)` condition: the most common — prevent overwriting an existing item. `PutItem` normally replaces existing item. With `ConditionExpression: attribute_not_exists(pk)`: only succeeds if item does not exist. Use for creating unique items (user registration: create account only if email not taken).<br> *`attribute_exists(pk)` update: ensures you only update an item that exists. Prevents phantom updates (writing to a non-existent item, which would create it). `UpdateItem` with `ConditionExpression: attribute_exists(pk)` → fails if item was deleted before update arrives.<br>

530. [ DynamoDB ] What is the recommended DynamoDB key design for a time-series workload to avoid hot partitions? ; Avoid using a plain timestamp as partition key (all "current second" writes hit one partition). Solutions: (1) Write sharding — add random suffix: `pk = "sensor:2026-10-30T10:15#3"` (1-N shards). (2) Composite partition key — `pk = device_id + "#" + date_bucket`. (3) Year/month/day as sort key with device_id as partition key. <br><br> *Write sharding N selection: N shards = N× write capacity on the time bucket. For 10,000 writes/second to a single minute bucket: N=10 → 1,000 writes/shard — within the 1,000 WCU/partition limit. Trade-off: reads must query all N shards (fan-out query) and aggregate results in application.<br> *Date bucketing by partition key: `pk = "sensor:2026-10-30"` for daily aggregation. Sort key = timestamp. Allows efficient Query by day. But if a popular date has >1,000 WPS: that partition throttles. Add device_id to partition key: `pk = "sensor:device001:2026-10-30"` — distributes further but requires knowing device_id for queries.<br> *TTL for time-series cleanup: set TTL on items to expire old data (e.g., older than 90 days). TTL deletes are asynchronous, free (no WCU), and appear in DynamoDB Streams (REMOVE event) — can trigger archival Lambda to move expired data to S3 Glacier before deletion.<br>


531. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift Spectrum maximum external table partition count? ; Redshift Spectrum partition limit: up to 10,000 partitions per external table (for a single query scan). Beyond 10,000 partitions: use partition pruning carefully, and consider partition projection (predicate pushdown) to reduce scanned partitions. Underlying data in S3 has no limit. <br><br> *Partition projection: Redshift external tables support partition projection — instead of Glue Data Catalog managing physical partitions, Redshift generates partition values at query time from expression rules. Eliminates Glue partition metadata overhead. Useful for date-partitioned tables with thousands of date partitions.<br> *Predicate pushdown to Spectrum: Redshift pushes filter predicates (WHERE clause on partition columns) to Spectrum workers — only matching S3 objects are read. Without partition pruning: Spectrum reads all S3 partitions (full scan, expensive). Design: always partition external tables by query date range.<br> *Cost model: Spectrum charges per TB of S3 data scanned ($5/TB). Every extra partition scanned = direct cost. Partition by year/month/day for time-series data; compress with Parquet/ORC columnar format; use column projection to select only needed columns. Optimal Spectrum: 10 GB scanned vs 10 TB scanned = 1000× cost difference.<br>

532. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift COPY command and what data formats does it support? ; COPY: parallel load from S3, DynamoDB, EMR, SSH. Formats: CSV, JSON, Avro, Parquet, ORC, fixed-width, custom delimiters. GZIP/BZIP2 compressed files supported. COPY is the recommended bulk load method — uses all slices in parallel for maximum throughput. <br><br> *COPY parallelism: each file in S3 is assigned to one slice. For maximum parallelism: split source data into as many files as (nodes × slices per node). For a 2-node dc2.large (2 slices/node = 4 total slices): split source data into 4+ files. One large file = one slice processes everything = slow.<br> *COPY MANIFEST: use a manifest JSON file to specify exact S3 files to load. Ensures load exactly the files you intend, even if the S3 prefix has extra files. Mandatory entries use `"mandatory": true` — COPY fails if file not present. Prevents partial loads from partially arrived S3 data.<br> *COPY vs INSERT: COPY throughput: millions of rows/minute. INSERT row-by-row from ETL: thousands of rows/minute. For any bulk load (>1,000 rows): always use COPY. For streaming inserts (Kinesis Firehose → Redshift): use COPY via S3 buffer (Firehose batches to S3 every 60-900 seconds, then Redshift triggers COPY).<br>

533. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift sort key selection strategy? ; Compound sort key: good when queries filter on multiple columns in ORDER (pk + sk). Interleaved sort key: equally weights multiple columns for independent filtering (better for ad-hoc analytics; maintenance overhead higher). Single column: simplest — choose the column most frequently used in WHERE and JOIN. <br><br> *Compound vs interleaved: Compound key `(year, month, day)` is efficient for `WHERE year=2026 AND month=10` but inefficient for `WHERE month=10` alone (year must be the leading key). Interleaved key `(year, month, day)` interleaved = each dimension weighted equally — `WHERE month=10` is efficient. Trade-off: interleaved VACUUM+ANALYZE is 3-4× slower than compound.<br> *Sort key and column compression: sort key columns should NOT use BYTEDICT or RUNLENGTH compression (reduces sort order efficiency). Use RAW encoding for sort key columns; apply compression only to non-sort-key columns.<br> *Zone maps: Redshift maintains min/max zone maps per 1 MB block for each sort key column. Sort key must have high cardinality for effective zone maps — a boolean sort key has cardinality 2 → zone maps eliminate few blocks. Use sort keys on timestamp, ID, or other high-cardinality columns.<br>

534. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Concurrency Scaling, and when should you enable it? ; Concurrency Scaling: on-demand read-only capacity that automatically activates when queue depth/wait time exceeds threshold. Handles read query bursts without affecting the main cluster. Charged per second (1 free hour/day per cluster). Write queries: do NOT route to concurrency scaling clusters. <br><br> *Enabling: in WLM (Workload Management) queue settings, set `concurrency_scaling = auto` for the queue. When queue depth > defined threshold: Redshift auto-provisions a transient cluster, routes new queries there. Queries see consistent data (data is on the main cluster, concurrency scaling clusters query it via network).<br> *1 free hour/day: Redshift provides 1 hour of free Concurrency Scaling per day per cluster (accumulates up to 30 hours). Above free tier: ~$0.37/node-hour (dc2.large pricing). For burst-tolerant reporting: free tier often covers occasional surges. For sustained burst: cost can accumulate.<br> *Not a replacement for Elastic Resize: Elastic Resize changes the cluster's permanent node count/type. Concurrency Scaling adds temporary burst capacity for reads only. If write throughput is the bottleneck or permanent larger cluster needed: Elastic Resize. For read-only query burst: Concurrency Scaling.<br>

535. [ Redshift ] What is the difference between Redshift Elastic Resize and Classic Resize? ; Elastic Resize: adds/removes nodes to existing cluster, typically 10-15 minutes, cluster remains available (brief connection drops during reconfig). Classic Resize: changes node type or makes large node-count changes, requires cluster rebuild, hours to days for large clusters, cluster read-only during resize. <br><br> *Elastic Resize constraints: node type MUST stay the same. 1 Elastic Resize per 24 hours (can schedule). Maximum node count change: 2× or 0.5× the current count. Example: 4 nodes → 8 nodes (Elastic Resize). 4 nodes → 24 nodes: not possible with Elastic Resize → requires Classic Resize.<br> *Classic Resize and RA3: RA3 nodes separate compute from managed storage (Redshift Managed Storage). Classic Resize on RA3 is faster than dc2 Classic Resize because only metadata moves, not the full data (data stays in RMS). RA3 Classic Resize: hours. DC2 Classic Resize: hours to days (copies all data).<br> *Exam tip: "fastest way to double Redshift cluster capacity" → Elastic Resize (15 minutes, minimal disruption). "Change node type from dc2 to ra3" → Classic Resize (requires full rebuild). "Temporary double capacity for month-end" → Elastic Resize + scale back after.<br>

536. [ Redshift ] What are the Redshift distribution styles and when to use each? ; EVEN: rows round-robined across slices — use when table rarely joins. KEY: rows with same key value on same slice — use for large join tables (eliminate redistribution). ALL: full copy on every slice — use for small dimension tables (eliminates broadcast). AUTO: default — Redshift chooses (ALL for small, EVEN for large). DISTSTYLE DIRECT: for external/Spectrum tables. <br><br> *JOIN optimization with KEY: large fact table (diststyle KEY on `order_id`) + large dimension table (diststyle KEY on `order_id`) JOIN on `order_id` → both tables' rows for the same order_id are co-located on the same slice → no redistribution needed. This is the ideal join scenario.<br> *ALL distribution cost: each write operation (INSERT, COPY, DELETE) must write to all nodes. For a 16-node cluster: ALL-distributed table has 16 copies of every row — 16× write cost. Use ALL ONLY for small dimension tables (<1 GB) that change infrequently and are used in many joins.<br> *Checking redistribution: `EXPLAIN SELECT...` output. DS_DIST_NONE = no redistribution (ideal). DS_DIST_INNER = one join table is redistributed. DS_BROADCAST = small table broadcast. DS_DIST_ALL_INNER = large table broadcast (worst — reconsider distribution style).<br>

537. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Data Sharing and how does it enable cross-cluster access? ; Data Sharing: share live data (no copy) between Redshift clusters in the same or different AWS accounts/regions. Producer cluster creates datashare objects. Consumer clusters query data as read-only without data movement. Near-zero latency since data is read from RA3/Managed Storage directly. <br><br> *Cross-account Data Sharing: producer (Account A) creates datashare, grants access to Account B. Consumer (Account B) associates the datashare, creates external database from it. Consumer queries producer's data in real time. Billing: data transfer charges apply for cross-region, within-region is free.<br> *Data Sharing constraints: producer must be RA3 cluster or Serverless (not dc2). Consumer must be RA3 or Serverless. dc2 clusters cannot participate as producers. If migrating to Data Sharing: must first migrate to RA3 nodes.<br> *Use case: central data warehouse (prod) shared with multiple analytics teams' clusters. Each team has a smaller Redshift cluster for isolation (one team's runaway query doesn't affect prod). Central data is always current — no ETL pipeline between producer and consumers.<br>

538. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift VACUUM command and why is it needed? ; VACUUM: reclaims storage from deleted/updated rows and re-sorts data into sort key order. DELETE in Redshift marks rows as "ghost rows" — visible to existing transactions but not physically removed. VACUUM physically removes them and compacts storage. Run after large DELETE operations. <br><br> *VACUUM types: VACUUM FULL (re-sort + reclaim), VACUUM SORT ONLY (re-sort, no reclaim), VACUUM DELETE ONLY (reclaim storage, no re-sort), VACUUM REINDEX (rebuild interleaved sort key statistics). For routine maintenance: schedule VACUUM after large deletes/updates.<br> *Automatic VACUUM: Redshift performs automatic background vacuum during idle periods (VACUUM SORT ONLY and VACUUM DELETE ONLY). Manual VACUUM still needed for interleaved sort keys (automatic vacuum skips REINDEX). For tables with interleaved sort keys: include VACUUM REINDEX in maintenance jobs.<br> *ANALYZE: updates statistics for the query planner. Run after large data loads or bulk changes. `VACUUM ANALYZE` in one command. Without fresh statistics: planner chooses suboptimal join orders and wrong index usage → slow queries. Redshift also auto-analyzes, but for large imports: manual ANALYZE immediately after COPY.<br>

539. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift Serverless and how does it differ from provisioned Redshift? ; Redshift Serverless: no cluster management (no nodes to choose), auto-scales RPUs (Redshift Processing Units) based on workload, pay per RPU-second. Provisioned: choose node type + count, pay per node-hour continuously. Serverless base capacity: 8-512 RPUs (adjustable). <br><br> *RPU pricing: ~$0.375/RPU-hour. At 8 RPU (base) for 1 hour: $3. Provisioned dc2.large 2-node: ~$0.50/node-hour × 2 = $1/hour continuously. Serverless cost advantage: scales to 0 during idle periods (no active queries). Provisioned is always on. For <8 hours/day usage: Serverless often cheaper.<br> *Serverless limitations: no concurrency scaling (built-in auto-scale serves the same purpose). No resize (RPU range is the scaling parameter). No RA3 Data Sharing as producer to/from provisioned (cross-cluster sharing between Serverless and provisioned is supported with some limitations — check current docs).<br> *Exam use case: "analytics team runs ad-hoc queries during business hours (8am-6pm), cluster idle nights/weekends" → Serverless (no idle cost, auto-scales). "24/7 ETL pipeline loading into data warehouse" → Provisioned RA3 (steady workload, provisioned cheaper).<br>

540. [ Redshift ] What is the WLM (Workload Management) and what are its queue types? ; WLM: routes queries to queues based on user groups, query groups, or SageMaker Machine Learning labels. Queue types: Superuser queue (DBA priority), User-defined queues, Default queue. Auto WLM: Redshift manages concurrency and memory allocation per queue dynamically. Manual WLM: you set concurrency (1-50) and memory % per queue. <br><br> *Auto WLM (recommended): Redshift observes actual query memory requirements and adjusts concurrency. No manual tuning needed. Best for: mixed-workload clusters with varying query types. Manual WLM: lock in specific concurrency for specific workload types (ETL queue: 2 concurrent, 40% memory; reporting queue: 5 concurrent, 30% memory).<br> *Priority queues: Short Query Acceleration (SQA) — classifies fast queries (<20 seconds) and routes them to a dedicated queue. Prevents short analytical queries from waiting behind long ETL jobs. Enable in WLM settings. No additional cost.<br> *Queue hopping: if a query exceeds its queue's execution time limit, it's cancelled (returns error). Queue hopping (moving to next queue) is NOT automatic in standard WLM — queries are NOT re-queued in a different queue. Design: separate ETL (long-running) and BI (short) into different queues with appropriate time limits.<br>


541. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune instance RAM recommendation, and why is it important? ; Neptune works best when the working set (active graph data + indices) fits in RAM. Undersized RAM → frequent disk I/O → significantly degraded query performance. Recommended: provision instances where the active dataset (typically 20-30% of total graph size) fits in RAM. Start with r5/r6g instances. <br><br> *Page cache warming: after a failover or restart, Neptune's buffer pool cache is cold. Queries that previously ran fast (from cache) are now I/O-bound until cache warms up. For production clusters: use the "replica" to pre-warm cache before failing over the writer. Or use automated pre-warming scripts.<br> *Buffer pool hit rate: Neptune exposes `BufferPoolHits` and `BufferPoolMisses` CloudWatch metrics. Hit rate = Hits / (Hits + Misses). Target: >99% hit rate for OLTP graph workloads. <90% hit rate: graph doesn't fit in RAM — scale up instance class.<br> *Neptune ML and instance sizing: Neptune ML (Amazon SageMaker integration for GNN training) requires separate SageMaker instances. Neptune cluster handles graph serving; SageMaker handles training. Neptune export to S3 (via Neptune bulk export) feeds SageMaker training jobs.<br>

542. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune openCypher query language and how does it compare to Gremlin? ; openCypher: declarative graph query language (originally Neo4j). Pattern matching with MATCH, WHERE, RETURN syntax. Gremlin: Apache TinkerPop traversal language, imperative step-by-step graph traversal. Neptune supports both — same underlying graph, different query paradigms. Also supports SPARQL for RDF. <br><br> *openCypher example: `MATCH (n:Person {name: 'Alice'})-[:KNOWS]->(m:Person) RETURN m.name`. Gremlin equivalent: `g.V().has('Person', 'name', 'Alice').out('KNOWS').values('name')`. Both query the same graph. Choose based on team familiarity and query complexity.<br> *SPARQL use case: RDF (Resource Description Framework) data model — subject/predicate/object triples. Used for semantic web, knowledge graphs, ontology-based queries. SPARQL QUERY returns triples matching patterns. Neptune supports SPARQL 1.1. Most new Neptune deployments use Gremlin or openCypher — SPARQL for legacy RDF/semantic-web workloads.<br> *Multi-model consistency: Neptune allows using Gremlin and openCypher on the SAME property graph data. You can query with Gremlin and then query the same vertices/edges with openCypher. SPARQL operates on a separate RDF model (not mixed with property graph).<br>

543. [ Neptune ] How does Neptune handle large graph traversals that return many results? ; For large result sets: use pagination with traversal limits. Neptune does not support lazy streaming of infinite result sets — queries must complete within the execution timeout. Use `limit()` in Gremlin, `LIMIT N` in openCypher, pagination tokens for subsequent pages. Large unbound traversals: timeout or OOM. <br><br> *Query timeout: Neptune default query timeout is 120 seconds (configurable via `neptune_query_timeout` DB parameter). A traversal exploring millions of nodes will timeout. Design queries to page: `g.V().has('type','product').range(0, 1000)`, then `range(1000, 2000)`, etc.<br> *Materialized views pattern: for expensive aggregation queries (e.g., "degree centrality of all nodes"), compute pre-materialized properties as vertex attributes. Nightly batch traversal stores `degree_centrality` as a property. Real-time query reads the property instead of computing traversal.<br> *Memory limits: Neptune has per-query memory limits. Large intermediate result sets (many millions of items in `.as()` steps, or complex `path()` operations) can exceed memory limits → query killed with OOM error. Decompose into smaller queries; avoid `path()` in large traversals (path tracking multiplies memory usage).<br>

544. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune bulk loader, and what are its format requirements? ; Neptune bulk loader: loads data from S3 in CSV (vertex/edge files), RDF (N-Triples, Turtle, etc.), or JSON. Requires S3 permissions (IAM role on the Neptune cluster). Initiated via Neptune Load API or console. Incremental and full loads supported. Does NOT require cluster restart. <br><br> *CSV format for property graph: vertex CSV (required columns: `~id`, `~label`, then properties). Edge CSV (required: `~id`, `~from`, `~to`, `~label`, then properties). Each file contains one vertex or edge type. Multiple files in one load job supported via manifest or S3 prefix.<br> *Load modes: RESUME (restart from last checkpoint if interrupted), BEGIN (start fresh), AUTO (Neptune decides). For large datasets: use RESUME mode so a network interruption doesn't restart the entire load.<br> *Concurrency: Neptune bulk loader supports concurrent loads (multiple load jobs simultaneously, up to 2 by default). Each load job processes S3 objects in parallel using Neptune's storage layer. For fastest load: split data into many small S3 files (load parallelism matches file count).<br>

545. [ Neptune ] What is Neptune's cross-region replication capability (as of mid-2026)? ; Neptune does NOT have native cross-region replication or Global Clusters. For cross-region DR: (1) Neptune bulk export → S3 → S3 Cross-Region Replication → Neptune bulk load in DR region (RPO: hours). (2) Neptune Streams → Lambda → write to DR Neptune (custom CDC pipeline, RPO: seconds to minutes). No managed cross-region like Aurora Global Database. <br><br> *Neptune Streams: change capture stream for Neptune graph — captures vertex/edge inserts, updates, deletes. 7-day retention. Consumer (Lambda) reads stream, applies changes to a DR Neptune cluster. This is a manually-built CDC pipeline — not a managed feature.<br> *RTO for Neptune DR: even with Neptune Streams CDC, the DR cluster is read-only during normal operation (it's a CDc-replicated copy). On failover: update DNS/application connection string to DR cluster, convert to active (it becomes the writer). No automated managed failover across regions — manual process.<br> *Exam implication: "Neptune with RPO <1 second cross-region" is NOT achievable with built-in features (no Global Clusters). Options: custom CDC via Streams. If exam asks "which AWS managed service provides cross-region replication with RPO <1 second for a graph database" → none natively (Neptune lacks this). Consider using DynamoDB if the graph use case can be adapted to key-value.<br>

546. [ Neptune ] What are the Neptune query execution plan tools and how do you use them? ; `explain()` (Gremlin): `g.V().explain()` shows query plan (strategy: iterateRange, CountGlobalStep, etc.). Neptune console visual explain plan. Neptune Query Stats API. CloudWatch metrics: `gremlinRequestsPerSec`, `sparqlRequestsPerSec`, query latency percentiles. <br><br> *`explain()` output: shows which traversal steps are optimized (pushed to storage layer) vs evaluated in-memory. Optimized steps: use indices efficiently. Non-optimized steps ("filtering" in explain): evaluated item-by-item. Non-optimized steps on large datasets = slow traversal. Refactor queries to use optimized steps.<br> *Neptune index: automatically maintained indices for `g.V().has(label, key, value)` lookups (vertex label + property), edge direction traversals (`out()`, `in()`), and `g.E().has(label)` edge lookups. Gremlin traversals leveraging these indices are fast. Full graph scans (no index usage) are O(total vertices/edges).<br> *Query profiling: use Neptune's `?profile=true` parameter in HTTP API calls for Gremlin/openCypher. Returns execution statistics including time per step, data accessed, and cache hits. More detailed than `explain()` for diagnosing specific slow steps.<br>

547. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune instance endpoint vs cluster endpoint routing? ; Cluster endpoint: routes to the primary writer instance (changes on failover). Reader endpoint: routes to one of the reader instances (load-balanced). Instance endpoint: direct connection to a specific instance (doesn't change on failover). For applications: always use cluster/reader endpoints, not instance endpoints. <br><br> *Failover DNS propagation: Neptune cluster endpoint DNS TTL is 30 seconds (same as Aurora). Application connection pools: respect DNS TTL. Neptune drivers often have built-in failover reconnection. Gremlin clients using Gremlin Server connections: detect connection error and reconnect (hitting new primary via cluster endpoint DNS).<br> *Custom endpoints (Neptune): Neptune does NOT support Aurora-style custom endpoints for routing specific query types to specific instances. Workaround: use instance endpoints directly for dedicated analytics queries, cluster endpoint for OLTP. Manage routing in application tier.<br> *Neptune read scaling: unlike Aurora (15 read replicas), Neptune also supports up to 15 replicas but graph workloads are usually write-heavy during bulk load and read-heavy during analytics. Use reader endpoint load-balancing during analytics phase; direct writer endpoint during bulk load.<br>

548. [ Neptune ] What are the Neptune IAM authentication options? ; IAM DB authentication: requires signing requests with AWS SigV4 signature (supported in Neptune SDK/drivers). Alternatively: VPC security group controls (no auth if inside VPC). Neptune does NOT support username/password authentication within the engine — all auth is at network level (SG) or request level (IAM SigV4). <br><br> *IAM auth for Neptune: use `NeptuneFullAccess` or custom IAM policy allowing `neptune-db:*` actions. IAM auth is optional — without it, any EC2 in the VPC with network access to Neptune can query it (SG controls only). For multi-tenant applications: enable IAM auth + per-service IAM roles with least privilege.<br> *TLS/SSL: Neptune enforces TLS. Connections without TLS are refused. Download Neptune's CA certificate from `https://truststore.pki.rds.amazonaws.com/global/global-bundle.pem`. Configure Gremlin/SPARQL/openCypher clients with the CA cert for SSL verification.<br> *VPC-only access: Neptune is NOT accessible from the internet. All connections must originate from within the same VPC or connected VPC (VPC peering/Transit Gateway). No public endpoint option. For external access: use a Gremlin proxy/jump host in the VPC, or API Gateway → Lambda → Neptune pattern.<br>

549. [ Neptune ] What Neptune CloudWatch metrics indicate cluster health issues? ; Key metrics: `CPUUtilization` (high = query overload), `GremlinRequestsPerSec` + `SparqlRequestsPerSec` (throughput), `BufferPoolHits/Misses` (cache efficiency), `ReadLatency/WriteLatency` (P99 latency), `NetworkReceiveThroughput` (replication lag proxy), `ServerlessMaxInstanceCapacity` (for Serverless). <br><br> *`WritesAverage` vs `ReadsAverage`: average writes and reads per second across all instances. Baseline these metrics during normal load. Alert when: CPU > 80%, latency P99 > 3× median (spike indicator), BufferPoolHits ratio < 95%.<br> *Replica lag: Neptune uses `AuroraReplicaLag` CloudWatch metric (shared infrastructure with Aurora storage). High replica lag: replicas are falling behind the writer. Reads from a lagging replica return stale data. For consistency-sensitive reads: connect to primary writer (cluster endpoint).<br> *`VolumeBytesUsed`: total storage used by the Neptune cluster (Aurora-like distributed storage). Monitor this against the 128 TiB limit. Growth rate projection: if growing 100 GB/month → 128 TiB capacity in ~10 years (typical). For large graph databases approaching limits: consider pruning or archiving historical edges/vertices.<br>

550. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune notebook integration and what is it used for? ; Neptune Workbench: Jupyter notebooks hosted on SageMaker, pre-configured with graph visualizations and Neptune SDK (neptune-python-utils, `%gremlin`, `%sparql`, `%oc` magics). Used for: graph data exploration, algorithm prototyping, Neptune ML workflow, graph visualizations (D3.js). Not for production query serving. <br><br> *`%gremlin` magic: run Gremlin queries in notebook cells, visualize graph results as interactive node-edge graphs (D3.js). `%oc` (openCypher) magic: run Cypher-style queries. Both connect to the Neptune cluster endpoint configured in the notebook environment.<br> *Neptune ML Jupyter workflow: (1) Export Neptune graph to S3 via `neptune-export` tool. (2) Process with `%neptune_ml export` magic. (3) Train GNN on SageMaker. (4) Deploy Neptune ML inference endpoint. (5) Query Neptune with ML predictions via `WITH neptuneML` Gremlin extension.<br> *Cost: Neptune Workbench uses SageMaker notebook instances (ml.t3.medium = ~$0.046/hour). Stop notebooks when not in use. Neptune Workbench notebooks persist in S3 (notebook lifecycle tied to SageMaker notebook instance). For production graph analytics: use SageMaker Studio over Workbench.<br>


551. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache Redis maxmemory policy, and which policy to use for a session cache? ; maxmemory-policy options: `noeviction` (returns error when memory full), `allkeys-lru` (evict least recently used among all keys), `volatile-lru` (LRU among keys with TTL), `allkeys-random`, `volatile-random`, `volatile-ttl` (evict nearest expiry first). For session cache: `allkeys-lru` or `volatile-lru` (sessions have TTL). <br><br> *noeviction pitfall: if maxmemory is hit and policy is noeviction, Redis returns OOM error to the client. Applications writing new data fail. Production caches MUST set an appropriate eviction policy. Use noeviction only for queues/counters where data loss is unacceptable — and ensure memory never fills.<br> *volatile-lru recommendation: best for mixed cache (some persistent keys + TTL-based cache entries). Persistent keys (no TTL) are never evicted. TTL-based cache entries are evicted by LRU when memory pressure. Set TTL on ALL cache entries you're okay losing; omit TTL on permanent data.<br> *allkeys-lru for pure cache: when ALL keys are cache entries (no permanent data), use allkeys-lru. Redis evicts cold keys regardless of TTL. Behaves like a standard LRU cache. Most common choice for application-level caches (API response cache, product catalog cache).<br>

552. [ ElastiCache ] What is the difference between ElastiCache Redis AUTH and RBAC? ; AUTH: single shared password for the entire cluster (set via AUTH command, configured as `auth-token` in ElastiCache). No per-user permissions. RBAC (Role-Based Access Control): per-user credentials with per-user permission policies (read-only, read-write, specific key patterns). RBAC requires Redis 6.0+. <br><br> *AUTH limitation: all clients share one password. If you rotate the password, all clients must update simultaneously. No granular control — a client with the password has full access. For multi-tenant or multi-service caches: RBAC provides service-level isolation.<br> *RBAC user policies: define user with ACL rules. `ON >password ~app:* &app:* +@read`: user enabled, password "password", can access keys matching `app:*`, can call commands in `@read` category. Restrict a reporting service to read-only on specific key prefix: `~report:* +@read -@write`.<br> *In-flight encryption + AUTH: ElastiCache enforces TLS for AUTH tokens. Auth token (password) is transmitted over encrypted connection. Without TLS: credentials exposed in network traffic. For ElastiCache Redis: always enable `transit-encryption-enabled` and `auth-token` together.<br>

553. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache Global Datastore feature? ; Global Datastore: cross-region active-passive replication for Redis. Primary region (active write + read). Secondary regions (read-only replicas with <1 second async replication lag). On failover: promote secondary to primary (takes ~1 minute, requires manual or automated promotion). <br><br> *Global Datastore vs DynamoDB Global Tables: DynamoDB Global Tables is ACTIVE-ACTIVE (write in any region). ElastiCache Global Datastore is ACTIVE-PASSIVE (write in primary only, read in all). For Redis: writes must go to primary region, reads can be served locally from secondary.<br> *Failover steps: (1) Detect primary region failure. (2) Call `failover-global-replication-group` API to promote secondary. (3) Update application connection strings to new primary endpoint. No automatic DNS failover — application/Route 53 health check routing must handle the switch.<br> *Global Datastore cost: charged for: primary cluster (normal ElastiCache pricing), secondary cluster (same node pricing), inter-region data transfer. Estimate: $0.02-$0.09/GB transferred (depends on regions). For frequently written data sets: cost can be significant.<br>

554. [ ElastiCache ] What is MemoryDB for Redis vs ElastiCache for Redis? ; MemoryDB: durable (Multi-AZ transaction log, data persists across restarts), acts as primary database. ElastiCache: cache tier (in-memory only, data can be lost on failure). MemoryDB: 99.99% availability SLA. ElastiCache: 99.99% with cluster mode enabled. MemoryDB: higher cost (~2-3× ElastiCache). <br><br> *MemoryDB durability: MemoryDB persists writes to a distributed transaction log (like Aurora storage) before acknowledging. On node failure: data is recovered from the transaction log — no data loss. ElastiCache with AOF (Append Only File): durability within a single node but not across AZ failures without replication.<br> *When to use MemoryDB: application uses Redis as its primary data store (not just cache), requires persistence, can tolerate slightly higher latency. When to use ElastiCache: application has a durable backing store (RDS, DynamoDB) and uses Redis for caching only — cache miss falls back to DB.<br> *Exam distinction: "session data that MUST survive Redis failures" → MemoryDB. "Session cache with DynamoDB as fallback" → ElastiCache. "Leaderboard data that must be durable" → MemoryDB. "Product catalog cache with Aurora as source of truth" → ElastiCache.<br>

555. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache Cluster Mode Enabled vs Disabled architecture? ; Cluster Mode Disabled: one shard (single primary + up to 5 replicas). All data on one shard — no horizontal data sharding. Scale: limited by single shard memory (up to r6g.16xlarge = 425 GB). Cluster Mode Enabled: up to 500 shards, each shard has primary + replicas. Data distributed across shards by key hash. <br><br> *Cluster Mode Enabled slot distribution: 16,384 hash slots distributed across shards. Key `user:alice` → `HASH_SLOT = CRC16(key) % 16384` → assigned shard. Client library (redis-cluster client) handles slot routing automatically. Applications must use cluster-aware client libraries (e.g., `ioredis` with cluster option, Python `redis-py-cluster`).<br> *Multi-key operations: Cluster Mode Enabled only supports multi-key operations (MSET, MGET, transactions) when all keys are in the same slot (use hash tags: `{user:alice}:session` and `{user:alice}:cart` both hash to the same slot — `{user:alice}` is the hash tag). Without hash tags: multi-key operations across slots fail.<br> *Scaling Cluster Mode Enabled: add/remove shards online (Online Resharding). Slots are redistributed with minimal impact. Scale from 5 shards to 10: takes minutes, cluster remains available. Cluster Mode Disabled: to scale horizontally, must enable cluster mode (requires cluster recreation).<br>

556. [ ElastiCache ] What is ElastiCache Memcached vs Redis, and when to choose each? ; Memcached: simple, multi-threaded, no persistence, no replication, no clustering with data affinity. Redis: single-threaded (per shard), data structures, persistence, replication, Cluster Mode. Choose Memcached: simple cache, need horizontal threads (CPU-bound, multi-core), no persistence, disposable. Choose Redis: data structures, Pub/Sub, Streams, persistence, HA, scripting. <br><br> *Memcached multi-threading: Memcached uses multiple threads per node, utilizing all CPU cores. Redis (pre-7.0) is single-threaded per shard. For CPU-bound workloads with many small cache operations: Memcached may outperform Redis per-node. With Redis Cluster Mode: distribute across many Redis shards to parallelize.<br> *ElastiCache Redis Valkey: AWS announced replacing Redis (GPL license) with Valkey (open-source Redis fork, BSD license) starting 2024. Valkey is AWS's managed Redis-compatible engine. Functionally equivalent to Redis 7.2 for application code. Exam likely still uses "ElastiCache for Redis" terminology.<br> *No use case for Memcached in new designs: AWS's investment is in Redis/Valkey. New features (Global Datastore, RBAC, MemoryDB) are Redis-only. Memcached remains for simple use cases but lacks operational features of Redis. For greenfield: always choose Redis.<br>

557. [ ElastiCache ] What is Redis pipelining and how does it improve performance? ; Pipelining: send multiple commands to Redis without waiting for individual responses. Client batches commands, server processes all, returns all responses at once. Reduces round-trip latency overhead from N commands × RTT to 1 RTT for the batch. <br><br> *Pipelining vs transactions: transactions (MULTI/EXEC) batch commands atomically (all-or-nothing). Pipelining is NOT atomic — if connection drops mid-pipeline: some commands may have executed. Use pipelining for performance (cache warmup, bulk reads); use MULTI/EXEC for atomicity (financial operations).<br> *Pipeline size: sweet spot is 50-1,000 commands per pipeline. Very large pipelines (10,000+) can cause memory pressure on the server-side output buffer. If pipeline triggers eviction: cluster may become unstable. Test pipeline sizes with representative workloads.<br> *Lua scripting vs pipelining: Lua scripts (`EVAL`/`EVALSHA`) are atomic and run server-side — no round trips for multi-step logic. For complex conditional operations (read-then-write with logic): use Lua. For bulk simple operations (MSET 500 keys): pipeline.
