Lua scripts are loaded once (`SCRIPT LOAD`) and reused by SHA hash — reduces latency for frequent complex operations.<br>

558. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache write-through caching pattern and when does it fail? ; Write-through: application writes to cache AND database (in same request, typically via application logic or cache proxy). Cache always has current data — no stale reads after writes. Fails when: database write succeeds but cache write fails (data in DB but not in cache) — next read causes cache miss + read from DB, self-healing. <br><br> *Write-through vs cache-aside: Cache-aside (lazy loading): on cache miss, read DB, populate cache, return. Writes go only to DB; cache is invalidated or expires. Cache-aside is simpler to implement but can have stale data between write and expiry. Write-through has current data but double-write cost on every write.<br> *Cache poisoning risk in write-through: if the cache write happens BEFORE the DB commit (wrong order): a crash between cache write and DB write leaves cache with data the DB never committed. Correct order: (1) Write to DB. (2) Write to cache. If step 2 fails: cache miss on next read → read from DB (self-healing).<br> *Write-through and TTL: even with write-through, set a TTL on all cache entries. TTL prevents stale cache if a write bypasses the cache layer (admin tools, migrations). TTL is a safety net, not a primary invalidation mechanism in write-through.<br>

559. [ ElastiCache ] What is Redis KEYS vs SCAN and why is KEYS dangerous in production? ; KEYS pattern: O(N) scan of all keys in keyspace, single-threaded operation, blocks Redis while executing. For 10M keys: blocks Redis for seconds → all other clients timeout. SCAN: iterates keys in chunks (cursor-based), O(N) total but non-blocking (small chunks processed between other commands). <br><br> *KEYS workaround for production: never use `KEYS *` in production. Alternatives: (1) Maintain key sets using Redis Sets (write `SADD active_sessions session:alice` when creating session). (2) Use namespace prefixes + SCAN with low COUNT for non-time-critical operations. (3) Design so you know the key you need (lookup key directly, never list all keys).<br> *SCAN COUNT parameter: `SCAN 0 COUNT 100` — not a hard limit but a hint. Redis may return more or fewer. SCAN continues until cursor returns 0. Total iterations = keyspace_size / COUNT (approximately). For 1M keys and COUNT 1000: ~1000 SCAN calls to iterate all keys. Non-blocking but many round trips.<br> *HSCAN, SSCAN, ZSCAN: same cursor-based iteration for Hash fields, Set members, and Sorted Set members. Use instead of HGETALL (blocked if hash has 1M fields), SMEMBERS (blocked if set has 1M members). Always use SCAN family for large data structures.<br>

560. [ ElastiCache ] How does ElastiCache for Redis handle failover, and what is the TTL behavior? ; On primary failure: automatic failover promotes a read replica to primary (within 60 seconds for standard, ~10 seconds for Cluster Mode). DNS endpoint updates. In-flight data on the failed primary is LOST (async replication). Cached data on surviving replicas is available immediately after failover. <br><br> *Data loss during failover: ElastiCache Redis with async replication: writes acknowledged by primary before replicated → primary fails → those writes are lost. For financial/critical data: use MemoryDB (transaction log, no data loss) or add DynamoDB as durable backend.<br> *Connection reconnection: applications must handle connection failure. Redis client reconnect logic: configure exponential backoff retry, max retry count. After primary failure + DNS propagation (30-60 seconds): client reconnects to new primary via same cluster endpoint DNS. Application sees connection error → retry → reconnect to new primary.<br> *Expired keys behavior during failover: if a key expired on the primary just before failover, the replica may not have processed the expiry yet. After promotion: the replica may still serve the expired key for up to 1 second (replication lag). `PERSIST TTL` on replicas: replicas maintain their own TTL timers independently (since Redis 3.2).<br>


561. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB change streams feature, and how does it compare to MongoDB Change Streams? ; DocumentDB Change Streams: change capture for inserts, updates, deletes. Retention: 3 hours (default) to 7 days (configurable). Consume via aggregation pipeline `$changeStream` stage. Compatible with MongoDB Change Streams API. Use case: CDC to Kafka, Lambda triggers, cross-region replication. <br><br> *Enable Change Streams: `db.adminCommand({modifyChangeStreams: 1, database: "mydb", collection: "orders", enable: true})`. Must enable per collection. Enabling applies to the DocumentDB cluster globally (not per instance). Disabling removes the stream buffer for that collection.<br> *Event types: `insert` (new document), `update` (modified document), `delete` (removed document), `replace` (full document replacement). `update` events include only the diff (changed fields), not the full document — use `fullDocumentLookup` option to request the full document state (extra read cost).<br> *Resume token: each change stream event has a resume token. On consumer failure: resume from last processed token (within retention window). Store resume tokens in a durable store (DynamoDB, S3). Lost resume token + events older than retention window: must re-initialize from a snapshot.<br>

562. [ DocumentDB ] What are the TLS enforcement differences between DocumentDB and MongoDB? ; DocumentDB: TLS ALWAYS enforced for connections to the cluster. Cannot be disabled. MongoDB (self-managed): TLS optional. Application code connecting to MongoDB without TLS: may work self-managed, fails on DocumentDB unless TLS is configured in the driver. Migration gotcha: applications assuming optional TLS need driver config update. <br><br> *Driver TLS config for DocumentDB: download Amazon RDS CA bundle (global-bundle.pem). Configure MongoDB driver with `ssl=true`, `ssl_ca_certs=/path/to/global-bundle.pem`. Example (Python pymongo): `MongoClient("mongodb://...", tls=True, tlsCAFile="/path/to/global-bundle.pem")`.<br> *DocumentDB port: 27017 (standard MongoDB port). If your MongoDB was on a custom port: update connection strings. DocumentDB also supports port 27016 as a secondary listener in some configurations — check current documentation.<br> *Compression: DocumentDB supports network compression (snappy, zlib). Enable in driver for bandwidth reduction (important for large document reads over slow connections). Not required but improves performance for document-heavy workloads.<br>

563. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB instance-based vs elastic cluster architecture? ; Instance-based (standard): provisioned instances (r5/r6g/t3), storage scales to 64 TiB, up to 15 replicas, Aurora-like distributed storage. Elastic Clusters (2023): serverless, scales writer throughput + storage automatically, supports sharding, up to 32 shards with 32 compute units per shard. <br><br> *Elastic Cluster sharding: DocumentDB Elastic Clusters automatically shard data using `_id` as the shard key by default. Applications don't need to specify a shard key. Under the hood: similar to MongoDB sharded clusters but fully managed. Scale: petabyte-scale with automatic resharding.<br> *Elastic Cluster limitations: as of 2024, Elastic Clusters have a subset of DocumentDB features (some aggregation pipeline stages, some index types not supported). Verify compatibility matrix before migrating to Elastic Clusters. Standard instance-based clusters have broader feature support.<br> *When to use Elastic: workloads that significantly exceed single-instance capacity (>300,000 writes/second), variable throughput needing serverless auto-scale, large document collections that outgrow standard 64 TiB limit. For moderate-scale: standard instance-based clusters are simpler and more feature-complete.<br>

564. [ DocumentDB ] What are the MongoDB-to-DocumentDB compatibility gaps customers commonly hit? ; Common gaps: `$lookup` (cross-collection join) limited aggregation pipeline operators, text search index (no full Atlas Search equivalent), geospatial limitations, transactions limited to 5 operations until DocumentDB 5.0, no `capped collections`, no `gridFS` native. Always run MongoDB compatibility report before migration. <br><br> *`$lookup` limitations: DocumentDB supports `$lookup` for left outer joins between collections in the same database. Complex `$lookup` with `let`/`pipeline` subqueries: limited support. For workloads heavily using `$lookup`: test each query in DocumentDB before committing to migration.<br> *Atlas Search equivalent: DocumentDB does not have Amazon OpenSearch-style full-text search built-in. Workaround: index DocumentDB Change Streams → Lambda → Amazon OpenSearch for full-text search tier. Queries requiring FTS: redirect to OpenSearch; other queries: DocumentDB.<br> *Driver compatibility: DocumentDB supports MongoDB 3.6, 4.0, and 5.0 API compatibility (engine version corresponds to compatibility). MongoDB 6.0+ features are NOT supported. Applications using 6.0+ features (time series collections, etc.) need refactoring before DocumentDB migration.<br>

565. [ DocumentDB ] What is DocumentDB's approach to ACID transactions? ; DocumentDB 4.0+: supports ACID transactions (`startSession().startTransaction()`). Multi-document atomic operations. MongoDB-compatible transaction API. DocumentDB 3.6: no multi-document transactions. Upgrade cluster to 4.0 or 5.0 engine for transactions. Within a transaction: up to 1,000 documents. <br><br> *Transaction isolation: DocumentDB transactions use snapshot isolation (read committed by default within transactions). Concurrent writes to the same document in separate transactions: last writer wins (optimistic concurrency). Use retry logic for transaction conflicts (`WriteConflict` error).<br> *Transaction timeout: DocumentDB transactions timeout after 60 seconds by default. Long-running transactions → automatic rollback. Keep transactions short: open transaction, make changes, commit — do not hold transactions open waiting for user input or external I/O.<br> *Transactions and replica lag: transactions committed on the writer are replicated to readers asynchronously. A read from a reader immediately after a transaction commit may return pre-transaction data. For read-after-write consistency: read from the primary (writer endpoint), or use causal consistency sessions with DocumentDB 4.0+.<br>

566. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB parameter group TTL index behavior? ; TTL indexes: DocumentDB supports TTL indexes on date fields for automatic document expiry. `db.orders.createIndex({expiresAt: 1}, {expireAfterSeconds: 0})`. Documents expire when `expiresAt` date passes. Background job runs every 60 seconds — documents may linger up to 60 seconds after TTL expiry. <br><br> *TTL index vs application-level delete: TTL index runs automatically — no application code needed. Documents deleted by TTL: appear in Change Streams (delete events). If consuming Change Streams for CDC: TTL deletes will appear — handle them in your consumer.<br> *Sharded TTL in Elastic Clusters: TTL indexes work in Elastic Clusters. The background TTL monitor runs on each shard. Documents are deleted from the shard holding them. No cross-shard coordination needed for TTL (TTL is per-document, evaluated per-shard independently).<br> *Partial TTL: DocumentDB supports partial indexes (documents matching a filter criteria get indexed). Combine with TTL: `db.collection.createIndex({ts: 1}, {expireAfterSeconds: 86400, partialFilterExpression: {status: "processed"}})` — only processed documents expire after 1 day. Active documents (status != "processed") are never expired by TTL.<br>

567. [ DocumentDB ] What are the DocumentDB storage encryption and key management options? ; Storage encryption: AES-256 at rest using AWS KMS. Configured at cluster creation — cannot enable/disable on existing clusters. Snapshots inherit cluster encryption. Cross-account snapshot copy: requires a customer-managed KMS key (CMK). IAM key policy grants the target account `kms:Decrypt` and `kms:ReEncrypt*`. <br><br> *Encryption in transit: DocumentDB enforces TLS for all connections (as noted in Q562). TLS certificates managed by AWS. Customer provides their own TLS CA bundle for driver validation. KMS encryption and TLS provide defense-in-depth: storage encrypted at rest + connections encrypted in transit.<br> *CMK vs AWS managed key: default encryption uses `aws/docdb` AWS-managed key. Cannot share snapshots encrypted with AWS-managed key to other accounts. For cross-account DR: create a CMK in KMS, use it for DocumentDB encryption at cluster creation. Grant target account access to the CMK.<br> *Snapshot sharing: encrypted snapshot can be shared cross-account. Target account must have access to the KMS key used for encryption. Target account copies the snapshot to their account (copies it to their own region if needed), re-encrypts with their own CMK, then restores. Full process: share KMS key access → share snapshot → target copies and re-encrypts → restore.<br>

568. [ DocumentDB ] How does DocumentDB handle index management for large collections? ; Background vs foreground index builds: DocumentDB builds indexes in the background (non-blocking). Build progress tracked via `db.currentOp()`. Large index builds on active collections: impact read latency during build (I/O competition). For new clusters/collections: build all indexes before loading data. <br><br> *Compound indexes: DocumentDB supports compound indexes (up to 32 fields). Key order matters: `{status: 1, createdAt: -1}` efficiently answers queries filtering on `status` and sorting by `createdAt`. Does NOT efficiently answer queries filtering only on `createdAt` (status must be in the filter for index prefix utilization).<br> *Index intersection: DocumentDB does NOT support index intersection (unlike some MongoDB versions). A query will use AT MOST one index. If a query needs both `status` and `amount` conditions: create a compound index `{status: 1, amount: 1}` rather than two separate indexes.<br> *`explain("executionStats")`: `db.collection.find({status: "active"}).explain("executionStats")`. Returns: `COLLSCAN` (full scan — needs index), `IXSCAN` (index scan — good), `nReturned`, `totalDocsExamined`. If `totalDocsExamined / nReturned > 100`: poor selectivity index or wrong index used.<br>

569. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB connection pooling recommendation? ; DocumentDB: use connection pooling in application layer. Max connections per instance: 2,500 (r6g.2xlarge), up to 11,000 (r6g.16xlarge). Without pooling: serverless/Lambda-based apps exhaust connections. Recommendation: DocumentDB Proxy (2024) or application-side pool with maxPoolSize=50-100 per process. <br><br> *Lambda connection exhaustion: Lambda scales to thousands of concurrent invocations, each potentially opening a new DocumentDB connection. 3,000 Lambda invocations × 1 connection each = 3,000 connections → exceeds most instance limits → `MongoServerError: too many connections`.<br> *Connection string pool config: `mongodb://...?maxPoolSize=50&minPoolSize=5&connectTimeoutMS=5000`. `maxPoolSize=50`: Lambda execution context reuses up to 50 connections (per Lambda container). Lambda containers themselves are pooled by AWS → actual total connections = concurrent Lambda containers × maxPoolSize.<br> *DocumentDB connection warmup: include MongoDB client initialization OUTSIDE the Lambda handler (module-level). Lambda execution context reuses initialized connections across invocations. Avoids connection overhead per invocation. Pattern: `let client; exports.handler = async (event) => { if (!client) { client = new MongoClient(uri); await client.connect(); } ... }`.<br>

570. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB Global Clusters feature RPO/RTO? ; DocumentDB Global Clusters (2024): storage-level cross-region replication, <1 second RPO. RTO: ~1 minute for managed failover (similar to Aurora Global Database). Secondary regions: read-only. Primary region: read+write. Planned failover: <30 seconds (detach secondary, promote, reconnect). Unplanned: ~1 minute. <br><br> *Global Clusters topology: one primary region (read+write cluster), up to 5 secondary regions (read-only clusters). Each cluster in each region has its own instances. Global replication happens at the storage layer — no application-level replication.<br> *Global Clusters and elastic vs instance-based: check current AWS documentation for Global Clusters with Elastic Clusters vs Instance-based clusters (feature availability may differ). As of 2024: Global Clusters announced for instance-based DocumentDB clusters first.<br> *Cost model: Global Clusters charge for: each cluster (normal DocumentDB pricing), cross-region data transfer ($0.02-$0.09/GB depending on regions). For write-heavy workloads replicated cross-region: transfer cost can be significant. Estimate: 1 GB/hour writes × $0.08/GB × 730 hours/month = $58/month transfer cost alone.<br>


571. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB journal format, and how is it queried? ; QLDB journal: append-only log of every committed transaction. Each journal block contains: metadata (transaction ID, timestamp, ledger ID) + set of document revisions. Journal blocks are cryptographically linked (hash chain). Journal is the immutable source of truth — current state is derived from journal. Query journal via `history()` function in PartiQL. <br><br> *`history()` function: `SELECT * FROM history(Orders, \`2026-01-01T00:00:00Z\`, \`2026-10-30T00:00:00Z\`) WHERE metadata.id = 'doc_id_123'`. Returns all revisions of a document within a time range with metadata (transaction timestamp, who committed). Audit trail query without any additional audit table.<br> *Journal vs current state: `SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE id = 'ord_001'` queries current state (latest revision). `SELECT * FROM history(Orders) AS h WHERE h.metadata.id = 'ord_001'` queries all past revisions including deleted/overwritten versions. Current state view is a derived view from journal.<br> *Journal immutability: journal blocks cannot be deleted, modified, or backdated. Even AWS cannot delete journal data (within the ledger lifetime). Once committed: permanent. For GDPR right-to-erasure: this is the primary tension — data once in the QLDB journal cannot be erased. Consider pseudonymization before writing to QLDB.<br>

572. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB GetDigest API and what does it return? ; GetDigest: returns the current digest of the QLDB ledger (SHA-256 hash of the entire journal tip). Used as the starting point for cryptographic verification. To verify a specific document revision: use GetDigest + GetRevision (returns proof chain from document revision to the current digest). If proof verification succeeds: revision is genuine and untampered. <br><br> *Proof verification flow: (1) `GetDigest` → current digest (hash of journal tip). (2) `GetRevision` with block address + digest tip → returns revision document + proof (sequence of hashes from revision to digest). (3) Verify locally: hash the revision document, chain hashes up to the digest, compare with GetDigest result. If match: the document is authentic.<br> *QLDB Ion format: QLDB stores documents in Amazon Ion format (superset of JSON with additional types: Int, Float, Decimal, Timestamp, Blob, Symbol, Struct, List). PartiQL queries work on Ion data. Verification uses Ion hash algorithm. Ion libraries available in Java, Python, Go, JavaScript.<br> *Verification use cases: auditor receives a document revision from a QLDB ledger, verifies it wasn't tampered with by running the proof chain against a trusted digest. Supports regulatory requirements for data integrity attestation (SOX, PCI, FDA 21 CFR Part 11).<br>

573. [ QLDB ] What are the QLDB PartiQL write limitations and throughput characteristics? ; QLDB is journal-append serialized: one transaction at a time per ledger. No concurrent writes in parallel (unlike most databases). Throughput: single-digit to low hundreds of transactions/second (depends on document size and complexity). Not designed for high-throughput OLTP (use RDS/Aurora for that). Designed for authoritative record-of-truth with low write throughput. <br><br> *OCC (Optimistic Concurrency Control): QLDB uses OCC — reads don't lock, but commits compare document version at transaction start vs current. Concurrent writes to the same document: one succeeds, others get `OccConflictException` (retry). For high-concurrency same-document updates: OCC retries add latency.<br> *Serialized journal: the journal is a single sequence of transactions (no parallel writes). This is what makes QLDB's hash chain work (linear sequence of blocks). Trade-off: no write parallelism. Application writes serialize at the ledger level. For financial systems with moderate transaction volume (<100 TPS): acceptable. For high-throughput (>1000 TPS): QLDB is not appropriate.<br> *Read throughput: reads are served from the current-state view (not journal). Read throughput scales with compute (QLDB is serverless). QLDB read throughput is higher than write throughput. History queries (journal reads) are slower than current-state queries.<br>

574. [ QLDB ] What are QLDB table access patterns supported by PartiQL? ; PartiQL on QLDB supports: SELECT (point lookups by document ID, range queries), INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE. Predicates on any field (with index for performance). No JOINs across tables. No subqueries in DML. Aggregations: SUM, COUNT, MIN, MAX, AVG. GROUP BY. LIMIT/OFFSET for pagination. <br><br> *QLDB index requirement for point lookup: `CREATE INDEX ON Orders (orderId)`. Without index: `SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE orderId = '123'` does a full table scan. With index: instant lookup. Maximum 5 indexes per table (including system-managed `id` index). QLDB does not support composite indexes.<br> *No JOINs: if application logic requires joining QLDB tables, do it in application code. Query both tables separately, join in-memory. This limits QLDB to independent entity storage (each table is an entity type — Orders, Accounts, etc. — queried independently).<br> *Document ID vs custom ID: every QLDB document has a system-generated `id` (accessible via `metadata.id`). You can add your own `orderId` field — but must create an index on it for efficient lookup. `metadata.id` is automatically indexed. Avoid relying on `metadata.id` in application logic (internal ID); use your own indexed field.<br>

575. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB Streams feature and how is it consumed? ; QLDB Streams: delivers journal blocks in real-time to Amazon Kinesis Data Streams. Consumer reads Kinesis → processes QLDB journal events (inserts, updates, deletes, committed transactions). Use for: real-time CDC, analytics, replication to other systems. Retention: Kinesis stream retention (1-365 days). <br><br> *Stream record format: QLDB delivers three record types to Kinesis: `CONTROL` (stream start/end markers), `BLOCK_SUMMARY` (journal block metadata without revisions), `REVISION_DETAILS` (full document revision). Consumer processes `REVISION_DETAILS` for CDC. Filter for specific tables in consumer (field `tableName`).<br> *Exactly-once delivery: QLDB Streams delivers at-least-once to Kinesis. Kinesis consumers (Lambda, KDA) must handle duplicates using the revision's `sequenceNo` as a deduplication key. Store last processed `sequenceNo` per table in DynamoDB for idempotent processing.<br> *QLDB Streams vs direct Kinesis: QLDB doesn't push individual document events directly to applications — all events go through QLDB Streams → Kinesis → consumer. For real-time use cases: Lambda as Kinesis consumer (EventSourceMapping). For analytics: Kinesis Firehose → S3 → Athena/Redshift Spectrum.<br>

576. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB GDPR right-to-erasure tension, and how do you address it? ; QLDB's immutable journal cannot delete data once committed — journal entries are permanent. GDPR right to erasure (Article 17) requires deleting personal data on request. Tension: delete in current state is trivial (DELETE FROM), but journal history still contains personal data. Solutions: (1) Pseudonymization before writing to QLDB. (2) Encryption-then-delete (store PII encrypted; delete the encryption key = data inaccessible). <br><br> *Pseudonymization approach: don't write PII (name, email, SSN) directly into QLDB. Write a pseudonym (customer_id → UUID). Store the UUID → PII mapping in a deletable database (RDS, DynamoDB). GDPR erasure: delete the RDS mapping. QLDB retains the UUID history (no PII). QLDB still provides audit trail of financial operations — PII linkage severed.<br> *Encryption-then-delete: store sensitive fields encrypted with a KMS CMK. On GDPR erasure: delete the KMS key. Encrypted data in QLDB journal becomes unreadable (all-zeros effectively). However: this is legally complex (data exists but is unreadable vs truly deleted). Regulators may not accept this as erasure. Consult legal before relying on this approach.<br> *QLDB design principle: only commit data to QLDB that you intend to retain permanently (or for the ledger lifetime). QLDB is NOT a general-purpose database — it is specifically for immutable audit trails of financial/critical transactions where permanence is a feature.<br>

577. [ QLDB ] What are the QLDB pricing dimensions? ; QLDB pricing: (1) Write I/Os: per million write requests. (2) Read I/Os: per million read requests. (3) Journal storage: per GB-month (journal is append-only, grows indefinitely). (4) Index storage: per GB-month. Compute is INCLUDED (serverless). No per-hour instance cost. <br><br> *Journal storage growth: journal grows monotonically — it is never compacted or truncated. A high-write-rate application accumulates journal history indefinitely. Estimate journal growth: average transaction size (bytes) × transactions/second × seconds/month. For 100 TPS at 1 KB/transaction: 100 × 1,024 × 2,592,000 seconds/month ≈ 256 GB/month journal growth → $25-50/month journal storage.<br> *Cost optimization: archive old ledger data to S3 if you have a retention policy (regulatorily required to keep 7 years → after 7 years, export to S3 Glacier for cheaper storage). However: you CANNOT delete QLDB journal data. "Archive" means you note the data is past your policy retention; the QLDB journal still grows.<br> *Compare to Aurora for financial: Aurora at $0.10/GB-month storage + $0.12-0.29/instance-hour. For same data volume: Aurora may be cheaper for high-throughput if QLDB journal growth is significant. QLDB's value: tamper-evident audit trail capability — not storage efficiency.<br>

578. [ QLDB ] What network access model does QLDB use, and why is it a common exam gotcha? ; QLDB is NOT in a VPC. Access is via QLDB public HTTPS endpoint. No VPC security groups. No VPC endpoints for QLDB. Applications access QLDB over the internet (HTTPS) or from within AWS using QLDB's DNS endpoint. IAM policies and TLS are the only access controls. <br><br> *VPC endpoint gap: unlike most AWS databases (RDS, DynamoDB, ElastiCache — all VPC-native), QLDB has NO VPC integration. A Lambda in a VPC that has restrictive outbound rules (only VPC endpoints, no internet) cannot access QLDB without adding a NAT Gateway or internet access.<br> *Exam scenario: "customer needs to query QLDB from a Lambda function in a private VPC subnet with no internet access" → solution requires NAT Gateway (private subnet → NAT → internet → QLDB endpoint). No VPC endpoint option exists. Cost: NAT Gateway charges apply.<br> *Security implication: QLDB access control relies entirely on IAM (SigV4 signed requests). There is no network-level isolation. Any entity with a valid IAM credential and internet access can reach the QLDB endpoint. Defense: strict IAM policies, no overly permissive roles, CloudTrail logging of all QLDB API calls.<br>

579. [ QLDB ] How does QLDB handle schema enforcement? ; QLDB is schemaless: documents in a table can have any structure (different fields, different types). No schema validation at write time. This is both flexible (no migrations needed) and risky (typos in field names accepted silently). Application layer must enforce schema consistency. <br><br> *Schema drift risk: write `{orderId: "123", amount: 100}` and `{order_id: "456", Amount: 99}` to the same table → both accepted. PartiQL queries for `orderId` miss the second document. Enforce schema at application level: use a schema validation library (JSON Schema validation before writing).<br> *Ion schema: Amazon Ion has a schema language (Ion Schema Language) for validating Ion documents. Can integrate with QLDB write path in application code. Validate Ion document against schema BEFORE committing to QLDB. Prevents malformed documents from entering the immutable journal.<br> *Ledger design: if schema enforceability is critical (e.g., regulated industry), use Ion Schema validation in the application write path. Treat the QLDB ledger as a confirmed-valid audit trail — only clean, validated transactions enter. Schema errors caught pre-write never pollute the immutable journal.<br>

580. [ QLDB ] What is the difference between a QLDB committed and uncommitted view, and what is the `digest tip` concept? ; QLDB provides two views: (1) committed (current-state view after all committed transactions), (2) `_ql_committed_` system view (same, with metadata). Every committed transaction advances the journal tip and updates the digest (Merkle tree root hash). `GetDigest` returns the digest of the current journal tip. <br><br> *Digest tip usage: the digest tip is a commitment — it represents the state of the ledger at a point in time. Archive the digest tip periodically (daily, monthly). When auditing: use the archived digest tip as the reference. Verify any document revision against that archived digest tip → confirms the document existed in that exact form at the digest creation time.<br> *Immutable verification workflow: (1) Store ledger digest monthly in a separate, trusted store (S3 with Object Lock, or third-party notary). (2) For annual audit: get the monthly digest from trusted store. (3) For each transaction being audited: `GetRevision` against that historical digest. Prove each transaction was in the ledger at that time.<br> *QLDB and regulatory compliance: QLDB's value is cryptographic proof without manual audit infrastructure. HIPAA, PCI DSS, SOX all have audit trail requirements. QLDB provides: immutable journal + cryptographic verification + managed infrastructure. Alternative: manually maintained audit tables in RDS + versioning — possible but operationally complex and easier to tamper with.<br>


581. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora I/O-Optimized pricing model and when should you use it? ; Aurora I/O-Optimized: no per-I/O charges; higher per-instance and storage cost (~20% more). Standard: lower instance+storage cost but charges per 1 million I/O requests. Break-even: ~25-30% storage-to-I/O cost ratio. If I/O cost > 25% of total Aurora bill → I/O-Optimized is cheaper. <br><br> *Calculation: Standard pricing includes $0.20 per 1M I/O requests. A high-IOPS application (1B I/Os/month) = $200/month I/O cost alone. I/O-Optimized instance (r6g.2xlarge): ~$0.60/hr vs Standard ~$0.48/hr = $87/month extra instance cost, $20/month extra storage. I/O-Optimized total savings: $200 - $107 = $93/month savings.<br> *When NOT to use I/O-Optimized: low-I/O workloads (dev/test, small datasets that fit in buffer pool = low I/O). I/O-Optimized base cost is always higher — savings only materialize when I/O charges are significant.<br> *Switching: can switch between Standard and I/O-Optimized without downtime (configuration change on running cluster). No migration needed. Try Standard first, monitor I/O costs in AWS Cost Explorer (filter by Aurora I/O usage type), switch to I/O-Optimized if I/O > 25% of total cost.<br>

582. [ RDS ] What is the RDS Proxy connection pooling architecture, and how does it reduce Lambda cold-start issues? ; RDS Proxy: maintains persistent connection pool to RDS/Aurora. Lambda connects to Proxy instead of RDS directly. Proxy re-uses connections for Lambda invocations. Lambda cold start opens a Proxy connection (fast, <10ms) vs DB connection (slow, 100-500ms). Proxy handles the persistent DB connections independently of Lambda lifecycle. <br><br> *Lambda + RDS without Proxy: Lambda function opens DB connection → Lambda invocation ends → connection lingers (waiting for Lambda to close it). Thousands of Lambda invocations = thousands of open connections → RDS `max_connections` exhausted → `FATAL: too many connections` error.<br> *RDS Proxy connection limit: Proxy maintains a pool scaled to RDS capacity. Lambda can have unlimited concurrent invocations connecting to the Proxy; Proxy multiplexes them to a fixed-size DB connection pool. Typical: Proxy pool = 100-200 connections; Lambda concurrency = 1,000 invocations (100× oversubscription handled by Proxy queue).<br> *RDS Proxy latency: adds ~1ms latency per connection (minimal). For latency-sensitive applications: acceptable trade-off for connection handling. RDS Proxy supports IAM authentication (Lambda IAM role → Proxy → DB credentials in Secrets Manager). No hard-coded passwords in Lambda code.<br>

583. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB hot partition key anti-pattern, and how does adaptive capacity help? ; Hot partition: single partition key receives disproportionate traffic (e.g., celebrity tweet — all reads for tweet_id "viral123"). Adaptive capacity: automatically shifts more provisioned capacity to hot partitions. Does NOT change the hard per-partition limit (1000 WCU, 3000 RCU) — it borrowing from cold partitions. Not a substitute for proper key design. <br><br> *Adaptive capacity example: table has 100 partitions, each with 10 WCU (1000 total WCU). One partition receives 80 WCU (hot). Without adaptive capacity: throttled at 10 WCU limit. With adaptive capacity: DynamoDB can boost the hot partition using unused WCUs from cold partitions (up to 3000 WCU/RCU instantaneously, sustained up to total table WCU).<br> *Partition split: DynamoDB may split a hot partition into two partitions to distribute traffic. After split: each sub-partition gets half the key space. Applications are unaware of splits (transparent). After split: throughput doubled for that key range. However: partition splits are NOT guaranteed or predictable — do not rely on them as a scaling mechanism.<br> *Key design fix for celebrity problem: use `author_id` + random suffix as partition key for fan-out scenarios. Store "viral tweet metadata" in multiple shards: `tweet:viral123:shard1` through `tweet:viral123:shard10`. Fan-out reads to all 10 shards and merge in Lambda. Writes distributed across 10 shards → no hot partition.<br>

584. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift AQUA (Advanced Query Accelerator)? ; AQUA: hardware-accelerated cache layer attached to Redshift Managed Storage (RA3 nodes). Pre-filters and pre-aggregates data near storage before sending to compute. For scan-heavy queries (WHERE, GROUP BY on large datasets): AQUA reduces data transferred from storage to compute → faster query execution. Automatic, no configuration needed. <br><br> *AQUA availability: enabled by default on RA3 clusters where available. Not available on dc2 (no Redshift Managed Storage). AQUA transparently intercepts qualifying queries and offloads to accelerator hardware. No query rewrite needed — standard SQL works.<br> *AQUA vs Redshift Spectrum: AQUA accelerates queries on local Redshift tables (data in RMS). Spectrum accelerates queries on external S3 tables. Complementary: AQUA for local data, Spectrum for data lake. AQUA does not apply to Spectrum queries (S3 data is not in RMS).<br> *Exam context: AQUA is an RA3-specific performance feature. Exam questions about "how to improve Redshift query performance" might include AQUA as an option for RA3 clusters. Not a separate service to configure — it's part of the RA3 architecture.<br>

585. [ Neptune ] What is Neptune's role in a knowledge graph for enterprise search? ; Neptune (knowledge graph pattern): model entities (people, documents, projects, concepts) as vertices. Relationships (person AUTHORED document, document REFERENCES concept) as edges. Query: find all documents related to a concept through multi-hop traversal. Power enterprise search with relationship-aware queries. <br><br> *Typical knowledge graph schema: vertices: `Person {name, dept}`, `Document {title, created}`, `Concept {name, taxonomy}`, `Project {name, budget}`. Edges: `AUTHORED`, `PART_OF`, `REFERENCES`, `MENTIONS`, `WORKS_ON`. Queries: "find all people who work on projects that produced documents referencing 'machine learning' in the last 6 months" — multi-hop traversal across 4 entity types.<br> *Neptune ML for recommendations: train GNN on knowledge graph (entity embeddings). Query: `g.V().has('Person', 'id', 'alice').neptuneML(args)` returns similar entities or predictions (e.g., "documents Alice would find relevant based on her colleague Bob's reading patterns"). Neptune ML uses SageMaker for GNN training, returns inference endpoints.<br> *Hybrid architecture: Neptune (relationship queries) + OpenSearch (full-text search) + DynamoDB (entity attributes). Query router: FTS queries → OpenSearch; relationship queries → Neptune; attribute lookups → DynamoDB. Sync via Neptune Streams → Lambda → OpenSearch for entity indexing.<br>

586. [ ElastiCache ] What is Redis RESP (REdis Serialization Protocol) and why does it matter for monitoring? ; RESP: binary-safe protocol used between Redis clients and server. All Redis commands are RESP-encoded. Version 2 (RESP2): array/bulk string/integer/error types. RESP3 (Redis 6.0+): adds maps, sets, doubles for richer client capabilities. Not typically visible to application developers — matters for: (1) building Redis clients, (2) monitoring with raw TCP inspection. <br><br> *RESP for monitoring: Redis commands in network traffic are human-readable RESP format. VPC Flow Logs captures packet metadata (no payload). For Redis command auditing: use Redis `MONITOR` command (see all commands in real-time) or `SLOWLOG GET` (commands exceeding slow-log threshold). Never use MONITOR in production — it's O(N) overhead.<br> *Slowlog configuration: `CONFIG SET slowlog-log-slower-than 10000` (10ms threshold, in microseconds). `CONFIG SET slowlog-max-len 128`. `SLOWLOG GET 10` returns last 10 slow commands with: command, args, execution time, client IP. Use for diagnosing: which commands are slow, which clients are issuing them.<br> *Redis INFO command: `INFO all` returns: memory usage, connected clients, commands per second, keyspace stats, replication info, persistence stats. Run from redis-cli for baseline health check. Key fields: `used_memory_rss` (actual OS memory), `mem_fragmentation_ratio` (>1.5 = fragmentation issue), `blocked_clients` (BRPOP/BLPOP waiters), `rdb_last_bgsave_status` (backup status).<br>

587. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB query plan cache and how do you manage it? ; DocumentDB (MongoDB-compatible) caches query plans. If data distribution changes significantly (after bulk insert, index creation): cached plan may be suboptimal. Force plan re-evaluation: `db.collection.getPlanCache().clear()` clears all cached plans for the collection. Plans are re-optimized on next query execution. <br><br> *Plan cache lifetime: DocumentDB evicts cached plans after a configurable period (or when the plan cache reaches memory limit). Plans are re-created from `explain()` analysis. If a query suddenly becomes slow: check if a plan cache eviction + re-planning chose a worse index. Compare `explain("executionStats")` before/after.<br> *Index selection: DocumentDB query optimizer evaluates available indexes and picks the one with the lowest estimated cost. If statistics are stale (after large data changes): wrong index may be chosen. Force specific index: `db.orders.find({status: "active"}).hint({status: 1})`. Hint bypasses optimizer — use sparingly (prevents optimizer from adapting).<br> *Multi-key index gotcha: if a field is an array and you create an index on it, it becomes a "multi-key index" (one index entry per array element). Multi-key indexes are less efficient for equality checks on the whole array value. For arrays: design index around `elemMatch` queries, and test query performance with realistic array sizes.<br>

588. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB `AS OF` syntax and when is it used? ; QLDB PartiQL `AS OF` clause: `SELECT * FROM Orders AS OF \`2026-01-15T10:00:00Z\`` — queries the committed state of the table at a specific historical timestamp. Returns the state of documents as they were at that time (historical point-in-time read). Different from `history()` which returns all revisions in a range. <br><br> *`AS OF` vs `history()`: `AS OF` = snapshot read at a point in time (current state AT that timestamp). `history()` = all revisions (audit trail). Use `AS OF` for: "what did the balance look like at end-of-quarter?" Use `history()` for: "show all changes to this account in January."<br> *`AS OF` performance: QLDB reconstructs historical state by replaying journal entries up to the specified timestamp. For long-lived ledgers with heavy write history: historical queries may be slower than current-state queries. Index-based current reads are always faster than journal replay.<br> *Legal discovery use case: `AS OF` allows reconstructing the exact state of financial records on any past date. For regulatory audits: "show me the state of all Accounts on 2026-03-31 (fiscal quarter end)." Run `SELECT * FROM Accounts AS OF \`2026-03-31T23:59:59Z\`` — QLDB reconstructs the exact state from journal without manual versioning.<br>

589. [ Aurora ] How does Aurora handle split-brain scenarios (network partition between writer and readers)? ; Aurora storage is shared — there is no split-brain at the storage layer (storage is independent of compute). In a network partition where a reader cannot reach the writer: the reader remains read-only (no promotion occurs automatically without a failing health check). AWS health checks determine failover, not the readers. <br><br> *Traditional split-brain (MySQL/PostgreSQL Multi-AZ): both primary and standby think they are primary → both accept writes → data divergence. Aurora cannot have this: compute is separate from storage. Even if a reader network-isolated from the writer tries to promote: it can only promote if the DNS flip (cluster endpoint) points to it. The actual data in storage is unchanged — no divergence.<br> *Aurora Multi-Master (deprecated): Aurora Multi-Master DID have a form of conflict resolution needed. It was deprecated in 2023 precisely because the conflict model was complex. Aurora single-writer + read replicas is the standard — no split-brain concern.<br> *Network partition in Cluster Mode: if a reader in AZ-B loses connectivity to AZ-A (where writer is), the reader can still serve stale reads (data already replicated). New data from the writer won't arrive until connectivity restores. The reader does NOT promote — no split-brain, just read staleness.<br>

590. [ All DBs ] A startup needs a database for a social network (users, follows, posts, likes) with 10M users and 100M relationships. What is the recommended architecture and why not a relational DB? ; Neptune (graph): friends-of-friends queries, influencer detection, relationship traversals are native O(log N) → O(1) per hop in graph. Aurora/RDS (relational) would require recursive CTEs or self-joins on a 100M-row edges table → O(N) or O(N log N). DynamoDB: fast for direct lookups but multi-hop relationship traversal requires N+1 queries. <br><br> *Graph query advantage: "mutual friends between Alice and Bob" → Neptune: `g.V('alice').out('follows').aggregate('aliceFriends').V('bob').out('follows').where(within('aliceFriends'))` → direct graph computation. Aurora: `SELECT a1.followee FROM follows a1 INNER JOIN follows a2 ON a1.followee = a2.followee WHERE a1.follower = 'alice' AND a2.follower = 'bob'` — self-join on 100M row table = slow.<br> *Hybrid: Neptune (relationship queries) + DynamoDB (user profiles, post content) + S3 (media). Query: "posts from people Bob follows sorted by likes" = Neptune traversal → list of user IDs → DynamoDB BatchGetItem for profiles/posts → merge in Lambda. This is the standard social network database architecture pattern.<br> *Scale limits: Neptune handles 100M+ vertices and 1B+ edges well with proper instance sizing. Above Neptune limits or requiring cross-region writes: consider Amazon Neptune with Global Clusters or evaluate JanusGraph/Apache TinkerPop on DynamoDB as a backing store (community-supported, not AWS-managed).<br>


591. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora Limitless Database, and when is it needed? ; Aurora Limitless Database (2024): distributed Aurora that shards writes across multiple writer compute instances using a distributed transaction manager. Enables millions of writes/second and petabyte-scale storage beyond the standard 128 TiB limit. MySQL-compatible. For workloads exceeding single Aurora cluster capacity. <br><br> *Limitless vs standard Aurora: standard Aurora = single writer (all writes serialized through one instance). Limitless = multiple router instances + multiple transaction processors (shards). Application connects to Limitless endpoint → routers distribute transactions → transaction processors handle per-shard execution → Aurora distributed storage.<br> *Limitless shard key: customer specifies a shard key (similar to DynamoDB partition key) — transactions for the same shard key value route to the same shard. Cross-shard transactions: supported but expensive (distributed 2PC). Design: minimize cross-shard transactions (similar to DynamoDB single-table design).<br> *Current state: Aurora Limitless is in preview/GA for limited regions as of mid-2026. For exam: know it exists, know it addresses the "what if we need >128 TiB or >single-writer Aurora throughput" scenario. Standard Aurora is the answer for most exam scenarios.<br>

592. [ RDS ] What is a Multi-AZ RDS failover trigger, and what situations do NOT trigger failover? ; Failover triggers: instance hardware failure, OS patching, AZ failure, loss of network connectivity to primary, storage failure. Does NOT trigger failover: OS-level application crashes (e.g., PostgreSQL process crash → auto-restart on same instance), routine reboots requested by customer (reboot WITH failover option is separate). <br><br> *Forced failover: `RebootDBInstance` with `ForceFailover=true` — forces a Multi-AZ failover for testing purposes. Simulates a primary failure, verifies failover works, DNS updates. Use in pre-production testing to validate application failover tolerance.<br> *Failover and read replicas: Read replicas are NOT promoted during a Multi-AZ failover. The standby (Multi-AZ) is promoted. Read replicas remain read replicas after failover (now replicating from the new primary). Read replica promotion (for DR) is a separate manual action.<br> *Application-level restart: if PostgreSQL crashes (OOM, bug), RDS automatically restarts PostgreSQL on the SAME instance (not failover). Failover only occurs if the physical host is unhealthy. Application should handle brief connection drops during PG restart (~30-60 seconds) with retry logic.<br>

593. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB Streams TRIM_HORIZON vs LATEST iterator type? ; TRIM_HORIZON: start reading from the oldest available record in the shard (beginning of 24-hour retention window). LATEST: start reading from now (only new records). For CDC pipelines on existing data: use TRIM_HORIZON (catch all historical events). For real-time new-events-only: LATEST. <br><br> *Lambda DynamoDB Streams trigger: Lambda EventSourceMapping uses TRIM_HORIZON by default for new configurations. On first enable: processes existing stream records (up to 24 hours back). If this is undesirable (don't want to process historical events): set `StartingPosition: LATEST`.<br> *Shard iterator expiry: a Streams shard iterator expires after 15 minutes if not used. For long-paused consumers: re-initialize the iterator. Managed consumers (Lambda, Kinesis Client Library) handle iterator renewal automatically.<br> *AT_SEQUENCE_NUMBER: read from a specific sequence number (used with resume tokens). Use when a consumer fails mid-way and resumes from the last processed event. Store the last processed `sequenceNumber` in a checkpoint store (DynamoDB or S3).<br>

594. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift's ENCODE AUTO and when should you override it? ; ENCODE AUTO: default since Redshift RA3. Redshift automatically determines the best compression encoding for each column based on data statistics. First load: AZ64 encoding applied initially. After VACUUM ANALYZE: Redshift re-evaluates and re-applies optimal encoding. Override needed: if you have domain knowledge that the automatic choice is wrong (e.g., boolean column forced to AZ64 when RAW is better). <br><br> *Manual encoding override: `CREATE TABLE orders (order_date DATE ENCODE RAW, status VARCHAR(20) ENCODE BYTEDICT, amount DECIMAL(10,2) ENCODE AZ64)`. BYTEDICT: best for low-cardinality strings (status has <256 values). AZ64: best for numeric/date (high compression, fast decode). LZO: best for long strings. RAW: sort key columns (no compression for sort performance).<br> *BYTEDICT for status columns: `status` with values "pending", "shipped", "delivered" = 3 distinct values. BYTEDICT stores a dictionary (3 values → 3 bytes) and uses 1 byte per row for the code. For 100M rows: 100MB vs 800MB+ uncompressed. Compression ratio 8× for low-cardinality strings.<br> *ANALYZE COMPRESSION: run `ANALYZE COMPRESSION tablename` to get Redshift's recommendation for each column without applying. Review recommendations, apply manually if you agree. Run after significant data loads when column statistics change.<br>

595. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune T-family vs R-family instance selection criteria? ; t3.medium: dev/test only (2 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, burstable). r5/r6g family: production (memory-optimized, 8-512+ GB RAM). Graph queries are memory-intensive (cache property graph in RAM). For any production Neptune: start with r5.xlarge (32 GB RAM) minimum. Scale based on working set size. <br><br> *Working set for Neptune: vertices + edges + properties + indices (automatically maintained by Neptune). Estimate: ~300-500 bytes per vertex, ~150-300 bytes per edge (depends on property count). 10M vertices + 100M edges: approximately 10M×400 + 100M×200 = 4GB + 20GB = 24 GB working set → r5.xlarge (32 GB) fits the working set.<br> *Memory impact of Gremlin traversals: complex traversals that materialize large intermediate sets (`.aggregate()`, `path()`) use heap memory proportional to result size. An unbound traversal on 10M vertices can OOM a small instance. For large traversals: use streaming iteration + pagination, limit result sets.<br> *Graviton (r6g) vs x86 (r5): r6g provides ~20% better price-performance. Neptune supports r6g instances. For new clusters: prefer r6g.xlarge over r5.xlarge at the same cost. r6g.2xlarge (64 GB, ~$0.58/hr) vs r5.2xlarge (64 GB, ~$0.72/hr): Graviton is cheaper per GB RAM.<br>

596. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache connection timeout best practice for application clients? ; Recommended timeouts: connection timeout 100-500ms (Redis is in same VPC, usually <1ms RTT). Command timeout: 100-500ms for cache reads. Never infinite timeout for cache operations — if Redis is slow, fail fast and serve from DB. Socket keepalive: 60 seconds (prevents silent dead connections). <br><br> *Fail fast principle: cache is a performance layer. If ElastiCache is slow/unavailable: application should fall back to the DB (cache-aside pattern), not wait indefinitely. A cache timeout should be faster than a DB query — if cache is slower than DB, the cache is hurting, not helping.<br> *Connection pool timeout: separate from command timeout. Pool connection acquisition timeout: 200-500ms (how long to wait for a connection from the pool). If pool is exhausted (all connections busy): queue the request briefly or fail. Infinite pool wait = request stacking + cascading failure.<br> *Half-open connection handling: TCP connections that appear open but are actually broken (firewall silently dropped). Redis pings (`PING` command) or application-level heartbeats detect these. Most Redis clients have `socket_keepalive=True` setting. For Elasticache: enable TCP keepalive at OS level and at Redis client level.<br>

597. [ DocumentDB ] What is the DocumentDB aggregation pipeline performance optimization? ; Aggregation pipeline stages in DocumentDB are push-down optimized where possible: `$match` at pipeline start (filters before heavy stages), `$sort` before `$limit` (sorted index scan + limit), `$project` early (reduce document size before later stages). Unoptimized pipeline order: significant performance impact. <br><br> *`$match` first rule: always put `$match` at the start of the pipeline. DocumentDB can use an index for the initial `$match` stage (index-based document selection). If `$match` comes after `$project` or `$unwind`: DocumentDB processes all documents through earlier stages before filtering — full collection scan.<br> *`$lookup` optimization: `$lookup` (join) scans the joined collection for each input document. O(N×M) without index. Ensure the joined collection has an index on the `localField`/`foreignField`. With index: O(N×log(M)). For N=10,000 documents and M=1,000,000 target: with index = 10,000 × 20 = 200,000 ops vs without = 10 billion ops.<br> *Pipeline memory limit: DocumentDB aggregation pipeline stages have a 16 MB memory limit per stage by default. `$sort` on large datasets: may exceed 16 MB. Use `{allowDiskUse: true}` option for `db.collection.aggregate([...], {allowDiskUse: true})` — allows spilling to disk. Performance impact: disk-based sort is 10-100× slower than in-memory.<br>

598. [ QLDB ] What is the QLDB Ion data type support, and what types does it add over JSON? ; Ion: superset of JSON. Adds over JSON: `timestamp` type (nanosecond precision, timezone-aware), `blob` (binary), `clob` (character large object), `symbol` (interned strings for efficiency), `decimal` (arbitrary precision — exact for financial), `int` (arbitrary precision integer), `float` (64-bit IEEE 754), `struct`/`list`/`sexp`. <br><br> *`decimal` type importance: JSON has only `number` (IEEE 754 float — not suitable for financial calculations due to rounding). Ion `decimal` is arbitrary precision: `3.1415926535897932384626433` stored exactly. For QLDB financial use: use `decimal` for amounts, not `float`. Rounding errors in financial records are an audit failure.<br> *`timestamp` precision: Ion timestamps support nanosecond precision with timezone offset. `2026-10-30T10:15:30.123456789-07:00`. Store transaction times with nanosecond precision in QLDB — important for high-frequency financial systems where microseconds matter for ordering.<br> *Ion vs BSON (MongoDB): DocumentDB uses BSON, QLDB uses Ion. Both are binary-encoded document formats. Ion is a superset of JSON (text + binary); BSON is binary-only JSON superset. QLDB's choice of Ion: Merkle tree hash verification of Ion documents using the Ion hash algorithm (well-specified, reproducible across languages).<br>

599. [ Aurora ] What is the Aurora Global Database managed planned failover vs unplanned failover? ; Planned failover (controlled): `switchover` API call → promotes secondary (RPO=0 if replication is caught up, RTO=1-2 minutes). All transactions complete on primary before switchover. Unplanned failover: primary region fails unexpectedly → `failover-global-cluster` API → promote secondary (RPO <1 second, RTO ~1 minute, may lose some in-flight transactions). <br><br> *Planned failover steps: (1) `switchover-global-cluster`. (2) Aurora Global Database detects all replication lag = 0. (3) Primary stops accepting writes. (4) Secondary promoted to primary. (5) Old primary becomes secondary (reverse replication). Total: 1-2 minutes. Zero data loss (all writes replicated before cutover).<br> *Unplanned failover steps: (1) Detect primary region unavailable (CloudWatch, Route 53 health check, custom Lambda alarm). (2) Call `failover-global-cluster` (manual trigger — no auto-failover for Global Database). (3) Secondary promoted in ~1 minute. (4) Application connection strings updated (Route 53 or manual). RPO: <1 second (async replication lag).<br> *Automation: AWS recommends building an automated failover solution: Route 53 health check on primary cluster endpoint → Route 53 health check event → Lambda → `failover-global-cluster` API. This approximates automatic failover for Global Database (which doesn't have native auto-failover). Documented in AWS Prescriptive Guidance.<br>

600. [ All DBs ] Summary question: Which AWS database service should be selected for each scenario? Scenario A: financial ledger requiring immutable audit trail. Scenario B: social network graph. Scenario C: sub-millisecond session cache. Scenario D: 500 TB analytics data warehouse. Scenario E: MongoDB app migration with minimal code change. ; A→QLDB, B→Neptune, C→ElastiCache (Redis/Valkey), D→Redshift, E→DocumentDB. These pairings reflect each service's core differentiator. <br><br> *Why QLDB for A: immutability + cryptographic verification are built-in. Alternatives (Aurora with trigger-based audit table, DynamoDB with versioning) can be tampered with. QLDB provides legally-defensible, tamper-evident audit trail by design.<br> *Why Redis for C: ElastiCache Redis provides <1ms latency for hot data. DynamoDB DAX also sub-millisecond but only for DynamoDB-backed data. For arbitrary cached objects (session state, API responses): ElastiCache Redis. MemoryDB if durability needed. ElastiCache Memcached if just simple string caching with multi-threading.<br> *Why Redshift for D: columnar MPP is the only AWS service designed for multi-hundred-TB analytics with SQL. Aurora/RDS: row-oriented, not optimized for analytics at 500 TB. DynamoDB: no aggregation/analytics. Redshift Serverless, Provisioned RA3, or Redshift Spectrum all handle 500 TB range (Spectrum: data stays in S3, Redshift queries it in-place).<br>


601. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's FAN-OUT read architecture when using the reader endpoint? ; Aurora reader endpoint performs DNS-level round-robin across all available read replicas in the cluster. Each new connection goes to a different replica. No session persistence — subsequent connections from the same client may go to different replicas. For session affinity: use custom endpoints or direct instance endpoints. <br><br> *Reader endpoint load-balancing: not layer-7 (no query-level routing). DNS response changes per-connection. Application that opens 10 connections → 10 connections distributed across replicas (approximately equal distribution for 10+ connections). For low connection count: distribution may be uneven (random).<br> *Aurora Auto Scaling with reader endpoint: add replicas based on `CPUUtilization` or `AuroraReplicaLagMaximum`. New replicas are automatically added to the reader endpoint load balancing. CloudWatch alarm → Application Auto Scaling → `aurora:cluster:ReadReplicaCount`. Scale out when CPU > 70%, scale in when < 30%.<br> *Replica failover order: Aurora Replicas have `promotion_tier` (0-15). Lower tier = promoted first if primary fails. For the replica used as analytics: set high promotion tier (15) to prevent it from becoming primary (analytics replica may be differently sized or configured). OLTP replicas: tier 0-1 for fast failover.<br>

602. [ RDS ] What is the RDS IAM database authentication and what are its limitations? ; IAM DB authentication: generate a temporary auth token (15-minute TTL) via `generate-db-auth-token` API. Token used as the DB password. Supported: MySQL, PostgreSQL on RDS and Aurora. Eliminates need for passwords in application code. IAM policy controls access. <br><br> *Token generation: `aws rds generate-db-auth-token --hostname mydb.cluster.rds.amazonaws.com --port 3306 --username mydbuser`. Returns a signed token (presigned URL format). Pass as password in JDBC connection string. Token is valid for 15 minutes — application must regenerate for long-lived connections or reconnects.<br> *IAM DB auth limitations: (1) SSL/TLS required (token transmitted as password — must be encrypted). (2) Only for native MySQL/PostgreSQL users — not RDS admin accounts (internal auth). (3) 256 new connections per second maximum for IAM auth token generation. (4) Token generation adds latency vs static passwords.<br> *Use case: Lambda functions connecting to RDS. Lambda IAM role has permission to call `rds-db:connect`. No secrets in Lambda environment variables. No rotation needed (token expires automatically). Best practice for serverless architectures over Secrets Manager (fewer API calls, but Secrets Manager supports rotation better for long-lived processes).<br>

603. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB BatchWriteItem request and its error handling semantics? ; BatchWriteItem: up to 25 PutItem/DeleteItem operations in one request, up to 16 MB total. NOT atomic (unlike TransactWriteItems). Each item succeeds or fails independently. `UnprocessedItems` in response: retry those items (exponential backoff). Does NOT support Update operations (use individual UpdateItem). <br><br> *UnprocessedItems retry pattern: `response = dynamodb.batch_write_item(RequestItems=items)` → if `response['UnprocessedItems']`: wait (jitter + backoff), retry `batch_write_item(RequestItems=response['UnprocessedItems'])`. AWS SDK v2 handles this automatically (built-in retry). SDK v1/manual: implement retry loop.<br> *BatchGetItem: up to 100 items, up to 16 MB. Returns `UnprocessedKeys` for unread items (retry). Reads across multiple tables in one call. Each item is read at its current state (no snapshot isolation across items). For strongly consistent reads: set `ConsistentRead: true` per table.<br> *BatchWriteItem vs `ConditionExpression`: BatchWriteItem cannot include conditions — each Put/Delete is unconditional. If you need conditional batch writes: use TransactWriteItems (up to 100 items, with conditions, but atomic). Trade-off: transactions cost 2× WCU.<br>

604. [ Redshift ] What is the Redshift UNLOAD command and how is it used? ; UNLOAD: export query results from Redshift to S3 in parallel. Each slice writes a file. Supports: CSV, Parquet, ORC. Compression: GZIP, BZIP2, ZSTD. Common use: export data for Athena queries, share data with other teams, archive old data. <br><br> *UNLOAD syntax: `UNLOAD ('SELECT * FROM orders WHERE year = 2025') TO 's3://my-bucket/exports/orders_2025_' IAM_ROLE 'arn:aws:iam::123:role/RedshiftExport' FORMAT PARQUET ALLOWOVERWRITE`. Each file gets a numeric suffix: `orders_2025_0000_part_00`, etc. Number of files = slices × files-per-slice (configurable).<br> *UNLOAD and parallel reads: exported files are pre-partitioned for parallel Athena reads. Athena reads each file in a separate worker. For optimal Athena performance: export to Parquet with Snappy compression, partition by query date columns. Athena pays per TB scanned — Parquet compression reduces cost.<br> *UNLOAD to Data Lake: Redshift → UNLOAD → S3 → Glue Catalog (Crawler discovers schema) → Athena/QuickSight. This is the standard Redshift-to-data-lake export pattern. For incremental exports: add WHERE clause filtering on updated_at > last_export_timestamp.<br>

605. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune connection pool recommendation for high-throughput applications? ; Neptune default connection timeout: 300 seconds idle, 30 seconds connection timeout. Connection pool per application instance: 5-20 connections (Neptune handles 10,000+ concurrent connections but each connection has overhead). For high-concurrency: Gremlin Server thread pool = CPU cores × 2. <br><br> *GremlinDriver connection pool (Java): `Cluster.build().addContactPoint(neptuneEndpoint).port(8182).minConnectionPoolSize(5).maxConnectionPoolSize(20).channelizer(SigV4WebSocketChannelizer.class).create()`. Pool maintains open WebSocket connections to Neptune. Each connection handles one in-flight request (no multiplexing on a single WebSocket).<br> *Connection fan-out across replicas: for read-heavy workloads, create separate Gremlin clusters pointing to different replica instance endpoints. Application load-balances read requests across Gremlin clusters. Manual implementation of what Aurora does automatically with the reader endpoint — Neptune does not have a managed multi-replica round-robin endpoint for Gremlin.<br> *WebSocket vs HTTP: Gremlin traversals use WebSocket (persistent connection, lower per-request overhead) or HTTP (stateless, higher overhead but simpler). openCypher uses HTTP. SPARQL uses HTTP. For high-throughput Gremlin: WebSocket connections from a pool outperform HTTP per-request.<br>

606. [ ElastiCache ] What is the ElastiCache Serverless offering and how does it differ from provisioned? ; ElastiCache Serverless (2024): no cluster configuration, scales ECU (ElastiCache Compute Units) automatically, pay per ECU-hour + data stored. No shard management, no node count selection. Provisioned: choose node type + cluster mode + shard count. Serverless: simpler ops, auto-scale, higher cost per ECU vs provisioned at high utilization. <br><br> *ElastiCache Serverless scaling: scales both memory (data size) and compute (request throughput) independently. No manual rebalancing. Minimum capacity: configurable (set min ECU to avoid cold starts). Maximum: auto-scale limits set by you. Vertical-equivalent scaling without operational work.<br> *Cost comparison: ElastiCache Serverless ECU pricing (~$0.034/ECU-hour, 1,000 ECU = $34/hour). Provisioned r6g.large (13 GB, ~$0.15/hour × 2 nodes = $0.30/hour). For steady workload: provisioned is cheaper. For variable/unpredictable workload or dev/test: Serverless avoids over-provisioning.<br> *Migration to Serverless: Create Serverless cache from existing provisioned cluster data via snapshot restore. Or use Online Migration tool (migrate without downtime). For production migrations: test throughput characteristics in Serverless before cutting over — ECU scaling behavior may differ from provisioned throughput.<br>

607. [ DocumentDB ] What are DocumentDB Elastic Clusters and what problem do they solve? ; Elastic Clusters: sharded DocumentDB (public preview 2022, GA 2023). Auto-shards data across up to 32 shards. Handles >millions of reads/writes per second. No instance type selection — serverless capacity units (OCUs). Solves: single-instance capacity limits of standard DocumentDB (max r6g.16xlarge per instance). <br><br> *Elastic Cluster architecture: data sharded by document `_id` hash. Each shard is a DocumentDB instance. Query router routes requests to the correct shard. Cross-shard queries (aggregations, sorts without shard key) fan out to all shards — higher latency. Shard-local queries (by `_id`) are fast (single shard).<br> *Feature parity: Elastic Clusters (as of mid-2026) support most DocumentDB 5.0 operations but have limitations: some aggregation pipeline operators, some index types (text indexes, geospatial indexes: check current docs for support status). For full feature parity: use instance-based clusters.<br> *When to choose Elastic: write throughput > 300,000 WPS, storage > 64 TiB, or unpredictable large-scale workloads. For most enterprise applications: instance-based clusters (r6g.8xlarge with 15 replicas) provide sufficient scale with full feature support.<br>

608. [ QLDB ] What are QLDB Streams record types and their processing order? ; QLDB Streams to Kinesis: three record types in order per transaction: (1) `CONTROL` (transaction begin marker), (2) `REVISION_DETAILS` (one per modified document in the transaction), (3) `BLOCK_SUMMARY` (transaction commit marker with block hash). Must process all three to reconstruct a complete transaction. <br><br> *Processing order importance: `CONTROL` first tells consumer a new transaction is starting. `REVISION_DETAILS` records follow (0 to N per transaction). `BLOCK_SUMMARY` last confirms the transaction committed. If consumer only processes `REVISION_DETAILS` without checking `BLOCK_SUMMARY`: may process revisions from a transaction that failed (impossible in QLDB — only committed transactions appear in streams — but design pattern still validates ordering).<br> *Transaction reconstruction: group stream records by block address: `{strandId, sequenceNo}`. The `BLOCK_SUMMARY` confirms the block is committed. `REVISION_DETAILS` records within the same block have the same `blockAddress`. Group: `{blockAddress: {strandId: X, sequenceNo: Y}: [revision1, revision2, ..., blockSummary]}`.<br> *Kinesis shard distribution: QLDB Streams writes all records for the same document to the same Kinesis shard (using document ID as partition key). This ensures ordering per document across shards. Consumer of that shard sees all revisions of a document in order. Multi-document transactions: revisions may span shards (different document IDs).<br>

609. [ Aurora ] What is Aurora's binlog (binary log) retention and why does it matter for AWS DMS? ; Aurora MySQL binlog retention: default 0 hours (binlogs NOT retained after use by replicas). For AWS DMS CDC (Change Data Capture): must set `binlog_retention_hours` to at least 24 hours. Without retention: DMS loses its replication position if it restarts → must re-do full load. <br><br> *Setting binlog retention: `CALL mysql.rds_set_configuration('binlog retention hours', 24)`. Only needed for DMS CDC sources. Aurora purges binlogs once all replicas consume them — if DMS falls behind, binlogs are purged before DMS reads them → DMS task fails with "binlog deleted" error.<br> *Binlog mode: DMS requires `binlog_format = ROW` (row-level binary logging captures full row changes). Aurora MySQL default: ROW format. Standard MySQL default: STATEMENT. If using non-Aurora MySQL as DMS source: set `binlog_format=ROW` explicitly.<br> *DMS task failure recovery: if DMS loses its binlog position: stop DMS task, set `StartReplicationTaskType=reload-target` (Full Load + CDC restart). To avoid this: (1) set adequate binlog retention, (2) monitor DMS task lag + binlog used position, (3) set DMS CloudWatch alarm on `CDCLatencySource` > 1 hour.<br>

610. [ All DBs ] What is the AWS DMS ongoing replication lag metric and how should it be monitored? ; DMS metrics in CloudWatch: `CDCLatencySource` (lag between source DB and DMS replication instance in seconds), `CDCLatencyTarget` (lag between DMS replication instance and target DB). For healthy CDC: both should be <30 seconds at steady state. Alert on `CDCLatencySource` > 300 seconds. <br><br> *Lag sources: source lag = replication instance not keeping up with source DB write rate (scale up replication instance). Target lag = target DB not keeping up with DMS write rate (scale up target DB, increase DMS parallel apply settings). Both lags = replication instance CPU bottleneck.<br> *DMS replication instance sizing: default `dms.t3.medium` is insufficient for high-throughput CDC. For production migrations: `dms.c5.2xlarge` minimum. Monitor CPU: if replication instance CPU > 70% consistently → scale up. Memory: DMS buffers in-flight transactions in memory — undersized memory = spill to disk = slower.<br> *LOB (Large Object) handling: LOB columns (TEXT, BLOB, CLOB, image) require special DMS handling. `Limited LOB mode` (default): truncates LOBs at `max-lob-size` bytes. `Full LOB mode`: fetches full LOB (slower). For DMS migration with LOBs: set max-lob-size appropriately or use full LOB mode and accept slower throughput.<br>


611. [ Aurora ] What are Aurora PostgreSQL supported extensions, and which are unique to Aurora? ; Aurora PG supports most standard PG extensions: pgvector, PostGIS, pg_stat_statements, pgcrypto, uuid-ossp, pg_trgm, btree_gin, citext, hstore, pgaudit. Aurora-specific: `aws_s3` (import/export to S3), `apg_plan_mgmt` (Aurora Query Plan Management). Some extensions requiring OS access (e.g., `pg_cron` background worker) are supported via Aurora's managed daemon. <br><br> *`pgvector` importance: vector similarity search for ML embedding storage. Aurora PG with pgvector = store embedding vectors (1,536 dimensions from text-embedding-ada-002) + similarity queries (`<->` L2, `<#>` inner product, `<=>` cosine). Use case: semantic search, RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) applications. Aurora PG + pgvector = managed alternative to dedicated vector databases.<br> *`aws_s3` extension: enables `aws_s3.table_import_from_s3()` and `aws_s3.query_export_to_s3()` directly from SQL. Requires IAM role attached to Aurora cluster. No external ETL needed for S3 ↔ Aurora data movement. Convenient for periodic CSV/Parquet imports from S3.<br> *Extensions not supported: `pg_cron` background jobs (PG scheduling): supported in Aurora PG 12.6+. Extensions requiring OS-level access or compiled C libraries not pre-approved: submit a support request. Custom extensions requiring superuser: use RDS Custom for Oracle/SQL Server instead.<br>

612. [ RDS ] What are the RDS PostgreSQL performance best practices for vacuum and autovacuum? ; Aurora PG and RDS PG autovacuum: runs automatically based on `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor` (2% of table = trigger) and `autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay` (throttle). For large tables: lower `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor` to 0.01 (1%) and adjust `autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit` higher for faster vacuuming. <br><br> *Table bloat: PG marks deleted rows as dead tuples (visible to old transactions for MVCC). Dead tuples not removed by autovacuum = table bloat → full table scans slower (more pages), index bloat (fragmented indexes). Monitor: `SELECT n_dead_tup, n_live_tup FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relname = 'orders'`. If `n_dead_tup / n_live_tup > 0.1` (10%): vacuum lag.<br> *Long transactions block vacuum: autovacuum cannot remove dead tuples visible to active transactions. Long-running queries (>30 minutes) prevent vacuum from cleaning up. Monitor: `SELECT pid, now() - pg_stat_activity.query_start AS duration FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state != 'idle'`. Kill long transactions blocking vacuum: `SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid)`.<br> *RDS PG autovacuum parameter group: override at RDS level: `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.01`, `autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay = 10` (ms), `autovacuum_max_workers = 5`. Apply parameter group changes — some require reboot, some apply live. Test in dev before applying to production.<br>

613. [ DynamoDB ] What is the DynamoDB SDK's automatic retry behavior and how do you configure it? ; AWS SDK automatically retries throttled DynamoDB requests (HTTP 429) with exponential backoff + jitter. Default max retries: SDK v2 (Java) = 10 attempts, SDK v3 (JavaScript) = 3 attempts (configurable). Backoff: 2^attempt × 50ms base, +jitter. Configure: `RetryMode.ADAPTIVE` (SDK v3) adjusts retry rate based on throttle rate feedback. <br><br> *Adaptive retry mode: `RetryMode.ADAPTIVE` — SDK learns throttle rate and proactively reduces send rate before throttles occur. More efficient than `RetryMode.STANDARD` which retries after throttle. For high-throughput applications: use `ADAPTIVE` to reduce wasted throttled requests.<br> *Jitter importance: without jitter, all clients retry simultaneously after exponential backoff → synchronized retry waves → all throttled again. With jitter: each client retries at a different random time → distributed retry load → faster recovery. Pattern: `sleep(2^attempt × base_ms × random(0.5, 1.5))`.<br> *When retries fail (ProvisionedThroughputExceededException after max retries): DynamoDB raises the exception to application. Application must handle it: queue the write for later, write to SQS for async retry, or alert operators. Never silently drop writes in financial applications — failed retries = data loss.<br>

614. [ Redshift ] What is Redshift's federated query capability and what databases does it support? ; Federated Query: query data in external data sources directly from Redshift SQL. Supported sources: Amazon RDS (MySQL, PostgreSQL), Amazon Aurora (MySQL, PostgreSQL), Amazon RDS (Microsoft SQL Server). External tables defined with `CREATE EXTERNAL SCHEMA`. No data movement — queries reach out to the external DB at query time. <br><br> *Federated query architecture: Redshift → IAM role → VPC → RDS/Aurora endpoint → query. Requires Redshift cluster in same VPC as RDS, or VPC peering + security group rules. Uses `CREATE EXTERNAL SCHEMA USING (TYPE POSTGRES ... URI ...)` with secrets in Secrets Manager for credentials.<br> *Performance: federated queries are NOT pushed to Redshift's MPP processing. Redshift sends SQL to external DB, receives results, joins with local Redshift data. Large external tables pulled into Redshift = slow. Best for: small to medium external reference tables. Avoid joining 1TB+ external tables via federation — use COPY to import first.<br> *Use case: real-time operational data blend. Redshift (historical analytics) + Aurora (live operational data) → federated query joins last 30 days of operational data (Aurora) with 2 years of historical data (Redshift) → combine in one SQL query. Analytics on current data without ETL delay.<br>

615. [ Neptune ] What is the Neptune full-text search integration pattern? ; Neptune does NOT have native full-text search. Pattern: Neptune (graph relationships) + Amazon OpenSearch Service (FTS). Sync: Neptune Streams → Lambda → OpenSearch. Query routing: FTS queries → OpenSearch → get vertex IDs → Neptune (lookup relationships by ID). Hybrid search: graph-aware full-text search. <br><br> *Sync architecture: Neptune Streams emits QLDB-style change events (vertex/edge inserts, updates, deletes). Lambda consumer reads stream, translates Neptune event to OpenSearch document. On vertex insert: index vertex properties in OpenSearch. On vertex delete: delete from OpenSearch. Neptune and OpenSearch stay in sync with eventual consistency (stream lag ~seconds).<br> *Hybrid query: (1) `GET /vertices/_search {query: {match: {description: "cloud architecture"}}}` → OpenSearch returns vertex IDs [vid1, vid2, vid3]. (2) `g.V(vid1, vid2, vid3).out('TAGGED_WITH').values('tag')` → Neptune returns tags of those vertices. Application merges results.<br> *OpenSearch index schema for Neptune: document structure: `{vertexId: "v123", label: "Article", title: "...", description: "...", tags: [...]}`. Index per vertex label (one OpenSearch index per Neptune vertex type). Enables label-scoped full-text search: search only within "Article" vertices.<br>

616. [ ElastiCache ] What is the Redis OBJECT ENCODING command and why does it matter for memory optimization? ; `OBJECT ENCODING key`: returns the internal representation of a Redis value. Strings: `embstr` (<44 bytes), `raw` (>44 bytes), `int` (integers). Lists: `listpack` (small) → `quicklist` (large). Sets: `listpack` (small) → `hashtable` (large). Sorted sets: `listpack` → `skiplist`. Hashes: `listpack` → `hashtable`. Compact encodings use less memory. <br><br> *listpack encoding: used for small containers (hash ≤ 128 fields AND each field ≤ 64 bytes). Stored as a compact array — more memory-efficient than hashtable. If you add a 65-byte value to a hash that was using listpack → encoding converts to hashtable → memory jumps.
