These protect the perimeter.<br> *East-west controls: TGW route tables (VPC-to-VPC routing control), centralized inspection VPC with Network Firewall/GWLB (intercept VPC-to-VPC traffic), SG micro-segmentation (within VPC, between instances), VPC peering with explicit route control. East-west threats: lateral movement after initial compromise, data exfiltration between internal services.<br> *Exam: questions about "preventing lateral movement after breach" → east-west controls (TGW segmented route tables + centralized inspection). Questions about "protecting against internet attacks" → north-south controls (WAF, Shield, ingress Network Firewall). Know the difference — wrong direction = wrong tool.<br>

674. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] A company has an Inspection VPC with AWS Network Firewall. Traffic from all spoke VPCs must traverse it. The architect notices that return traffic from Spoke B to Spoke A bypasses the firewall. What is the root cause and fix? ; Root cause: TGW Appliance Mode not enabled on the Inspection VPC attachment. Return traffic routes through a different AZ's TGW endpoint, bypassing the AZ-local Network Firewall endpoint that processed the original flow. Fix: enable Appliance Mode on the Inspection VPC TGW attachment. <br><br> *Technical detail: TGW routes packets per-AZ by default. If Spoke A is in AZ-1 and Spoke B is in AZ-2: original packet flows through Inspection VPC AZ-1 endpoint. Stateful firewall in AZ-1 creates session state. Return packet (Spoke B → Spoke A): TGW may choose AZ-2 path → Inspection VPC AZ-2 → but firewall in AZ-2 has no state for this session → may drop or allow without full inspection depending on stateless rules.<br> *Appliance Mode solution: TGW flow hash uses 5-tuple (src IP, dst IP, src port, dst port, protocol). All packets with same 5-tuple → same AZ endpoint. Symmetric flow guaranteed. Stateful firewall sees full bidirectional session. The original packet AND return packet both traverse AZ-1 inspection endpoint.<br> *Exam: any scenario involving stateful inspection via TGW + reports of bypassed or asymmetric traffic → Appliance Mode. It's the specific TGW feature for this exact problem. "inspection VPC not seeing return traffic" = Appliance Mode missing. Always enable Appliance Mode on stateful inspection attachments.<br>

675. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the recommended segmentation architecture for a multi-account AWS Landing Zone with centralized networking? ; Networking account owns TGW + DXGW + centralized Egress VPC + Inspection VPC. Spoke accounts attach VPCs to shared TGW via RAM. TGW route tables segment prod/dev/shared. All internet egress routes through Egress VPC. All east-west routes through Inspection VPC. Firewall Manager enforces SG policies across all accounts. <br><br> *AWS Landing Zone / Control Tower standard: network hub account (often called "Shared Services" or "Network" account) with TGW shared via RAM to all spoke accounts. Security account runs Firewall Manager, GuardDuty master, Security Hub. Log archive account stores CloudTrail, Flow Logs, Config. Management account only for Org-level controls. This 3-account minimum structure is the SAP-C02 reference architecture for organizational complexity.<br> *TGW route table design in Landing Zone: RT-PROD (prod account VPCs + on-prem via DX/VPN), RT-DEV (dev/test VPCs + internet egress only), RT-SHARED (shared services VPCs — reachable by all), RT-INSPECT (inspection VPC, sees all VPCs, routes allowed traffic to destination). SCP prevents spoke accounts from modifying TGW route tables.<br> *Exam: "company is implementing AWS Landing Zone with centralized networking" → hub-and-spoke TGW + RAM + segmented route tables + centralized egress/inspection. This pattern appears in Domain 1 (organizational complexity) scenarios. Any single account with full-mesh peering is the wrong answer at Landing Zone scale.<br>

676. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What AWS service provides network-level segmentation for workloads running in AWS Outposts? ; AWS Outposts uses a Local Gateway (LGW) for connectivity between the Outpost and the local on-premises network. Standard VPC controls (SG, NACL) apply on Outpost subnets. For traffic to/from on-premises LAN via LGW: customer-managed routing on customer equipment. <br><br> *Outpost networking: Outpost rack appears as a subnet extension of an AWS Region VPC. Instances on Outpost get VPC IP addresses. SGs apply identically. NACLs apply identically. For communication with on-premises systems (not going back to Region): LGW provides direct L3 path to local network. No TGW required for local traffic.<br> *Segmentation on Outpost: use SGs to restrict which on-premises hosts can reach Outpost instances. If on-premises IP range is 192.168.0.0/16: DB-SG allows 5432 from 192.168.10.0/24 (specific app server subnet) only. NACLs on Outpost subnet can block traffic from unauthorized on-premises CIDRs.<br> *Exam: Outpost questions focus on LGW for local connectivity vs Service Link (back to Region). Segmentation on Outpost = same VPC security controls (SG + NACL). For compliance where all traffic must stay on-premises (not going to Region): Outpost + LGW + local SGs. TGW is NOT used for local Outpost ↔ on-prem traffic (TGW would route traffic to Region first).<br>

677. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] How do Security Groups handle traffic between two instances in the same SG? ; By default, a new Security Group does NOT allow inbound traffic from other instances in the SAME SG. You must explicitly add an inbound rule with source = the SG itself (self-referencing rule) to allow intra-SG communication. The default SG (created automatically with VPC) DOES allow all inbound from itself. <br><br> *Self-referencing SG rule: add inbound rule: All Traffic, Source = sg-xxxxxxxx (the SG's own ID). This allows any instance assigned this SG to communicate with any other instance assigned the same SG on any port. Common use: ECS service mesh, cluster nodes that need all-to-all communication (Kafka brokers, Elasticsearch nodes).<br> *Default SG behavior: the default SG auto-created with every VPC has a self-referencing inbound rule allowing all inbound from instances in the same SG. This is intentionally permissive for ease of use. Best practice: do NOT use the default SG for production. Create custom SGs with explicit minimal rules.<br> *Exam trap: "two EC2 instances in the same SG cannot communicate" — this is TRUE by default for a custom SG with no self-referencing rule. Adding a second instance to an existing SG does not automatically allow it to talk to the first. You must add the self-referencing rule. Common confusion because the default SG works differently.<br>

678. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the difference between Network Firewall's stateless and stateful rule groups? ; Stateless rules: evaluated first, packet-by-packet, no session tracking. Like NACL — match on 5-tuple (src/dst IP, src/dst port, protocol), action: pass/drop/forward-to-stateful. Stateful rules: session-aware (track TCP/UDP state), Suricata IDS/IPS signatures, domain filtering. Applied after stateless rules pass traffic. <br><br> *Stateless rule use: high-volume known-good traffic (e.g., always allow internal DNS port 53 → forward without stateful engine overhead). Block known bad IPs immediately without stateful inspection overhead. Stateless rules are processed at line rate — no performance penalty. Pass to stateful = additional processing cost.<br> *Stateful rule groups: Suricata-compatible (industry standard IDS/IPS rule format). Can write custom rules or use AWS managed threat intelligence rules. Example: `alert http any any -> any any (msg:"SQL injection attempt"; content:"' OR 1=1"; http_uri; sid:1000001;)`. Domain list = special stateful rule type for L7 domain filtering.<br> *Default stateful action: "drop established" vs "alert established" vs "pass" — configure default actions for traffic not matching any stateful rule. Fail-open (pass all unmatched) vs fail-closed (drop all unmatched). Production: fail-closed for strict security. Dev: fail-open to avoid blocking legitimate new traffic during rule development.<br>

679. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] A company wants to implement Zero Trust networking in AWS. Which combination of services best supports this? ; Zero Trust = never trust, always verify. AWS implementation: (1) IAM roles + STS for identity-based access (not network location), (2) PrivateLink for service-to-service with IAM policy + VPC endpoint policy, (3) mTLS via ACM PCA for mutual authentication, (4) AWS App Mesh / Envoy sidecar for service mesh with L7 policies, (5) VPC SG micro-segmentation as network layer fallback. <br><br> *Zero Trust principle applied to AWS: traditional perimeter security says "inside VPC = trusted." Zero Trust says "inside VPC ≠ trusted — every service must authenticate." Implementation: each microservice has IAM role, calls other services via PrivateLink + Sigv4 signing. Network connectivity exists but is not sufficient — must also authenticate via IAM.<br> *mTLS for east-west: each service has its own certificate from ACM Private CA. TLS client certificate sent with every request. Receiving service validates client cert against trusted CA. Mutual authentication: both sides prove identity. App Mesh automates mTLS certificate rotation. This adds authentication on top of SG network controls.<br> *Exam: "Zero Trust network architecture in AWS" → combination of SG micro-segmentation (network layer) + IAM-based service authorization (identity layer) + mTLS (transport layer). Wrong answer: "put everything in a private subnet" — that's network isolation, not Zero Trust. Zero Trust adds authentication to every hop, not just network-level blocking.<br>

680. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What are the key differences between AWS Network Firewall and AWS WAF? ; Network Firewall: L3-L7 firewall for VPC-level traffic (any IP traffic — TCP, UDP, ICMP). Deployed inline in VPC. WAF: L7 HTTP/S firewall for web application traffic only. Deployed on ALB, CloudFront, API Gateway, AppSync. Use both: WAF for web-layer protection, Network Firewall for network-layer protection. <br><br> *Network Firewall capabilities: stateless/stateful rules, Suricata IPS signatures, domain filtering, protocol-based blocking (block SMB, allow only HTTPS). Deployed as endpoint in VPC subnet. All traffic routes through it. Inspects ANY IP traffic — not limited to HTTP.<br> *WAF capabilities: HTTP/S request inspection — headers, body, URI, cookies. OWASP rules (SQL injection, XSS, LFI/RFI). Rate limiting. IP reputation lists. Geo-blocking. Bot control. Managed rule groups (AWS and 3rd-party via Marketplace). Applied at ALB/CloudFront — only inspects HTTP/S arriving at those services.<br> *Exam: "block SQL injection attacks on web application" → WAF. "block all non-HTTP outbound traffic from VPC" → Network Firewall. "inspect all traffic between VPCs" → Network Firewall. "rate-limit API requests" → WAF. They are complementary, not alternatives. A complete security architecture uses both: WAF at the edge, Network Firewall in the VPC interior.<br>

681. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What happens to traffic if a Network Firewall endpoint goes down in one AZ? ; Traffic in that AZ that routes through the failed endpoint will fail — there is no automatic failover across AZs. Best practice: deploy a Network Firewall endpoint in EACH AZ and configure AZ-local routing (each AZ's route table sends traffic to its own local firewall endpoint). <br><br> *Multi-AZ Network Firewall deployment: Network Firewall service is regional, but endpoints are AZ-level. Create an endpoint in each AZ where you have workloads. In each AZ's private subnet route table: 0.0.0.0/0 → local firewall endpoint (in same AZ). If AZ-1 endpoint fails: AZ-1 traffic fails (no cross-AZ fallback). But AZ-2 and AZ-3 continue working — partial failure, not total outage.<br> *Why no cross-AZ fallback: stateful inspection requires symmetric routing (both directions through same endpoint). If AZ-1 fails and we route AZ-1 traffic to AZ-2 endpoint: AZ-2 endpoint has no state for existing AZ-1 flows → connections drop anyway. Cross-AZ failover doesn't help stateful inspection. New connections would work after health check routes them, but existing sessions break regardless.<br> *Cost implication: each Network Firewall endpoint = ~$0.395/hour + $0.065/GB. Three AZs = ~$1.185/hour + data transfer. This is significant cost for high-throughput workloads. Evaluate: single AZ for dev (lower cost, accept single-AZ risk), multi-AZ for prod (HA). GWLB alternative: auto-scales NVA fleet across AZs, potentially more cost-effective at scale.<br>

682. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is a "black hole route" in TGW and when is it used for segmentation? ; A black hole route in a TGW route table is a static route with no attachment as next-hop — matched packets are silently dropped. Used to block specific CIDRs from being routed within the TGW even if propagation would otherwise install a route for them. <br><br> *Use case: Dev VPC CIDR 10.1.0.0/16 accidentally propagates into Prod route table (misconfiguration or automation error). Add a static black hole route 10.1.0.0/16 in RT-PROD → overrides propagated route (static > propagated) → Dev traffic silently dropped. Defense against misconfigured propagation.<br> *Priority: TGW route selection: most-specific route wins (longest prefix match). For same prefix: static route > propagated route. Black hole routes are always static → always win over propagated routes for the same CIDR. Use black hole + static to enforce explicit-deny semantics in TGW routing.<br> *Exam: "TGW route table should never route traffic to VPC X even if propagation is accidentally enabled" → add black hole static route for VPC X's CIDR in the restricting route table. This is the TGW equivalent of a NACL DENY rule — explicit rejection that cannot be overridden by propagation. Pattern used in multi-account org segmentation for regulatory compliance.<br>

683. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] A company runs containerized workloads in Amazon EKS. How can they achieve pod-level network segmentation? ; Kubernetes NetworkPolicy + Amazon VPC CNI (with network policy support) or Calico for pod-level segmentation. With VPC CNI network policy: each pod gets a VPC IP, NetworkPolicy rules translate to Security Group-like rules enforced at the pod ENI level (via eBPF). Alternatively, assign each pod its own Security Group (SGP — Security Groups for Pods). <br><br> *Security Groups for Pods (SGP): EKS feature (requires VPC CNI + supported instance types). Each pod gets its own ENI branch with its own SG. Pod SG rules work identically to EC2 SG rules. OrdersPod-SG allows 8080 from APIGatewayPod-SG. Enables micro-segmentation at pod level using existing SG infrastructure.<br> *VPC CNI Network Policy: AWS VPC CNI v1.14+ supports Kubernetes NetworkPolicy (enforced via eBPF at node level). Kubernetes NetworkPolicy: `podSelector` + `namespaceSelector` based ingress/egress rules. Enforced at pod level (not subnet level). Complements SG for pod-level micro-segmentation without requiring one ENI per pod.<br> *Exam: "pod-level network segmentation in EKS" → Security Groups for Pods (if you need SG-level control, compatible with existing SG tooling) OR VPC CNI network policy (if you prefer Kubernetes-native NetworkPolicy). Both are valid. SGP requires branch ENIs (limited by instance type). VPC CNI NetworkPolicy requires newer VPC CNI version. Know both options exist.<br>

684. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is VPC Lattice and how does it differ from traditional VPC-level segmentation? ; AWS VPC Lattice: service-to-service connectivity and segmentation at the APPLICATION layer (L7), not network layer. Define services, service networks, and auth policies using IAM. Services in different VPCs, accounts, or even on-premises connect via Lattice without VPC peering or TGW. Segmentation via IAM policies (who can call which service), not IP-based SG rules. <br><br> *VPC Lattice architecture: Service Network = logical grouping of services. VPC associates with a Service Network (one VPC can associate with multiple Service Networks). Service = your workload (backed by ALB, instance group, Lambda, ECS). Auth policy on the service: only allow calls from IAM principal with specific IAM role. No IP-based routing — fully identity-based.<br> *Comparison to TGW + SG: TGW + SG = network-layer segmentation (IP/port based, requires VPC peering or TGW attachment). VPC Lattice = application-layer segmentation (IAM-based, works across VPCs without routing setup). VPC Lattice supports overlapping CIDRs (like PrivateLink). TGW cannot handle overlapping CIDRs without NAT.<br> *Exam (newer service, increasing on SAP-C02): "service-to-service connectivity across VPCs without managing complex routing" + "identity-based access control between services" → VPC Lattice. "need to block all non-HTTP traffic between VPCs" → Network Firewall + TGW. VPC Lattice is for L7 service mesh connectivity, not network-level firewall replacement.<br>

685. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the cost structure for TGW attachments and how should it influence architecture decisions? ; TGW attachment: $0.05/hour per attachment regardless of traffic. TGW data processing: $0.02/GB. VPC peering: no hourly fee, only standard data transfer fee. Decision: for many VPCs with moderate traffic, TGW > peering in cost but wins in manageability. For 2-3 low-traffic VPCs: peering may be cheaper. <br><br> *Attachment cost math: 50 VPCs on TGW = 50 × $0.05/hr = $2.50/hr = $1,800/month in attachment fees alone (before data transfer). At 10TB/month data: 10,000 × $0.02 = $200 additional. VPC peering: $0 attachment + standard cross-AZ data transfer ($0.01/GB within region). For 50 VPCs at 10TB: $200 data transfer only. Peering would be cheaper — BUT 50 VPCs = 1,225 peering connections = unmanageable.<br> *TGW architectural value: the attachment fee buys centralized routing control, segmentation via route tables, shared TGW across accounts (RAM), ECMP for active-active VPN, centralized inspection. These capabilities justify the cost at enterprise scale. Cost optimization: remove unused attachments (idle VPC attachments still charge $0.05/hr). Consolidate small VPCs onto fewer larger VPCs where possible.<br> *Exam: "company wants to reduce networking costs" + currently using TGW for 3 VPCs + no special routing requirements → migrate to VPC peering. "company has 30+ VPCs with complex routing requirements" → TGW cost is justified. Always consider manageability vs. raw cost when TGW vs. peering is the question.<br>

686. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What does "implicit deny" mean in the context of AWS Security Groups, and why is it important for segmentation? ; Security Groups have an implicit DENY ALL at the end of their rule list for INBOUND traffic. Any packet not matching an explicit allow rule is dropped. There is also an implicit ALLOW ALL for OUTBOUND by default (though the default outbound rule allowing 0.0.0.0/0 can be removed). The implicit inbound deny makes SGs a default-secure control. <br><br> *Practical implication: a new Security Group with NO rules = allows NO inbound traffic (implicit deny). No configuration needed to block unknown traffic. This is the fail-secure principle — new resource added with no SG rules = maximum restriction. Contrast with NACLs: new NACL with no rules = DENY ALL (both inbound and outbound, since there's no implicit allow).<br> *Default outbound rule: `ALLOW ALL outbound 0.0.0.0/0`. This allows instances to initiate connections outbound freely. For highly regulated workloads: remove the default outbound rule and add explicit outbound rules (allow 443 to specific SG only, allow 5432 to DB-SG only). Reduces risk of data exfiltration via outbound connections.<br> *Exam: "new EC2 instance has a SG with no rules — can it receive traffic from the internet?" → NO (implicit inbound deny). "can it initiate outbound connections?" → YES (default outbound allow-all rule exists). The asymmetry matters. Remove default outbound rule for zero-trust instance profiles where all outbound must be explicitly approved.<br>

687. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is an Egress-only Internet Gateway (EIGW) and how does it contribute to IPv6 segmentation? ; EIGW: allows outbound-only IPv6 traffic from VPC instances to internet, blocks inbound-initiated IPv6 connections. IPv6 equivalent of NAT Gateway for egress-only access. All IPv6 addresses are public (no private IPv6 space), so EIGW is required to prevent inbound internet access while allowing outbound. <br><br> *IPv6 segmentation challenge: unlike IPv4 (private RFC1918 space + NAT), IPv6 has no NAT — every IPv6 address is publicly routable. Without EIGW, any instance in a VPC with an IPv6 address and a route to IGW is reachable from the internet. EIGW solves this by statefully blocking inbound-initiated connections while allowing outbound.<br> *EIGW vs NAT Gateway for IPv4: NAT GW handles IPv4 private-to-public translation (hides private IP). EIGW handles IPv6 outbound-only (no address translation — the IPv6 address is preserved, just inbound connections blocked). Cannot use NAT GW for IPv6. Must use EIGW for IPv6 outbound-only access.<br> *Route table configuration: add route ::/0 → EIGW in the private subnet route table. Instances get IPv6 addresses. Can initiate outbound IPv6 connections. Cannot receive inbound IPv6 connections from internet. SGs still apply and can further restrict which outbound ports are allowed. EIGW is free (no hourly charge, only data transfer).<br>

688. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the AWS recommendation for segmenting workloads that have different data classification levels (e.g., public, internal, confidential, restricted)? ; Each classification level in a separate VPC, ideally separate AWS account for confidential/restricted. Public-facing resources in public subnet. Internal in private subnet. Confidential in isolated subnet with VPC endpoints. Restricted (PCI, PHI) in dedicated account with no internet path and SCPs preventing connectivity creation. <br><br> *Data classification → network zone mapping: Public data → public subnet (ALB, CloudFront). Internal use data → private subnet (app servers, internal APIs). Confidential data → isolated subnet (databases, encryption keys). Restricted (regulated) data → dedicated VPC + dedicated account. Each zone enforced by subnet placement + SG + NACL. Lateral movement between zones is prevented by SG design and TGW route table segmentation.<br> *Account-level classification enforcement: restricted data account gets SCP: deny all internet gateway creation, deny all VPC peering outside approved accounts, require S3 bucket policy with vpc-endpoint condition. Even if an employee with admin rights is compromised in a different account, they cannot create paths into the restricted account.<br> *Exam: "PHI data must not be accessible from internet or other accounts" → dedicated account + isolated subnets + VPC endpoints + SCP deny internet gateway + bucket policy requiring VPC endpoint. Multiple defense layers. Wrong answer: "put PHI in a private subnet in the same VPC as other workloads" — insufficient isolation (same VPC, same SG namespace, shared blast radius).<br>

689. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is AWS Security Hub's role in traffic segmentation monitoring? ; Security Hub aggregates security findings from GuardDuty (anomalous network traffic), AWS Config (SG compliance rules), Macie (data exposure), Inspector (vulnerability findings), and Firewall Manager (policy violations). Provides centralized visibility into segmentation effectiveness — detects when segmentation fails or is misconfigured. <br><br> *Relevant Security Hub controls for segmentation: CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark checks SG rules (e.g., no SG allows unrestricted 0.0.0.0/0 on port 22, 3389). NIST SP 800-53 controls check VPC Flow Logs enabled, NACL configurations. Custom findings from Config rules: "SG allows 0.0.0.0/0 on any port" → HIGH finding in Security Hub.<br> *GuardDuty network findings relevant to segmentation: UnauthorizedAccess:EC2/SSHBruteForce (inbound SSH brute force — SG should block 22 from internet), Recon:EC2/PortProbeUnprotectedPort (port scan detected on instance with open SG), Backdoor:EC2/C&CActivity.B (instance communicating with known C2 — east-west or outbound traffic that SG allowed but shouldn't). These indicate segmentation failures.<br> *Exam: "detect segmentation violations across 100 accounts" → Security Hub with delegated administrator in security account + Config rules for SG compliance + GuardDuty for runtime network anomalies + Firewall Manager for policy enforcement. This is the complete detect-and-enforce segmentation stack at org scale.<br>

690. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] When would you choose AWS PrivateLink over VPC peering for cross-VPC service access? ; PrivateLink when: (1) only ONE specific service needs to be accessible (not entire VPC), (2) VPCs have overlapping CIDRs, (3) providing SaaS service to many external customers, (4) consumer should not have network-level access to provider. VPC peering when: (1) multiple services need to communicate freely, (2) bidirectional access needed, (3) same account/OU with no CIDR overlap, (4) low traffic (no per-endpoint cost). <br><br> *PrivateLink access model: consumer accesses only the specific service endpoint (NLB in provider VPC). No direct routing to provider VPC CIDR. Provider cannot initiate connections to consumer. Consumer cannot scan or reach any other resource in provider VPC. Pure service exposure — network isolation is preserved.<br> *VPC peering access model: full routing between VPCs (with explicit routes). Both VPCs can initiate connections to each other's CIDRs. Firewall/SG rules must be carefully configured to limit access. One compromised instance in VPC A can attempt to reach anything in VPC B that its SG allows. More flexible but larger potential blast radius.<br> *Exam: "company A wants to expose an internal API to company B without giving B access to A's network" → PrivateLink. "company A and B are same org and need 10 microservices to communicate bidirectionally" → VPC peering or TGW. "overlapping CIDRs between provider and consumer" → PrivateLink (only option that works). PrivateLink = scoped access. Peering = network access.<br>

691. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the maximum number of VPCs that can be attached to a single Transit Gateway? ; Default: 5,000 VPC attachments per TGW (soft limit, can be increased). Also: 20 TGW route tables per TGW, 10,000 routes per TGW route table, 50 peering attachments per TGW. These limits define the scalability of TGW-based segmentation architectures. <br><br> *Practical limits at enterprise scale: 5,000 VPC attachments supports very large AWS Organizations. AWS internal usage guides suggest a single TGW per region per landing zone is sufficient for most enterprises. If exceeding 5,000 VPCs: add a second TGW and peer them (TGW peering attachment).<br> *Route table limits: 20 route tables per TGW means you can define 20 distinct security zones/segments. Most enterprises need 4-6 zones (prod, dev, staging, shared, sandbox, restricted). 20 is sufficient. If more granularity needed: use prefix lists and black hole routes within a route table to handle exceptions within a zone.<br> *Exam: TGW limits rarely appear as exact numbers on SAP-C02 (associate exam tests limits more). More important: know TGW scales to thousands of VPCs (vs VPC peering which breaks down above 10-20 VPCs), and that route table limits exist (20 tables, 10K routes each). "company has 5000 VPCs" → TGW (possibly multiple TGWs with peering) is the answer, not VPC peering mesh.<br>

692. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the "shared VPC" (VPC sharing) model and how does it differ from TGW-based multi-account networking? ; VPC sharing (RAM): share subnets from one VPC across multiple AWS accounts. Participant accounts deploy resources into shared subnets (using owner's VPC). All resources share the same VPC, CIDRs, and network controls. TGW: each account has its own VPC; TGW connects them. VPC sharing = shared network space. TGW = separate networks connected at hub. <br><br> *VPC sharing advantages: reduced VPC count (fewer VPCs to manage, fewer TGW attachments). Resources in same VPC subnet can communicate via local routing (no TGW hop, lower latency, no per-GB charge). Centralized network team owns VPC; application teams consume subnets. Security: owner account controls NACL, route tables, flow logs for the entire VPC.<br> *VPC sharing disadvantages: blast radius = all accounts sharing the VPC. If an account's workload is compromised, east-west lateral movement is within the same VPC (only SG/NACL protect). For strict isolation: TGW is better (account-level VPC isolation). VPC sharing suits accounts with same trust level (e.g., all dev accounts from same team).<br> *Exam: "reduce TGW attachment costs and latency for closely related workloads" → VPC sharing. "strict network isolation between accounts" → separate VPCs per account + TGW. "compliance requires no shared network plane between environments" → TGW (each account/environment has own VPC). The choice between VPC sharing and TGW is a classic SAP-C02 tradeoff question.<br>

693. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the correct way to allow traffic from on-premises to only SPECIFIC services in a VPC (not full VPC access)? ; Use AWS PrivateLink to expose specific services. On-premises connects via DX or VPN → VPC → Interface Endpoint → Service. Alternatively: route specific CIDRs from on-premises to specific subnets via VGW/TGW, and restrict with SG/NACL to only allow the specific service ports. PrivateLink is cleaner — no VPC CIDR exposure. <br><br> *PrivateLink from on-premises: on-premises → DX Private VIF → Endpoint VPC → Interface Endpoint for Service A. The endpoint has a private IP; on-premises resolves the service DNS name to the private IP. On-premises can only reach the NLB-backed service — no ability to scan or reach the rest of the VPC. Requires Route 53 Resolver to propagate private DNS to on-premises.<br> *SG-based restriction alternative: on-premises connects via VPN/DX to VPC. VGW/TGW provides network-level access to VPC CIDR. SG on each service restricts inbound to specific on-premises CIDRs and ports. Less clean than PrivateLink (on-premises has network-level access to VPC CIDR) but simpler for existing architectures where migration to PrivateLink is not feasible.<br> *Exam: "on-premises team should only access a specific API, not the entire VPC network" → PrivateLink exposed via DX. "on-premises needs to access multiple services in VPC with different ports" → DX + VGW/TGW + per-service SG rules. PrivateLink is more secure (scoped access); VGW/TGW with SG is more flexible (allows expansion to more services later). Know the trade-off.<br>

694. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the role of Amazon GuardDuty in detecting segmentation failures? ; GuardDuty uses VPC Flow Logs, DNS logs, and CloudTrail to detect network anomalies indicating segmentation failure: unauthorized port scans, unusual cross-VPC communication, known C2 callbacks, cryptocurrency mining traffic, and SSH/RDP brute force. It detects AFTER a failure — complementing proactive SG/NACL/firewall controls. <br><br> *Key GuardDuty network finding types: Recon:EC2/PortProbeUnprotectedPort — someone is scanning your instance (SG may be too open). UnauthorizedAccess:EC2/SSHBruteForce — SG allows SSH from internet. CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B — instance making crypto mining connections (indicating compromise + outbound SG too permissive). Backdoor:EC2/C&CActivity.B — command and control traffic (east-west exfiltration or outbound).<br> *GuardDuty Malware Protection: scans EBS volumes of potentially compromised instances. If GuardDuty detects a C2 callback, you can: (1) isolate the instance (remove from SG, move to quarantine SG that blocks all outbound), (2) snapshot EBS for forensics, (3) terminate and replace. Quarantine SG = new SG with no outbound rules + inbound from forensics team only.<br> *Exam: "detect when a VPC SG is accidentally opened to internet" → AWS Config rule (reactive, configuration check). "detect when an EC2 instance is actually communicating with a malicious IP via the open SG" → GuardDuty (behavioral detection). Both are needed. Config = configuration compliance. GuardDuty = runtime behavioral detection. Different tools, complementary roles.<br>

695. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the difference between "allow-list" (whitelist) and "deny-list" (blacklist) approaches to segmentation, and which does AWS prefer? ; AWS SGs are allow-list only (implicit deny, explicit allow). NACLs support both (numbered rules, explicit deny OR allow). AWS Network Firewall stateless rules can allow or deny. Best practice: default-deny with explicit allow (allow-list). Deny-lists require knowing all bad actors — impossible. Allow-lists require knowing all legitimate traffic — manageable. <br><br> *Allow-list (whitelist) implementation: SG: only add rules for known-good traffic. New port/service → add rule explicitly. Unknown traffic = denied by default. Network Firewall: default action = DROP; add pass rules for approved traffic. Strictest security posture — any unrecognized traffic is blocked.<br> *Deny-list (blacklist) limitations: cannot enumerate all malicious IPs/ports. New attack vectors bypass the list. Requires constant updates. AWS threat intelligence lists (managed prefix lists for known bad IPs) can help, but are incomplete. Use deny-list as a SUPPLEMENT to allow-list, not as primary control.<br> *Exam: "company wants to block known malicious IP ranges while allowing all other traffic" → NACL DENY rules for specific CIDRs + Network Firewall with threat intelligence rules (deny-list supplement). "company wants to ensure only approved traffic flows" → SG allow-list + Network Firewall default-drop with explicit pass rules (allow-list primary). SAP-C02 almost always favors allow-list architecture for regulated workloads.<br>

696. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] How does traffic segmentation apply to serverless workloads (Lambda) within a VPC? ; Lambda in VPC: function runs in a VPC subnet, gets ENI with a SG attached. SG controls what Lambda can reach (outbound to DB, APIs). Functions without VPC: runs in AWS-managed network (no customer VPC access, internet access only). VPC-attached Lambda: private resource access + SG outbound control = segmented. <br><br> *Lambda VPC networking: Lambda creates HyperPlane ENIs (shared across concurrent invocations) in specified subnets. SG attached to Lambda function controls outbound from Lambda. Inbound to Lambda: only from the Lambda service itself (invocation via API) — SG inbound rules on Lambda are irrelevant for invocation. Inbound SG rules matter if Lambda is a SOURCE — other resources' SGs must allow from Lambda SG.<br> *Segmentation pattern: Lambda-SG allows outbound 5432 to RDS-SG only. RDS-SG allows inbound 5432 from Lambda-SG only. Lambda cannot reach anything except RDS on port 5432. Lambda in isolated subnet: cannot reach internet (only reaches AWS services via VPC endpoints). Pattern: Lambda + isolated subnet + VPC endpoints + SG = fully segmented serverless.<br> *Exam: "Lambda needs to access RDS without internet exposure AND cannot reach internet" → Lambda in VPC (private subnet) + SG chaining (Lambda-SG → RDS-SG) + no NAT GW in subnet. Wrong answer: Lambda without VPC (can't reach private RDS). Wrong answer: Lambda in public subnet (can reach internet). Lambda must be in private/isolated subnet for network segmentation to apply.<br>

697. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the "defense in depth" principle as applied to AWS network segmentation, and give a concrete example? ; Defense in depth: multiple independent segmentation layers, each capable of blocking unauthorized traffic. If one layer fails, others catch it. Example: ALB SG (allows 443 only) → private subnet NACL (allows from ALB subnet only) → App SG (allows from ALB-SG only) → isolated subnet NACL (allows from app subnet only) → DB SG (allows 5432 from App-SG only). Five independent controls between internet and database. <br><br> *Why multiple layers: a single misconfigured SG (e.g., dev accidentally opens App-SG to 0.0.0.0/0) is caught by the NACL (which allows only ALB subnet) and DB-SG (which only allows App-SG). The misconfiguration exists but doesn't create a path to the database. Two independent controls must fail simultaneously for a breach.<br> *Cost of defense in depth: minimal operational overhead — NACLs are free and stateless (fast). SG rules are free. The complexity cost is in management: more rules to understand and update. Compensate with: Terraform/CDK automation, Security Hub compliance checks, Firewall Manager policy enforcement. Don't sacrifice defense in depth for simplicity in production environments.<br> *Exam: defense in depth in networking = SG + NACL + route table controls + inline inspection (Network Firewall/GWLB) + egress filtering. SAP-C02 will present a scenario where "a single security control was misconfigured and data was exfiltrated" → the correct remediation adds MULTIPLE layers. Single-control architectures = wrong answer for production/regulated environments.<br>

698. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is a Transit Gateway Connect attachment and when is it used for segmentation? ; TGW Connect: GRE (Generic Routing Encapsulation) tunnel attachment over an existing VPC or Direct Connect attachment. Used for SD-WAN appliances (Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN, VMware VeloCloud, etc.) that speak GRE natively. Supports BGP for dynamic routing. Bandwidth: up to 20 Gbps per Connect attachment (4 tunnels × 5 Gbps). Used when SD-WAN vendor appliances terminate in a VPC and need TGW integration. <br><br> *TGW Connect architecture: SD-WAN virtual appliance runs in Transit VPC (or Appliance VPC). TGW Connect attachment = GRE tunnel from TGW to the SD-WAN appliance (over the VPC attachment). BGP session over GRE: SD-WAN advertises branch site routes to TGW; TGW advertises VPC routes to SD-WAN. SD-WAN handles QoS, encryption, and per-application routing policies (segmentation at application layer).<br> *Segmentation with SD-WAN: SD-WAN creates Virtual Routing and Forwarding (VRF) instances per business unit/segment. Each VRF maps to a TGW route table. Traffic from branch office for "Finance VRF" → TGW Finance route table → Finance VPCs only. Traffic for "HR VRF" → TGW HR route table → HR VPCs only. SD-WAN + TGW = end-to-end application-aware segmentation from branch to cloud.<br> *Exam: "customer uses Cisco SD-WAN for branch connectivity and needs to integrate with AWS TGW" → TGW Connect attachment (GRE). Not site-to-site VPN (IPSec, not GRE). Not Direct Connect (requires physical setup). TGW Connect is specifically designed for SD-WAN appliance integration using GRE with BGP.<br>

699. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] How do you implement network segmentation for an AWS Multi-Region architecture? ; Each region has its own VPC(s) and TGW. Cross-region connectivity via TGW peering (private, over AWS backbone). Route tables control which regions can communicate. For east-west segmentation: TGW route tables in each region + peering route tables for cross-region flows. North-south: regional Network Firewall + GWLB per region. <br><br> *TGW peering for segmentation: TGW in us-east-1 peers with TGW in eu-west-1. In us-east-1 TGW route table: add static route for eu-west-1 CIDRs → TGW peering attachment. In eu-west-1 TGW route table: add static route for us-east-1 CIDRs → TGW peering attachment. Segmentation: only explicitly routed CIDRs can communicate cross-region. No automatic propagation across peering (static routes only).<br> *Cross-region inspection: traffic from us-east-1 to eu-west-1 can be inspected in either region's Inspection VPC. Architecture: us-east-1 spoke → us-east-1 TGW → us-east-1 Inspection VPC → us-east-1 TGW (back to peering attachment) → eu-west-1 TGW → eu-west-1 Inspection VPC → eu-west-1 spoke VPC. Double inspection adds latency — evaluate if compliance requires cross-region inspection or if regional inspection suffices.<br> *Exam: "multi-region architecture, Dev in us-east-1 must not reach Prod in eu-west-1" → TGW peering with static routes: in us-east-1 Dev TGW route table, do NOT add a route for eu-west-1 Prod CIDR (or add black hole route). Without a route, traffic cannot reach the destination regardless of peering connection. TGW peering does not propagate routes automatically — routes must be added explicitly.<br>

700. [ Traffic-Segmentation ] What is the Well-Architected Framework Security pillar recommendation for network protection in AWS? ; WAF Security pillar: implement layered network controls at all levels — VPC boundary (subnets), within VPC (SG/NACL), at ingress/egress points (Network Firewall/WAF), and for all service-to-service traffic (PrivateLink, VPC endpoints). Reduce network surface area: no public IPs unless necessary, minimize internet exposure, keep workloads in private subnets. <br><br> *Specific WAF guidance: SEC 5 (Protect networks): create network layers (public/private/isolated subnets), control traffic at every layer with appropriate security controls. Limit network access: use SG inbound deny-by-default. Use VPC endpoints to avoid internet paths. Monitor with Flow Logs, GuardDuty, Security Hub. Test network controls (penetration testing, chaos engineering).<br> *Network surface area reduction: every public IP address is a potential attack vector. Minimize: (1) Use ALB with EIP instead of public IPs on individual instances. (2) Use Session Manager instead of SSH bastion hosts (zero open SG ports). (3) Use VPC endpoints instead of NAT GW for AWS service calls. (4) Use PrivateLink for internal service exposure instead of ELB with public subnet. Each reduction lowers attack surface.<br> *Exam: "Well-Architected Review finds too many resources with public IPs and open SG ports" → remediation plan: migrate to private subnets + ALB + Session Manager for management + VPC endpoints for AWS services + SG refactoring to eliminate 0.0.0.0/0 inbound. This is a standard SAP-C02 "Continuous Improvement" domain question pattern.<br>



**VGW / DCG / TGW Questions**

701. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How many VPCs can a single Virtual Private Gateway (VGW) be attached to? ; One. A VGW is attached to exactly one VPC. To connect on-premises to multiple VPCs, you need either multiple VGWs (one per VPC, multiple VPN connections) or a Transit Gateway (one attachment serves many VPCs). <br><br> *VGW is a VPC-level resource: it is created and associated with a single VPC at creation time. There is no multi-VPC attachment capability on VGW. Each VPC that needs on-premises connectivity via VGW requires its own VGW.<br> *The exam will present "connect on-premises to 5 VPCs" scenarios — the correct answer is TGW (or DXGW + 5 VGWs for DX-only, but TGW is simpler and more scalable). VGW-per-VPC is operationally expensive at scale.<br> *VGW detach/re-attach: a VGW can be detached from one VPC and re-attached to another (same account, same region) — but only one VPC at a time. This is used during VPC migrations, not for simultaneous multi-VPC connectivity.<br>

702. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] Does a Virtual Private Gateway support transitive routing between VPCs? ; No. VGW does not support transitive routing. Traffic from on-premises arrives at VGW → enters the attached VPC. It cannot be forwarded from that VPC to another VPC via the VGW. Even if VPC peering exists between VPCs, return traffic from the peered VPC cannot transit back through VGW to on-premises. <br><br> *Transitive routing means: A → B → C (B is in the middle forwarding traffic). VGW acts as a termination point, not a router. The VPC route table can route on-premises traffic to local resources but cannot forward to peered VPCs via VGW.<br> *The correct solution for transitive routing from on-premises through AWS to multiple VPCs: use Transit Gateway. TGW is explicitly designed as a transitive hub — traffic from a VPN attachment can be routed to any VPC attachment via TGW route tables.<br> *This is a classic exam trap: "on-premises needs to access VPC-A and VPC-B, VPC-A and VPC-B are peered, on-premises connects via VGW to VPC-A" — VPC-B is NOT reachable from on-premises via this setup. Add TGW.<br>

703. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum VPN bandwidth achievable with a VGW, and why cannot it be exceeded? ; 1.25 Gbps per VPN tunnel. A Site-to-Site VPN connection has two tunnels — but on VGW, only one tunnel is active at a time (active/passive). Therefore the effective maximum is 1.25 Gbps. ECMP is not supported on VGW, so you cannot aggregate multiple tunnels for higher bandwidth. <br><br> *VGW VPN is active/passive: both tunnels are established (BGP sessions up on both), but traffic flows through only one. If the active tunnel fails, the passive tunnel takes over (failover in seconds). This provides HA but not bandwidth aggregation.<br> *To exceed 1.25 Gbps with VPN: use Transit Gateway with ECMP. TGW supports multiple VPN connections with ECMP — traffic is distributed across all active tunnels. Two VPN connections × 2 tunnels = 4 paths → ~5 Gbps aggregate.<br> *For bandwidth beyond what VPN can provide: use Direct Connect (DX). DX provides 1/10/100 Gbps dedicated ports. DX + VGW (Private VIF) can use the full DX port bandwidth. DX is the correct answer for multi-Gbps on-premises connectivity requirements.<br>

704. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the Direct Connect Gateway (DXGW) and what problem does it solve? ; DXGW is a globally scoped AWS resource that decouples a Direct Connect connection from specific VPCs or regions. Without DXGW, a DX Private VIF connects to exactly one VGW (one VPC, one region). With DXGW, one DX connection (via a single Private VIF to the DXGW) can reach VPCs in multiple AWS regions. <br><br> *Before DXGW: to reach VPCs in us-east-1, eu-west-1, and ap-southeast-1 from on-premises via DX, you needed three separate DX connections (or three VIFs, one per region's VGW). DXGW eliminates this — one Private VIF → DXGW → VGWs in any region.<br> *DXGW is a global resource: it is not created in a specific region. It exists globally and can associate with VGWs in any AWS region (except China). This makes it the standard solution for multi-region hybrid connectivity over DX.<br> *DXGW does NOT route traffic between VPCs: it is a spoke-to-on-premises bridge only. On-premises can reach VPC-A and VPC-B via DXGW, but VPC-A cannot reach VPC-B through DXGW. For VPC-to-VPC: add TGW.<br>

705. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How many VGWs can be associated with a single Direct Connect Gateway? ; 10 VGWs per DXGW. Each VGW association corresponds to one VPC (since one VGW = one VPC). So a single DXGW can provide on-premises access to up to 10 VPCs across any number of AWS regions. <br><br> *This limit is a soft limit in most cases but is important for exam scenarios. If a company has >10 VPCs needing on-premises DX connectivity: use DXGW + TGW (Transit VIF) instead. One TGW association can serve thousands of VPC attachments.<br> *The 3 TGW limit per DXGW also applies: a DXGW can be associated with up to 3 Transit Gateways simultaneously. Each TGW can have thousands of VPC attachments, so the effective scale is far beyond 10 VPCs.<br> *VPC CIDR overlap constraint: all VPCs associated with the same DXGW (directly via VGW or via TGW) must have non-overlapping CIDR blocks. DXGW enforces this at association time — overlapping CIDRs cause association failure.<br>

706. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What VIF type is required to connect a Direct Connect connection to a Transit Gateway? ; Transit VIF. A Transit VIF is the only VIF type that can connect a DX connection to a TGW (via DXGW). The path is: DX Connection → Transit VIF → DXGW → TGW. You cannot attach a Transit VIF directly to a TGW without a DXGW in the middle. <br><br> *VIF types and their targets: Private VIF → VGW or DXGW (for VPC access). Public VIF → AWS public services (S3, DynamoDB public endpoints). Transit VIF → DXGW only (then DXGW → TGW). Mixing them up on the exam is a common error.<br> *The DXGW is mandatory for Transit VIF: this is a hard architectural requirement, not optional. AWS does not support Transit VIF → TGW directly. Always insert DXGW between the Transit VIF and TGW.<br> *Why is DXGW required for Transit VIF? DXGW provides the global routing boundary and prefix filtering between the DX connection and the TGW. It also enforces the non-overlapping CIDR requirement and provides the BGP session termination point toward the on-premises router.<br>

707. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] Does a Direct Connect Gateway support routing traffic between two VPCs that are both associated with it? ; No. DXGW does not support VPC-to-VPC routing. Traffic flows only between on-premises and each associated VPC individually. VPC-A → DXGW → VPC-B is explicitly not supported. Each VPC's traffic is isolated — the DXGW is a bridge to on-premises, not a VPC interconnect. <br><br> *This is the most commonly tested DXGW limitation. The exam will describe a scenario where a company uses DXGW for multi-region VPC access and then asks "what is needed to also allow VPC-to-VPC communication?" — the answer is TGW (not DXGW alone).<br> *The underlying technical reason: DXGW BGP does not re-advertise VPC prefixes to other VPC associations. It only advertises on-premises routes to VPCs and VPC routes to on-premises. VPC-A's CIDR is never sent to VPC-B's VGW through DXGW.<br> *Solution when both multi-region VPC access AND VPC-to-VPC routing are needed: DXGW + TGW combination. TGW handles VPC-to-VPC routing within a region; DXGW provides the on-premises → TGW bridge across regions.<br>

708. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is Transit Gateway and how does it differ from VPC peering for multi-VPC connectivity? ; TGW is a regional hub-and-spoke router. VPCs attach to TGW (spoke) and TGW routes traffic between them. VPC peering is a point-to-point connection between exactly two VPCs — N VPCs need N*(N-1)/2 peering connections (full mesh). TGW replaces full mesh with N attachments to one hub. TGW also supports VPN and DX attachments; VPC peering does not. <br><br> *Scalability comparison: 10 VPCs with full mesh peering = 45 peering connections to manage. 10 VPCs with TGW = 10 attachments + 1 TGW. 100 VPCs: peering = 4,950 connections; TGW = 100 attachments. TGW wins at scale.<br> *Transitive routing: VPC peering does NOT support transitive routing (A→B peering + B→C peering does not mean A can reach C). TGW explicitly supports transitive routing via route tables — any attached VPC can reach any other attached VPC if routing allows.<br> *Cost difference: VPC peering has no hourly fee — only data transfer costs. TGW charges per attachment per hour (~$0.05/hr) AND per GB processed ($0.02/GB). For low-traffic, few-VPC scenarios: peering may be cheaper. For many VPCs with complex routing: TGW operational savings outweigh cost.<br>

709. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is ECMP on Transit Gateway and what bandwidth does it enable for VPN connections? ; ECMP (Equal-Cost Multi-Path) on TGW distributes traffic across multiple VPN tunnels simultaneously. With two VPN connections (4 tunnels total, all active), TGW ECMP enables approximately 5 Gbps aggregate VPN bandwidth. Each IPSec tunnel supports ~1.25 Gbps; ECMP aggregates multiple active tunnels. <br><br> *Requirements for ECMP: (1) multiple VPN connections to the same TGW, (2) BGP must be configured (ECMP only works with dynamic routing), (3) on-premises router must support ECMP and BGP multipath, (4) equal BGP routes (same AS path, same MED) for all paths.<br> *VGW vs TGW ECMP: VGW VPN is active/passive — only one of the two tunnels per connection carries traffic. TGW VPN supports active/active across all tunnels of all VPN connections. This is the key reason to migrate from VGW to TGW when bandwidth exceeds 1.25 Gbps.<br> *For even higher bandwidth: use Direct Connect instead. A 10 Gbps DX port via TGW (Transit VIF → DXGW → TGW) provides 10 Gbps dedicated. DX is the correct answer for consistent multi-Gbps on-premises connectivity; ECMP VPN is a cost-effective alternative when DX is not feasible.<br>

710. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does TGW route table isolation work, and what is a common use case? ; Each TGW attachment is associated with one route table (receives routes from it) and can propagate its routes into one or more route tables. By creating multiple route tables with different route sets, you can isolate traffic: production VPCs in one route table cannot reach dev VPCs in another, even though both are attached to the same TGW. <br><br> *Default behavior: all attachments go into the default route table, all can reach all. For isolation: create separate route tables per security domain. Associate prod VPC attachments with prod route table (contains only prod VPC CIDRs + on-premises). Associate dev VPC attachments with dev route table (contains only dev VPC CIDRs — no on-premises, no prod).<br> *Shared services pattern: create a "shared services" route table that all spoke VPCs can reach (contains Shared Services VPC CIDR — DNS, AD, NTP). Add Shared Services VPC route to both prod and dev route tables. Shared Services VPC's route table contains all spoke VPC CIDRs (for return traffic).<br> *Centralized inspection: route all outbound traffic from spoke VPCs through an Inspection VPC (firewall/IDS) by setting Inspection VPC as the next-hop in TGW route tables. Inspection VPC then forwards approved traffic to destination. TGW route tables are the enforcement point.<br>

711. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] Can a Transit Gateway be shared across AWS accounts, and how? ; Yes. TGW is shared via AWS Resource Access Manager (RAM). The TGW owner account creates the TGW and shares it via RAM with other accounts (individually or via AWS Organizations). Recipient accounts can then create TGW attachments (VPCs, VPNs) in their account to the shared TGW. <br><br> *Central networking pattern: a dedicated network account owns the TGW. It shares the TGW to all member accounts via AWS Organizations. Each team's account attaches its VPCs to the shared TGW. The network account controls route tables; application accounts cannot modify TGW route tables (IAM separation).<br> *Benefits: one TGW instead of per-account TGWs, centralized routing policy, centralized visibility (TGW Network Manager), no need for complex cross-account peering. This is the AWS recommended pattern for large organizations.<br> *Attachment billing: the attachment cost ($0.05/hr per attachment) is charged to the account that OWNS the attachment (the account that created the attachment to the shared TGW), not to the TGW owner account. Data processing fees also charged to the attachment owner's account.<br>

712. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is TGW Connect and when would you use it over a standard VPN attachment? ; TGW Connect is a GRE (Generic Routing Encapsulation) tunnel attachment type on TGW. It runs over an existing VPC or DX attachment (the transport attachment) and supports up to 5 Gbps per Connect peer (vs 1.25 Gbps per IPSec tunnel). It is used for SD-WAN virtual appliances (Cisco, VMware, Palo Alto) running in a VPC that need high-throughput connectivity to TGW. <br><br> *Architecture: SD-WAN NVA (Network Virtual Appliance) runs in an EC2 instance in a VPC. The VPC has a standard TGW VPC attachment (transport). TGW Connect creates a GRE tunnel FROM TGW TO the NVA inside the VPC. BGP runs over the GRE tunnel for dynamic routing.<br> *Why TGW Connect over VPN: (1) GRE has less overhead than IPSec (no encryption overhead — NVA handles its own encryption). (2) 5 Gbps per peer vs 1.25 Gbps per IPSec tunnel. (3) Designed for SD-WAN integration where the NVA is the encryption endpoint, not TGW. (4) Lower cost per Gbps than running many VPN connections for ECMP.<br> *When to use standard VPN: direct on-premises to TGW connectivity without an NVA, or when IPSec encryption at the AWS boundary is required (compliance requirement). TGW Connect requires an NVA in a VPC as the GRE tunnel endpoint — not suitable for direct on-premises router connections without AWS-side NVA.<br>

713. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company has a single DX connection and needs to access VPCs in us-east-1 and eu-west-1 from on-premises. What is the most cost-effective architecture? ; Use a Direct Connect Gateway (DXGW) with Private VIF associations to VGWs in both regions. One DX connection → one Private VIF → DXGW → VGW in us-east-1 (VPC A) + VGW in eu-west-1 (VPC B). No TGW needed if VPC-to-VPC routing is not required — no TGW attachment fees. <br><br> *Cost optimization: DXGW is free to use (no hourly charge for the gateway itself). DX port costs are unavoidable. VGW is free. The only cost is the DX connection itself (port + data transfer). Adding a TGW would add $0.05/hr per VPC attachment + $0.02/GB processing — unnecessary if VPC-to-VPC routing is not needed.<br> *The question tests DXGW's global scope: one Private VIF to DXGW can reach VGWs in any region. Without DXGW, you would need two separate DX connections or two VIFs (one per region) — more expensive.<br> *When to add TGW to this architecture: if (a) more than 10 VPCs are needed, (b) VPC-to-VPC routing is required, (c) VPN backup is needed on the same hub, or (d) shared services VPC must be reachable by all other VPCs. Those requirements justify TGW attachment fees.<br>

714. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What happens to BGP routes when a Direct Connect connection fails, and how does VPN backup work with VGW? ; When DX fails, BGP session over the DX Private VIF drops. Routes learned via DX BGP are withdrawn from the VPC route table (if route propagation is enabled). A VPN connection to the same VGW (configured as backup) has its routes with lower preference (higher BGP MED or longer AS path). When DX routes are withdrawn, VPN routes become active and traffic fails over to VPN automatically. <br><br> *BGP failover timing: BGP keepalive default is 10 seconds, hold timer 30 seconds. DX failure → BFD (Bidirectional Forwarding Detection) detects failure in <1 second (if configured). BFD on DX is recommended for fast failover. Without BFD, failover takes 30 seconds (hold timer expiry).<br> *Route preference configuration: to prefer DX over VPN, advertise DX routes with lower MED (e.g., MED=100) and VPN routes with higher MED (e.g., MED=200). Or use AS path prepending on VPN routes (longer AS path = lower preference). DX = preferred primary; VPN = standby backup.<br> *Same VGW for both: DX Private VIF and VPN connection can terminate on the SAME VGW. The VGW handles both attachments simultaneously and uses BGP route attributes to determine active path. No separate gateway needed for backup VPN.<br>

715. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum number of BGP prefixes that on-premises can advertise to AWS via a Private VIF, and what happens if exceeded? ; 100 BGP prefixes (routes) per Private VIF. If on-premises advertises more than 100 prefixes, the BGP session is terminated by AWS. The DX connection goes down. You must reduce the number of advertised prefixes (summarize routes using supernetting / CIDR aggregation) before the session can re-establish. <br><br> *For Transit VIF (to DXGW → TGW): the limit is 20,000 BGP prefixes, which is much more permissive. This is one reason enterprises use TGW over direct VGW associations — higher prefix capacity for complex on-premises route tables.<br> *Route summarization to stay under limit: instead of advertising 200 specific /24 routes, advertise one /16 supernet that covers all of them. Requires on-premises network planning to ensure subnets fit within the summary CIDR. AWS receives one route that covers all prefixes.<br> *VGW to on-premises: VGW advertises the VPC CIDR block(s) to on-premises via BGP. Each additional CIDR block (e.g., secondary VPC CIDR) adds one more advertised prefix. Design VPCs with few, well-summarized CIDRs.<br>

716. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] Can TGW peering connections use BGP for dynamic route exchange between regions? ; No. TGW peering connections use static routes only — there is no BGP between peered TGWs. When you create a TGW peering attachment between TGW-us-east-1 and TGW-eu-west-1, you must manually add static routes in each TGW's route table pointing to the peer TGW attachment for the remote region's CIDRs. <br><br> *This is a key limitation vs. on-premises BGP: within a region, TGW uses BGP with VPN and DX connections for dynamic routing. But across TGW peering connections, routing is entirely static. Any CIDR change in a remote region requires manual route table updates.<br> *Implication for large multi-region deployments: as the number of VPCs grows, maintaining static routes across TGW peerings becomes operationally burdensome. Automate via CloudFormation, Terraform, or TGW Network Manager. Some teams use Route 53 Resolver + DNS-based discovery to reduce reliance on precise cross-region routing.<br> *Contrast with AWS Cloud WAN: AWS Cloud WAN (built on TGW under the hood) supports BGP-like dynamic routing across regions via a global network policy, avoiding manual static route management. For very large multi-region networks, Cloud WAN is the modern alternative.<br>

717. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company needs to inspect all traffic between on-premises and VPCs before it reaches application workloads. Which gateway type best supports centralized inspection? ; Transit Gateway with a dedicated Inspection VPC (running firewall appliances + Gateway Load Balancer). TGW route tables redirect all inbound traffic from the VPN/DX attachment to the Inspection VPC first. After inspection, approved traffic is forwarded to destination VPC attachments. VGW and DXGW alone do not support traffic redirection to an inspection path. <br><br> *Architecture: On-premises → TGW (VPN or DX attachment) → TGW route table directs traffic to Inspection VPC attachment → Firewall NVA + GWLB inspects and forwards → TGW routes approved traffic to app VPC. Return traffic: app VPC → TGW → Inspection VPC → TGW → on-premises.<br> *Gateway Load Balancer (GWLB) role: GWLB distributes traffic to firewall NVA fleet and returns inspected traffic to the same flow. GWLB endpoints in the Inspection VPC are the ingress/egress points. TGW route tables point to GWLB endpoint as the next hop for traffic requiring inspection.<br> *Why VGW/DXGW cannot do this: VGW terminates VPN/DX and delivers traffic directly to the attached VPC — there is no route table hop to redirect to another VPC. TGW's programmable route tables are the enabler for centralized inspection architectures.<br>

718. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the procedure to migrate connectivity from a VGW-based VPN to a TGW-based VPN with minimal downtime? ; (1) Create TGW in the region. (2) Create a VPC attachment on TGW for the VPC currently using VGW. (3) Create a new Site-to-Site VPN connection on TGW. (4) Update on-premises router with new VPN configuration. (5) Use BGP route preferences (lower MED on TGW VPN, higher MED on VGW VPN) to shift traffic gradually to TGW. (6) Verify connectivity. (7) Update VPC route tables to use TGW instead of VGW. (8) Delete VGW VPN and detach VGW. <br><br> *Critical step: both VGW VPN and TGW VPN run simultaneously during migration. This is the cutover window — traffic flows through VGW until BGP prefers TGW routes. No downtime if BGP reconvergence is fast (BFD helps here).<br> *Route table change: VPC subnet route tables initially have routes propagated from VGW. After migration, enable TGW route propagation and disable VGW propagation (or replace with TGW attachment). This change is the actual traffic cutover point — it is fast but causes a brief routing disruption (~seconds).<br> *Test thoroughly: before cutting over production, test TGW VPN end-to-end. Verify all required routes are present in TGW route tables, VPC route tables, and on-premises routing table. Test application connectivity before removing VGW.<br>

719. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the cost structure difference between connecting 10 VPCs to on-premises via DXGW+VGW vs TGW? ; DXGW + VGW: DX port cost + data transfer only. No attachment fees, no per-GB TGW processing fee. TGW: $0.05/hr per VPC attachment (10 VPCs = $0.50/hr = ~$360/month) + $0.02/GB all traffic processed by TGW + VPN attachment fee if using VPN. For 10 VPCs with no VPC-to-VPC routing, DXGW+VGW is significantly cheaper. <br><br> *When TGW cost is justified: (a) more than 10 VPCs (exceeds DXGW VGW limit), (b) VPC-to-VPC routing required, (c) centralized inspection needed, (d) RAM sharing across accounts required, (e) ECMP VPN bandwidth needed. These features are only available on TGW and cannot be replicated with DXGW+VGW alone.<br> *High-traffic workloads: at $0.02/GB, 1 TB/day of TGW-processed traffic = $20/day = $600/month just for processing. Compare to DXGW+VGW where only DX data transfer costs apply. For high-throughput workloads: evaluate whether TGW features justify processing overhead cost.<br> *Hybrid cost optimization: use DXGW+VGW for simpler VPCs (no VPC-to-VPC, multi-region DX access), use TGW only where its features are needed (complex routing, centralized inspection, shared services). Mixing both patterns in the same organization is valid.<br>

720. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company uses AWS Organizations with 100 accounts. They want all VPCs to share one on-premises DX connection. What is the recommended architecture? ; Central network account: create TGW + DXGW. Connect DX via Transit VIF → DXGW → TGW. Share TGW via RAM to all 100 accounts (via AWS Organizations). Each account attaches its VPCs to the shared TGW. Central network team manages TGW route tables; application account teams attach VPCs. <br><br> *This pattern is the AWS recommended multi-account networking design. Central network account owns: DX connection, DXGW, TGW. It shares TGW via RAM → Organizations. App accounts: attach VPCs to shared TGW. No per-account gateways needed.<br> *Billing: DX port and TGW hourly fee charged to network account. Each VPC attachment ($0.05/hr) charged to the account owning the attachment. Data processing ($0.02/GB) also charged to attachment owner's account. This enables chargeback: each team pays for their own traffic.<br> *Security isolation: TGW route tables enforce isolation. Prod accounts' VPCs in prod route table (access on-premises + shared services). Dev accounts' VPCs in dev route table (access dev resources + shared services, no on-premises). Route tables are owned by network account — app teams cannot modify routing.<br>

721. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is VPN route propagation on a VPC route table, and why is it important? ; Route propagation allows a VGW or TGW to automatically inject BGP-learned routes into a VPC subnet route table. Without it, you must manually add static routes for every on-premises CIDR block to each subnet route table. With route propagation enabled, when a new on-premises prefix is advertised via BGP, it appears in the VPC route table automatically. <br><br> *Enable via console: VPC → Route Tables → select table → Route Propagation tab → enable VGW or TGW. Or via CLI: `aws ec2 enable-vgw-route-propagation --route-table-id rtb-xxx --gateway-id vgw-xxx`. TGW route propagation is configured separately in TGW route tables (propagation of attachment routes).<br> *Security consideration: route propagation is convenient but can introduce unexpected routes if on-premises accidentally advertises broad or overlapping CIDRs. For high-security environments: disable propagation, use explicit static routes (only specific, approved CIDRs). Review propagated routes in production regularly.<br> *TGW propagation vs VPC route table propagation: TGW has its own propagation mechanism — each attachment can propagate routes INTO TGW route tables. Separately, VPC subnet route tables can have routes pointing to the TGW attachment as next hop. These are two different levels of routing configuration.<br>

722. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the difference between a Private VIF connected to a VGW vs connected to a DXGW? ; Private VIF → VGW: connects DX to a single VPC (one VGW, one region). Private VIF → DXGW: connects DX to DXGW, which then can be associated with up to 10 VGWs across any AWS region. The Private VIF → DXGW path enables multi-region, multi-VPC access from a single DX connection. <br><br> *When to use VIF → VGW directly: simple single-VPC connectivity, dev/test environments, no multi-region requirement. Saves one DXGW management step (though DXGW is free). Works fine for one VPC.<br> *When to use VIF → DXGW: any scenario requiring VPCs in multiple regions, or future-proofing for growth. DXGW is free; the only overhead is configuration. Best practice: always use DXGW even for single-VPC if multi-region access might be needed later — adding DXGW after the fact requires VIF reconfiguration.<br> *VIF per DX connection limit: up to 50 Private VIFs or Transit VIFs per DX connection. Using one Private VIF → DXGW is more efficient than one Private VIF per VGW (which would consume one VIF per region). Conserving VIF count matters for large deployments.<br>

723. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] Can you use both a VGW and a TGW in the same VPC simultaneously? ; Yes, but with caveats. A VPC can have one VGW attached AND one TGW VPC attachment. However, routing must be explicit — subnet route tables determine which traffic goes to VGW vs TGW. Route propagation from both can cause conflicts. In practice, this is a transition state during migration from VGW to TGW, not a permanent dual-gateway design. <br><br> *Why you'd have both: during migration (VGW-to-TGW cutover), both gateways exist simultaneously. BGP route preferences on on-premises router determine which path carries active traffic. Once migration is validated, remove VGW.<br> *Route conflict risk: if both VGW and TGW propagate the same on-premises CIDRs to the VPC route table, there is a conflict. AWS uses longest prefix match and route priority. To avoid: disable route propagation from one gateway while the other is active primary.<br> *Not recommended as permanent design: having both VGW and TGW long-term adds operational complexity and unnecessary cost. Choose one: VGW (simple single-VPC) or TGW (scalable multi-VPC). The dual-gateway state should be temporary.<br>

724. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the BGP Autonomous System Number (ASN) used by VGW and TGW, and can it be customized? ; VGW default ASN: 64512. TGW default ASN: 64512. Both can be customized during creation (not after). VGW: any private ASN (64512–65534 for 16-bit, or 4200000000–4294967294 for 32-bit). TGW: same range. On-premises router uses a different ASN (must not overlap with AWS side). <br><br> *Why customize ASN: if multiple VGWs or TGWs exist in a BGP topology, each needs a unique ASN to avoid routing loops. Also, if on-premises uses ASN 64512 (common default), the AWS side must use a different ASN to establish BGP (eBGP requires different ASNs on each end).<br> *Cannot change after creation: ASN is set at creation time and is immutable. If you need a different ASN on an existing VGW/TGW: create a new gateway with the desired ASN and migrate. This is a disruptive operation requiring reconfiguration of all attached VPN/DX connections.<br> *64512 is widely used: many organizations default to 64512 for internal BGP. If your on-premises uses 64512 and your VGW also uses 64512 (default), BGP sessions will fail (same ASN = loop prevention drops routes). Always verify ASN uniqueness before deploying.<br>

725. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does hybrid DNS work when on-premises needs to resolve AWS private hosted zone records and vice versa? ; Use Route 53 Resolver. R53 Resolver Inbound Endpoint: allows on-premises DNS servers to forward queries for AWS private hosted zones to Route 53 (via IP addresses in VPC subnets). R53 Resolver Outbound Endpoint: allows VPC DNS to forward queries for on-premises domains to on-premises DNS servers. Both endpoints exist in a VPC; routing between VPC and on-premises via VGW, DXGW, or TGW carries DNS traffic. <br><br> *Architecture with TGW: create a Shared DNS VPC. Place R53 Resolver endpoints in this VPC. Attach Shared DNS VPC to TGW. All spoke VPCs use TGW route to reach Shared DNS VPC for resolver endpoints. On-premises DNS servers forward AWS zone queries to Inbound Endpoint IPs. R53 Resolver Outbound Endpoints forward on-premises domain queries to on-premises DNS.<br> *Why centralized DNS via TGW: without TGW, each VPC needs its own resolver endpoints (costly, complex). With TGW: one set of endpoints in Shared DNS VPC, all VPCs route DNS through it. Centralized, cost-effective hybrid DNS.<br> *VGW limitation for DNS: VGW allows DNS traffic through (it is just IP routing). But VGW cannot share R53 Resolver endpoints across VPCs — each VPC needs its own. TGW enables the centralized DNS pattern because it allows VPCs to route to Shared DNS VPC's endpoints.<br>

726. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is a TGW Network Manager and what visibility does it provide? ; TGW Network Manager is a centralized console for viewing and monitoring global network topology across TGWs, VPNs, DX connections, and SD-WAN devices. It shows a visual map of all registered TGWs, their peerings, attachments, and connectivity to on-premises sites. CloudWatch metrics are integrated for per-attachment bandwidth and packet metrics. <br><br> *Registration: TGWs must be registered with Network Manager (free). SD-WAN devices (on-premises) can also be registered as "customer gateway devices" to show the full end-to-end topology from on-premises through TGW to VPCs.<br> *Event history: Network Manager logs topology changes (new attachments, route changes, BGP session changes) as events. Useful for troubleshooting: "when did this route disappear?" answered by event history. CloudWatch metrics show bandwidth trends per attachment.<br> *Route Analyzer: Network Manager includes a Route Analyzer tool — input source/destination IP, it traces the path through TGW route tables and shows which route table entry forwards the packet. Invaluable for debugging TGW routing issues without packet captures.<br>

727. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company's on-premises router is advertising a default route (0.0.0.0/0) via BGP to AWS. What is the impact on VPC routing? ; If route propagation is enabled on VPC route tables, the default route (0.0.0.0/0) learned from on-premises BGP is injected into subnet route tables. This causes ALL traffic from instances in that subnet (including internet traffic) to be routed back to on-premises through the VPN/DX — "forced tunneling." Internet-bound traffic is backhauled to on-premises and must exit through the corporate internet breakout. <br><br> *Intended use case for forced tunneling: security policy requires all internet traffic to pass through corporate security stack (proxy, DLP, firewall). Common in financial services and government. All VPC internet traffic → VPN → on-premises → internet. AWS internet gateway is not used for egress.<br> *Unintended consequence: if the on-premises default route advertisement is accidental (misconfiguration), all internet traffic breaks (on-premises may not be configured to forward AWS traffic to internet). Symptoms: VPC instances lose internet connectivity, AWS service API calls fail if not using VPC endpoints.<br> *Resolution: remove default route from on-premises BGP advertisement, or add explicit static route 0.0.0.0/0 → Internet Gateway in VPC route table (more specific local route overrides BGP-learned default route via longest prefix match).<br>

728. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the Well-Architected Reliability pillar recommendation for DX connectivity resiliency, and which gateway type best enables it? ; Maximum resiliency: two DX connections at two separate DX locations (four physical paths). Transit Gateway enables this because multiple DX connections can all terminate on the same TGW (via DXGW + multiple Transit VIFs). TGW ECMP then load-balances across all active DX paths. VGW supports only one DXGW association per VIF attachment. <br><br> *High resiliency minimum: two connections, two locations. Maximum resiliency: four connections (two per location), two locations. TGW accommodates all connections: DXGW-1 (location 1) → TGW, DXGW-2 (location 2) → TGW. Multiple DX connections per DXGW (up to 20). TGW selects best route via BGP and ECMP.<br> *VPN as additional backup layer: beyond DX resiliency, add Site-to-Site VPN to TGW as the final fallback (internet path). BGP route preferences: DX primary (lowest MED), VPN backup (highest MED). VPN activates only when all DX connections fail.<br> *DXGW + TGW vs DXGW + VGW for resiliency: TGW is recommended because: (1) ECMP across multiple DX connections, (2) single routing policy for all connections (route tables), (3) VPN backup on same TGW, (4) TGW handles active/active path selection. VGW is passive-only — less optimal for maximum resiliency.<br>

729. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What does the term "Transit VIF" mean and how does it differ from a Private VIF in terms of routing capability? ; A Transit VIF is a Direct Connect Virtual Interface specifically designed to connect to a DXGW → TGW path. It supports up to 20,000 BGP prefix advertisements (vs 100 for Private VIF). A Private VIF connects to a VGW or DXGW for direct VPC access. The Transit VIF enables scalable multi-VPC routing through TGW, while Private VIF is for direct VPC access with lower prefix limits. <br><br> *One Transit VIF per DX connection per DXGW: you can have only one Transit VIF per DX connection to a given DXGW. However, one DXGW can have up to 20 DX connections (20 Transit VIFs from different DX connections). This enables redundant DX paths to the same DXGW → TGW architecture.<br> *Cannot mix Private VIF and Transit VIF on same DXGW for same VPC: a VPC can only be reached via DXGW either through a VGW association (Private VIF) OR through a TGW association (Transit VIF) — not both simultaneously to the same DXGW. Choose one path.<br> *BGP prefix capacity comparison: Private VIF = 100 prefixes (on-premises to AWS), Transit VIF = 20,000 prefixes. For enterprises with complex on-premises routing tables: Transit VIF eliminates the route summarization constraint that Private VIF imposes.<br>

730. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] An enterprise needs its AWS VPCs to communicate with each other AND with on-premises, all through a single network hub. Which AWS service provides this? ; Transit Gateway. TGW acts as the central hub: VPC attachments for VPC-to-VPC routing, VPN attachment for on-premises VPN connectivity, and DX via DXGW Transit VIF attachment for on-premises DX connectivity. All traffic between any two attached networks passes through TGW. <br><br> *Hub-and-spoke model: TGW is the hub. Spokes are: VPCs, VPN connections, DX (DXGW), TGW peers. Any-to-any connectivity is enabled by TGW route tables (subject to routing policy). Without TGW: you'd need full-mesh VPC peering + per-VPC VPN/DX connections — unmanageable at scale.<br> *DXGW alone cannot do this: DXGW provides on-premises → VPC connectivity but no VPC-to-VPC. VGW alone: one VPC, no other VPCs. Only TGW provides the combined VPC-to-VPC + VPC-to-on-premises hub functionality.<br> *Correct exam answer when asked "single hub for all network connectivity": Transit Gateway. If the question says "multi-region single hub": TGW + TGW peering across regions (with DXGW for DX, separate TGW per region, static routes for inter-region peering).<br>
