731. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum bandwidth of a single TGW VPC attachment? ; Up to 50 Gbps burst per VPC attachment. This is the theoretical maximum — actual throughput depends on instance types in the VPC and application traffic patterns. There is no guaranteed bandwidth minimum per attachment; bandwidth is shared across the TGW fabric. <br><br> *Practical bandwidth: for most workloads, TGW VPC attachment bandwidth is not the bottleneck — instance network bandwidth or application throughput limits come first. Single EC2 instance network bandwidth ranges from 12.5 Gbps (c5n.4xlarge) to 200 Gbps (u-series). TGW 50 Gbps burst covers most scenarios.<br> *TGW is not a dedicated connection: unlike DX where you have a dedicated port with guaranteed bandwidth, TGW is a shared fabric. For predictable, consistent high-bandwidth on-premises connectivity: DX via DXGW → TGW is the right answer (DX provides the bandwidth guarantee; TGW provides the routing hub).<br> *When bandwidth is a concern: monitor TGW BytesIn/BytesOut and PacketsDropped CloudWatch metrics. Packet drops indicate TGW congestion (rare but possible at extreme throughput). Adding more VPC attachments (subnets in multiple AZs) improves resilience but not raw throughput.<br>

732. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does a TGW handle Availability Zone (AZ) resiliency for VPC attachments? ; When creating a TGW VPC attachment, you specify subnets in each AZ you want the attachment to cover. TGW places an attachment endpoint in each specified AZ. Traffic from instances in an AZ uses the local AZ's attachment endpoint (AZ-affinity) to avoid cross-AZ data transfer charges. If one AZ's endpoint fails, TGW fails over to another AZ's endpoint. <br><br> *Best practice: specify subnets in all AZs used by the VPC when creating a TGW attachment. This ensures: AZ-local traffic paths (lower latency, no cross-AZ charges), and resiliency if one AZ attachment point has issues.<br> *Cross-AZ TGW traffic: if a VPC only attaches TGW in one AZ's subnet, instances in other AZs still work but their traffic crosses AZs to reach the TGW endpoint. This incurs $0.01/GB cross-AZ data transfer (standard cross-AZ rate). Minimize by attaching TGW in all AZs.<br> *TGW itself is regional multi-AZ: the TGW control plane and routing fabric are inherently multi-AZ — AWS manages this. You do not need to provision TGW redundancy; only the VPC attachment subnet selection affects AZ coverage.<br>

733. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the difference between active/passive and active/active VPN on VGW vs TGW? ; VGW VPN: active/passive. Two tunnels per VPN connection; only one is active at a time. Failover from active to passive tunnel is automatic (BGP detects failure) but traffic only flows on one tunnel. TGW VPN: active/active (with ECMP). Multiple VPN connections, multiple tunnels, all can carry traffic simultaneously via ECMP — providing both higher bandwidth and faster failover. <br><br> *Practical implication: with VGW, one tunnel failure causes a brief outage while failover to passive tunnel occurs (~seconds with BFD). With TGW ECMP, one tunnel failure simply reduces available bandwidth (remaining tunnels still carry traffic) — no outage, no reconvergence needed for the surviving paths.<br> *Configuration requirement for TGW ECMP: BGP must be enabled (no static routing for ECMP), on-premises router must support ECMP/multipath BGP, and all ECMP paths must have equal BGP attributes (same MED, same AS path length). If one path has a better BGP route, only that path carries traffic — ECMP requires equal-cost paths.<br> *Exam scenario: "customer needs VPN bandwidth > 1.25 Gbps and high availability" → TGW with ECMP VPN. "Customer needs simple failover, <1.25 Gbps, single VPC" → VGW active/passive VPN is sufficient and cheaper.<br>

734. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the DXGW global scope significance in terms of data transfer pricing? ; DX data transfer pricing is based on the AWS Region where the VPC resides, not the DX location. With DXGW, one DX connection (at a US DX location, for example) can reach VPCs in eu-west-1 and ap-southeast-1. Data transferred to those VPCs is billed at the respective region's DX data transfer rate, even though the physical connection is in the US. <br><br> *The DX connection exits the DX PoP (US), travels the AWS backbone to the destination region (EU/AP), and exits to the VPC. The customer is billed at the destination region's DX data transfer rate. AWS backbone transit is included in the DX rate — no additional cross-region transit fee.<br> *Cost implication: routing all traffic through DXGW to VPCs in cheaper DX regions (e.g., us-east-1) vs more expensive regions (e.g., ap-southeast-1) affects total cost. Plan DXGW associations considering regional DX data transfer rates.<br> *Compare to internet-based VPN: VPN over internet has internet egress charges at both ends (AWS DTO + on-premises ISP). DX data transfer rates are lower than internet DTO for high-volume traffic — another reason DXGW+DX beats VPN for high-bandwidth multi-region connectivity.<br>

735. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company wants to connect 200 VPCs across 5 AWS accounts to a single on-premises data center using one DX connection. Design the architecture. ; Central network account → DX connection → DXGW → Transit VIF → TGW (in each relevant region). Share TGW via RAM to all 5 accounts. Each account attaches its VPCs to the shared TGW. DXGW bridges the DX connection to TGW. TGW provides VPC-to-VPC routing and handles all 200 VPCs. <br><br> *Why this architecture: DXGW alone cannot handle 200 VPCs (10 VGW limit). TGW supports 5,000 VPC attachments. DXGW + TGW via Transit VIF is the correct solution for large-scale multi-VPC on-premises connectivity.<br> *Account isolation via RAM: TGW is owned by network account, shared to other 4 accounts. Each account creates VPC attachments to the shared TGW (billed to each account). Network account team manages route tables — full centralized routing control with delegated attachment creation.<br> *Route table design for 200 VPCs: segment by environment (prod/dev/staging route tables). Each has routes to on-premises (via DXGW attachment). Shared services VPC accessible from all route tables. Prod and dev route tables isolated from each other (prod route table does not contain dev VPC CIDRs).<br>

736. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum number of Transit Gateways that can be associated with a single Direct Connect Gateway? ; 3 Transit Gateways per DXGW. This allows connecting one DX connection (via DXGW) to up to 3 TGWs across different regions. Each TGW can serve thousands of VPCs. The combination supports very large multi-region deployments from a single DX connection. <br><br> *3 TGW limit design implication: if you need more than 3 regions served from one DXGW, you need either multiple DXGW resources or accept that some regions connect via TGW peering from an adjacent region's TGW (static routes). Most enterprises have ≤ 3 primary AWS regions, making this limit non-binding.<br> *DXGW + 3 TGW routing: DXGW advertises on-premises routes to all 3 associated TGWs. Each TGW propagates its VPC CIDRs back to DXGW → on-premises router learns all VPC CIDRs. Non-overlapping CIDR requirement: all VPCs across all 3 TGWs must have non-overlapping CIDRs.<br> *Compare to 10 VGW limit: 3 TGWs × thousands of VPCs per TGW >> 10 VGWs × 1 VPC each. Always use Transit VIF + TGW (not Private VIF + VGW) when scale exceeds 10 VPCs or VPC-to-VPC routing is needed.<br>

737. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What happens to existing routes in a TGW route table when a VPC attachment is deleted? ; Routes that were propagated by the deleted attachment are automatically removed from all TGW route tables where they were propagated. Static routes pointing to the deleted attachment remain but become "blackhole" routes (next hop is inactive). Manual cleanup of blackhole static routes is required — TGW does not auto-delete static routes pointing to deleted attachments. <br><br> *Blackhole route risk: if a static route points to a deleted VPN attachment (e.g., "10.0.0.0/8 → vpn-attachment-xxx"), and that VPN is deleted, the route stays as a blackhole. Traffic matching that prefix is silently dropped. Periodically audit TGW route tables for blackhole routes using the AWS console or CLI.<br> *CLI to find blackhole routes: `aws ec2 search-transit-gateway-routes --transit-gateway-route-table-id tgw-rtb-xxx --filters Name=state,Values=blackhole`. Review and clean up any found. Automate this audit via Lambda on a schedule.<br> *Propagated routes are safe: propagated routes have lifecycle tied to the attachment — they clean up automatically. Only manually-created static routes need manual cleanup. Best practice: prefer route propagation over static routes where possible, to reduce operational overhead.<br>

738. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does BFD (Bidirectional Forwarding Detection) improve resiliency for DX and VPN connections? ; BFD detects link failures in milliseconds (sub-second) vs BGP hold timer detection (30 seconds default). On DX: BFD is supported on Private and Transit VIFs — enables fast failover from DX to VPN backup. On VPN: BFD is supported on TGW VPN connections — enables fast failover between ECMP tunnels. Fast detection = faster routing reconvergence = lower application impact during failures. <br><br> *BFD configuration: AWS enables BFD on DX VIFs automatically when you request it (or it may be enabled by default depending on configuration). On-premises router must also support and enable BFD. BFD runs over the same VIF as BGP. BFD failure → BGP session immediately marked as down → routes withdrawn immediately → backup path activates.<br> *Without BFD: BGP hold timer = 30 seconds. A DX physical failure that doesn't immediately drop BGP keepalives (e.g., silent failure) takes up to 30 seconds to detect. During those 30 seconds, traffic is blackholed. BFD reduces this to <1 second.<br> *BFD on TGW VPN: TGW supports BFD for VPN tunnels as well. Enable IKEv2 and set BFD parameters on on-premises VPN device. For critical hybrid workloads: always enable BFD on both DX and VPN paths to TGW.<br>

739. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the significance of non-overlapping CIDR requirements for VPCs associated with the same DXGW? ; All VPCs associated with the same DXGW (whether via VGW directly or via TGW) must have non-overlapping CIDR blocks. This is enforced by DXGW at association time. If two VPCs have overlapping CIDRs (e.g., both use 10.0.0.0/16), the second association attempt fails. On-premises cannot route to both VPCs if their CIDRs overlap. <br><br> *Why non-overlapping is required: on-premises router learns VPC CIDRs via BGP from DXGW. If two VPCs have the same CIDR, on-premises cannot distinguish which VPC to route traffic to — routing is ambiguous. DXGW prevents this by rejecting overlapping CIDR associations upfront.<br> *Planning implication for enterprises: when planning VPC CIDRs in a multi-region organization, allocate non-overlapping ranges per region and per environment. Use an IP Address Management (IPAM) tool — AWS VPC IPAM (free within Organizations) allocates unique CIDRs and prevents overlap.<br> *TGW allows overlapping CIDRs (partially): TGW itself can have VPC attachments with overlapping CIDRs, using specific static routes to differentiate. But when those VPCs are also associated with DXGW (for on-premises access), the DXGW non-overlap requirement applies. Design for non-overlapping from the start.<br>

740. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the Azure Virtual WAN equivalent to AWS Transit Gateway, and what are the key differences? ; Azure Virtual WAN (vWAN) is the Azure equivalent of TGW. Both are regional hub-and-spoke network hubs. Key differences: vWAN is fully managed with automated BGP and built-in routing across regions (vWAN hubs can BGP peer with each other). TGW peering is manual/static. vWAN Standard tier includes Azure Firewall in the hub natively. TGW requires a separate Inspection VPC for firewall. vWAN is more managed; TGW gives more routing control. <br><br> *vWAN Basic vs Standard: Basic = site-to-site VPN only. Standard = all connection types (VPN, ExpressRoute, VNet), hub-to-hub routing, Azure Firewall. TGW has only one tier (all features available). vWAN Standard is the correct Azure equivalent for full TGW-like functionality.<br> *Routing control: TGW route tables give fine-grained control (associate, propagate, static routes per attachment). vWAN routing intent is configured via routing policies — simpler but less granular. For complex routing segmentation: TGW is more flexible. For simpler managed setup: vWAN.<br> *Transit Gateway Connect vs vWAN NVA integration: TGW Connect (GRE) for SD-WAN NVAs in VPC. vWAN also supports NVA integration in the hub (Barracuda, Fortinet NVAs can be deployed in vWAN hub). Both support SD-WAN integration, with different mechanisms.<br>

741. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company migrates from on-premises to AWS over 18 months. How should TGW support phased migration? ; Deploy TGW at migration start. Connect on-premises via VPN (or DX) to TGW. As each workload migrates to a new VPC, attach that VPC to TGW. On-premises systems reach migrated workloads via TGW throughout migration. Remaining on-premises workloads reach AWS via TGW. Post-migration: TGW remains as the network hub for all VPC-to-VPC and any residual on-premises connectivity. <br><br> *Consistent network architecture: TGW provides the same connection model throughout migration (on-premises always reaches AWS VPCs via TGW). No need to reconfigure on-premises routing as each new VPC is added — just attach the VPC to TGW and propagate its routes.<br> *Phased DX upgrade: start with VPN to TGW (faster to set up, <1 week). When migration matures and bandwidth requirements grow: add DX connection via DXGW → TGW. VPN remains as backup. No change to VPC attachments or on-premises VPC routing when upgrading connectivity type.<br> *Avoid VGW for migration scenarios: starting with VGW (for initial simple connectivity) and later migrating to TGW is extra work. If the final state will be TGW (multi-VPC, multi-account), start with TGW to avoid two migrations.<br>

742. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the AWS recommendation for connecting branch offices to AWS using SD-WAN appliances? ; TGW Connect (GRE tunnel) with SD-WAN NVAs in a transit/hub VPC. Branch offices connect to the SD-WAN NVA over the internet (SD-WAN managed connectivity). The NVA then connects to TGW via TGW Connect GRE tunnel. TGW routes traffic from NVA to destination VPCs. Alternatively: Cisco Meraki, VMware SASE, Palo Alto Prisma directly integrate with TGW Connect. <br><br> *Architecture: Branch → (SD-WAN fabric/internet) → SD-WAN NVA EC2 in Hub VPC → TGW Connect GRE tunnel → TGW → Spoke VPCs. Branch offices get AWS VPC access via the SD-WAN fabric without needing per-branch VPN connections to AWS.<br> *TGW Connect advantages over per-branch VPN: (1) SD-WAN vendor manages branch connectivity centrally. (2) Single TGW Connect attachment handles all branches (no per-branch VPN). (3) SD-WAN applies QoS, WAN optimization. (4) 5 Gbps per Connect peer vs 1.25 Gbps per VPN tunnel.<br> *When to use direct VPN to TGW instead: branch offices without SD-WAN appliances, or requirements for AWS-managed IPSec endpoints. TGW Connect requires an NVA in a VPC as the GRE endpoint; without NVA, standard Site-to-Site VPN to TGW is the answer.<br>

743. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What TGW attachment type should be used to connect VPCs in different AWS accounts without VPC peering? ; TGW VPC attachment in each account, where the TGW is shared via RAM. Account A attaches its VPC to the shared TGW. Account B attaches its VPC to the same shared TGW. TGW route tables control whether Account A's VPC can reach Account B's VPC. No VPC peering needed — TGW is the interconnect. <br><br> *RAM sharing mechanics: TGW owner account shares TGW via RAM → AWS Organizations. Recipient accounts see the shared TGW and can create VPC attachments (but not modify the TGW itself or its route tables). The TGW owner controls routing policy.<br> *Vs VPC peering: cross-account VPC peering is a point-to-point solution. For 3 VPCs in 3 accounts: 3 peering connections needed (full mesh). For 10 VPCs across 10 accounts: 45 peering connections. TGW with RAM: 10 VPC attachments to one TGW — O(N) vs O(N²). TGW is the scalable solution.<br> *Access control: IAM policies control which accounts can create attachments to the shared TGW. TGW route tables (managed by the owner) control which attachments can route to each other — security at the routing layer, regardless of who created the attachments.<br>

744. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What are the two BGP session types used on a Site-to-Site VPN with TGW, and what is tunnel redundancy? ; Each Site-to-Site VPN connection has two tunnels. Each tunnel has one BGP session (one eBGP session per tunnel endpoint). TGW supports both tunnels as active paths (ECMP). The two tunnels terminate on different AWS endpoints in different AZs — this is the built-in tunnel redundancy. If one AWS VPN endpoint fails, the other tunnel (different AZ) remains active. <br><br> *Two tunnels = two outside IP addresses on AWS side. On-premises router must configure both. BGP peers: on-premises to tunnel-1 AWS IP, and on-premises to tunnel-2 AWS IP. Both BGP sessions are up simultaneously (with TGW ECMP). Traffic flows across both when ECMP is configured.<br> *IKEv1 vs IKEv2: Site-to-Site VPN supports both. IKEv2 is recommended (more secure, faster rekeying, supports BFD, supports ECMP better). IKEv1 limitations: no BFD, active/passive only. For TGW ECMP and BFD: use IKEv2.<br> *VPN tunnel idle timeout: AWS VPN tunnels go into a "DOWN" state if no traffic flows for approximately 10 seconds. Solution: enable "tunnel inside IP" keepalives, or use DPD (Dead Peer Detection) with "restart" action. Always configure the on-premises router to initiate the tunnel (not rely on AWS to initiate).<br>

745. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the operational difference between "associated route table" and "propagated route table" in TGW configuration? ; Associated route table: the route table that an attachment LOOKS UP for routing decisions. An attachment can only be associated with ONE route table. Propagated route table: route tables that an attachment INJECTS its routes INTO. An attachment can propagate into multiple route tables. Association = "what routes does this attachment use?" Propagation = "which route tables receive this attachment's routes?" <br><br> *Example: Spoke VPC attachment is associated with Spoke-RT (so it uses Spoke-RT to route traffic). Spoke VPC attachment propagates into Hub-RT (so Hub-RT learns the spoke VPC CIDR). On-premises VPN attachment associated with Hub-RT, propagates into Spoke-RT. Now spoke VPCs can reach on-premises (Spoke-RT has on-premises routes via VPN propagation), and on-premises can reach spoke VPCs (Hub-RT has spoke routes via VPC propagation).<br> *Static routes override propagated routes: if you add a static route in a route table for a CIDR that is also propagated dynamically, the static route takes precedence (static > dynamic in TGW). Use static routes for traffic engineering (force specific paths), dynamic propagation for general routing.<br> *Isolation via association: if a dev VPC is associated with Dev-RT, it only sees routes in Dev-RT. If Dev-RT contains only dev VPC CIDRs + shared services, dev VPC cannot reach prod VPCs (not in Dev-RT) — even if prod VPC propagated its routes into a different route table. This is how TGW enforces network segmentation.<br>

746. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A customer reports that on-premises servers can reach some VPCs via DX but not others. DXGW is in use. What are the likely causes? ; (1) VPC CIDR not associated with DXGW — the VGW for that VPC is not associated with DXGW. (2) VPC CIDR overlaps with another associated VPC — association blocked. (3) DXGW prefix limit reached (10 VGW associations). (4) Route propagation not enabled on the VPC's subnet route tables for the VGW. (5) Security group or NACL blocking traffic within the target VPC. <br><br> *Troubleshooting order: (1) Verify all VGWs are associated with DXGW (console: DXGW → Associations tab). (2) Check DXGW BGP routes — does the DX connection advertise the target VPC CIDR to on-premises? (3) Check VPC route tables — is VGW route propagation enabled? (4) Test with EC2 instance VPC reachability tool. (5) Check NACL/SG rules.<br> *DXGW BGP route filtering: DXGW supports prefix filtering for allowed prefixes (on-premises → AWS direction). If allowed prefix list is misconfigured, some on-premises routes may not reach the target VPC. Verify allowed prefix lists match the required CIDRs.<br> *Common mistake: VPC added to AWS environment but VGW not associated with existing DXGW. Each new VPC requires: create VGW → associate with DXGW → enable route propagation. Automating this via CloudFormation/Service Catalog prevents the "new VPC unreachable" pattern.<br>

747. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the centralized egress pattern with TGW, and why would a company use it? ; Centralized egress: a dedicated Egress VPC has an Internet Gateway and NAT Gateways. All VPCs route internet-bound traffic (0.0.0.0/0) to TGW → TGW routes to Egress VPC attachment → NAT Gateway in Egress VPC → Internet Gateway → internet. No Internet Gateway or NAT Gateway is needed in each spoke VPC. <br><br> *Benefits: (1) Cost reduction — one NAT Gateway fleet instead of per-VPC NAT Gateways. NAT Gateway pricing: $0.045/hr + $0.045/GB. At 50 VPCs with low egress, paying for 50 NAT Gateways is wasteful. (2) Centralized internet policy — apply WAF, firewall, proxy in Egress VPC for all outbound traffic. (3) One Elastic IP pool for egress — consistent source IPs for allowlisting by external services.<br> *Route configuration: in each spoke VPC TGW attachment, routes for 0.0.0.0/0 → TGW. In TGW route tables: 0.0.0.0/0 → Egress VPC attachment. In Egress VPC route tables: return traffic → TGW attachment → original spoke VPC CIDRs.<br> *Trade-off: all internet egress traffic pays TGW data processing fee ($0.02/GB) + cross-AZ traffic if spoke VPC and NAT Gateway are in different AZs. For high-volume internet egress: compare centralized cost vs distributed NAT Gateway per VPC. For security/compliance that mandates centralized inspection: centralized egress is required regardless of cost.<br>

748. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the Well-Architected Security pillar recommendation for encryption of DX traffic, given that DX Private VIF is not encrypted by default? ; DX Private VIF: private connection (not traversing internet) but NOT encrypted at the link layer by default. AWS recommendation: (1) Use MACsec on DX Dedicated connections (link-layer AES-256 encryption between on-premises router and AWS DX router). (2) Alternatively: run IPSec VPN over the DX connection (Public VIF + VPN terminates on TGW/VGW) — double-encapsulation providing encryption over the private DX link. <br><br> *MACsec (IEEE 802.1AE): available on DX Dedicated connections (not hosted). MACsec encrypts all frames between the on-premises router and AWS DX edge device. End-to-end encryption at Layer 2. Key Agreement via 802.1X EAP-TLS. Requires MACsec-capable router on-premises (Cisco ASR, Juniper MX).<br> *VPN over DX: use a Public VIF (access AWS public endpoints including VPN endpoints), then establish Site-to-Site VPN. Traffic traverses DX link (private, not internet) AND is encrypted by IPSec. Works on both Dedicated and Hosted connections. Lower throughput than pure DX (IPSec overhead). Used when MACsec is not supported on on-premises hardware.<br> *Compliance contexts: PCI DSS, HIPAA, and government requirements often mandate encryption in transit even on private links. In these scenarios: MACsec or VPN-over-DX is required. Standard DX Private VIF alone does not meet encryption-in-transit requirements.<br>

749. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] When is it appropriate to use VGW instead of TGW for a new AWS deployment? ; VGW is appropriate when: (1) Only one VPC needs on-premises connectivity, (2) VPC-to-VPC routing through the gateway is not required, (3) VPN bandwidth requirement is < 1.25 Gbps, (4) No need to share connectivity across multiple AWS accounts, (5) Simple architecture with lowest cost. Single-VPC startups, isolated dev/test environments, or single-VPC departmental deployments are valid VGW use cases. <br><br> *VGW keeps simplicity: no TGW attachment fees, no per-GB processing cost, no route table management beyond the standard VPC route table. For a simple two-site (one DC + one VPC) topology: VGW + VPN is the right answer. KISS principle applies.<br> *Green flags for VGW: exam question says "single VPC", "simple connectivity", "cost-optimize small deployment", "startup", or "dev environment". VGW is correct unless the question introduces multi-VPC, multi-account, ECMP bandwidth, or VPC-to-VPC routing needs.<br> *Future-proofing concern: if growth to multi-VPC is likely, starting with TGW avoids later migration. However, premature TGW adoption incurs unnecessary cost. Decide based on documented requirements, not hypothetical future needs — you can migrate VGW → TGW later.<br>

750. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the AWS Transit Gateway peering attachment, and what traffic can flow across it? ; TGW peering creates a direct connection between two TGWs in different regions (or same region, different TGWs). Traffic between VPCs attached to TGW-1 and VPCs attached to TGW-2 can flow across the peering attachment, using static routes configured in each TGW's route table. All standard IP traffic is supported; no additional encapsulation overhead. <br><br> *Setup: TGW-1 owner sends peering request to TGW-2 owner. TGW-2 owner accepts. Peering attachment created. Each TGW owner adds static routes in their route table: TGW-1 adds "TGW-2 VPC CIDRs → peering attachment", TGW-2 adds "TGW-1 VPC CIDRs → peering attachment".<br> *Inter-region traffic pricing: data transferred over TGW peering incurs inter-region data transfer charges (same as standard AWS inter-region rates, ~$0.02/GB). No TGW peering attachment hourly fee. Budget for inter-region traffic costs when designing cross-region TGW topologies.<br> *TGW peering vs PrivateLink for cross-region service access: TGW peering exposes the full VPC CIDR to the remote region. PrivateLink exposes only a specific service endpoint. For exposing services (not full VPC-to-VPC): PrivateLink is more secure (least privilege). For full VPC interconnect: TGW peering. Choose based on whether full VPC access or service-specific access is needed.<br>

751. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the difference between a TGW attachment "association" and "propagation" action in terms of who controls the route? ; Association: the TGW attachment owner does not control this — the TGW owner (network team) associates the attachment to a specific route table, determining what routes the attachment can USE. Propagation: the TGW route table receives the attachment's routes automatically when propagation is enabled. Both association and propagation are configured by the TGW owner, not the attachment creator (for RAM-shared TGWs). <br><br> *In shared TGW scenarios: Account A creates a VPC attachment to the shared TGW. The TGW owner (network account) then: (1) associates the attachment with appropriate route table, (2) enables propagation from the attachment into relevant route tables. Account A cannot do these steps — they do not own the TGW.<br> *Automation gap: new VPC attachments from member accounts require TGW owner to manually configure association/propagation. Automate via: EventBridge rule watching for TGW attachment creation events → Lambda automatically associates and propagates new attachments based on tags (e.g., tag "Environment=prod" → associate with prod-rt, propagate into prod-rt).<br> *Route table association = one route table per attachment (strict). Propagation = one attachment can propagate into many route tables (flexible). Example: Shared Services VPC attachment propagates its CIDR into ALL route tables (prod-rt, dev-rt, staging-rt) so all environments can reach shared services.<br>

752. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] An on-premises network needs to reach a shared services VPC (DNS, AD) that is accessible to all VPCs on TGW. What is the TGW routing configuration? ; Shared Services VPC attachment propagates its CIDR into all TGW route tables (prod-rt, dev-rt, on-prem-rt). On-premises VPN/DX attachment propagates on-premises CIDR into the Shared Services RT. Shared Services VPC route table has a route for all spoke VPC CIDRs + on-premises CIDR → TGW attachment. Result: on-premises can reach Shared Services, all VPCs can reach Shared Services, but VPCs cannot reach each other through Shared Services. <br><br> *Route table per environment: prod-rt contains prod VPC CIDRs + on-premises CIDR (via propagation) + shared services CIDR (via propagation). Dev-rt contains dev VPC CIDRs + shared services CIDR (no on-premises, no prod). Shared-rt contains shared services CIDR + all spoke CIDRs + on-premises (for return traffic).<br> *Why "propagate into all route tables" for Shared Services: every spoke (prod, dev, on-prem) needs to reach shared services. Propagating shared services CIDR into all route tables ensures this without needing full routing between all spokes (isolation maintained).<br> *Return traffic: Shared Services VPC must know how to reach all callers. Its TGW attachment's associated route table (Shared-RT) must contain all spoke VPC CIDRs and on-premises CIDR. These come from propagation (each spoke propagates into Shared-RT) and on-prem VPN/DX attachment propagation.<br>

753. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the AWS Site-to-Site VPN accelerated option, and how does it differ from standard VPN? ; Accelerated VPN uses AWS Global Accelerator to route VPN traffic through AWS's global network (instead of the public internet) from the nearest AWS edge location to the TGW endpoint. Standard VPN traverses the public internet end-to-end. Accelerated VPN reduces latency and improves stability for geographically distant on-premises locations. Only available with TGW (not VGW). <br><br> *How it works: on-premises router connects VPN to the nearest AWS edge location (Anycast entry point via Global Accelerator). Traffic enters AWS backbone at the edge, traverses to the TGW region. Public internet traversal is minimized to the on-premises → nearest edge hop (much shorter).<br> *Cost: additional charge for Global Accelerator ($0.025/hr per accelerator + data transfer premium). For on-premises locations far from the TGW region (e.g., on-premises in Southeast Asia, TGW in us-east-1): improved stability and lower latency can justify the premium.<br> *When to use: geographically distant on-premises locations, high jitter on standard VPN, SLA-sensitive applications. Not needed for on-premises in the same country/region as TGW — public internet path is short and stable enough. Always benchmark standard VPN latency before paying for accelerated.<br>

754. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the AWS Cloud WAN service and how does it relate to Transit Gateway? ; AWS Cloud WAN is a managed global WAN service built on top of TGW infrastructure. It provides automated multi-region network management via a global network policy (JSON). Cloud WAN creates TGW-based segments in each region and automates route propagation across regions — eliminating the manual static route configuration required with TGW peering. Cloud WAN is the modern successor for large-scale multi-region network management. <br><br> *TGW vs Cloud WAN: TGW = regional hub, manual peering, static cross-region routes, customer manages route tables. Cloud WAN = global network, policy-driven routing, automatic cross-region BGP-like route exchange, AWS manages underlying TGW infrastructure.<br> *When to use Cloud WAN: 5+ regions with complex cross-region routing, need for automated network policy enforcement, SD-WAN integration at global scale. When to stick with TGW: 1-3 regions, stable routing requirements, team has TGW expertise and does not need global automation.<br> *Cloud WAN cost: higher than raw TGW (additional Cloud WAN management fee + TGW costs underneath). Operational savings (reduced manual route management) justify cost for large global networks. For < 3 regions: TGW with peering is simpler and cheaper.<br>

755. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company has DX with DXGW connected to 10 VPCs via VGW associations. They want to add VPC-to-VPC routing. What is the least disruptive migration path? ; (1) Create a TGW in the same region. (2) Share TGW via RAM if needed for multi-account. (3) Create VPC attachments for all 10 VPCs on the new TGW. (4) Create a new Transit VIF on the DX connection (or existing DX connection) pointing to the same DXGW. (5) Associate TGW with DXGW (replaces or supplements VGW associations). (6) Update VPC route tables to point to TGW. (7) Remove VGW associations from DXGW after verification. <br><br> *Critical constraint: running both VGW and TGW simultaneously for the same VPC during migration is possible but routing must be carefully managed. Use BGP route preferences to control which path is active. Test end-to-end on TGW before cutting over VPC route tables.<br> *DXGW can have both VGW associations and TGW associations simultaneously (during migration). Maximum: 10 VGWs + 3 TGWs per DXGW. Ensure VPC CIDRs remain non-overlapping throughout — this does not change with migration.<br> *After migration: remove VGW associations (not deleting VGWs — may need them for VPN if still in use). Update subnet route tables to use TGW attachment as next hop instead of VGW. Verify VPC-to-VPC routing works via TGW. Then detach VGWs from VPCs if no longer needed.<br>

756. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum number of static routes per TGW route table? ; 10,000 static routes per TGW route table (some sources say 10,000 static + 20,000 dynamic/propagated routes, for a total of up to 20,000). For most enterprise deployments with well-designed CIDR summarization, this limit is not reached. Design VPC CIDRs in summarized blocks to stay well under limits. <br><br> *Practical guidance: with good CIDR design (e.g., all prod VPCs in 10.0.0.0/12, all dev in 172.16.0.0/12), a single summary route covers many VPCs. You don't need one route per VPC — summary routes work if TGW has appropriate attachments. Per-VPC routes are needed only when routing must distinguish between specific VPCs (e.g., different policies).<br> *On-premises prefix limit: via BGP propagation (VPN or DX), on-premises routes propagated into TGW route tables count against dynamic route limits. With complex on-premises routing (thousands of prefixes): summarize on-premises routes before advertising to TGW. Transit VIF supports 20,000 prefixes from on-premises — more than most deployments need.<br> *Monitoring: CloudWatch metric `RoutesInRouteTable` per TGW route table ID is not natively exposed, but you can query via `aws ec2 get-transit-gateway-route-table-routes` and count. Set up automated alerts if route count approaches 80% of limit.<br>

757. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does TGW handle multi-account VPC attachment billing — who pays the attachment cost? ; The AWS account that creates the VPC attachment (the "spoke" account) pays the attachment hourly cost ($0.05/hr per attachment), not the TGW owner account. Data processing fees ($0.02/GB for traffic processed by TGW) are also charged to the attachment owner's account. The TGW owner pays only the TGW hourly fee ($0.05/hr per TGW). <br><br> *Chargeback alignment: this billing model naturally aligns costs with consumers — each team's account is billed for their VPC attachments and their traffic. Network account (TGW owner) pays only the base TGW cost (~$36/month per TGW). Application accounts pay for their usage.<br> *Cost visibility: in AWS Cost Explorer, filter by service=VPC and operation=TransitGatewayAttachment to see per-account TGW attachment costs. Enable AWS Cost Allocation Tags on TGW attachments (e.g., Environment, Team, CostCenter) for granular cost allocation.<br> *Exam trap: "who pays TGW attachment cost in a RAM-shared TGW?" → the account that CREATES the attachment (the member account that attaches their VPC), not the TGW owner account. This is opposite of many "shared resource" mental models where the owner pays.<br>

758. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What monitoring metrics should you use to detect TGW performance issues? ; Key TGW CloudWatch metrics: (1) `BytesIn`/`BytesOut` per attachment — traffic volume. (2) `PacketsIn`/`PacketsOut` — packet rate. (3) `PacketDropCountBlackhole` — traffic hitting blackhole routes (misconfiguration indicator). (4) `PacketDropCountNoRoute` — traffic with no matching route (routing gap). (5) `BytesDropCountBlackhole`/`BytesDropCountNoRoute` — byte volume dropped. Alarm on PacketDropCount* metrics. <br><br> *PacketDropCountBlackhole: indicates traffic matches a route pointing to a deleted or inactive attachment (blackhole route). This is a routing misconfiguration. Audit and clean up blackhole static routes immediately. Source attachment and destination prefix identify the affected traffic flow.<br> *PacketDropCountNoRoute: traffic arrived at TGW with a source/destination combination not matching any route in the associated route table. Indicates missing route — check VPC CIDR propagation, missing static routes, or incorrect route table association for the attachment.<br> *VPN-specific metrics: `TunnelState` (0=down, 1=up) per VPN tunnel. `TunnelDataIn`/`TunnelDataOut` per tunnel. Set alarm on `TunnelState = 0` for any production VPN tunnel — triggers immediate investigation and potential failover verification.<br>

759. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] In a Well-Architected review, a customer has 30 VPCs all peered in a full mesh. What is the recommendation and migration approach? ; Replace full-mesh VPC peering with TGW. 30 VPCs in full mesh = 435 peering connections. TGW reduces this to 30 VPC attachments. Migration: (1) Create TGW. (2) Create VPC attachments for all VPCs. (3) Enable route propagation in TGW route table. (4) For each VPC, update subnet route tables: add routes to other VPCs via TGW (alongside existing peering routes). (5) Verify traffic. (6) Remove peering connections one by one. (7) Remove old peering routes from subnet route tables. <br><br> *Why peering does not scale: 30 VPCs with full mesh = every VPC admin manages 29 peering connections. Adding VPC #31 requires 30 new peering connections. TGW: adding VPC #31 = 1 new TGW attachment. Operational complexity grows linearly vs quadratically.<br> *Migration risk: full-mesh peering → TGW changes the routing path. Traffic that previously went directly VPC-A → VPC-B (peering, no TGW processing fee) now goes VPC-A → TGW → VPC-B ($0.02/GB TGW processing). For high-volume VPC-to-VPC traffic: cost may increase. Run cost analysis before migration.<br> *Parallel routing during migration: both peering and TGW routes can coexist temporarily. Use more specific TGW routes to gradually shift traffic, VPC by VPC. Remove peering connections only after verifying TGW route for that VPC pair is working correctly.<br>

760. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the significance of the TGW "default route table" and "default route association" settings? ; When creating a TGW, "Default Route Table Association" (enabled by default) means all new attachments are automatically associated with the default route table. "Default Route Table Propagation" (enabled by default) means all new attachments automatically propagate their routes into the default route table. This gives full any-to-any connectivity by default — every new VPC/VPN/DX attachment can immediately reach all other attachments. <br><br> *Disabling defaults for security: for organizations requiring network segmentation, disable default association and propagation at TGW creation. Then manually associate each attachment with the appropriate route table and configure propagation selectively. This prevents accidental full connectivity when a new VPC is attached.<br> *Cannot change on existing TGW: default route table settings cannot be modified after TGW creation. Plan the routing model BEFORE creating the TGW. For a new enterprise TGW: disable defaults if segmentation is needed. For a simple hub-and-spoke with no isolation needed: keep defaults for ease of use.<br> *Exam question: "A new VPC attachment is created to a shared TGW. The TGW has default association and propagation enabled. Does the new VPC automatically have full connectivity to all other VPCs?" → Yes, automatically (until an admin changes route table configuration). This behavior is why security-conscious organizations disable TGW defaults.<br>

761. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the difference between a "hosted VIF" and a "dedicated VIF" in the context of DX connections to DXGW or VGW? ; A hosted VIF is provisioned by a DX Partner on a hosted connection. The partner shares bandwidth from their port. A dedicated VIF is provisioned by the customer on a dedicated connection (1/10/100 Gbps) where they own the entire port. Both types of VIFs (Private, Transit, Public) can be created on either connection type. However, MACsec is only available on dedicated connections. <br><br> *VIF capacity: dedicated connection VIFs can use the full port bandwidth. Hosted connection VIFs have a provisioned bandwidth (50 Mbps to 10 Gbps) set by the partner. You cannot exceed the provisioned bandwidth on a hosted connection.<br> *Sub-1Gbps requirement: if you need <1 Gbps DX (e.g., 200 Mbps for a small branch), you MUST use a hosted connection (dedicated connections start at 1 Gbps). This determines the connection type independent of the VIF type.<br> *For DXGW with Transit VIF: both dedicated and hosted connections can have a Transit VIF. Use dedicated for high-bandwidth (10/100 Gbps) enterprise deployments. Use hosted for smaller branch offices or cost-sensitive scenarios where sub-1Gbps is acceptable.<br>

762. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] How does the VGW handle a Site-to-Site VPN connection where the on-premises router does not support BGP (static routing only)? ; Configure the VPN connection with static routing. Manually enter the on-premises CIDR blocks in the VPN connection configuration (static routes). AWS side: manually add static routes in the VPC route table pointing to VGW for those on-premises CIDRs. Without BGP: no automatic failover — if the active tunnel fails, you need to manually update routes or the backup tunnel must be pre-configured with the same static routes. <br><br> *Static routing limitations: no automatic prefix exchange, no automatic failover via BGP convergence. Failover from active to passive tunnel still happens at the IKE layer (tunnel detection), but route table updates must be manual or scripted (e.g., Lambda + CloudWatch alarm on tunnel state).<br> *When static routing is used: legacy on-premises routers without BGP capability, very simple environments where BGP is an unnecessary complexity. For any production workload: BGP dynamic routing is strongly recommended for automatic failover and route management.<br> *TGW static routing: TGW also supports static routing for VPN connections. Same limitations apply — no automatic BGP-driven failover. ECMP is not available with static routing on TGW (ECMP requires BGP). Static routing with TGW gives connectivity but loses the ECMP bandwidth benefits.<br>

763. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is a "customer gateway" in the context of AWS Site-to-Site VPN, and is it the same as VGW? ; Customer Gateway (CGW) is an AWS resource representing the on-premises VPN device (router/firewall). It stores the public IP address and optional ASN of the on-premises device. It is NOT the VGW — CGW represents the on-premises side, VGW/TGW represents the AWS side. The Site-to-Site VPN connection links the CGW to the VGW or TGW. <br><br> *One CGW per on-premises device: create one CGW for each physical on-premises VPN router. If you have two on-premises routers (for HA): create two CGWs. Connect each CGW to the VGW or TGW with separate VPN connections for redundancy.<br> *CGW with dynamic IPs: if the on-premises router has a dynamic public IP (not recommended for production), AWS supports a CGW with a dynamic IP if you use certificate-based authentication (instead of pre-shared key). For stable production environments: use a static public IP on the on-premises VPN device.<br> *CGW device configuration: AWS provides downloadable configuration files for common CGW devices (Cisco ASA, Cisco IOS, Juniper, Palo Alto, etc.) after creating the VPN connection. These configs include the tunnel IPs, pre-shared keys, and BGP settings for the on-premises device.<br>

764. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] A company needs to access an AWS service endpoint (e.g., S3, DynamoDB) from on-premises via DX without traversing the internet. What DX configuration achieves this? ; Use a Public VIF on the DX connection. Public VIF provides access to all AWS public services (S3, DynamoDB, SNS, SQS, CloudWatch, etc.) using their public endpoints over the DX connection instead of the internet. Traffic exits the DX link at the DX PoP and enters the AWS backbone to reach the public service endpoint. <br><br> *Public VIF does not use DXGW: Public VIFs connect directly to AWS's public IP space — no DXGW or VGW needed. Create a Public VIF, advertise your on-premises public IP blocks (for source IP filtering), and receive AWS service IP prefixes via BGP.<br> *Alternative for private endpoint access: for S3 and DynamoDB via Private VIF (using private IPs, not public endpoints): create VPC Gateway Endpoints (S3, DynamoDB) in the VPC. Traffic from on-premises → Private VIF → VGW/TGW → VPC → Gateway Endpoint → S3/DynamoDB. This uses private addressing and never touches AWS public IPs.<br> *Exam distinction: "access S3 from on-premises via DX using public IPs" → Public VIF. "Access S3 from on-premises via DX using private IPs/VPC Endpoint" → Private VIF + VPC Gateway Endpoint. These are different architectures with different routing and security characteristics.<br>

765. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the maximum number of VPN connections per Transit Gateway? ; 5,000 Site-to-Site VPN connections per TGW. Each VPN connection is to a unique Customer Gateway. This enables massive scale — thousands of branch offices or partner sites can connect to a single TGW. Compare to VGW: up to 10 VPN connections per VGW. <br><br> *5,000 VPN connections use case: large enterprise with thousands of branch offices, retail chains (each store = one VPN to TGW), or managed service providers connecting customer networks. The TGW VPN model scales to telco-grade connectivity requirements.<br> *BGP session management at scale: 5,000 VPN connections = up to 10,000 BGP sessions (2 tunnels per connection). TGW manages all BGP sessions. Monitor memory and CPU on TGW is not applicable (AWS manages TGW infrastructure) — but on-premises network management of 5,000 VPN configs requires automation (HashiCorp Terraform, AWS CloudFormation).<br> *Per-tunnel bandwidth: each of the 5,000 VPN connections has 1.25 Gbps per tunnel. With ECMP across 2 tunnels: ~2.5 Gbps per VPN connection. For branch offices: this is ample. Aggregate TGW bandwidth scales with the number of connections.<br>

766. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] An architect proposes using VPC peering for connectivity between a shared services VPC and 50 spoke VPCs, with all VPCs in one region. Why is TGW a better choice? ; 50 VPCs peered to shared services = 50 peering connections on the shared services VPC (and 1 on each spoke). With VPC peering, spoke VPCs cannot communicate with each other (peering is not transitive). If spoke-to-spoke communication is ever needed, the peering count explodes. TGW provides transitive routing, scales to 5,000 attachments, and replaces 50 peering connections with 51 TGW attachments (50 spokes + 1 shared services). <br><br> *Route table scaling: 50 VPC peering connections = 50 entries in the shared services VPC route table (one per spoke CIDR). TGW: shared services VPC attachment propagation handles this automatically. No per-spoke manual route table entries in shared services VPC.<br> *Operational simplicity: adding VPC #51 with peering = create new peering connection, update both route tables, update security groups. Adding VPC #51 with TGW = create TGW attachment. Route propagation handles routing automatically. Security group rules remain the same (allow from TGW CIDR or use VPC CIDRs).<br> *Cost comparison: 50 peering connections = no hourly fee, only DTO costs. 51 TGW attachments = 51 × $0.05/hr = $2.55/hr (~$1,836/month) + $0.02/GB processing. If traffic volume is high, TGW processing cost is significant. For shared services VPC pattern with <10 spokes and no transitive routing: peering may be cheaper. For 50+ spokes: TGW operational savings justify cost.<br>

767. [ VGW-DCG-TGW ] What is the role of the VGW in a software VPN (e.g., Cisco CSR, Palo Alto VM-Series) deployment on EC2, vs the AWS-managed VGW? ; Software VPN on EC2 replaces VGW entirely — the EC2 instance running the VPN software is the VPN termination point. No VGW is used. Pros: full feature control (custom protocols, advanced routing, third-party firewall integration). Cons: you manage EC2 HA, patching, scaling. AWS VGW is managed by AWS (HA, failover, patching included). Use software VPN for advanced requirements not met by AWS VGW/TGW; use AWS-managed VGW/TGW for standard IPSec/BGP needs. <br><br> *Software VPN EC2 as TGW Connect peer: software VPN on EC2 can be a TGW Connect (GRE) peer — this is the TGW Connect SD-WAN integration pattern. The EC2 NVA connects to TGW via GRE (TGW Connect), handles encryption itself, and connects to on-premises via its own VPN/MPLS.<br> *HA for software VPN: use EC2 Auto Scaling Group (min 2, in 2 AZs), Network Load Balancer, and ECMP/ECMP-capable routing to distribute VPN traffic. Recovery from EC2 failure: Auto Scaling replaces the failed instance. BGP failover: on-premises detects BGP session drop and fails over to surviving instance. More complex than AWS-managed VGW failover.<br> *When software VPN wins: custom tunneling protocols (DMVPN, GETVPN), deep packet inspection at the VPN endpoint, specific firewall policy at VPN termination, integration with security platforms that don't have AWS VGW connectors, or existing licensing justifying the appliance approach.<br>
